# Lexicon

English headword, the French form as the book spells it, how the English was arrived at, and what the book says. References point to the French text (section ¶ paragraph, n footnote).

## A

### A. Bonnetty — *A. Bonnetty* {#e211}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Struck by the mystery of the Nativity narratives and unable to ascribe them to a celestial phenomenon, he asks who the Magi were and what to think of the star, blaming rationalism and ontologism for having made forgotten how the ancient peoples of the East kept the primitive traditions [INTRO.7 ¶29–31]. Source: Documents historiques sur la Religion des Romains, t. II, p. 564 [INTRO.7 n10].

> «Qui sont ces Mages, et que faut-il penser de cette étoile ?» (*Who are these Magi, and what are we to think of this star?*) [INTRO.7 ¶30]

### Abbo — *Abbon* {#e284}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; Abbo is the usual English form.*

Author of a poem on the siege of Paris by the Normans, in which, the text says, he reports several features attesting the marvellous properties of the water of the well of Saint-Germain-des-Prés [PARIS.2 ¶21].

> «Abbon, dans son poème sur le siège de Paris par les Normands, rapporte plusieurs traits qui attestent les propriétés merveilleuses de l’eau du puits de Saint-Germain-des-Prés» (*Abbo, in his poem on the siege of Paris by the Normans, reports several features attesting the marvellous properties of the water of the well of Saint-Germain-des-Prés*) [PARIS.2 ¶21]

### Abbé Ansault — *abbé Ansault* {#e154}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of La Croix avant Jésus-Christ (Paris, V. Rétaux, 1894), cited as amply proving that the cross is an ancient symbol of all religions, not specially Christian [INTRO.4 ¶4, INTRO.4 n1].

> «La croix est un symbole fort ancien, employé de tous temps, en toutes religions, chez tous les peuples, et l’on aurait tort de le considérer comme un emblème spécial au christianisme, ainsi que le démontre surabondamment l’abbé Ansault.» (*The cross is a very ancient symbol, used at all times, in all religions, among all peoples, and one would be wrong to consider it an emblem peculiar to Christianity, as abbé Ansault amply demonstrates.*) [INTRO.4 ¶4]

### Abbé Roze — *abbé Roze* {#e444}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named among those who read the Amiens wheel-fire as Ezekiel's vision [AMIENS ¶4]. Durand prefers Roze's name Apostasy for the relief of the man leaving a church [AMIENS ¶11].

> «il nous semble que le mot Apostasie proposé par l’abbé Roze convient mieux au sujet représenté.» (*it seems to us that the word Apostasy proposed by the abbé Roze fits the subject better.*) [AMIENS ¶11]

### Abbé Vilain — *abbé Vilain* {#e427}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited for the small portal of Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie, made by Flamel in 1389 and covered with figures, with an angel holding a stone circle in which Flamel set a black marble disc with a gold cross [PARIS.8 ¶2]. His work is the Histoire critique de Nicolas Flamel, Paris, Desprez, 1761 [PARIS.8 n1].

> «L’abbé Vilain nous apprend que le petit portail de Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie, que l’Adepte fit exécuter en 1389, était couvert de figures.» (*The abbé Vilain tells us that the small portal of Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie, which the Adept had made in 1389, was covered with figures.*) [PARIS.8 ¶2]

### Abraham the Jew — *Abraham le Juif* {#e278}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of the book of figures that came to Flamel [PARIS.3 ¶17; PARIS.2 ¶16]. The text says he was also known as Eléazar and that he used the symbol of the serpent coiled on a golden rod, the image of the philosophers' Mercury [PARIS.3 ¶17]. His third figure (hollow oak and fountain) is compared to the Paris relief and to a figure of Trismosin [PARIS.2 ¶17–18, ¶20].

> «Abraham le Juif, connu aussi sous le nom d’Eléazar, en fit usage dans le livre qui échut à Flamel» (*Abraham the Jew, also known by the name of Eleazar, made use of it in the book that fell to Flamel*) [PARIS.3 ¶17]

### Absolute — *Absolu* {#e181}

*term; Literal translation.*

The name by which ancient alchemists designated the philosophers' stone; the text notes it is close to Absolum, name of the Cnossos labyrinth [INTRO.5 ¶7]. On a ceiling panel of the Hôtel Lallemant chapel, the dead serpent below is mercury and the seal of Solomon on top of the ingots is the Absolute: a method of one way, one matter, one operation [BOURGES.2 ¶24, BOURGES.2 ¶28].

> «Or, nous ferons remarquer que ce terme est voisin d’Absolu, qui est le nom par lequel les alchimistes anciens désignaient la pierre philosophale.» (*Now we shall point out that this term is close to Absolu, which is the name by which the ancient alchemists designated the philosophers' stone.*) [INTRO.5 ¶7]

> «En bas, le mercure ; en haut, l’Absolu.» (*Below, the mercury; above, the Absolute.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶28]

Identified in the text with: [Philosophers' stone](#e215)

### Adept — *Adepte* {#e006}

*term; Usual English term.*

Interpreted: one who has achieved the Work (Canseliet speaks of the event that forced the Adept to flee the world [PREF1 ¶4]). The Phrygian cap is the attribute of 'Adeptat' (adepthood) on the alchemist of Notre-Dame [PARIS.1 ¶14]; a note calls it the distinctive sign of the Initiates [PARIS.1 n4]. Flamel is called an Adept [PARIS.2 ¶16]; Jacques Cœur had the reputation of a proven Adept [BOURGES.1 ¶3]. The Adept, his wish fulfilled, keeps his vow of silence: 'the Adept must forever / keep silent' [CONCL ¶14, CONCL ¶15, CONCL ¶16]. Canseliet: the new Adept aligns his destiny with his rare predecessors, like beacons along the road, and casts off the 'old man', leaving only the trace of a name [PREF2 ¶3]; the Adept flees the world's homage [PREF1 ¶4].

> «Coiffé du bonnet phrygien, attribut de l’Adeptat négligemment posé sur la longue chevelure aux boucles épaisses, le savant, serré dans la cape légère du laboratoire, s’appuie d’une main sur la balustrade» (*Wearing the Phrygian cap, attribute of Adepthood, set carelessly on the long thick-curled hair, the sage, wrapped in the light laboratory cape, leans one hand on the balustrade*) [PARIS.1 ¶14]

> «Dans la Science, dans le Bien, l’Adepte doit à jamais» (*In Science, in Good, the Adept must forever*) [CONCL ¶15]

### Aegidius de Vadis — *Ægidius de Vadis* {#e465}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited, inside the commentary on La Lumière sortant des Ténèbres quoted by Fulcanelli, as showing in his Dialogue de la Nature that a portion of the first Chaos remains in this world, known but despised by all and sold publicly [AMIENS ¶28].

> «Ægidius de Vadis, dans son Dialogue de la Nature, fait voir clairement et comme en lettres d’or qu’il est resté, dans ce monde, une portion de ce premier Chaos, connue, mais méprisée d’un chacun, et qui se vend publiquement.» (*Aegidius de Vadis, in his Dialogue of Nature, shows clearly and as in letters of gold that there has remained, in this world, a portion of that first Chaos, known but despised by everyone, and sold publicly.*) [AMIENS ¶28]

### Aeneas — *Enée* {#e197}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Enée; Æneas in the Latin).*

In the legend recalled by Varro, Aeneas saves his father and household gods from the flames of Troy and after long wanderings reaches the fields of Laurentum, end of his voyage [INTRO.7 ¶1]. From Troy onward he saw the star of Venus every day and by day until the Laurentine fields, where it disappeared, which told him these were the lands appointed by Fate [INTRO.7 ¶2]. The legend opens the series of texts on the star [INTRO.6 ¶15].

> «Varron, dans ses Antiquitates rerum humanarum, rappelle la légende d’Enée, sauvant son père et ses pénates des flammes de Troie, et aboutissant, après de longues pérégrinations, aux champs de Laurente, terme de son voyage.» (*Varro, in his Antiquitates rerum humanarum, recalls the legend of Aeneas, saving his father and his household gods from the flames of Troy, and arriving, after long wanderings, at the fields of Laurentum, the end of his journey.*) [INTRO.7 ¶1]

### Albert Poisson — *Albert Poisson* {#e429}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Called 'the famous occultist' [PARIS.8 ¶2]. Cited for Flamel's chapel of the Saint-Gervais hospital, covered with figures, and for the escutcheon on Flamel's first arcade at the charnel house of the Innocents, which he says imitates one attributed to saint Thomas Aquinas and appears at the end of Lagneau's Harmonie Chymique [PARIS.8 ¶2]; his description of it follows [PARIS.8 ¶3]. He gives a fire base of 50° rising to about 300° centigrade for the coction [AMIENS ¶8]. His work is Histoire de l'Alchimie. Nicolas Flamel, Paris, Chacornac, 1893 [PARIS.8 n2].

> «Albert Poisson donne la base de 50° avec augmentation progressive jusque vers 300° centigrades.» (*Albert Poisson gives a base of 50° with progressive increase up to about 300° centigrade.*) [AMIENS ¶8]

### Alchemical Fortress — *Forteresse alchimique* {#e457}

*term; Literal translation.*

On the north (Saint-Firmin) portal of Amiens a quatrefoil shows a citadel with a massive bolted door, crenellated towers, two storeys and a barred vent [AMIENS ¶16]. Fulcanelli asks whether it symbolizes the esotericism of the other quatrefoils, or is the mother idea of the 'Forteresse alchimique' taken up and modified by Khunrath in 1609, or the closed Palace of the king of the Art spoken of by Basile Valentin and Philalèthe; citadel or royal dwelling, it seems built to keep a treasure or secret, entered only with the key [AMIENS ¶17]. Elsewhere the Philosophers' obscurity is a wall raised as the enclosure of their citadel [PARIS.2 ¶6], and alchemical furnaces are drawn as keeps or small fortresses [AMIENS ¶13; PARIS.2 ¶25].

> «Ou bien devons-nous voir, en ce motif de l’an 1225, l’idée mère de la Forteresse alchimique, reprise et modifiée par Khunrath en 1609 ?» (*Or should we see, in this motif of the year 1225, the mother idea of the Alchemical Fortress, taken up and modified by Khunrath in 1609?*) [AMIENS ¶17]

> «Bâti pour conserver quelque trésor ou recéler quelque important secret, il semble qu’on ne puisse y pénétrer qu’en possédant la clef des puissantes serrures qui le garantissent contre toute effraction.» (*Built to keep some treasure or conceal some important secret, it seems one can enter it only by possessing the key to the powerful locks that guard it against any break-in.*) [AMIENS ¶17]

### Alchemical stove of Winterthur — *poêle alchimique de Winterthur* {#e495}

*place; Name as given in the text.*

Stove painted in 1702 by P. H. Pfau, kept at the Winterthur museum (Switzerland) [BOURGES.2 ¶14; BOURGES.2 n6]; its paintings show an angler and a fish [BOURGES.2 ¶14] and the straw beehive with its bees, a subject also on the Lallemant chapel ceiling [BOURGES.2 ¶28].

> «Non loin de là, une ruche commune, en paille, est figurée entourée de ses abeilles, sujet fréquemment reproduit, particulièrement sur le poêle alchimique de Winterthur.» (*Not far off, a common straw beehive is shown surrounded by its bees, a subject often reproduced, particularly on the alchemical stove of Winterthur.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶28]

## T

### The alchemist of Notre-Dame — *alchimiste de Notre-Dame* {#e261}

*place; Literal translation of the text's own name for the figure.*

A large stone old man on the second gallery of Notre-Dame de Paris, near the central axis at the re-entrant angle of the north tower, among the chimeras (pl. III) [PARIS.1 ¶13]. He wears the Phrygian cap, attribute of Adeptship, and a laboratory cape; one hand on the balustrade, the other stroking his beard; he does not meditate but observes, in extreme emotion and surprise [PARIS.1 ¶14]. Fulcanelli reads him as the old master watching the evolution of mineral life and at last beholding the marvel his faith foresaw [PARIS.1 ¶15]. Notre-Dame thus 'keeps its alchemist' [PARIS.1 ¶12].

> «Il ne médite pas, il observe.» (*He is not meditating, he is observing.*) [PARIS.1 ¶14]

> «Quelle splendide figure que celle du vieux maître qui scrute, interroge, anxieux et attentif, l’évolution de la vie minérale, puis contemple enfin, ébloui, le prodige que sa foi seule lui laissait entrevoir !» (*What a splendid figure is this old master who scrutinizes and questions, anxious and attentive, the evolution of mineral life, then at last contemplates, dazzled, the marvel his faith alone let him glimpse!*) [PARIS.1 ¶15]

## A

### Alchemy — *alchimie* {#e068}

*term; Usual English term.*

The science the book finds in the monuments. At Notre-Dame alchemy is a woman on a throne with sceptre, two books, one closed (esotericism), one open (exotericism), and the nine-step ladder [PARIS.1 ¶3]; the cathedral rests on alchemical science, which studies the transformations of the original substance [PARIS.1 ¶5]. It is as positive and exact as optics or geometry and must be purified of the stains its own followers left [PARIS.7 ¶29]. It was also called 'celestial Agriculture', its Adepts 'Labourers' [PARIS.4 n2]. Canseliet: for man, alchemy is the search for and awakening of the Life asleep under the envelope of beings, a purification to perfection on the planes of matter and spirit [PREF3 ¶11].

> «La science que nous étudions est aussi positive, aussi réelle, aussi exacte que l’optique, la géométrie ou la mécanique ; ses résultats aussi tangibles que ceux de la chimie.» (*The science we study is as positive, as real, as exact as optics, geometry or mechanics; its results as tangible as those of chemistry.*) [PARIS.7 ¶29]

> «L’alchimie portait encore le nom d’Agriculture céleste, et ses Adeptes celui de Laboureurs.» (*Alchemy also bore the name of celestial Agriculture, and its Adepts that of Labourers.*) [PARIS.4 n2]

### Alchimie — Alchemy — *Alchimie* {#e025}

*work; Name as spelled in the text; English title is our literal translation.*

Book cited in notes: 'Alchimie, p. 137. J.-J. Pauvert éditeur', for Canseliet's study on the Golden Fleece [PREF2 n1, PREF2 ¶20]; cited again on the Golden Fleece fable [BOURGES.2 n10] and on the St Marcel pillar dragon [PARIS.7 n9]. The text implies but does not state that it is Canseliet's book [PREF2 ¶20].

> «Conf. Alchimie, op. cit.» (*Cf. Alchimie, op. cit.*) [BOURGES.2 n10]

### Alkahest — *Alkaest* {#e340}

*term; The usual English term for the text's 'Alkaest'.*

A name some gave to the dissolvent whose first magnetic agent is the Green Lion [PARIS.4 ¶17]; the grammar leaves open whether 'Alkaest' names the dissolvent or that agent. Stated: the 'servus fugitivus', a mineral water, solid, brittle, stone-like, easily liquefied, this coagulated water, is the Alkahest and the universal solvent [PARIS.5 ¶7]. The dissolution of alchemical gold by the Alkahest solvent marks the first way; that of common gold by 'our mercury' the second [PARIS.5 ¶14]. Identified with the universal solvent (e321).

> «La dissolution de l’or alchimique par le Dissolvant Alkaest caractérise la première voie ; celle de l’or vulgaire par notre mercure indique la seconde.» (*The dissolution of alchemical gold by the Alkahest Solvent marks the first way; that of common gold by our mercury indicates the second.*) [PARIS.5 ¶14]

Identified in the text with: [Universal solvent](#e321)

### Aludel — *aludel* {#e407}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

Named only in Grillot de Givry's passage: the bishop of the right portal of Notre-Dame is perched on the aludel where the philosophical mercury, chained in limbo, is sublimed [PARIS.7 ¶2].

> «l’évêque juché sur l’aludel où se sublime, enchaîné dans les limbes, le mercure philosophal.» (*the bishop perched on the aludel where the philosophical mercury, chained in limbo, is sublimed.*) [PARIS.7 ¶2]

### Amyraut — *Amyraut* {#e163}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of a Paraphrase of the First Epistle of Saint Peter (Saumur, 1646) [INTRO.4 n2], quoted on the stone rejected by the builders that became the 'maistresse pierre du coin' [INTRO.4 ¶6].

> «« La pierre que les édifians ont rejettée, écrit Amyraut, a esté faite la maistresse pierre du coin, sur qui repose toute la structure du bastiment» (*'The stone that the builders rejected,' writes Amyraut, 'has been made the master stone of the corner, on which the whole structure of the building rests*) [INTRO.4 ¶6]

### Amédée de Ponthieu — *Amédée de Ponthieu* {#e239}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of Légendes du Vieux Paris [INTRO.9 n1]. Fulcanelli calls his explanation of Monsieur Legris the best, valuable because he was no hermetist and judged without bias [INTRO.9 ¶10]; he describes the sacred monolith Phœbigène, Maître Pierre, messire Legris [INTRO.9 ¶11–13]. The legend of Saint Christopher is transcribed from him, after Jacobus de Voragine [BOURGES.2 ¶9; BOURGES.2 n3].

> «La meilleure explication est celle que nous en fournit Amédée de Ponthieu, et elle nous semble d’autant plus digne d’intérêt que l’auteur, qui n’était point hermétiste, juge sans parti pris et prononce sans idée préconçue» (*The best explanation is the one Amédée de Ponthieu gives us, and it seems all the more worthy of interest since the author, who was no hermetist, judges without bias and pronounces without preconceived idea*) [INTRO.9 ¶10]

### Ancienne Guerre des Chevaliers — The Ancient War of the Knights — *Ancienne Guerre des Chevaliers* {#e309}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

Work quoted for the 'poisoned worm that infects everything with its venom', which the text identifies with the serpent of Mercury [PARIS.3 ¶18]. The note says it was augmented with a commentary by Limojon de Saint-Didier in the Triomphe hermétique [PARIS.3 n8].

> «C’est là ce « ver empoisonné qui infecte tout par son venin », dont parle l’Ancienne Guerre des Chevaliers.» (*That is the 'poisoned worm which infects everything with its venom' of which the Ancient War of the Knights speaks.*) [PARIS.3 ¶18]

### Animated Mercury — *Mercure animé* {#e352}

*term; Literal translation.*

Stated: the single body, of middle quality and homogeneous substance, that results when the eagle (volatile) and the lion (fixed) have fought until the eagle loses its wings and the lion its head [PARIS.4 ¶11]. It is achieved by the second way, the dissolution of common gold by 'our mercury' [PARIS.5 ¶14]. In the animation of mercury the gold or king is dissolved little by little without violence [PARIS.5 ¶12]. The dog and doves of the Paris north porch appear in the animation of the exalted mercury [PARIS.6 ¶12].

> «jusqu’à ce qu’enfin l’aigle ayant perdu ses ailes, et le lion son chef, les antagonistes ne fassent plus qu’un même corps, de qualité moyenne et de substance homogène, le Mercure animé.» (*until at last, the eagle having lost its wings and the lion its head, the antagonists make only one body, of middle quality and homogeneous substance, the Animated Mercury.*) [PARIS.4 ¶11]

### Ansate cross (ankh) — *croix ansée* {#e156}

*term; The usual English term; the text gives the reading ank [INTRO.4 ¶4].*

With the apse added to the choir, the plan of the great churches takes the form of the Egyptian ansate cross, read ank, which designates the universal Life hidden in things; example on a Christian sarcophagus from Saint-Honorat at Arles, in the Saint-Germain-en-Laye museum [INTRO.4 ¶4]. Its hermetic equivalent is the emblem of Venus or Cypris, common copper [INTRO.4 ¶4].

> «épouse la forme du signe hiératique égyptien de la croix ansée, qui se lit ank, et désigne la Vie universelle cachée dans les choses.» (*takes the shape of the Egyptian hieratic sign of the ansate cross, which reads ank and designates the universal Life hidden in things.*) [INTRO.4 ¶4]

### Antiquitates rerum humanarum — Antiquities of Human Things — *Antiquitates rerum humanarum* {#e196}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.*

Work of Varro in which he recalls the legend of Aeneas saving his father and his household gods from the flames of Troy and reaching, after long wanderings, the fields of Laurentum; Venus's star, seen every day and by day, vanished there, showing him these were the lands appointed by Fate [INTRO.7 ¶1–2]. The French gloss is the book's.

> «Depuis son départ de Troie, il vit tous les jours et pendant le jour, l’étoile de Vénus, jusqu’à ce qu’il arrivât aux champs Laurentins, où il cessa de la voir» (*From his departure from Troy he saw every day, and during the day, the star of Venus, until he reached the Laurentine fields, where he ceased to see it*) [INTRO.7 ¶2]

### Aphorismes Basiliens — Basilian Aphorisms — *Aphorismes Basiliens* {#e379}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work also called Canons Hermétiques de l'Esprit et de l'Ame, printed after the Oeuvres of R. P. de Castaigne, Paris, de la Nove, 1681 [PARIS.5 ¶6; PARIS.5 n3]. Its canon XVI is cited on the weights: the work begins with the conjunction of the three principles in a proportion where the body should equal the spirit and soul nearly by half [PARIS.5 ¶6].

> «nous commençons notre oeuvre hermétique par la conjonction des trois principes préparés sous une certaine proportion, laquelle consiste au poids du corps, qui doit égaler l’esprit et l’âme presque de sa moitié.» (*we begin our hermetic work by the conjunction of the three principles prepared in a certain proportion, which lies in the weight of the body, which must equal the spirit and the soul almost by half.*) [PARIS.5 ¶6]

### Apocalypse — The Apocalypse — *Apocalypse* {#e535}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Its ch. IV, v. 6–7 (the four animals before the throne) is quoted with Ezekiel for the four ages [HENDAYE ¶13; HENDAYE n3]. The S of the Hendaye cross is a theoretical image of the beast of the Apocalypse, the dragon vomiting fire and sulphur at the Judgment [HENDAYE ¶9]. The alchemical books are closed like the Apocalypse by cabalistic seals, to be broken one by one [PARIS.3 ¶34].

> «Vos livres sont fermés, comme celui de l’Apocalypse, par des sceaux cabalistiques. Il vous faut les briser un à un.» (*Your books are closed, like that of the Apocalypse, by cabalistic seals. You must break them one by one.*) [PARIS.3 ¶34]

> «C’est une image théorique de la bête de l’Apocalypse, du dragon qui vomit, aux jours du Jugement, le feu et le soufre sur la création macrocosmique.» (*It is a theoretical image of the beast of the Apocalypse, of the dragon that spews, in the days of Judgment, fire and sulphur over the macrocosmic creation.*) [HENDAYE ¶9]

### Apollo — *Apollon* {#e483}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; English the usual form.*

In Callimachus, quoted for the sublimations, the swans circled Delos seven times and Apollo was born before the eighth song [PARIS.4 ¶5–9]. Apollo killing the monster Python with arrows is one of the fables of the combat whose chemical bodies yield the secret dissolvent [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. Apollo and Diana appear as brother and sister whose union makes the compost or philosophic rebis [INTRO.3 n4].

> «C’est Cadmos perçant le serpent contre un chêne ; Apollon tuant à coups de flèches le monstre Python et Jason le dragon de Colchide» (*It is Cadmos piercing the serpent against an oak; Apollo killing the monster Python with arrows, and Jason the dragon of Colchis*) [BOURGES.1 ¶9]

### Apollonius of Tyana — *Apollonios de Tyane* {#e143}

*person; Name as spelled in the text, with the usual English form.*

Said to share the Language of the Birds [INTRO.3 ¶9]. The note calls him a philosopher whose legendary life seems very hypothetical; his name seems only a mytho-hermetic image of the compost or philosophical rebis, made by the union of brother and sister, Gabritius and Beya, Apollo and Diana; hence the wonders told by Philostratus are chemical [INTRO.3 n4].

> «Le nom de ce personnage quasi fabuleux ne nous paraît être qu’une image mytho-hermétique du compost, ou rebis philosophal, réalisé par l’union du frère et de la sœur, de Gabritius et de Beya, d’Apollon et de Diane.» (*The name of this almost fabulous person seems to us only a mytho-hermetic image of the compost, or philosophical rebis, made by the union of the brother and the sister, of Gabritius and Beya, of Apollo and Diana.*) [INTRO.3 n4]

### Arcanum — *arcane* {#e011}

*term; Usual English term.*

A hidden secret of the Work. Fulcanelli hopes to show that the meaning of the arcanum hidden under the stone bark of the cathedral can be recovered [INTRO.2 ¶8]; the old masters covered the arcanum with a thick veil by mixing the qualities of the substances across the four black phases [PARIS.3 ¶6]. Canseliet: the key of the 'major arcanum' is given by one of the book's figures and consists in a colour shown to the artisan at the first work [PREF1 ¶11]; the anonymous letter says the arcanum cannot be found by reason alone [PREF2 ¶8]; the star is a 'marvellous arcanum' [PREF2 ¶20].

> «montrer aux amateurs de l’occulte qu’il n’est pas impossible de retrouver le sens de l’arcane dissimulé sous l’écorce pétrifiée du prodigieux grimoire.» (*show lovers of the occult that it is not impossible to recover the meaning of the arcanum hidden under the petrified bark of the prodigious grimoire.*) [INTRO.2 ¶8]

> «Et cette clef consiste tout uniment en une couleur, manifestée à l’artisan dès le premier travail.» (*And this key consists quite simply in a colour, shown to the craftsman from the first work.*) [PREF1 ¶11]

### Archimic — *archimique* {#e046}

*term; Our coinage, copying the French word, which the text does not gloss.*

Canseliet: the star is not peculiar to the Great Work; it can be met in many 'archimic' combinations, particular processes and lesser spagyric operations, always indicating a transformation of the bodies it settles on [PREF2 ¶40]. Interpreted: 'archimic' designates operations of lesser rank than the Great Work, as the sentence pairs them with lesser spagyric operations [PREF2 ¶40].

> «On peut la rencontrer dans une foule de combinaisons archimiques, de procédés particuliers et d’opérations spagyriques de moindre importance.» (*It can be met in a host of archimic combinations, particular processes and spagyric operations of lesser importance.*) [PREF2 ¶40]

### Argonauts — *argo-nautes* {#e131}

*term; Literal translation; the text hyphenates argo-nautes to show the link with argot [INTRO.3 ¶5].*

The argotiers are hermetic descendants of the argo-nautes, who sailed the Argo, spoke the argotic language, and went to Colchos to win the Golden Fleece; the 'nautes' builders knew the road to the Garden of the Hesperides [INTRO.3 ¶5]. The last medallion of the Notre-Dame porch is a replica of the Argonauts' expedition: Jason by the ram with the golden fleece and the tree of the Hesperides [PARIS.5 ¶15].

> «Les argotiers, ceux qui utilisent ce langage, sont descendants hermétiques des argo-nautes, lesquels montaient le navire Argo, parlaient la langue argotique, — notre langue verte, — en voguant vers les rives fortunées de Colchos pour conquérir la fameuse Toison d’Or.» (*The argotiers, those who use this language, are hermetic descendants of the argo-nauts, who manned the ship Argo and spoke the argotic language — our langue verte — as they sailed toward the happy shores of Colchis to win the famous Golden Fleece.*) [INTRO.3 ¶5]

> «Au surplus, cette image est une réplique de l’expédition fameuse des Argonautes ; nous y voyons Jason auprès du bélier à la toison d’or et de l’arbre aux fruits précieux du Jardin des Hespérides.» (*Moreover, this image is a replica of the famous expedition of the Argonauts; we see Jason beside the ram with the golden fleece and the tree with the precious fruits of the Garden of the Hesperides.*) [PARIS.5 ¶15]

### Argot — *argot* {#e130}

*term; Literal: kept in italics as argot, since English 'slang' does not carry the text's cabalistic sense.*

A language for those who wish to communicate without being understood, hence a 'spoken cabala' [INTRO.3 ¶5]. Its speakers, the argotiers, are hermetic descendants of the Argonauts; all Initiates spoke it, from the truands of the Cour des Miracles with Villon to the medieval Freemasons who built the 'argotic' cathedrals [INTRO.3 ¶5]. Today it is the language of the outcasts called voyous, that is, voyants [INTRO.3 ¶6]. It derives from the Language of the Birds [INTRO.3 ¶8]. Rabelais's Gargantua is called a 'roman d'argot' [INTRO.3 n1].

> «Or, les dictionnaires définissent l’argot comme étant « un langage particulier à tous les individus qui ont intérêt à se communiquer leurs pensées sans être compris de ceux qui les entourent ». C’est donc bien une cabale parlée.» (*Now the dictionaries define argot as 'a language peculiar to all individuals who have an interest in communicating their thoughts without being understood by those around them'. It is therefore indeed a spoken cabala.*) [INTRO.3 ¶5]

> «Ajoutons enfin que l’argot est une des formes dérivées de la Langue des Oiseaux, mère et doyenne de toutes les autres, la langue des philosophes et des diplomates.» (*Let us add finally that argot is one of the derived forms of the Language of the Birds, mother and eldest of all the others, the language of philosophers and diplomats.*) [INTRO.3 ¶8]

### Ariadne — *Ariane* {#e176}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Ariane), with the usual English form.*

Read cabalistically: Ariane is a form of airagne, spider, by metathesis; our soul is the spider weaving our body [INTRO.5 ¶5]. Via Greek αιρην it is the magnet, the virtue in the body the Sages call their magnesia; via Provençal aran/iran, iron, it is Hiram and the Ram; via αρυαν it is the Orient, the rising sun [INTRO.5 ¶5]. The mystic spider left the trace of her web at Amiens [INTRO.5 ¶6]. In sublimation, Theseus, manifested light, separates from Ariadne, the spider at the centre of her web, the empty husk [PARIS.4 ¶3].

> «Ariane est une forme d’airagne (araignée), par métathèse de l’i.» (*Ariane is a form of airagne (spider), by metathesis of the i.*) [INTRO.5 ¶5]

> «αρυαν, ou ariane est donc l’Orient, par permutation de voyelles.» (*αρυαν, or ariane, is therefore the Orient, by permutation of vowels.*) [INTRO.5 ¶5]

### Ariadne's thread — *fil d’Ariane* {#e173}

*term; The usual English term; literal.*

What the artist needs to find the way out of the labyrinth of the Work and not wander among its windings [INTRO.5 ¶3]. Batsdorff wrote a special treatise on it (Le Filet d'Ariadne) [INTRO.5 ¶4, PARIS.3 n7]; Fulcanelli instead offers cabalistic readings of Ariadne's name [INTRO.5 ¶4, ¶5].

> «C’est ici que le fil d’Ariane lui devient nécessaire, s’il ne veut errer parmi les méandres de l’ouvrage sans parvenir à en découvrir l’issue.» (*It is here that Ariadne's thread becomes necessary to him, if he does not wish to wander among the windings of the work without finding the way out.*) [INTRO.5 ¶3]

### Aristotle — *Aristote* {#e246}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; the usual English form.*

Cited for Mercury's emblematic colour, grey or violet [INTRO.9 ¶15]. In a quoted passage of the Cosmopolite he is among those who learned from the 'mirror' how the world was created [PARIS.5 ¶4]. A four-petalled flower on the Saint-Marcel pillar expresses universal matter, the quaternary of first elements according to his doctrine, widespread in the Middle Ages [PARIS.7 ¶11; PARIS.7 ¶22].

> «exprimant la matière universelle, quaternaire des éléments premiers, selon la doctrine d’Aristote répandue au moyen-âge.» (*expressing universal matter, the quaternary of the first elements, according to the doctrine of Aristotle widespread in the Middle Ages.*) [PARIS.7 ¶22]

### Armillary sphere — *sphère armillaire* {#e512}

*term; The established English term.*

On the Lallemant chapel ceiling, an armillary sphere on a fiery ground, very like an engraving in the Azoth treatise, with the brazier in Atlas' place, is the symbol of sulphur and its extraction from the first matter [BOURGES.2 ¶28].

> «C’est une sphère armillaire, posée sur un fond ardent, et qui offre la plus grande ressemblance avec l’une des gravures du traité de l’Azoth.» (*It is an armillary sphere, set on a burning ground, closely resembling one of the engravings of the treatise of the Azoth.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶28]

### Arnold of Villanova — *Arnaud de Villeneuve* {#e361}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; Arnold of Villanova is the usual English form.*

'Maître Arnaud de Villeneuve' is quoted on the green names of the Green Lion: our water takes the names of the leaves of all trees and of everything green, to deceive the foolish [PARIS.4 ¶17].

> «Nostre eau prend les noms des feuilles de tous les arbres, des arbres mesmes, et de tout ce qui prend une couleur verte, afin de tromper les insensés» (*Our water takes the names of the leaves of all trees, of the trees themselves, and of all that takes a green colour, in order to deceive the foolish*) [PARIS.4 ¶17]

### Artephius — *Artephius* {#e314}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also 'Arthépius').*

With Pontanus and the Epistola de Igne Philosophorum, recommended for careful reading on the nature of the secret fire, the watery fire or fiery water [PARIS.3 ¶20; PARIS.3 n9]. He and Philalèthe speak of the 'chien de Corascène' to be separated from the compost as black powder [PARIS.6 ¶12]. Quoted: 'Notre Art ... est entièrement cabalistique', which Fulcanelli endorses: the cabala let him say things not found in his predecessors' books [AMIENS ¶21].

> «« Notre Art, disait jadis Artephius, est entièrement cabalistique »» (*'Our Art,' Artephius once said, 'is entirely cabalistic'*) [AMIENS ¶21]

### Artist — *artiste* {#e544}

*term; Literal translation; in the text the alchemist at work.*

The text's name for the alchemist who carries out the Work: on Notre-Dame the artist, after long wandering, finds the fountain [PARIS.2 ¶5] and watches over the cooking of the compost [PARIS.2 ¶25]. He must make his vessel himself, 'a maxim of the art' [BOURGES.2 ¶34]. Canseliet says the key is a colour shown to the artisan from the first work [PREF1 ¶11]; the same preface uses 'artiste' plainly for the painter Julien Champagne [PREF1 ¶12].

> «L’artiste doit faire lui-même son vaisseau ; c’est une maxime de l’art.» (*The artist must make his vessel himself; it is a maxim of the art.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶34]

> «Et cette clef consiste tout uniment en une couleur, manifestée à l’artisan dès le premier travail.» (*And this key consists quite simply in a colour, shown to the artisan from the first work.*) [PREF1 ¶11]

### Assation — *assation* {#e450}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

The text names assation the dry second period of the coction, begun by a second turn of the wheel and ended when the content of the egg, calcined, appears granular or powdery, as crystals, sand or ash [AMIENS ¶7].

> «La seconde période, caractérisée par la sécheresse, — ou assation, — commence alors, par un second tour de roue» (*The second period, marked by dryness, or assation, then begins, with a second turn of the wheel*) [AMIENS ¶7]

### Atalanta Fugiens — Atalanta Fleeing — *Atalanta Fugiens* {#e102}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with a literal English rendering of the Latin.*

Work by Michel Maier; its 50th emblem shows a dragon entwining a woman in her grave, which Canseliet matches to the Saint Marcel legend and its two ways [PREF3 ¶43].

> «sur lequel le dragon enlace une femme vêtue, dans l’épanouissement de sa maturité et gisant inerte, au creux de sa fosse semblablement violée.» (*on which the dragon entwines a clothed woman, in the full bloom of her maturity and lying inert in the hollow of her grave, likewise violated.*) [PREF3 ¶43]

### Athanor — *Athanor* {#e091}

*term; Usual English term.*

The furnace of the Work. On Notre-Dame's porch a small crenellated tower is 'the instrument of the Great Work, the Athanor, the occult furnace with two flames, potential and virtual' [PARIS.2 ¶25]; another relief shows its cross-section and the support for the philosophical egg [PARIS.4 ¶2]. The Rebis lies at its centre [PARIS.5 ¶9]. At Amiens the 'church' is an athanor, its tower the secret furnace holding the egg, with openings for watching [AMIENS ¶12]; its parts are ash-pit, tower and dome, and old prints show furnaces as keeps [AMIENS ¶13]. The St Marcel dragon grips the athanor [PARIS.7 ¶21]. Canseliet quotes Grillot: a celestial ray is needed to light its fire [PREF3 ¶28].

> «Ce curieux donjon, burg en miniature, c’est l’instrument du Grand Oeuvre, l’Athanor, l’occulte four aux deux flammes, — potentielle et virtuelle, — que tous les disciples connaissent» (*This curious keep, a castle in miniature, is the instrument of the Great Work, the Athanor, the occult furnace with two flames, — potential and virtual, — which all disciples know*) [PARIS.2 ¶25]

> «On y retrouve les parties constituantes du fourneau alchimique : cendrier, tour et dôme.» (*One finds in it the parts of the alchemical furnace: ash-pit, tower and dome.*) [AMIENS ¶13]

### Avicenna — *Avicenne* {#e375}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named only inside the Cosmopolite's passage on the mirror in the kingdom of Sulphur: whoever looks into it learns the three parts of the wisdom of the world, as Aristotle, Avicenna and many others did, who saw in this mirror how the world was created [PARIS.5 ¶4].

> «il deviendra très sçavant en ces trois règnes, comme ont été Aristote, Avicenne et plusieurs autres» (*he will become very learned in these three kingdoms, as were Aristotle, Avicenna and many others*) [PARIS.5 ¶4]

### Azoth — Azoth, or the Means of Making the Hidden Gold of the Philosophers — *Azoth* {#e270}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering of the full title given in the footnote.*

Treatise attributed to Basil Valentine, which the text thinks rather the work of Senior Zadith [PARIS.2 ¶8; PARIS.7 ¶5]; edition Paris, Pierre Moët, 1659 [PARIS.2 n4; PARIS.7 n3]. It has a woodcut of a crowned nymph or siren swimming in the sea and spurting milk from her breasts into the waves [PARIS.2 ¶8]. Its second part seems to envisage the dry way: the Art learnt in under twelve hours and perfected in eight days [PARIS.7 ¶5]. One of its engravings closely resembles an armillary sphere on the Lallemant ceiling [BOURGES.2 ¶24; BOURGES.2 ¶28].

> «Car cet Art peut estre appris en moins de douze heures, et de l’espace de huit jours mené à perfection, quand il y a en soy son propre principe» (*For this Art can be learnt in less than twelve hours, and brought to perfection within eight days, when it has its own principle within itself.*) [PARIS.7 ¶5]

> «une figure sur bois représentant une nymphe ou sirène couronnée, nageant sur la mer et faisant jaillir, de ses seins rebondis, deux jets de lait qui se mélangent avec les flots.» (*a woodcut showing a crowned nymph or siren swimming on the sea and spurting from her full breasts two jets of milk that mingle with the waves.*) [PARIS.2 ¶8]

## B

### Babylonian Dragon — *Dragon babylonien* {#e422}

*term; Literal translation of the old authors' epithet as the text gives it.*

After the pure, coloured principles have risen, the residue is ready to give the mercurial salt, volatile and fusible, which the old authors often called the Babylonian Dragon [PARIS.7 ¶20]. On the Saint Anne pedestal the dragon rises from the flames, its tail seeming to come out of the human figure, and grips the athanor with its claws [PARIS.7 ¶21]. The dragon is the image of mercury, and its bands of circles, the solar sign, show it can transmute in quantity after the multiplications [PARIS.7 ¶23; PARIS.7 ¶27].

> «le résidu est prêt, dès lors, à fournir le sel mercuriel, volatil et fusible, auquel les vieux auteurs ont souvent donné l’épithète de Dragon babylonien.» (*the residue is then ready to furnish the mercurial salt, volatile and fusible, to which the old authors often gave the epithet Babylonian Dragon.*) [PARIS.7 ¶20]

### Balaam — *Balaam* {#e212}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

The text finds the first mention of the star in Balaam's mouth; said to be born at Pethor on the Euphrates and to have lived about 1477 B.C., a prophet or Magus in Mesopotamia [INTRO.7 ¶32]. His words (Num. XXIV) are quoted [INTRO.7 ¶33–34].

> «Une étoile se lève de Jacob et le sceptre sort d’Israël» (*A star rises out of Jacob and the sceptre comes forth from Israel*) [INTRO.7 ¶34]

### Baleus — *Baleus* {#e071}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Voice in the Tourbe cited by Canseliet: 'the mother takes pity on her child, but the child is very hard toward her' [PREF3 ¶13].

> «Plusieurs alchimistes ont dit, et la Tourbe en particulier, par la voix de Baleus, que “la mère prend pitié de son enfant, mais que celui-ci est très dur envers elle.”» (*Several alchemists have said, and the Turba in particular, through the voice of Baleus, that 'the mother takes pity on her child, but the child is very hard toward her.'*) [PREF3 ¶13]

### Basil Valentine — *Basile Valentin* {#e017}

*person; Name as spelled in the text ('frère Basile Valentin'); English form is the usual one.*

Cited Adept, 'the monk of Erfurth'; the Azoth attributed to him may rather be by Senior Zadith [PARIS.2 ¶8, PARIS.7 ¶5]. He named the Green Lion 'green Vitriol' for its hot, burning, saline nature [PARIS.4 ¶17]; he counsels dissolving and feeding the true Lion with the blood of the Green Lion [PARIS.4 ¶18]; he used the cock-fox in his third Key [PARIS.4 ¶19, PARIS.5 ¶12]; the eagle-and-dragon combat [BOURGES.1 ¶9]; the body of Vitriol as 'Mirror of philosophical Science' [PARIS.5 ¶4]; a white swan given to the double igneous man (weights) [PARIS.5 ¶6]; Saturn's lament [PARIS.5 ¶9]. Lallemant is called his equal [BOURGES.2 ¶38]. Canseliet: Fulcanelli's first initiator [PREF2 ¶5].

> «Dissous et nourris le vray Lion du sang du Lion vert, parquoy ils sont tous deux de même nature.» (*Dissolve and feed the true Lion with the blood of the green Lion, for they are both of the same nature.*) [PARIS.4 ¶18]

> «Certains Adeptes, Basile Valentin est de ceux-là, l’ont nommé Vitriol vert, pour déceler sa nature chaude, ardente et saline» (*Certain Adepts, Basil Valentine among them, have named it green Vitriol, to reveal its hot, burning and saline nature*) [PARIS.4 ¶17]

### Bath of the stars — *Bain des astres* {#e401}

*term; Literal translation.*

A small relief under the Portal of the Virgin expresses the condensation of the universal Spirit, which once materialised forms the Bath of the stars, where the chemical sun and moon must bathe, change nature and grow young [PARIS.6 ¶22]. It shows a child falling from a crucible held by an archangel against a starry night sky, which Fulcanelli reads as Flamel's Massacre of the Innocents [PARIS.6 ¶22]. In Abraham the Jew's fourth figure sun and moon come down to bathe in the vat of the children's blood [PARIS.2 ¶16; PARIS.2 ¶19].

> «Il synthétise et exprime la condensation de l’Esprit universel, lequel forme, aussitôt matérialisé, le fameux Bain des astres où le soleil et la lune chimiques doivent se baigner, changer de nature et rajeunir.» (*It sums up and expresses the condensation of the universal Spirit, which, once materialised, forms the famous Bath of the stars where the chemical sun and moon must bathe, change their nature and grow young again.*) [PARIS.6 ¶22]

### Bather — *baigneur* {#e492}

*term; Literal translation of the popular name of the cake figurine; the text's own gloss 'l’or dans son bain' (BOURGES.2 ¶14).*

Stated: the baigneur in the Kings' Cake is the Child Jesus carried by Offerus, 'gold in its bath'; it is also the bean (fève), the sabot, the cradle or the cross of honour, and the fish swimming in the philosophic sea [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. It sits in the flaky dough like a bookmark [BOURGES.2 ¶16]. The swaddled 'mineral child' in the flames of the Saint-Marcel pillar is likened to the porcelain baigneur of the Epiphany galette [PARIS.7 ¶11; PARIS.7 n9]. Whoever finds the bean (fève) will never lack money, doubly king by science and fortune [BOURGES.2 n7].

> «C’est l’Enfant-Jésus porté par Offerus, le serviteur ou le voyageur ; c’est l’or dans son bain, le baigneur ; c’est la fève, le sabot, le berceau ou la croix d’honneur» (*It is the Child Jesus carried by Offerus, the servant or traveller; it is gold in its bath, the bather; it is the bean, the clog, the cradle or the cross of honour*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

### Batsdorff — *Batsdorff* {#e175}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Presumed author of a classic work, Le Filet d'Ariadne, which others attribute to Gaston de Claves [PARIS.3 ¶15, PARIS.3 n7]; he wrote a special treatise on Ariadne's thread [INTRO.5 ¶4]. Quoted: putrefaction appears with blackness and separates pure from impure; its stench is sensed by the understanding, not the nose [PARIS.3 ¶15]. He says the Philosophers called the vessel of coction Green Lion, without giving a reason [BOURGES.2 ¶5].

> «Il appert donc que par cette putréfaction on fait la séparation du pur et de l’impur.» (*It is therefore clear that by this putrefaction the separation of the pure from the impure is made.*) [PARIS.3 ¶15]

> «Batsdorff, dans son Filet d’Ariadne, dit que les Philosophes ont appelé Lion vert le vaisseau servant à la coction, mais sans en fournir aucune raison.» (*Batsdorff, in his Thread of Ariadne, says the Philosophers called the vessel used for coction Green Lion, but without giving any reason.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶5]

### Bernard Husson — *Bernard Husson* {#e092}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named only in Canseliet's third preface (voice CANSELIET). He gave a date to the photograph published by Grillot de Givry and called it a daguerreotype [PREF3 ¶29]. Following Marcel Clavelle (Jean Reyor), he recently revived, thirty-two years later, the dispute over Fulcanelli's correction of Cambriel, adopting Reyor's opinion entirely [PREF3 ¶34]. He proposed that 'XVIe siècle' on page 407 of the Musée des Sorciers was a misprint corrected to 'XVIIe siècle' in the caption, a correction Canseliet says is not there [PREF3 ¶47]. Canseliet says he and Clavelle were blinded by the pleasure of catching Fulcanelli in error [PREF3 ¶49].

> «dont Bernard Husson, délibérément, fixe la date et fait un daguerréotype» (*of which Bernard Husson deliberately fixes the date and makes a daguerreotype*) [PREF3 ¶29]

### Bernard of Treviso — *Bernard Trévisan* {#e237}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also 'messire Bernard, comte de Trévise', 'le Trévisan'); English form is the usual one.*

Named among the masters of old times [INTRO.9 ¶2]. The Mysterious Fountain carved at Notre-Dame is the one he describes in the final Parable of his book on the natural philosophy of metals [PARIS.2 ¶4]. Quoted (La Parole délaissée) on the blackening: the compound soaked with permanent water turns like melted pitch, black as coal, and receives many names; this blackness is the key and sign of right working [PARIS.3 ¶14; PARIS.3 n4]. The 'Fountain of Trévisan' where the King strips off his purple mantle appears in a quoted text [PARIS.4 ¶14]. His parable of the old priest and the king of the country, whom the fountain attracts and engulfs, matches a Notre-Dame medallion [PARIS.5 ¶12]. The Verbum dimissum of the Trévisan is the Lost Word of the medieval freemasons [PARIS.6 ¶25].

> «quand nostre compost commence à estre abreuvé de nostre eau permanente, lors est tout le compost tourné en manière de poix fondue, et est tout noircy comme charbon.» (*when our compound begins to be soaked with our permanent water, then the whole compound is turned like melted pitch, and is all blackened like coal.*) [PARIS.3 ¶14]

> «C’est là le Verbum dimissum du Trévisan, la Parole perdue des francs-maçons médiévaux» (*That is the Verbum dimissum of the Trévisan, the Lost Word of the medieval freemasons*) [PARIS.6 ¶25]

### Beya — *Beya* {#e146}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

The sister in the brother-sister pair Gabritius and Beya, whose union makes the compost or rebis [INTRO.3 n4].

> «réalisé par l’union du frère et de la sœur, de Gabritius et de Beya, d’Apollon et de Diane.» (*made by the union of the brother and the sister, of Gabritius and Beya, of Apollo and Diana.*) [INTRO.3 n4]

### Bezants — *besants* {#e420}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

The restorers replaced the bezants and flowers of the Saint Anne pedestal with Romanesque scrolls [PARIS.7 ¶13]. On the original pedestal, two bezants on the right side are the metallic matters or natures, subject and solvent, with which the Work must begin; on the front, changed by preliminary operations, they become rosettes; on the left, flowers with visible calyx, showing the metallic roots opened and ready to show their seed principle [PARIS.7 ¶18].

> «Sur le socle cubique vous remarquerez, au côté droit, deux besants en relief, massifs et circulaires ; ce sont les matières ou natures métalliques, — sujet et dissolvant, — avec lesquelles on doit commencer l’Œuvre.» (*On the cubic pedestal you will notice, on the right side, two bezants in relief, massive and circular; they are the metallic matters or natures, subject and solvent, with which the Work must begin.*) [PARIS.7 ¶18]

## T

### The Bible of Amiens — *The Bible of Amiens* {#e443}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of Ruskin, named for his reading of the Amiens wheel-fire as Ezekiel's vision [AMIENS ¶4].

> «Jourdain et Duval, Ruskin (The Bible of Amiens), l’abbé Roze et, après eux, Georges Durand» (*Jourdain and Duval, Ruskin (The Bible of Amiens), the abbé Roze and, after them, Georges Durand*) [AMIENS ¶4]

## B

### Bibliographie générale de l'Occulte — General Bibliography of the Occult — *Bibliographie générale de l’Occulte* {#e221}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.*

Work of Pierre Dujols, quoted on Elias Schadius's inscription to Isis and on Bigarne's interpretation of Isis as the Virgo paritura and mother of the gods [INTRO.8 ¶4].

> «Déjà, dit l’érudit Pierre Dujols, dans sa Bibliographie générale de l’Occulte, le savant Elias Schadius avait signalé, dans son livre De dictis Germanicis, une inscription analogue» (*Already, says the erudite Pierre Dujols in his General Bibliography of the Occult, the learned Elias Schadius had pointed out, in his book De dictis Germanicis, a similar inscription*) [INTRO.8 ¶4]

### Bird of Hermes — *oiseau d'Hermès* {#e317}

*term; Literal translation.*

Stated (Philalèthe, quoted with approval): the philosophers' Mercury is the bird of Hermes, also called Goose or Swan, sometimes Pheasant [PARIS.4 ¶4]. The swans of Callimachus circling Delos seven times are the sublimations [PARIS.4 ¶5, ¶9]. Basile Valentin (quoted) says to give a white swan to the double igneous man, on the weights [PARIS.5 ¶6]. Tollius (quoted) mocks seekers of their red 'faysan' in glass vessels [PARIS.3 ¶36]. Identified with the Philosophical Mercury (e307).

> «Et notre Mercure philosophique est l’oiseau d’Hermès, à qui l’on donne aussi le nom d’Oie ou de Cygne, et quelquefois celui de Faysan.» (*And our Philosophical Mercury is the bird of Hermes, which is also given the name of Goose or Swan, and sometimes that of Pheasant.*) [PARIS.4 ¶4]

Identified in the text with: [Philosophical Mercury](#e307)

### Black colour — *couleur noire* {#e291}

*term; Literal translation.*

The first of the three principal colours of the Work, which go in invariable order from black to red through white [PARIS.3 ¶25]. In the cooking of the Rebis it is the first appearance of decomposition and the sign of exact preparation; its symbol is the Raven [PARIS.3 ¶2–3]. Le Breton (quoted) counts four putrefactions, in each of which blackness appears [PARIS.3 ¶5], which lets authors mix up the operations [PARIS.3 ¶6]; Trévisan and Batsdorff (quoted) describe it (pitch-black compound, 'clef', sign of the good path; 'noirceur très noire' as sign of true putrefaction) [PARIS.3 ¶14–15]. Symbolically black was given to Saturn (lead, the Black Hen), is the colour of darkness, Satan, the primitive Chaos, heraldic sable, earth, night and death [PARIS.3 ¶27]. Paracelsus (quoted) calls black the root of the other colours [PARIS.3 ¶30]. The Virgin's blue corresponds to black [PARIS.3 ¶31]. On cathedral roses the colours run from darkness, figured by absence of light and black, to ruddy light [INTRO.6 ¶2]. At Bourges the death of the body shows a dark blue or black colour, given to the Raven [BOURGES.2 ¶25]. Fulcanelli also warns that the clear descriptions of colours of the 'régimes' are false and that one should learn how one régime differs from the next rather than one colour from another [PARIS.3 ¶33–35]. Identified with the Raven (e282).

> «Il exprime, en effet, dans la cuisson du Rebis philosophal, la couleur noire, première apparence de la décomposition consécutive à la mixtion parfaite des matières de l’Œuf.» (*It expresses, in fact, in the cooking of the philosophical Rebis, the black colour, first appearance of the decomposition that follows the perfect mixing of the matters of the Egg.*) [PARIS.3 ¶3]

> «c’est le sable de la science héraldique et l’emblème de l’élément terre, de la nuit et de la mort.» (*it is the sable of heraldry and the emblem of the element earth, of night and of death.*) [PARIS.3 ¶27]

Identified in the text with: [Raven](#e282)

### Black Hen — *Poule noire* {#e328}

*term; Literal translation.*

The black colour was given to Saturn, who became in magic the Black Hen [PARIS.3 ¶27]. On the Lallemant ceiling a 'géline' or partridge perches on the horn of Amalthea; whether the emblem is the black hen or the red partridge changes nothing to its hermetic meaning [BOURGES.2 ¶28]. Interpreted: through Saturn, the hen belongs to the black colour of the Work [PARIS.3 ¶27].

> «La couleur noire fut donnée à Saturne qui devint, en spagyrie, l’hiéroglyphe du plomb, en astrologie une planète maléfique, en magie la Poule noire, etc.» (*The black colour was given to Saturn, who became, in spagyrics, the hieroglyph of lead, in astrology a malefic planet, in magic the Black Hen, etc.*) [PARIS.3 ¶27]

### Black Virgins — *Vierges noires* {#e216}

*term; The usual English term.*

Statues of Isis that became, after the coming of Christianity to Gaul, the Black Virgins, venerated in the crypts; same symbolism, same inscription Virgini parituræ [INTRO.8 ¶4]. In hermetic symbolism they figure the primitive earth that the artist must choose as subject: the first matter as ore, a black, heavy, brittle substance; hence their colour and their underground dwelling [INTRO.8 ¶4]. Examples: Chartres (Notre-Dame-sous-Terre, Notre-Dame-du-Pilier), Le Puy, Marseille, Rocamadour, Vichy, Quimper, and a Paris Virgin in the Observatory cellars [INTRO.8 ¶5–11]. Only green candles were burnt in their processions, a detail called useful to the hermetist [INTRO.8 ¶12].

> «Elles figurent, dans la symbolique hermétique, la terre primitive, celle que l’artiste doit choisir pour sujet de son grand ouvrage.» (*In hermetic symbolism they figure the primitive earth, the one the artist must choose as the subject of his great work.*) [INTRO.8 ¶4]

> «Dans le cérémonial prescrit pour les processions de Vierges noires, on ne brûlait que des cierges de couleur verte.» (*In the ceremonial prescribed for processions of Black Virgins, only green candles were burnt.*) [INTRO.8 ¶12]

### Blaise de Vigenère — *Blaise de Vigenère* {#e053}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Canseliet: with Naxagoras, he wrote at length on the value of a long preliminary coction of lead, which, though dead, can be revived by being fed with igneous substance [PREF2 ¶43].

> «Blaise de Vigenère et Naxagoras, par exemple, se sont étendus sur l’opportunité d’une longue coction préalable.» (*Blaise de Vigenère and Naxagoras, for instance, dwelt on the advisability of a long preliminary coction.*) [PREF2 ¶43]

### Brass (latten) — *laiton* {#e158}

*term; Literal translation; the text also spells it laton, and names airain beside it.*

Airain and laiton are names some gave to common copper, the metal of Venus, to veil the sense [INTRO.4 ¶4]; all good authors repeat 'whiten the laiton and burn your books' [INTRO.4 ¶4]. Bernard Trévisan (QUOTED) lists 'laton non net' (unclean latten) among the names of the blackened compost [PARIS.3 ¶13, PARIS.3 ¶14]. Venus is to airain [PARIS.6 ¶4].

> «« Blanchis le laiton et brûle tes livres », nous répètent tous les bons auteurs.» (*'Whiten the latten and burn your books,' all the good authors repeat to us.*) [INTRO.4 ¶4]

> «Et en cet endroit est appelé nostre compost : la poix noire, le sel bruslé, le plomb fondu, le laton non net, la Magnésie et le Merle de Jean.» (*And at this point our compost is called: the black pitch, the burnt salt, the molten lead, the unclean latten, the Magnesia and John's Blackbird.*) [PARIS.3 ¶14]

### Brothers of Heliopolis — *Frères d'Héliopolis* {#e002}

*term; Literal translation.*

Named only in Canseliet's first preface: he leaves to the Brothers of Heliopolis the joy of receiving this synthesis, set out by 'one of theirs' [PREF1 ¶1], and thanks the illustrator in their name and his own [PREF1 ¶12]. The text says nothing more about who they are. Interpreted: 'one of theirs' makes the author, Fulcanelli, a member [PREF1 ¶1]; the signature 'F. C. H.' under Canseliet's name [PREF1 ¶14] is not explained by the text.

> «comme aux Frères d’Héliopolis la joie de recueillir cette synthèse, si magistralement exposée par un des leurs.» (*as [I leave] to the Brothers of Heliopolis the joy of receiving this synthesis, so masterfully set out by one of their own.*) [PREF1 ¶1]

### Building on boulevard Péreire — *immeuble du boulevard Péreire* {#e353}

*place; Name as given in the text's note.*

A building Fulcanelli saw being built when he was a student; above the door two children, boy and girl, lift a veil; on the corner a bas-relief shows the symbolic combat of the eagle and the lion, an emblem he thinks imposed on the architect by a superior will [PARIS.4 ¶12]. The note places it at the corner of boulevard Péreire and rue de Monbel, 17th arrondissement, six storeys of cut stone [PARIS.4 n2].

> «il offre le combat symbolique de l’aigle et du lion, dont nous venons de parler, et l’on devine aisément que l’architecte eut quelque peine à loger l’emblème encombrant» (*it shows the symbolic combat of the eagle and the lion, of which we have just spoken, and one easily guesses that the architect had some trouble fitting in the cumbersome emblem*) [PARIS.4 ¶12]

### Bull — *Taureau* {#e365}

*term; Literal translation.*

The Bull is the sculpted figure that follows the fox-cock on the Paris stylobate [PARIS.3 ¶1, PARIS.4 ¶20]. As a zodiac sign it is the second month of the preparatory operations in the first work, and the first regimen of the elemental fire in the second [PARIS.4 ¶21]. As a figure of practice it stands for Sulphur, the male principle: bull and ox are sacred to the sun as the cow is to the moon, and Hermes calls the sun the Father of the stone [PARIS.4 ¶21]. Bull and cow, sun and moon, sulphur and mercury mean the same thing: the two contrary primitive natures before their conjunction, which the Art extracts from imperfect mixtures [PARIS.4 ¶21]. On the Portal of the Virgin, Taurus comes after Aries among the spring signs that mark the start of the work [PARIS.6 ¶20].

> «Le taureau et la vache, le soleil et la lune, le soufre et le mercure sont donc les hiéroglyphes de sens identique et désignent les natures primitives contraires, avant leur conjonction» (*The bull and the cow, the sun and the moon, sulphur and mercury are therefore hieroglyphs of identical meaning, and denote the contrary primitive natures, before their conjunction*) [PARIS.4 ¶21]

## C

### Cabala — *cabale* {#e056}

*term; Usual English term.*

Fulcanelli's reading of words by sound. The origin of 'gothic' must be sought in its 'cabalistic origin' rather than its literal root [INTRO.3 ¶3]; by the 'phonetic law' governing the traditional cabala regardless of spelling, art gothique is argotique; argot is a 'spoken cabala' [INTRO.3 ¶5]. People of wit, punning, are cabalists unknowingly [INTRO.3 ¶7]. The phonetic cabala reads Christophe as Chrysophe, 'gold-bearer' [INTRO.9 ¶15]; 'Saba' or 'Caba' points to the cabalists [INTRO.1 ¶14]; in cabala sabot equals cabot or chabot [INTRO.1 n4]. Artephius: 'Our Art is entirely cabalistic'; the cabala let the author say things without lying or breaking his oath [AMIENS ¶21]. Canseliet derives cabale from χαβάλλης or Latin caballus, horse, and distinguishes it from the Jewish kabbale [PREF2 ¶45].

> «Pour nous, art gothique n’est qu’une déformation orthographique du mot argotique, dont l’homophonie est parfaite, conformément à la loi phonétique qui régit, dans toutes les langues et sans tenir compte de l’orthographe, la cabale traditionnelle.» (*For us, art gothique is merely a spelling distortion of the word argotique, with which it is perfectly homophonous, in keeping with the phonetic law that governs the traditional cabala in all languages, regardless of spelling.*) [INTRO.3 ¶5]

> «« Notre Art, disait jadis Artephius, est entièrement cabalistique » ; la cabale, en effet, nous a toujours été d’une grande utilité.» (*'Our Art,' Artephius once said, 'is entirely cabalistic'; the cabala has indeed always been of great use to us.*) [AMIENS ¶21]

### Cadmus — *Cadmus* {#e266}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also Cadmos).*

The adept of the Fountain bas-relief disdains the bow and arrows with which, like Cadmus, he pierced the dragon [PARIS.2 ¶5]. A quoted text says Philalethes describes the vessel through the fable of the serpent Python which Cadmus pierced through against an oak [PARIS.4 ¶14]. The Hippocrene fountain was said to be discovered by Cadmos [PARIS.5 ¶2]. Cadmos piercing the serpent against an oak is one of the fables of the combat of chemical bodies whose combination gives the secret dissolvent (and the vessel of the compound) [BOURGES.1 ¶9].

> «C’est Cadmos perçant le serpent contre un chêne ; Apollon tuant à coups de flèches le monstre Python et Jason le dragon de Colchide» (*It is Cadmus piercing the serpent against an oak; Apollo killing the monster Python with arrows, and Jason the dragon of Colchis*) [BOURGES.1 ¶9]

### Caduceus — *caducée* {#e308}

*term; The usual English term.*

The serpent coiled on the golden rod, image of the Philosophical Mercury on the second Paris relief; Abraham the Jew used it and the symbol runs through the Middle Ages [PARIS.3 ¶17]. Stated: the serpent shows the incisive, dissolving nature of Mercury, which greedily absorbs the metallic sulphur; it is Mercury in its first state; the golden rod is the corporeal sulphur added; the resulting body keeps the image of the caduceus [PARIS.3 ¶18].

> «Ce reptile est le type du Mercure dans son premier état, et la verge d’or, le soufre corporel qui lui est ajouté.» (*This reptile is the type of Mercury in its first state, and the golden rod the corporeal sulphur added to it.*) [PARIS.3 ¶18]

### Calcination — *Calcination* {#e311}

*term; The usual English term.*

Personified on the Paris stylobate by a woman with flame-like hair pressing to her breast the disc of the Salamander [PARIS.3 ¶19]. The spagyric image: violent fire destroys the combustible parts of a body, leaving the fixed central salt, extractable by lixiviation [PARIS.3 ¶19–20]. The masters stress that philosophical calcination differs from common laboratory calcination: it uses no common fire or reverberatory furnace but an occult agent, a secret fire more like water than flame [PARIS.3 ¶20]. Limojon (quoted): the secret fire calcines the matter, dissolves, sublimes it and resolves it into dry water [PARIS.3 ¶22]. At Bourges philosophical calcination is shown as a pomegranate in a goldsmith's vase on the fire, with '3 R' marking the three reiterations [BOURGES.2 ¶28]; the calcination of the Sulphur must be reiterated three times to make the three works [BOURGES.2 ¶32]; the compound is heated in its sealed vessel until complete calcination, and this is repeated [BOURGES.2 ¶36]. The Paris figures follow the Elixir 'depuis sa calcination première' [PARIS.2 ¶2].

> «Celle-ci ne se fait par aucun feu vulgaire, ne nécessite point le secours du réverbère, mais demande l’aide d’un agent occulte, d’un feu secret» (*The latter is done by no common fire, needs no reverberatory furnace, but requires the help of an occult agent, a secret fire*) [PARIS.3 ¶20]

### Callimachus — *Callimaque* {#e349}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; Callimachus is the usual English form.*

Fulcanelli says Callimachus describes the sublimations in the Hymn to Delos, speaking of the swans that circled Delos seven times before Apollo was born [PARIS.4 ¶5–9].

> «Ce sont ces sublimations que décrit Callimaque, dans l’Hymne à Délos (v. 250, 255), lorsqu’il dit, en parlant des cygnes» (*It is these sublimations that Callimachus describes in the Hymn to Delos (lines 250, 255), when he says, speaking of the swans*) [PARIS.4 ¶5]

### Calyougam — *Calyougam* {#e539}

*term; Name as spelled in the text.*

The fourth and last age, of misery, misfortune and decrepitude; the text identifies it with the iron age, 'which is ours', when the cow of Virtue barely balances on one foot [HENDAYE ¶14]. Its seal is Death [HENDAYE ¶15].

> «C’est le quatrième et dernier âge, le Calyougam, âge de misère, d’infortune et de décrépitude.» (*It is the fourth and last age, the Calyougam, age of misery, misfortune and decrepitude.*) [HENDAYE ¶14]

Identified in the text with: [Iron age](#e540)

### Camille Flammarion — *Camille Flammarion* {#e232}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Reports a statuette of the Virgin he saw in the cellars of the Paris Observatory on 24 September 1871, placed there in 1671 and invoked as Nostre-Dame de dessoubs terre [INTRO.8 ¶11]. Source: L’Atmosphère (Paris, Hachette, 1888, p. 362) [INTRO.8 n3].

> «A l’angle de l’une des galeries souterraines, on remarque une statuette de la Vierge, placée là cette même année 1671, et que des vers gravés à ses pieds invoquent sous le nom de Nostre-Dame de dessoubs terre.» (*At the corner of one of the underground galleries there is a statuette of the Virgin, placed there that same year 1671, which verses carved at her feet invoke under the name of Our Lady beneath the earth.*) [INTRO.8 ¶11]

### Caput mortuum (dead head) — *caput mortuum* {#e510}

*term; The established Latin term kept, with a literal English gloss.*

On a Lallemant chapel capital, a winged skull on oak leaves expresses new generation from the putrefaction after death [BOURGES.2 ¶25]. The body's death shows a dark blue or black colour, given to the Raven, hieroglyph of the caput mortuum of the Work: sign of dissolution, separation of the elements and future generation of sulphur [BOURGES.2 ¶25]. The calcined black ash then leaves its impurities and a pure salt is born [BOURGES.2 ¶25].

> «La mort du corps laisse apparaître une coloration bleu foncé ou noire, affectée au Corbeau, hiéroglyphe du caput mortuum de l’Œuvre.» (*The death of the body lets a dark blue or black colour appear, assigned to the Raven, hieroglyph of the caput mortuum of the Work.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶25]

### Cathars — *Cathares* {#e331}

*term; The usual English term.*

In Pierre Dujols's passage quoted by Fulcanelli on white: the Cathars, the sect to which the Whites of Florence belonged, were the Pure (from Greek Katharos) [PARIS.3 ¶28].

> «Les Cathares, secte à laquelle appartenaient les Blancs de Florence, étaient les Purs (du grec Καθαρος).» (*The Cathars, the sect to which the Whites of Florence belonged, were the Pure (from the Greek Katharos).*) [PARIS.3 ¶28]

### Celestial Agriculture — *Agriculture céleste* {#e355}

*term; Literal translation.*

Stated: a former name of alchemy, whose Adepts were called 'Laboureurs' (ploughmen) [PARIS.4 n2]. Interpreted: Fulcanelli's address to operators 'vous qui avez déjà tant labouré' plays on this name [AMIENS ¶37].

> «L’alchimie portait encore le nom d’Agriculture céleste, et ses Adeptes celui de Laboureurs.» (*Alchemy also bore the name of Celestial Agriculture, and its Adepts that of Ploughmen.*) [PARIS.4 n2]

### Central salt — *sel central* {#e313}

*term; Literal translation.*

Stated: what the Salamander designates: the incombustible, fixed salt that keeps its nature even in the ashes of calcined metals, named by the Ancients 'Semence métallique'; in the violence of fire the combustible parts are destroyed and only the pure, unalterable parts resist, which can be extracted by lixiviation [PARIS.3 ¶19]. The commentator of the Lumière sortant des Ténèbres (quoted) says the subject of the art must be sought in the mineral kingdom only, 'où réside la semence métallique' [AMIENS ¶28]. Identified with the Salamander (e312).

> «et que les Anciens ont nommé Semence métallique.» (*and which the Ancients named metallic Seed.*) [PARIS.3 ¶19]

Identified in the text with: [Salamander](#e312)

### Ceres — *Cérès* {#e229}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Isis is called the Egyptian Ceres [INTRO.8 ¶14]. Greeks and Egyptians kept absolute silence on the mysteries of Ceres; disclosure was punished by death and the temple barred to the uninitiated [INTRO.8 ¶14]. From the four grades of her priests (Hierophant; Torch-bearer, representing the Sun; Herald, representing Mercury; Minister of the Altar, representing the Moon) Fulcanelli infers that her mysteries were of the same order as those of Hermetic Science [INTRO.8 ¶14]. At Rome the Cerealia began on 12 April and lasted eight days, with an egg, symbol of the world, carried in procession [INTRO.8 ¶14]. Isis, Ceres and Cybele are 'three heads under the same veil' [INTRO.8 ¶15].

> «les renseignements qui nous ont été transmis sur la hiérarchie des grands prêtres nous autorisent à penser que les mystères de Cérès devaient être du même ordre que ceux de la Science hermétique.» (*the information handed down to us on the hierarchy of the high priests allows us to think that the mysteries of Ceres must have been of the same order as those of the Hermetic Science.*) [INTRO.8 ¶14]

Identified in the text with: [Isis](#e214)

### Ch. Bigarne — *Ch. Bigarne* {#e218}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of Considérations sur le Culte d’Isis chez les Eduens (Beaune, 1862) [INTRO.8 n1]. He speaks of several statues of Isis under the same title as the Black Virgins [INTRO.8 ¶4]. In the passage quoted from Pierre Dujols, Bigarne explains Isis before conception as the Virgo paritura, the earth before fecundation, and as mother of the gods [INTRO.8 ¶4]. Fulcanelli adds that one cannot better define the esoteric sense of the Black Virgins [INTRO.8 ¶4].

> «Isis, avant la conception, c’est, dit Bigarne, dans la théogonie astronomique, l’attribut de la Vierge que plusieurs monuments, bien antérieurs au christianisme, désignent sous le nom de Virgo paritura» (*Isis before conception is, says Bigarne, in astronomical theogony, the attribute of the Virgin whom several monuments, long before Christianity, designate by the name Virgo paritura*) [INTRO.8 ¶4]

### Chalcidius — *Chalcidius* {#e206}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

A 4th-century philosopher who, according to his editor Mullachius, held that the gods of Greece, of Rome and foreign gods should be worshipped; he preserved a mention of the star of the Magi and the scholars' explanation of it, after speaking of a star the Egyptians called Ahc which announced misfortunes [INTRO.7 ¶16]. He tells of a holier story in which the most learned Chaldeans saw the star at night and sought the newborn God [INTRO.7 ¶17]. Source: Comm. in Timæum Platonis, c. 125; the footnote adds that Chalcidius plainly addresses an initiate [INTRO.7 n8].

> «Les plus savants des Chaldéens, ayant vu cette étoile en voyageant pendant la nuit, en hommes parfaitement exercés à la contemplation des choses célestes, recherchèrent, à ce qu’on raconte, la naissance récente d’un Dieu» (*The most learned of the Chaldeans, having seen this star while travelling by night, as men perfectly practised in contemplating heavenly things, sought, so it is told, the recent birth of a God*) [INTRO.7 ¶17]

### Chaos — *Chaos* {#e035}

*term; Literal translation.*

The Sages' name for the confused first matter. The Arab 'Modhallan', 'dark and gloomy sea', marks the elemental confusion the Sages attribute to their Chaos or first matter [PARIS.2 ¶9]; the primitive water issues from the Chaos of the Sages [PARIS.2 ¶7]. Black is the colour of the primitive Chaos, where the seeds of all things are mixed [PARIS.3 ¶27]. 'The metallic Chaos contains all metals and is not metal' [AMIENS ¶25]; a portion of the first Chaos remains in this world, known but despised and sold publicly [AMIENS ¶28]. Possessing the right subject, one has the 'Chaos of the Sages' in which all secrets are in potency (Philalèthe) [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. Light was drawn from the dark Chaos [PARIS.6 ¶26]. Canseliet: heaven and earth are confused in the original cosmic Chaos [PREF2 ¶31].

> «Le Chaos métallique produit des mains de la nature contient en soy tous les métaux et n’est point métail.» (*The metallic Chaos produced by the hands of nature contains in itself all the metals and is not metal.*) [AMIENS ¶25]

> «ce qui marque bien la confusion élémentaire que les Sages attribuent à leur Chaos ou matière première.» (*which clearly marks the elemental confusion the Sages attribute to their Chaos or first matter.*) [PARIS.2 ¶9]

Identified in the text with: [First matter](#e094)

### Charles Vincens — *Charles Vincens* {#e235}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Confirms the closeness of Isis and Cybele by describing a bas-relief of Cybele, inscribed Matri Deum, seen for centuries on the outside of the parish church of Pennes (Bouches-du-Rhône); it disappeared about 1610 but is engraved in Grosson's Recueil [INTRO.8 ¶15].

> «Ce curieux morceau, nous dit-il, a disparu vers 1610 seulement, mais il est gravé dans le Recueil de Grosson (p. 20).» (*This curious piece, he tells us, disappeared only around 1610, but it is engraved in Grosson's Recueil (p. 20).*) [INTRO.8 ¶15]

### Charnel house of the Innocents — *charnier des Innocents* {#e431}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Flamel built two decorative arcades there, in 1389 and 1407; the first bore, among other hieroglyphic plaques, an escutcheon that seems imitated from one attributed to saint Thomas Aquinas [PARIS.8 ¶2]. Fulcanelli concludes Lagneau had his engraving copied from this arcade; of four plaques three remained in Gohorry's time, about 1572 [PARIS.8 ¶4].

> «Flamel éleva encore deux arcades décoratives au charnier des Innocents, l’une en 1389, la seconde en 1407.» (*Flamel also raised two decorative arcades at the charnel house of the Innocents, one in 1389, the second in 1407.*) [PARIS.8 ¶2]

### Chartres cathedral — *cathédrale de Chartres* {#e166}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Its nave labyrinth, called 'la lieue', once showed Theseus and the Minotaur [INTRO.5 ¶1]. The north portal rose renders the movements of matter excited by fire [INTRO.6 ¶12]. It has two Black Virgins: Notre-Dame-sous-Terre in the crypt, inscribed Virgini parituræ, and Notre-Dame-du-Pilier; it passes for the oldest pilgrimage, originally an Isis statuette [INTRO.8 ¶5, INTRO.8 ¶11]. Its figure of a man in his shirt is, for Fulcanelli, an alchemist dressed lightly for the furnace heat [AMIENS ¶11, ¶12]. Its royal portal shows the four animals around Christ [HENDAYE ¶12].

> «Avec sa Vierge souterraine, Chartres passe pour être le plus ancien de tous les pèlerinages.» (*With its underground Virgin, Chartres is held to be the oldest of all pilgrimages.*) [INTRO.8 ¶5]

> «Quant à la tenue légère que revêt l’artisan dans le motif de Chartres, elle se justifie par la chaleur dégagée du four.» (*As for the light dress the craftsman wears in the Chartres motif, it is explained by the heat given off by the furnace.*) [AMIENS ¶12]

### Chemical heaven — *ciel chimique* {#e027}

*term; Literal translation.*

Canseliet: the hermetic star, first admired in the mirror of the art, is then found in the chemical heaven, where it shines more discreetly [PREF2 ¶20]; it is a nocturnal star at the pole of the hermetic heaven, the 'terrestrial heaven' of Lavinius and Tollius [PREF2 ¶27, PREF2 ¶28]. Heaven and earth are one in substance in the original Chaos but differ in quality [PREF2 ¶31]; the inert alchemical earth contains the philosophical heaven, which the artist separates with the secret fire and universal spirit, extracting light from darkness: thus one knows the 'heaven of the Sages' [PREF2 ¶32]. Tollius's book is titled Le Chemin du Ciel Chymique [PARIS.3 n15].

> «J. Tollius, Le Chemin du Ciel Chymique.» (*J. Tollius, The Road to the Chemical Heaven.*) [PARIS.3 n15]

> «La terre alchimique, chaotique, inerte et stérile, ne contient-elle pas néanmoins le ciel philosophique ?» (*Does not the alchemical earth, chaotic, inert and sterile, nonetheless contain the philosophical heaven?*) [PREF2 ¶32]

### Child gold — *or enfant* {#e489}

*term; Literal translation of the text's phrase.*

Stated: gold dissolved in mercury and brought back to its first state is young or rejuvenated gold, 'in a word child gold' [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The mercury then carries the child — the Son of the Sun, the Little King (Roitelet) — like a true mother, since gold is reborn in its bosom; hence mercury is called Fountain of Youth [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The Kings' Cake with its baigneur is the popular version of this truth [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. At Amiens the hermetic star announces the birth of the 'fils du soleil' [AMIENS ¶40]. Interpreted: the 'petit roi' or mineral child in the flames of the Saint-Marcel pillar is the same figure [PARIS.7 ¶11; PARIS.7 n9].

> «Cette disposition géométrique subsiste et apparaît avec plus de netteté lorsqu’on a mis l’or à dissoudre dans le mercure pour le ramener à son premier état, celui d’or jeune ou rajeuni, en un mot d’or enfant.» (*This geometric pattern persists and appears more clearly once gold has been put to dissolve in the mercury to bring it back to its first state, that of young or rejuvenated gold, in a word child gold.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

> «Les Philosophes parlent donc clairement lorsqu’ils enseignent que le mercure, dès la dissolution effectuée, porte l’enfant, le Fils du Soleil, le Petit Roi (Roitelet), comme une mère véritable, puisqu’en effet l’or renaît dans son sein.» (*So the Philosophers speak plainly when they teach that the mercury, once the dissolution is done, carries the child, the Son of the Sun, the Little King (Roitelet), like a true mother, since gold is indeed reborn in its bosom.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

### Church of Brixen — *église de Brixen* {#e275}

*place; Name as spelled in the text ('la petite église de Brixen (Tyrol)').*

A small church in the Tyrol which held a painted 'replica' of the fable of the fountain whose water seemed to flow like milk from two breasts [PARIS.2 ¶7; PARIS.2 ¶10]. The painting, described by Misson and reported by Witkowski, showed Jesus letting the blood of his side flow into a basin and the Virgin pressing milk from her breasts into the same vessel; the overflow fell into a gulf of flames where the souls of Purgatory received it [PARIS.2 ¶10]. Fulcanelli says it seems to be the religious version of the same chemical theme [PARIS.2 ¶10]. Beneath it a Latin inscription ends 'Et sit Fons Vitae, Fons et Origo boni' [PARIS.2 ¶11–15], which the book renders as the blood and milk mingling and becoming 'la Fontaine de Vie et la Source du Bien' [PARIS.2 n6].
