> «Toutes les descriptions que nous ont laissées les Philosophes de leur sujet, ou matière première qui contient l’agent indispensable, sont fort confuses et très mystérieuses.» (*All the descriptions the Philosophers have left us of their subject, or first matter which contains the indispensable agent, are very confused and most mysterious.*) [AMIENS ¶27]

Identified in the text with: [Mirror of the Art](#e026), [Chaos](#e035)

### First stone — *première pierre* {#e009}

*term; Literal translation.*

By the sign of the Cross the plan of the church reveals the qualities and preparation of the first matter, which leads to the 'First stone', cornerstone of the Great Work; on this stone Jesus built his Church, and the medieval freemasons followed the example; before being cut, the rough stone was often given the image of the devil [INTRO.4 ¶5]. At Notre-Dame this was the devil figure called Maistre Pierre du Coignet, symbol of 'our cornerstone' [INTRO.4 ¶6]. In the practice, after the second coagulation of the cock-fox operation 'the first stone' is born, neither wholly fixed nor wholly volatile, to be perfected by a third reiteration [AMIENS ¶15]. Canseliet: all truth, philosophy and religion rest on this 'unique and sacred stone' [PREF1 ¶6].

> «ce qui aboutit, pour les alchimistes, à l’obtention de la Première pierre, pierre angulaire du Grand Œuvre philosophal.» (*which leads, for the alchemists, to obtaining the First stone, cornerstone of the philosophical Great Work.*) [INTRO.4 ¶5]

> «Ainsi naîtra la première pierre, non absolument fixe ni absolument volatile, toutefois assez permanente au feu, très pénétrante et très fusible» (*Thus will be born the first stone, neither absolutely fixed nor absolutely volatile, yet fairly permanent in the fire, very penetrating and very fusible*) [AMIENS ¶15]

### Fish — *poisson* {#e493}

*term; Literal translation.*

Stated: the fish is the hieroglyph of the Philosophers' stone in its first state, because the stone, like the fish, is born and lives in water [BOURGES.2 ¶14]; it is the fish 'that swims in our philosophic sea' of the Cosmopolite, to be caught with a net, image of the crossed lines on the galette [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The Cosmopolite's fish Echinéis leads to the pole star [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. A porcelain sole once replaced the baigneur in a cake: 'sole' from Latin Sol, the sun [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The fish found in the cake is the 'royal fish'; the dolphin, royal fish, gave the heir's title Dauphin; the Greek Ichtus has the same origin [BOURGES.2 ¶15].

> «Le poisson est l’hiéroglyphe de la pierre des Philosophes dans son premier état, parce que la pierre, comme le poisson, naît dans l’eau et vit dans l’eau.» (*The fish is the hieroglyph of the Philosophers' stone in its first state, because the stone, like the fish, is born in water and lives in water.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

> «Au baigneur, on avait substitué un poisson en porcelaine, et ce poisson était une sole (lat. Sol, solis, le soleil).» (*In place of the bather a porcelain fish had been put, and this fish was a sole (Lat. Sol, solis, the sun).*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

### Fish eyes — *yeux de poisson* {#e300}

*term; Literal translation; the quoted text glosses it itself ('c'est-à-dire des petites bouteilles sur la superficie') [PARIS.3 ¶11].*

Stated (in the Clef du Cabinet hermétique, quoting Philalèthe): one sign of the first putrefaction is that the matter looks like fish eyes, that is, small bubbles on its surface, as if it were foaming, a mark that it ferments and boils; this fermentation is long and done by the secret fire [PARIS.3 ¶11].

> «Philalèthe dit qu’il faut qu’elle paroisse comme des yeux de poisson, c’est-à-dire des petites bouteilles sur la superficie, et qu’il paroisse qu’elle écume» (*Philalethes says it must look like fish eyes, that is, little bubbles on the surface, and that it should seem to foam*) [PARIS.3 ¶11]

### Flamboyant Gothic — *Gothique flamboyant* {#e191}

*term; The usual English term for this style.*

Name given to the style of the 14th and 15th centuries; the text explains it by the predominance of the fiery symbol in the last period of medieval art, following its reading of rose windows as images of fire and of matter moved by fire [INTRO.6 ¶12].

> «Dans l’architecture des XIVe et XVe siècles, la prépondérance du symbole igné, qui caractérise nettement la dernière période de l’art médiéval, a fait donner au style de cette époque le nom de Gothique flamboyant.» (*In the architecture of the 14th and 15th centuries, the predominance of the fiery symbol, which clearly marks the last period of medieval art, caused the style of that time to be called Flamboyant Gothic.*) [INTRO.6 ¶12]

### Flogging of the Alleluia — *Flagellation de l’Alleluia* {#e118}

*term; Literal translation.*

A yearly custom in Langres cathedral, among those in which 'a hermetic sense, often very pure' shows through: the choirboys drove their humming tops out of the nave with whips [INTRO.1 ¶15].

> «comme la Flagellation de l’Alleluia, dans laquelle les enfants de chœur chassaient, à grands coups de fouet, leurs sabots ronflants hors des nefs de la cathédrale de Langres» (*such as the Flogging of the Alleluia, in which the choirboys drove their humming tops with great lashes of the whip out of the naves of Langres cathedral*) [INTRO.1 ¶15]

### Foliated earth — *terre feuillée* {#e504}

*term; Literal translation.*

Stated: the prepared mercury is a stony, friable, flaky mass whose crystalline plates lie like the leaves of a book; hence the names terre feuillée, terre des feuilles, livre aux feuillets [BOURGES.2 ¶16]. The Kings' Cake's flaky dough confirms this [BOURGES.2 ¶16]. The Cosmopolite says the weight of the water must be plural and that of the white or red foliated earth singular [PARIS.5 ¶6].

> «Les lames cristallines qui en composent la substance se trouvent, en effet, superposées comme les feuillets d’un livre ; pour cette raison, elle a reçu l’épithète de terre feuillée, terre des feuilles, livre aux feuillets, etc.» (*The crystalline plates composing its substance are indeed stacked like the leaves of a book; for this reason it received the epithet of foliated earth, earth of leaves, book of leaves, etc.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶16]

> «Le poids de l’eau, dit-il, doit estre pluriel, et celui de la terre feuillée blanche ou rouge doit estre singulier.» (*The weight of the water, he says, must be plural, and that of the white or red foliated earth must be singular.*) [PARIS.5 ¶6]

### Forest of Morois — *forêt de Morois* {#e482}

*term; Name as spelled in the text.*

The setting of the Tristan and Yseult corbel [BOURGES.1 ¶8]. Later the text calls it the forest 'de Mort-Roi', strange because the tree behind Tristan is laden with huge fruits, and would liken it to the Garden of the Hesperides [BOURGES.1 ¶10]. The text gives no further comment on the spelling Mort-Roi.

> «Etrange forêt, en vérité, que celle de Mort-Roi, et combien nous serions porté à l’assimiler au fabuleux et mirifique Jardin des Hespérides !» (*A strange forest indeed, that of Mort-Roi, and how inclined we would be to liken it to the fabulous and marvellous Garden of the Hesperides!*) [BOURGES.1 ¶10]

### Fountain of Life — *Fontaine de Vie* {#e276}

*term; Literal translation; the book itself renders the Latin 'Fons Vitae' of the Brixen inscription as 'Fontaine de Vie' [PARIS.2 n6].*

The Brixen inscription has the blood of Christ and the Virgin's milk mingle 'et sit Fons Vitae' [PARIS.2 ¶15]; the book's French gives 'la Fontaine de Vie et la Source du Bien' [PARIS.2 n6]. The text says the esoteric tradition of the 'Fontaine de Vie ou Fontaine de Jouvence' was materialised in the sacred wells of medieval Gothic churches, whose water was held to heal [PARIS.2 ¶21]. In the Bourges chapter the text states why mercury is called the Fountain of Youth: when gold is dissolved in it to bring it back to its first state, 'young' or 'child' gold, the gold is reborn in its womb [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Stated: the Fontaine de Jouvence is the philosophers' mercury in which gold is rejuvenated [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Interpreted: the name ties this entry to the occult fountain sculpted on the Paris portal (the dissolvent) [PARIS.2 ¶4–6].

> «C’est la raison pour laquelle le mercure, — loyal serviteur et Scel de la terre, — est nommé Fontaine de Jouvence.» (*That is why mercury, the loyal servant and Seal of the earth, is called the Fountain of Youth.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

> «Disons enfin que la tradition ésotérique de la Fontaine de Vie ou Fontaine de Jouvence se trouve matérialisée dans les Puits sacrés» (*Let us say finally that the esoteric tradition of the Fountain of Life or Fountain of Youth is found materialised in the Sacred Wells*) [PARIS.2 ¶21]

Identified in the text with: [Mercury](#e028)

### Four ages of the world — *quatre âges du monde* {#e533}

*term; Literal translation.*

On the Hendaye pedestal a circle cut by two perpendicular diameters bears an A in each sector, the four ages of the world [HENDAYE ¶11]; the four great centuries, with their rulers Alexander, Augustus, Charlemagne, Louis XIV, figure them historically [HENDAYE ¶10]. In the Middle Ages the four phases are shown by the four evangelists or four animals around Christ [HENDAYE ¶12]. Hindu myth figures them by the cow of Virtue standing on four, three, two, then one foot: Crédayougam, Trédayougam, Touvabarayougam, Calyougam [HENDAYE ¶14]; ours is the iron age [HENDAYE ¶14–15].

> «Ceux-ci sont chargés d’un A qui les désigne comme les quatre âges du monde, dans cet hiéroglyphe complet de l’univers, formé des signes conventionnels du ciel et de la terre» (*These bear an A that marks them as the four ages of the world, in this complete hieroglyph of the universe, formed of the conventional signs of heaven and earth*) [HENDAYE ¶11]

### Fox — *renard* {#e364}

*term; Literal translation.*

Stated: the fox, 'notre renard hermétique' or goupil, is the state of provisional fixity the volatile cock (mercury) must take when held long in the fire; then the water has become earth and the mercury sulphur [AMIENS ¶15]. This dry earth must be redissolved in its own water so the fox regains the wings and tail of the cock; repeated, the process yields the first stone, and a third reiteration is needed [AMIENS ¶15]. On the Paris stylobate a cock-fox monster shows the extraction of the red, incombustible Sulphur [PARIS.4 ¶19]; at Amiens the fox tries to reach the cock on the oak [AMIENS ¶14].

> «Elle change alors d’emblème, et son endurance au feu, sa qualité pondéreuse lui font attribuer le renard comme enseigne de sa nouvelle nature.» (*It then changes its emblem, and its endurance in the fire, its heavy quality, cause the fox to be given it as the sign of its new nature.*) [AMIENS ¶15]

### Fr. Noel — *Fr. Noel* {#e501}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also 'Noël', PARIS.2 n3).*

Author of the Dictionnaire de la Fable (Paris, Le Normant, 1801) [BOURGES.2 n9; PARIS.2 n3], cited on the Cista of Bacchus [BOURGES.2 ¶15] and as reference for the fountain Libéthra [PARIS.2 ¶7; PARIS.2 n3].

> «dans laquelle, nous dit Fr. Noel, était renfermé ce qu’il y avait de plus mystérieux» (*in which, Fr. Noel tells us, was enclosed what was most mysterious*) [BOURGES.2 ¶15]

### Franciscan convent of Cimiez — *couvent franciscain de Cimiez* {#e029}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Canseliet: one of the small alchemical paintings of this convent shows the two stars, with the Latin legend Cum luce salutem expressing the saving virtue of nocturnal starlight [PREF2 ¶23, PREF2 ¶24].

> «Ne sont-ce pas ces deux étoiles que nous montre l’une des petites peintures alchimiques du couvent franciscain de Cimiez, accompagnée de la légende latine exprimant la vertu salvatrice inhérente au rayonnement nocturne et stellaire :» (*Are these not the two stars shown us by one of the small alchemical paintings of the Franciscan convent of Cimiez, with the Latin legend expressing the saving virtue inherent in the nocturnal, starry radiance:*) [PREF2 ¶23]

### François Cambriel — *François Cambriel* {#e085}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also L. P. François Cambriel, Louis-François Cambriel).*

Author of the Cours de Philosophie hermétique (1843) [PARIS.7 n11]. He solved the enigma of the St Marcel pillar exactly, at least in part [PARIS.7 ¶17]; Fulcanelli's reading broadly agrees with his, but rejects his extending the symbolism to the statue [PARIS.7 ¶28] and his claim that multiplication is not shown [PARIS.7 ¶23]. His engraving is called faked: bearded bishop, short crosier, finger to lips, a gesture 'entirely from his excessive imagination' [PARIS.7 ¶30, PARIS.7 ¶31, PARIS.7 ¶32, PARIS.7 ¶35]. Canseliet defends this verdict against Grillot de Givry and Jean Reyor [PREF3 ¶23, PREF3 ¶32, PREF3 ¶47, PREF3 ¶50].

> «Cette énigme du travail alchimique, solutionnée d’une manière exacte, — au moins en partie, — par François Cambriel, lui valut d’être cité par Champfleury dans ses Excentriques, et par Tcherkapoff dans ses Fous littéraires.» (*This enigma of the alchemical work, solved exactly, — at least in part, — by François Cambriel, earned him a mention by Champfleury in his Excentriques and by Tcherkapoff in his Fous littéraires.*) [PARIS.7 ¶17]

> «Quant au geste fameux des personnages du Mutus Liber et d’Harpocrate, il est sorti tout entier de l’imagination excessive de Cambriel.» (*As for the famous gesture of the figures of the Mutus Liber and of Harpocrates, it came entirely from Cambriel's excessive imagination.*) [PARIS.7 ¶32]

### François Rabelais — *François Rabelais* {#e138}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; also called 'le bon curé de Meudon' [INTRO.3 n1].*

Author of the Life of Gargantua and Pantagruel, an esoteric work and a 'roman d'argot'; the curé de Meudon shows himself a great initiate and a first-rank cabalist [INTRO.3 n1].

> «Le bon curé de Meudon s’y révèle comme un grand initié doublé d’un cabaliste de premier ordre.» (*The good curé of Meudon reveals himself there as a great initiate and a cabalist of the first order besides.*) [INTRO.3 n1]

### François Villon — *François Villon* {#e065}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

The poet Villon was at the head of the truands of the Cour des Miracles, who, like the medieval Frimasons, expressed themselves in argot, as all Initiates did [INTRO.3 ¶5]. Canseliet's third preface opens with lines from his Testament, stanzas XXXVI and XXXVII [PREF3 ¶1, PREF3 ¶8, PREF3 ¶9].

> «Tous les Initiés s’exprimaient en argot, aussi bien les truands de la Cour des Miracles, — le poète Villon à leur tête, — que les Frimasons, ou francs-maçons du moyen âge» (*All the Initiates expressed themselves in argot, the vagabonds of the Court of Miracles, — the poet Villon at their head, — as much as the Frimasons, or freemasons of the Middle Ages*) [INTRO.3 ¶5]

### Freemasons — *francs-maçons* {#e072}

*term; Literal translation; the text also uses 'Frimasons'.*

The medieval Frimasons or freemasons, 'logeurs du bon Dieu', built the argotic masterpieces, spoke argot like all Initiates, and knew the road to the Garden of the Hesperides [INTRO.3 ¶5]. They followed symbolically the divine example of building on the stone [INTRO.4 ¶5]. The cathedrals were built by them to transmit hermetic symbols and doctrine [PARIS.8 ¶1]. Their 'Lost Word' is the Verbum dimissum [PARIS.6 ¶25]. Canseliet: Freemasonry still seeks it [PREF3 ¶14].

> «Edifiées par les Frimasons médiévaux pour assurer la transmission des symboles et de la doctrine hermétique, nos grandes cathédrales exercèrent, dès leur apparition, une influence marquée sur nombre de spécimens plus modestes de l’architecture civile ou religieuse.» (*Built by the medieval Frimasons to ensure the transmission of the hermetic symbols and doctrine, our great cathedrals exerted from their appearance a marked influence on many humbler examples of civil or religious architecture.*) [PARIS.8 ¶1]

### Frédéric Portal — *Frédéric Portal* {#e326}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of Des Couleurs Symboliques (1857) [PARIS.3 n12], cited for the language of colours united to religion [PARIS.3 ¶26]. Within Pierre Dujols's quoted passage, Portal says the black Osiris at the beginning of the funerary Ritual represents the soul passing from night to day, death to life [PARIS.3 ¶28].

> «L’Osiris noir, qui paraît au commencement du Rituel funéraire, dit Portal, représente cet état de l’âme qui passe de la nuit au jour, de la mort à la vie.» (*The black Osiris, who appears at the beginning of the funerary Ritual, says Portal, represents that state of the soul passing from night to day, from death to life.*) [PARIS.3 ¶28]

### Fulcanelli — *Fulcanelli* {#e007}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also misprinted 'Fulcanellli' [PREF2 ¶3]).*

The author of the book, Canseliet's Master [PREF1 ¶1, PREF1 ¶5]. Canseliet: he 'is no more'; he disappeared when the Sign was accomplished [PREF1 ¶4, PREF1 ¶5]; in 1922 he had not yet received the Gift of God but was close to it, and kept anonymity [PREF2 ¶1]; the name is a pseudonym 'wanted by the Tradition' [PREF2 ¶2]; he received initiation orally [PREF2 ¶4]; Basile Valentin was his first initiator, an anonymous letter-writer his true one [PREF2 ¶5, PREF2 ¶6]; he knew Julien Champagne in 1905 [PREF3 ¶10]. The epigraph signed FULCANELLI [PREF2 ¶51, PREF2 ¶52] repeats the author's own words on dissecting words [INTRO.3 ¶7]. In the main text he says the problem of the secret fire stopped him for more than twenty years [PARIS.3 ¶20].

> «Que de merveilles, que de choses insoupçonnées ne découvririons-nous pas si nous savions disséquer les mots, en briser l’écorce et libérer l’esprit, divine lumière qu’ils renferment !» (*What wonders, what unsuspected things would we not discover if we knew how to dissect words, break their bark and free the spirit, the divine light they enclose!*) [INTRO.3 ¶7]

> «Fulcanelli n’est plus.» (*Fulcanelli is no more.*) [PREF1 ¶5]

## G

### G. J. Witkowski — *G. J. Witkowski* {#e114}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of L'Art profane à l'Eglise, quoted repeatedly as a source on church imagery (QUOTED: Witkowski): the satirical procession on a capital at Strasbourg [INTRO.1 ¶14]; the window of the Conception of Saint Romain at Saint-Jean, Rouen, where a star on the coverlet figured conception [INTRO.7 ¶36]; Notre-Dame-du-Pilier at Chartres [INTRO.8 ¶5]; a stone Isis at Saint-Etienne, Metz [INTRO.8 ¶13]; the Brixen painting of Christ's blood and the Virgin's milk [PARIS.2 ¶10].

> «La conception était figurée par une étoile qui brillait sur la couverture en contact avec le ventre de la femme…» (*The conception was figured by a star shining on the coverlet in contact with the woman's belly…*) [INTRO.7 ¶36]

> «Cette figure en pierre d’Isis, écrit l’auteur, mesurant 0 m. 43 de haut sur 0 m. 29 de large, provenait du vieux cloître.» (*This stone figure of Isis, writes the author, measuring 0.43 m high by 0.29 m wide, came from the old cloister.*) [INTRO.8 ¶13]

### Gabritius — *Gabritius* {#e145}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named with Beya as the brother and sister whose union produces the compost or philosophical rebis, paralleled with Apollo and Diana [INTRO.3 n4].

> «réalisé par l’union du frère et de la sœur, de Gabritius et de Beya, d’Apollon et de Diane.» (*made by the union of the brother and the sister, of Gabritius and Beya, of Apollo and Diana.*) [INTRO.3 n4]

### Gall (oak-gall) — *galle* {#e507}

*term; Usual English for the oak growth; the text gives Latin galla.*

The oak bears galls (noix de galle, Latin galla) [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. The text sets galla, Gallia, gallus side by side — gall, Gaul, cock — the cock being emblem of Gaul and attribute of Mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. Stated: the gall gives the name of the raw mercurial matter, while the kermès names the prepared substance [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. The Golden Fleece hangs on the oak like gall and kermes [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. Greek gala means milk, and mercury is called Lait de Vierge [BOURGES.2 ¶23].

> «Or, si nous rapprochons trois mots de la même famille latine : galla, Gallia, gallus, nous obtenons galle, Gaule, coq.» (*Now, if we bring together three words of the same Latin family, galla, Gallia, gallus, we obtain gall, Gaul, cock.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶22]

> «Toutefois, tandis que la galle donne le nom de la matière mercurielle brute, le kermès (en arabe girmiz, qui teint en écarlate) caractérise la substance préparée.» (*However, while the gall gives the name of the raw mercurial matter, the kermes (Arabic girmiz, which dyes scarlet) characterizes the prepared substance.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶22]

### Garden of the Hesperides — *Jardin des Hespérides* {#e134}

*term; The usual English term; literal translation.*

The medieval builder 'nautes' knew its road [INTRO.3 ¶5]. Pegasus carried Perseus to the Hesperides [PARIS.5 ¶2]. The last Notre-Dame medallion shows the tree with the precious fruits of the Garden beside Jason and the ram [PARIS.5 ¶15]. Fulcanelli would liken the fruit-laden forest of Mort-Roi in the Tristan group to it [BOURGES.1 ¶10].

> «Eux-mêmes, ces nautes constructeurs, connaissaient la route du Jardin des Hespérides…» (*They themselves, these builder-sailors, knew the road to the Garden of the Hesperides…*) [INTRO.3 ¶5]

> «Etrange forêt, en vérité, que celle de Mort-Roi, et combien nous serions porté à l’assimiler au fabuleux et mirifique Jardin des Hespérides !» (*A strange forest indeed, that of Mort-Roi, and how inclined we would be to liken it to the fabulous and wondrous Garden of the Hesperides!*) [BOURGES.1 ¶10]

### Gaston de Claves — *Gaston de Claves* {#e305}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named only as the author to whom 'others' attribute the classic work (Le Filet d'Ariadne) of which Batsdorff is the presumed author [PARIS.3 ¶15; PARIS.3 n7].

> «Batsdorff, auteur présumé d’un ouvrage classique, que d’autres attribuent à Gaston de Claves» (*Batsdorff, presumed author of a classic work which others attribute to Gaston de Claves*) [PARIS.3 ¶15]

### Geber — *Geber* {#e455}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Huginus à Barma says the old masters, Geber, Lully and Paracelsus, each had his own process [PARIS.7 ¶3]. Geber is quoted praising the Most High for creating this Mercury, without which the alchemists' work would be useless [AMIENS ¶15]. The threefold calcination of the Sulphur yields the three philosophical works 'according to the doctrine of Geber' [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. He is called Magister magistrorum, with whom Jean Lallemant deserves to commune [BOURGES.2 ¶38].

> «Béni soit le Très-Haut, s’écrie Geber, qui a créé ce Mercure et lui a donné une nature à laquelle rien ne résiste !» (*Blessed be the Most High, cries Geber, who created this Mercury and gave it a nature that nothing resists!*) [AMIENS ¶15]

### Genèse — Genesis — *Genèse* {#e290}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with the usual English name.*

Hermetic philosophy, the text says, often invokes Genesis as an analogy for the first work of the Work [PARIS.2 ¶26]. As day succeeds night in Genesis, light succeeds darkness, and its signature is the white colour [PARIS.3 ¶28]. Interpreted: the same analogy is drawn without naming the book where the Spirit moving on the waters and the six days of God's 'Grand Œuvre' are recalled [PARIS.6 ¶26–27], and where many authors liken the forming of the philosophical compound to the genesis of the world [PARIS.5 ¶12].

> «La Philosophie hermétique invoque fréquemment le témoignage de la Genèse pour servir d’analogie au premier travail de l’Œuvre» (*Hermetic Philosophy frequently invokes the testimony of Genesis to serve as an analogy for the first labour of the Work*) [PARIS.2 ¶26]

### Georges Aurach — *G. Aurach* {#e485}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (full form 'Georges Aurach' in BOURGES.2 n1).*

Georges Aurach of Strasbourg wrote and painted by his own hand, in 1415, the manuscript Le Très précieux Don de Dieu [BOURGES.2 n1]. It shows a glass matras half full of a green liquor and says its name shows its colour, in the discussion of the vessel called Lion vert; the third figure of the Toison d'Or is almost identical [BOURGES.2 ¶5].

> «Le manuscrit de G. Aurach montre un matras de verre, rempli à moitié d’une liqueur verte, et ajoute que son nom même indique sa couleur.» (*G. Aurach's manuscript shows a glass matras half filled with a green liquor, and adds that its very name indicates its colour.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶5]

### Georges Durand — *Georges Durand* {#e440}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of the Monographie de l'Eglise Cathédrale d'Amiens [AMIENS n1]. With others he reads the Amiens wheel-fire quatrefoil as Ezekiel's vision of the wheels; Fulcanelli finds two errors in this [AMIENS ¶4; AMIENS ¶5]. He calls another relief a replica of Paris's seventh medallion and prefers for it the name Apostasy proposed by the abbé Roze [AMIENS ¶10; AMIENS ¶11]. On the cock and fox he notes the dogs of the fable are missing [AMIENS ¶14].

> «Prenant naïvement le texte au pied de la lettre, l’artiste a réduit la vision à sa plus simple expression.» (*Naively taking the text literally, the artist has reduced the vision to its simplest expression.*) [AMIENS ¶4]

### Gift of God — *Don de Dieu* {#e014}

*term; Literal translation.*

The final grace of achieving the Work. Canseliet: in 1922 Fulcanelli had not yet received the Gift of God but was so close to 'supreme Illumination' that he chose to wait and remain anonymous [PREF2 ¶1]. The letter of his master congratulates the recipient on having truly had the gift of God, a great and rare Grace; he now possesses 'the treasure of treasures' [PREF2 ¶8]. Distinct from the manuscript title Le Très précieux Don de Dieu of Georges Aurach [BOURGES.2 n1].

> «Cette fois, vous avez eu vraiment le don de dieu ; c’est une grande Grâce, et pour la première fois, je comprends combien cette faveur est rare.» (*This time you have truly had the gift of God; it is a great Grace, and for the first time I understand how rare this favour is.*) [PREF2 ¶8]

### Goetic art — *Art goétique* {#e129}

*term; Literal translation; the text glosses goétique as 'magique' [INTRO.3 ¶4].*

A view Fulcanelli reports (QUOTED: 'quelques auteurs perspicaces'): struck by the likeness of gothique and goétique, some authors linked Gothic art with goetic or magical art [INTRO.3 ¶4]. He calls them perspicacious but gives his own reading, argotique [INTRO.3 ¶5].

> «Quelques auteurs perspicaces, et moins superficiels, frappés de la similitude qui existe entre gothique et goétique, ont pensé qu’il devait y avoir un rapport étroit entre l’Art gothique et l’Art goétique ou magique.» (*Some perspicacious and less superficial authors, struck by the similarity between gothique and goétique, thought there must be a close connection between Gothic Art and Goetic or magical Art.*) [INTRO.3 ¶4]

### Golden age — *âge d’or* {#e531}

*term; The usual English term.*

From the promised land where life takes refuge, the elect will witness the return of the golden age; finding its location is left to us [HENDAYE ¶9]. In the Hindu scheme the first age, Crédayougam or age of innocence, answers to the Greeks' golden age [HENDAYE ¶14].

> «Quant à la situation géographique de cette terre promise, d’où les élus assisteront au retour de l’âge d’or, c’est à nous de la rechercher.» (*As for the geographical position of this promised land, from which the elect will witness the return of the golden age, it is for us to seek it.*) [HENDAYE ¶9]

### Golden Fleece — *Toison d’Or* {#e132}

*term; The usual English term; literal translation.*

The fable is a complete enigma of the hermetic work leading to the Philosophers' Stone [BOURGES.2 ¶20]. In the Adepts' language the Golden Fleece is both the matter prepared for the Work and the final result, differing only in purity, fixity and maturity [BOURGES.2 ¶20]. The Hôtel Lallemant chapel relief shows the ram's fleece guarded by a dragon among oaks [BOURGES.2 ¶17, BOURGES.2 ¶18, BOURGES.2 ¶19]; oak and ram are one thing under two aspects [BOURGES.2 ¶21], and the Fleece hangs on the oak like the gall and the kermes [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. The Argonauts sailed to win it [INTRO.3 ¶5, PARIS.5 ¶15]. Canseliet recalls his own study titled Toison d'Or on the star [PREF2 ¶20].

> «La fable de la Toison d’or est une énigme complète du travail hermétique qui doit aboutir à la Pierre Philosophale.» (*The fable of the Golden Fleece is a complete enigma of the hermetic work that is to end in the Philosophers' Stone.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶20]

> «Dans le langage des Adeptes, on appelle Toison d’or la matière préparée pour l’Œuvre, ainsi que le résultat final.» (*In the language of the Adepts, Golden Fleece is the name for the matter prepared for the Work, as well as for the final result.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶20]

### Gothic art — *art gothique* {#e128}

*term; Literal translation; the text's own cabalistic reading derives it from argotique [INTRO.3 ¶5].*

The French art of the 12th to 15th centuries [INTRO.3 ¶1]. Fulcanelli rejects derivations from the Goths or from 'barbarous' [INTRO.3 ¶2] and says the people kept 'Art gothique' against the Academy's 'Art ogival'; the explanation lies in the word's cabalistic origin [INTRO.3 ¶3]. For him 'art gothique' is a spelling distortion of 'argotique': the cathedral is a work of 'art goth' or argot [INTRO.3 ¶5]. Gothic art is also 'art got ou cot', the art of Light or of the Spirit [INTRO.3 ¶6]. The fire symbol gave the late style the name Flamboyant Gothic [INTRO.6 ¶12].

> «Pour nous, art gothique n’est qu’une déformation orthographique du mot argotique, dont l’homophonie est parfaite» (*For us, art gothique is only a spelling distortion of the word argotique, with which it is perfectly homophonous*) [INTRO.3 ¶5]

> «L’art gothique est, en effet, l’art got ou cot (Χο), l’art de la Lumière ou de l’Esprit.» (*Gothic art is, indeed, the art got or cot (Χο), the art of Light or of the Spirit.*) [INTRO.3 ¶6]

### Gothic cathedral — *cathédrale gothique* {#e008}

*term; Literal translation.*

The book's object. The cathedral is a sanctuary of Tradition, Science and Art, not only a monument to Christianity [INTRO.1 ¶6]; an encyclopedia of medieval knowledge [INTRO.2 ¶3]; 'books of images' with pages of stone [INTRO.1 ¶2]. 'Gothic' is read cabalistically as argotique: the cathedral is a work of 'art goth' or argot [INTRO.3 ¶5]. Its cross plan is the hieroglyph of the crucible [INTRO.4 ¶1]. The whole cathedral is a mute glorification of the science of Hermes, the alchemical temple par excellence [PARIS.1 ¶12]; it was built by the medieval Frimasons to transmit hermetic symbols and doctrine [PARIS.8 ¶1]. Canseliet: thanks to Fulcanelli it gives up its secret [PREF1 ¶6].

> «Car la cathédrale tout entière n’est qu’une glorification muette, mais imagée, de l’antique science d’Hermès, dont elle a su, d’ailleurs, conserver l’un des anciens artisans.» (*For the whole cathedral is nothing but a mute, yet pictured, glorification of the ancient science of Hermes, of which it has moreover kept one of the old craftsmen.*) [PARIS.1 ¶12]

> «La cathédrale est une œuvre d’art goth ou d’argot.» (*The cathedral is a work of art goth, or of argot.*) [INTRO.3 ¶5]

### Grasset d’Orcet — *Grasset d’Orcet* {#e529}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited as having stated the rules of the 'Diplomatique' clearly enough, in his analysis of the Songe de Polyphile published by the Revue Britannique, that Fulcanelli need not repeat them [HENDAYE ¶9].

> «Quelques auteurs, et particulièrement Grasset d’Orcet, dans l’analyse du Songe de Polyphile, publiée par la Revue Britannique, les ont données assez clairement pour nous dispenser d’en parler après eux.» (*Some authors, and particularly Grasset d'Orcet, in the analysis of the Dream of Poliphilo published by the Revue Britannique, have given them clearly enough to spare us from speaking of them after them.*) [HENDAYE ¶9]

### Great Book of Nature — *Grand Livre de la Nature* {#e505}

*term; Literal translation.*

The first matter of the Work is shown as a book, open or closed according as it has been worked or only extracted from the mine; closed, sometimes sealed with seven bands, the seven successive operations that open it [BOURGES.2 ¶16]. Such is the Great Book of Nature, of simple style, easy to read once found and opened [BOURGES.2 ¶16]. On Notre-Dame, Alchemy holds two books, one closed (esotericism), one open (exotericism) [PARIS.1 ¶3]. The Lallemant ceiling shows a child with an open book beside a dead serpent, and an open book devoured by fire [BOURGES.2 ¶28].

> «Tel est le Grand Livre de la Nature, qui renferme en ses pages la révélation des sciences profanes et celle des mystères sacrés.» (*Such is the Great Book of Nature, which holds in its pages the revelation of the profane sciences and that of the sacred mysteries.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶16]

> «Aussi, voyons-nous la première matière de l’Œuvre exprimée symboliquement par un livre tantôt ouvert, tantôt fermé, selon qu’elle a été travaillée ou seulement extraite de la mine.» (*So we see the first matter of the Work expressed symbolically by a book, sometimes open, sometimes closed, according as it has been worked or only extracted from the mine.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶16]

### Great Days of Solomon — *Grands Jours de Salomon* {#e039}

*term; Literal translation.*

Canseliet: the separation of the pure, luminous parts from the dense, dark parts of the alchemical earth, extracting light from darkness, is the work of the first of the Great Days of Solomon [PREF2 ¶32]. The text gives no further explanation. Interpreted: this parallels Fulcanelli's remark that in God's Great Work light was separated on the first of six days [PARIS.6 ¶27].

> «Or, cette séparation doit être accomplie, qui consiste à extraire la lumière des ténèbres et à réaliser le travail du premier des Grands Jours de Salomon.» (*Now this separation must be accomplished, which consists in extracting light from darkness and carrying out the work of the first of the Great Days of Solomon.*) [PREF2 ¶32]

### Great Work — *Grand Œuvre* {#e019}

*term; Usual English term.*

The alchemical operation as a whole, ending in the Philosophers' Stone. The alchemy figure on the Notre-Dame trumeau is 'the seal of the lay Great Work on the brow of the Christian Great Work' [PARIS.1 ¶4]; God took six days to complete 'his Great Work', light being separated on the first [PARIS.6 ¶27]. Its cornerstone is the First stone [INTRO.4 ¶5]. Its colours run black, white, red [PARIS.3 ¶25]; it may be done by two ways, dry and humid [PARIS.7 ¶3, PARIS.7 ¶4]; three identical operations give Sulphur, Elixir and Stone [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. The fable of the Golden Fleece is a complete enigma of it [BOURGES.2 ¶20]. Canseliet: the letter gives an idea of the 'sublime domain' of the Great Work [PREF2 ¶6]; the artist imitates Nature and the divine Great Work [PREF2 ¶32].

> «l’empreinte, le sceau du Grand Oeuvre laïque au front du Grand Oeuvre chrétien.» (*the imprint, the seal of the lay Great Work on the brow of the Christian Great Work.*) [PARIS.1 ¶4]

> «Enfin, souvenez-vous que Dieu employa six jours à parfaire son Grand Œuvre ; que la lumière fut séparée le premier jour» (*Finally, remember that God took six days to complete his Great Work; that the light was separated on the first day*) [PARIS.6 ¶27]

### Green Lion — *Lion vert* {#e357}

*term; The usual English term; literal translation.*

Stated: the first magnetic agent used to prepare the dissolvent; called green not so much for its colour as because it has not acquired the mineral characters of the adult state: a green, sour fruit, metallic youth with a latent germ, the embryo of our stone [PARIS.4 ¶17]. Other names: Green Vitriol (Basile Valentin), Emerald of the Philosophers, May Dew, Saturnian Herb, Vegetable Stone [PARIS.4 ¶17]. The Red Lion is the same matter brought to the quality of hermetic gold [PARIS.4 ¶18]. The occult salt of the Red Lion is extracted with the spirit of the Green Lion [PARIS.5 ¶2]; the old man and the Palace are a variant of the Green and Red Lions, the dissolvent and the body to dissolve [PARIS.5 ¶11]. Tristan 'de Léonois' figures the making of the Green Lion [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. The secret vessel too is called Green Lion; authors (Batsdorff, the Cosmopolite, G. Aurach, Ripley) are quoted on it without a clear reason being given [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. Identified with Vitriol and May Dew (e358, e359).

> «est appelé Lion vert, non pas tant parce qu’il possède une coloration verte, que parce qu’il n’a point acquis les caractères minéraux qui distinguent chimiquement l’état adulte de l’état naissant.» (*is called Green Lion, not so much because it has a green colour as because it has not acquired the mineral characters that chemically distinguish the adult state from the nascent state.*) [PARIS.4 ¶17]

> «Généralement, il est appelé œuf philosophique et Lion vert.» (*Generally, it is called philosophical egg and Green Lion.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶5]

Identified in the text with: [Vitriol](#e358), [May Dew](#e359)

### Gregory of Tours — *Grégoire de Tours* {#e285}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; Gregory of Tours is the usual English form.*

Cited as the authority that the water of the well of Saint-Marcel in Paris, dug inside the church near the bishop's tombstone, was a powerful remedy for several ailments [PARIS.2 ¶21].

> «se révélait, d’après Grégoire de Tours, comme un puissant spécifique de plusieurs affections.» (*proved, according to Gregory of Tours, a powerful specific for several ailments.*) [PARIS.2 ¶21]

### Grey — *gris* {#e241}

*term; Literal translation; the text glosses the old meaning of gris as fire [INTRO.9 ¶11].*

In the quoted account of the Parvis monolith, it was called messire Legris when gris meant fire, particularly firedamp, will-o'-the-wisp [INTRO.9 ¶11]. The colossal Saint Christopher of Notre-Dame was painted grey [INTRO.9 ¶14]. According to Aristotle, grey or violet is the emblematic colour of Mercury, which the text says explains why statues of Saint Christopher were coated in that tone [INTRO.9 ¶15]. In the rose window one of the two quarrelling children is violet grey [PARIS.5 ¶18].

> «D’après Aristote, le Mercure a pour couleur emblématique le gris ou le violet, ce qui suffit à expliquer pourquoi les statues de saint Christophe étaient revêtues d’un enduit du même ton.» (*According to Aristotle, Mercury has grey or violet as its emblematic colour, which is enough to explain why the statues of Saint Christopher were given a coating of the same tone.*) [INTRO.9 ¶15]

> «il se nommait aussi messire Legris, alors que gris signifiait feu, et particulièrement feu grisou, feu follet» (*it was also named Messire Legris, at a time when gris meant fire, and particularly firedamp, will-o'-the-wisp*) [INTRO.9 ¶11]

### Griffin — *griffon* {#e343}

*term; Literal translation.*

The eighth figure of the Paris stylobate [PARIS.4 ¶3, ¶16]. Stated: a monster with the head and breast of the eagle and the rest of the lion, it teaches the contrary qualities that must be joined in the philosophical matter; it is the hieroglyph of the first conjunction [PARIS.4 ¶3]. In it the Lion is the fixed, basic part, which loses its head (its form) on contact with the opposing volatility [PARIS.4 ¶16].

> «Le monstre mythologique dont la tête et la poitrine sont celles de l’aigle, et qui emprunte au lion le reste du corps, initie l’investigateur aux qualités contraires qu’il faut nécessairement assembler dans la matière philosophale» (*The mythological monster whose head and breast are those of the eagle, and which borrows the rest of its body from the lion, initiates the seeker into the contrary qualities that must be joined in the philosophical matter*) [PARIS.4 ¶3]

### Gril — *Gril* {#e051}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Latin 'Grillus').*

In Helvetius's account translated by Canseliet: a goldsmith of The Hague, practised in alchemy but very poor; he poured a specially prepared spirit of salt on lead in a glass jar; after two weeks a silver Star floated on it, which he took for the visible star of the Philosophers, probably learned from Basile Valentin; after cupellation a pound of the lead gave twelve ounces of silver, and from these two ounces of gold [PREF2 ¶41]. Canseliet cites it to show the star accompanies internal changes of bodies treated philosophically, but warns that two essential data are missing [PREF2 ¶42].

> «D’où Gril, rempli d’une immense joie, nous annonça avoir déjà vu l’étoile visible des Philosophes, sur laquelle, probablement, il s’était instruit dans Basile (Valentin).» (*Whereupon Gril, filled with immense joy, told us he had already seen the visible star of the Philosophers, about which he had probably learned in Basil (Valentine).*) [PREF2 ¶41]

### Grillot de Givry — *Grillot de Givry* {#e089}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also Emile-Jules Grillot de Givry).*

Author of Le Grand Œuvre (1907), quoted: the bishop on the Notre-Dame south portal stands on the aludel where philosophical mercury sublimes; the door kept closed all year shows the 'non-vulgar way' [PARIS.7 ¶2, PARIS.7 n1]. Canseliet: in his Musée des Sorciers he claimed a third, 16th-century statue to excuse Cambriel, which Canseliet rejects as baseless; he died a week before it appeared [PREF3 ¶27, PREF3 ¶29, PREF3 ¶33, PREF3 ¶49].

> «« Vois, dit Grillot de Givry, sculpté sur le portail droit de Notre-Dame de Paris, l’évêque juché sur l’aludel où se sublime, enchaîné dans les limbes, le mercure philosophal.» (*'See,' says Grillot de Givry, 'carved on the right portal of Notre-Dame de Paris, the bishop perched on the aludel where the philosophical mercury sublimes, chained in limbo.*) [PARIS.7 ¶2]

### Guillaume de Paris — *Guillaume de Paris* {#e242}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also 'Guillaume le Parisien').*

Some saw his features in the Parvis monolith, which he was said to have erected together with the portal of Notre-Dame [INTRO.9 ¶12]. Foreseeing the damage of time, he had the medallions of the great porch reproduced in the glass of the central rose, which preserves their colours [PARIS.5 ¶17]. A legend says he hid the philosophers' stone in one of the pillars of the nave, its place fixed by the gaze of the carved raven; Victor Hugo is quoted on him [PARIS.6 ¶18]. His hermetic work influenced the decoration of the great porch of Amiens [AMIENS ¶1].

> «En maître avisé, il fit reproduire minutieusement les motifs des médaillons sur les vitraux de la rose centrale.» (*As a far-sighted master, he had the subjects of the medallions reproduced in detail in the glass of the central rose.*) [PARIS.5 ¶17]

> «aurait caché la pierre philosophale dans l’un des piliers de l’immense nef.» (*is said to have hidden the philosophers' stone in one of the pillars of the immense nave.*) [PARIS.6 ¶18]

### Gérard Dubois of Orléans — *Gérard Dubois d’Orléans* {#e097}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Latin: Gerardo Dubois Aurelianensi).*

Father Gérard Dubois of Orléans reports, in his History of the Church of Paris, the legend of Saint Marcel and the serpent, counted among the bishop's last deeds; Canseliet summarizes and translates it from the Latin [PREF3 ¶40, ¶41, ¶42].

> «qui est rapporté par le Père Gérard Dubois d’Orléans (Gerardo Dubois Aurelianensi)» (*which is reported by Father Gérard Dubois of Orléans (Gerardo Dubois Aurelianensi)*) [PREF3 ¶40]

## H

### Harmonie Chymique — Chemical Harmony — *Harmonie Chymique* {#e435}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of Lagneau, published by Claude Morel in 1601, at whose end the escutcheon copied from Flamel's arcade appears [PARIS.8 ¶2; PARIS.8 ¶4].

> «l’Harmonie chymique parut chez Claude Morel en 1601.» (*the Harmonie chymique appeared from Claude Morel in 1601.*) [PARIS.8 ¶4]

### Harpocrates — *Harpocrate* {#e088}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; English form is the usual one.*

Named for his 'famous gesture', shared with figures of the Mutus Liber, which Cambriel wrongly gave to the St Marcel statue [PARIS.7 ¶32, PREF3 ¶26]. Interpreted: the gesture is the finger to the lips commanding silence, as the same passage describes Cambriel's drawing [PARIS.7 ¶31].

> «Quant au geste fameux des personnages du Mutus Liber et d’Harpocrate, il est sorti tout entier de l’imagination excessive de Cambriel.» (*As for the famous gesture of the figures of the Mutus Liber and of Harpocrates, it came entirely from Cambriel's excessive imagination.*) [PARIS.7 ¶32]

### Haymon — *Haymon* {#e462}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of the Epistola de Lapidibus Philosophicis (treatise 192, vol. VI of the Theatrum Chemicum, Argentorati, 1613) [AMIENS n7]. Fulcanelli quotes him as an example of the symbolic language describing where the first agent is found [AMIENS ¶25–26].

> «Pour obtenir le premier agent, il faut se rendre à la partie postérieure du monde, là où l’on entend gronder le tonnerre, souffler le vent, tomber la grêle et la pluie» (*To obtain the first agent, one must go to the hinder part of the world, where one hears the thunder roll, the wind blow, the hail and rain fall*) [AMIENS ¶26]

### Henckel — *Henckel* {#e415}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

J.-F. Henckel, author of the Traité de l'Appropriation [PARIS.7 n7]. Cited for his reflection that, since the artist Elias claimed the stone is begun and finished in four days and showed it still stuck to the crucible shards, the alchemists' 'great months' might be days, and a method might consist in keeping the matters long in the highest fluidity by a violent fire with bellows [PARIS.7 ¶10].

> «il me semble qu’il ne seroit pas si absurde de mettre en question si ce que les alchymistes appellent des grands mois ne seroit pas autant de jours» (*it seems to me it would not be so absurd to ask whether what the alchemists call great months might not be so many days*) [PARIS.7 ¶10]

### Henri de Linthaut — *Henri de Linthaut* {#e412}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited as a witness for the dry path: the secret lets one reach a great projection in little time, without much care or work, and he refers to Isaac Hollandois [PARIS.7 ¶10]. Fulcanelli regrets he is no more forthcoming than others [PARIS.7 ¶10]. The footnote spells him H. de Lintaut and gives his work L'Aurore, an Arsenal manuscript [PARIS.7 n6].

> «Ce secret icy surpasse tous les secrets du monde, car vous pouvés en peu de tems, sans grand soin ny travail, parvenir à une grande projection» (*This secret here surpasses all the secrets of the world, for you can in little time, without great care or labour, arrive at a great projection*) [PARIS.7 ¶10]

### Hermes — *Hermès* {#e304}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited as an authority of the Art. According to Hermes, sun and moon, sulphur and mercury, are the parents of the stone [PARIS.3 ¶39]; he calls the sun metaphorically the Father of the stone [PARIS.4 ¶21]. Trévisan quotes him: having seen the blackness, believe you have followed a good path [PARIS.3 ¶14]. The king born of fire must rejoice in the marriage 'afin, dit Hermès, que les choses occultes deviennent manifestes' [PARIS.7 ¶19]. In his Emerald Table he says the wind carried it in its belly; the text glosses the wind as the winged, volatile mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The 'termes' were busts of Hermes (Mercury) [INTRO.9 n4]. Kermès has in the Gay Science the same meaning as Hermès, both meaning Mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. Maia was mother of Hermes [PARIS.6 ¶25]. Alchemy is 'l'antique science d'Hermès' [PARIS.1 ¶12].

> «puisque le soleil est dit métaphoriquement, par Hermès, le Père de la pierre.» (*since the sun is called metaphorically, by Hermes, the Father of the stone.*) [PARIS.4 ¶21]

### Hermeticist — *hermétiste* {#e010}

*term; Literal rendering of the French term.*

A student of the hermetic science. Hermeticists know that the king of metals is figured by the solar sign [PARIS.7 ¶27]; a detail of the Black Virgin processions (green candles only) is 'useful for the hermeticist' [INTRO.8 ¶12]; Amédée de Ponthieu 'was no hermeticist' and so judged without bias [INTRO.9 ¶10]; Chaudet, who drew the escutcheon of St Thomas Aquinas, was one [PARIS.8 ¶5]. Canseliet: hermeticists will find in the book that from the confrontation of the Book and the Building the Spirit emerges and the letter dies [PREF1 ¶9].

> «Or, les hermétistes savent que le roi des métaux est figuré par le signe solaire, c'est-à-dire une circonférence avec ou sans point central.» (*Now, hermeticists know that the king of metals is figured by the solar sign, that is, a circle with or without a central point.*) [PARIS.7 ¶27]

### Hermès dévoilé — Hermes Unveiled — *Hermès dévoilé* {#e411}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Book of Cyliani, Paris, F. Locquin, 1832 [PARIS.7 n5]. Its Preface is cited for the two paths, dry and wet: Cyliani follows the wet 'by duty', though the dry, done with a single matter, is very familiar to him [PARIS.7 ¶8-9].

> «Je suis cette dernière, de préférence, par devoir, quoique la première me soit très familière : elle se fait avec une matière unique.» (*I follow the latter, by preference, out of duty, although the former is very familiar to me: it is done with a single matter.*) [PARIS.7 ¶9]

### Herodotus — *Hérodote* {#e234}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Quoted on the yearly feast of Isis at Busiris, where an ox was sacrificed and myriads of men and women beat themselves, for a god he refused to name as an impiety; cited to show the mysterious nature of her cult [INTRO.8 ¶14].

> «Pour quel dieu ils se frappent, j’estime que ce serait de ma part une impiété que de le dire.» (*For which god they strike themselves, I judge it would be an impiety on my part to say.*) [INTRO.8 ¶14]

### Hieroglyph — *hiéroglyphe* {#e545}

*term; Literal translation.*

The text's term for a carved or painted image standing for an alchemical thing or operation. Examples stated: the cross is the alchemical hieroglyph of the crucible [INTRO.4 ¶1]; the wheel that of the time of coction and of coction itself [INTRO.6 ¶3]; the Raven that of the black colour [PARIS.3 ¶3]; the fish that of the stone in its first state [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Cathedral sculpture is called a 'prodigieux grimoire' read through such signs [INTRO.2 ¶8]. Canseliet quotes Cambriel's 'hiéroglyphe' of the bishop with a finger to his lips, which Fulcanelli showed to be invented [PREF3 ¶24–26].

> «Or, la croix est l’hiéroglyphe alchimique du creuset, que l’on nommait jadis cruzol, crucible et croiset» (*Now, the cross is the alchemical hieroglyph of the crucible, formerly called cruzol, crucible and croiset*) [INTRO.4 ¶1]

> «Or, la roue est l’hiéroglyphe alchimique du temps nécessaire à la coction de la matière philosophale et, par suite, de la coction elle-même.» (*Now, the wheel is the alchemical hieroglyph of the time needed for the coction of the philosophic matter and, consequently, of coction itself.*) [INTRO.6 ¶3]

### Hiram — *Hiram* {#e178}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Reached through Provençal aran/iran, iron: the Masonic Hiram, the divine Ram, the architect of Solomon's Temple [INTRO.5 ¶5].

> «C’est l’Hiram maçonnique, le divin Bélier, l’architecte du Temple de Salomon.» (*It is the Masonic Hiram, the divine Ram, the architect of Solomon's Temple.*) [INTRO.5 ¶5]

### Histoire critique de Nicolas Flamel — Critical History of Nicolas Flamel — *Histoire critique de Nicolas Flamel* {#e428}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of the abbé Vilain, Paris, Desprez, 1761, source of the description of Flamel's portal at Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie [PARIS.8 n1; PARIS.8 ¶2].

> «Abbé Vilain, Histoire critique de Nicolas Flamel. Paris, Desprez, 1761.» (*Abbé Vilain, Critical History of Nicolas Flamel. Paris, Desprez, 1761.*) [PARIS.8 n1]

### Histoire de l'Alchimie — History of Alchemy — *Histoire de l'Alchimie* {#e430}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of Albert Poisson, with the subtitle Nicolas Flamel, Paris, Chacornac, 1893 [PARIS.8 n2]; source of the passages on Flamel's buildings [PARIS.8 ¶2].

> «Albert Poisson, Histoire de l’Alchimie. Nicolas Flamel. Paris, Chacornac, 1893.» (*Albert Poisson, History of Alchemy. Nicolas Flamel. Paris, Chacornac, 1893.*) [PARIS.8 n2]

### Histoire de l'Eglise de Paris — History of the Church of Paris — *Histoire de l’Eglise de Paris* {#e098}

*work; Title as spelled in the text (Latin: Historia Ecclesiae Parisiensis), with a literal English rendering.*

Work by Father Gérard Dubois of Orléans, from whose Latin text Canseliet summarizes the legend of Saint Marcel taming the serpent that defiled a noblewoman's tomb [PREF3 ¶40, ¶41, ¶42].

> «dans son Histoire de l’Eglise de Paris (in Historia Ecclesiae Parisiensis)» (*in his History of the Church of Paris (in Historia Ecclesiae Parisiensis)*) [PREF3 ¶40]

### Histoire de la Philosophie Hermétique — History of Hermetic Philosophy — *Histoire de la Philosophie Hermétique* {#e348}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

Work of Lenglet-Dufresnoy, Paris, Coustelier, 1742. Its volume II contains Philalèthe's 'L'Entrée au Palais Fermé de Roy', source of the passage on the flying Eagles [PARIS.4 n1; PARIS.4 ¶3–4], and the 'Règles du Philalèthe', source of the passage on the degree of heat and on boiling [AMIENS n3; AMIENS ¶8–9].

> «Lenglet-Dufresnoy, Histoire de la Philosophie Hermétique. L’Entrée au Palais Fermé de Roy, t. II, p. 35. Paris, Coustelier, 1742.» (*Lenglet-Dufresnoy, History of Hermetic Philosophy. The Entry into the Closed Palace of the King, vol. II, p. 35. Paris, Coustelier, 1742.*) [PARIS.4 n1]

### Holmat — *Holmat* {#e272}

*term; Name as spelled in the text.*

Name of the Mysterious Fountain among the Arab authors, whose waters gave immortality to the prophet Elias; they place it in the Modhallan [PARIS.2 ¶9].

> «Chez les auteurs arabes, cette fontaine porte le nom d’Holmat ; ils nous enseignent, de plus que ses eaux donnèrent l’immortalité au prophète Elie» (*Among the Arab authors this fountain bears the name Holmat; they teach us, moreover, that its waters gave immortality to the prophet Elias*) [PARIS.2 ¶9]

### Holy Earth — *Terre sainte* {#e509}

*term; Literal translation; Latin Terra sancta is the text's.*

Names of the raw subject of the Work: Magnesia lunarii; 'more sincerely' Plomb des Sages, Saturnie végétable; for Philalèthe, Basile Valentin and the Cosmopolite, Son or Child of Saturn; for all, Terre sainte; its celestial hieroglyph is Aries [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. The names refer to its magnetic attraction of sulphur or its easy fusibility [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. Opening this matter and isolating its pure part gives the Kermès or tinging mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶23].

> «Pour tous, c’est la Terre sainte (Terra sancta) ; enfin, ce minéral a pour hiéroglyphe céleste le signe astronomique du Bélier (Aries).» (*For all of them it is the Holy Earth (Terra sancta); and this mineral has for celestial hieroglyph the astronomical sign of the Ram (Aries).*) [BOURGES.2 ¶23]

> «Quant au sujet grossier de l’Œuvre, les uns le nomment Magnesia lunarii ; d’autres, plus sincères, l’appellent Plomb des Sages, Saturnie végétable.» (*As for the gross subject of the Work, some call it Magnesia lunarii; others, more sincere, call it Lead of the Sages, vegetable Saturnia.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶23]

### Holy Grail — *saint Graal* {#e523}

*term; The usual English term.*

Named once: Jean Lallemant, alchemist and knight of the Round Table, deserves a place around the Holy Grail with Geber and Roger Bacon [BOURGES.2 ¶38]. The Round Table romances are called traditional hermetic legends [BOURGES.1 ¶7].

> «Jean Lallemant, alchimiste et chevalier de la Table Ronde, mérite de prendre place autour de saint Graal» (*Jean Lallemant, alchemist and knight of the Round Table, deserves to take his place around the Holy Grail*) [BOURGES.2 ¶38]

### Hooligans (seers) — *voyous* {#e133}

*term; Gloss: the text itself reads voyous as 'c'est-à-dire de voyants', seers [INTRO.3 ¶6].*

The epithet given to the minority who speak argot outside received laws; the text reads voyous as voyants (seers) and adds the name Sons or Children of the Sun [INTRO.3 ¶6].

> «auxquels on applique l’épithète de voyous, c'est-à-dire de voyants, et celle, plus expressive encore, de Fils ou Enfants du soleil.» (*to whom the epithet voyous is applied, that is to say voyants (seers), and the even more expressive one of Sons or Children of the sun.*) [INTRO.3 ¶6]

### Horn of Amalthea — *corne d'Amalthée* {#e370}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

On the second lower medallion of Paris the Initiator holds a mirror and raises the horn of Amalthea, beside the Tree of Life; the mirror marks the beginning of the work, the tree its end, the horn of plenty its result [PARIS.5 ¶1, PARIS.5 ¶3]. The Stone, Elixir or Tree of Life gives its owner knowledge, fortune and health: it is the horn of plenty, the unfailing source of material happiness [PARIS.5 ¶4]. On the chapel ceiling of the Hôtel Lallemant a horn of Amalthea overflowing with flowers and fruits is a perch for a hen or partridge [BOURGES.2 ¶28].

> «Le miroir symbolise le début de l’ouvrage, l’arbre de vie en marque la fin, et la corne d’abondance le résultat.» (*The mirror symbolises the beginning of the work, the tree of life marks its end, and the horn of plenty the result.*) [PARIS.5 ¶3]

### Huginus à Barma — *Huginus à Barma* {#e204}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of Le Règne de Saturne changé en Siècle d’or (Paris, 1780) [INTRO.7 n7]. In its Pratique he describes the matter of the Great Work, on which the star appears, in the same terms as Saint Ignatius uses for the star [INTRO.7 ¶15]. His Sigillum Sapientum shows the artist holding a balance in which one pan outweighs the other in an apparent ratio of one to two, which the text links to Basil Valentine's 'white swan' and the question of weights [PARIS.5 ¶6]. Against Pernety, he affirms that the old masters, Geber, Lulle, Paracelsus, each had a process of his own [PARIS.7 ¶3].

> «Prenez de la vraie terre, dit-il, bien imprégnée des rayons du soleil, de la lune et des autres astres.» (*Take true earth, he says, well impregnated with the rays of the sun, the moon and the other stars.*) [INTRO.7 ¶15]

### Hymne à Délos — Hymn to Delos — *Hymne à Délos* {#e350}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

Poem of Callimachus, lines 250 and 255, quoted in Greek [PARIS.4 ¶6–8] with the book's French: the swans circled Delos seven times and had not yet sung the eighth time when Apollo was born [PARIS.4 ¶9]. Fulcanelli reads it as the sublimations (the eagles) and compares Joshua's seven circuits of Jericho [PARIS.4 ¶5, ¶10].

> «(Les Cygnes) tournèrent sept fois autour de Délos… et ils n’avaient pas encore chanté la huitième fois, lorsqu’Apollon naquit.» (*(The Swans) circled Delos seven times... and they had not yet sung the eighth time when Apollo was born. (the book's French rendering of the Greek)*) [PARIS.4 ¶9]

### Hôtel Lallemant — *Hôtel Lallemant* {#e472}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

A modest bourgeois house in Bourges, perfectly preserved, unrestored and unmutilated [BOURGES.1 ¶1; BOURGES.2 ¶1]. Its so-called kitchen is read as an alchemical laboratory, and the 'pestle' of its corbel as a long-necked matras [BOURGES.2 ¶3–4]; laboratory and vessel make it one of the rarest 'demeures philosophales' [BOURGES.2 ¶7]. The loggia holds a painted Saint Christopher [BOURGES.2 ¶8]; the chapel's decoration is wholly hermetic: the Golden Fleece relief, the emblem ceiling, the credence with RERE, RER [BOURGES.2 ¶18–36]. Its archives show Jean Lallemant belonged to the alchemical Fraternity of the Knights of the Round Table [BOURGES.2 n4].

> «le laboratoire et le vase de l’Œuvre, le lieu où besogne l’Adepte et celui où la nature agit, sont les deux certitudes qui frappent l’initié au début de sa visite» (*the laboratory and the vessel of the Work, the place where the Adept toils and the one where nature acts, are the two certainties that strike the initiate at the start of his visit*) [BOURGES.2 ¶7]

> «Toute l’ornementation est profane, tous les motifs qui la décorent sont empruntés à la science hermétique.» (*All the ornament is profane; all the motifs that decorate it are borrowed from hermetic science.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶18]

## I

### Image-maker — *imaigier* {#e113}

*term; Our coinage, a literal rendering of the old French imaigier / tailleur d'imaiges (carver of images), which has no exact English equivalent.*

The medieval carvers of images. Their chisel confirms the burlesque festivals [INTRO.1 ¶14]; the 'tailleur d'imaiges' decorated the projecting parts while the ceramist did the floor [INTRO.5 ¶1]. At Notre-Dame the imaigier transposed Leo and Cancer in the zodiac to signal the conjunction of the philosophical ferment with the mercurial compound [PARIS.6 ¶21]. Fulcanelli stresses their scrupulous exactness in translating their thought [BOURGES.2 ¶4]; they isolated supernatural attributes with a band of clouds [AMIENS ¶6].

> «L’imaigier a donc voulu indiquer, par cette habile transposition, la conjonction du ferment philosophique, — ou Lion, — avec le composé mercuriel» (*The image-maker thus meant to indicate, by this skilful transposition, the conjunction of the philosophical ferment — or Lion — with the mercurial compound*) [PARIS.6 ¶21]

> «nous connaissons assez la conscience, le soin, le scrupule d’exactitude qu’apportaient les imaigiers d’autrefois dans la traduction de leur pensée» (*we know well enough the conscientiousness, the care, the scruple of exactness that the image-makers of old brought to the rendering of their thought*) [BOURGES.2 ¶4]

### Imitation de Jésus-Christ — The Imitation of Christ — *Imitation de Jésus-Christ* {#e468}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited (book II, ch. 1, v. 6 [AMIENS n12]) to close the quotations on the first matter: its thought matches an anonymous author's remark that the Philosophers rightly hid the mystery of the Matter, which God took pleasure in hiding in things that seem useful to men [AMIENS ¶32].

> «Celui qui estime les choses ce qu’elles valent, et n’en jugent pas selon le mérite ou l’estime des hommes, possède la vraie Sagesse.» (*He who values things for what they are worth, and does not judge them by men's merit or esteem, possesses true Wisdom.*) [AMIENS ¶32]

### Initiate — *Initié* {#e332}

*term; Literal translation.*

White is the colour of the Initiates, because the man leaving darkness for light passes from the profane state to that of Initiate, of pure [PARIS.3 ¶28]; Dujols (quoted) makes 'bienheureux' a synonym of Initiate and says the Initiates were in white [PARIS.3 ¶28]. All Initiates spoke argot, from the truands of the Cour des Miracles to the medieval Frimasons [INTRO.3 ¶5]. The Phrygian bonnet was the distinctive sign of the Initiates and, in Dujols's account, 'la marque suprême de l'Initiation' [PARIS.1 n4]. Isis alone dispenses Revelation and Initiation [AMIENS ¶39]. Philosophical calcination is the one 'que l'Initié opère' [PARIS.3 ¶20].

> «La couleur blanche est celle des Initiés, parce que l’homme qui abandonne les ténèbres pour suivre la lumière passe de l’état profane à celui d’Initié, de pur.» (*White is the colour of the Initiates, because the man who leaves darkness to follow the light passes from the profane state to that of Initiate, of pure one.*) [PARIS.3 ¶28]

### INRI — *INRI* {#e532}

*term; The inscription kept; both expansions are the text's own (HENDAYE ¶10).*

Carved on the front arm of the Hendaye cross, matching the cycle diagram on the base [HENDAYE ¶10]. Exoterically it reads Iesus Nazarenus Rex Iudæorum; from the cross it takes its secret meaning Igne Natura Renovatur Integra, for our hemisphere will soon be tried by and in fire, and as fire parts gold from impure metals the good will be separated from the wicked at the Judgment [HENDAYE ¶10].

> «C’est là ce qu’enseigne l’épigraphe INRI, que l’on traduit exotériquement par Iesus Nazarenus Rex Iudæorum, mais qui emprunte à la croix sa signification secrète : Igne Natura Renovatur Integra.» (*That is what the inscription INRI teaches, which is rendered exoterically as Iesus Nazarenus Rex Iudaeorum, but which takes from the cross its secret meaning: Igne Natura Renovatur Integra (by fire Nature is renewed whole).*) [HENDAYE ¶10]

### Iron age — *âge de fer* {#e540}

*term; The usual English term.*

Our age, the fourth, identified with the Calyougam [HENDAYE ¶14]. Its only seal is Death; its hieroglyph the skeleton with Saturn's attributes, the empty hourglass and the scythe, the latter reproduced in the figure seven, number of transformation and annihilation [HENDAYE ¶15]. Its Gospel is Saint Matthew's, the Gospel according to Science [HENDAYE ¶15].

> «L’âge de fer n’a point d’autre sceau que celui de la Mort.» (*The iron age has no other seal than that of Death.*) [HENDAYE ¶15]

Identified in the text with: [Calyougam](#e539)

### Isaac the Hollander — *Isaac Hollandois* {#e414}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named only by Henri de Linthaut as speaking at length of the secret by which a great projection is reached in little time [PARIS.7 ¶10].

> «de laquelle voyés Isaac Hollandois qui en parle amplement.» (*on which see Isaac the Hollander, who speaks of it at length.*) [PARIS.7 ¶10]

### Iseult — *Yseult* {#e480}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; English the usual form.*

On the Jacques-Cœur corbel she wears a crown she steadies with her right hand [BOURGES.1 ¶8]. As queen she is wife both of the old king and of the young hero, keeping the hermetic tradition that makes king, queen and lover the mineral triad of the Great Work [BOURGES.1 ¶10].

> «Remarquons encore que la reine est à la fois l’épouse du vieillard et du jeune héros, afin de maintenir la tradition hermétique qui fait du roi, de la reine et de l’amant la triade minérale du Grand Œuvre.» (*Let us note too that the queen is wife at once of the old man and of the young hero, so as to keep the hermetic tradition that makes the king, the queen and the lover the mineral triad of the Great Work.*) [BOURGES.1 ¶10]

### Isis — *Isis* {#e214}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Statues of Isis once dwelt in the underground chambers of temples and became, with Christianity in Gaul, the Black Virgins, with identical symbolism and the same inscription Virgini parituræ [INTRO.8 ¶4]. Quoting Bigarne via Dujols: Isis before conception is the Virgo paritura, the earth before its fecundation, and also the mother of the gods [INTRO.8 ¶4]. Uncristianized statuettes are rarer still, e.g. one at Metz with red skin and black drapery [INTRO.8 ¶13]. Isis is the Egyptian Ceres; her cult was mysterious and its mysteries were of the same order as those of Hermetic Science [INTRO.8 ¶14]. Isis, Ceres and Cybele are taken as expressions of one principle, 'three heads under the same veil' [INTRO.8 ¶15]. Fulcanelli states that Isis is mother of all things and sole dispenser of Revelation and Initiation [AMIENS ¶39].

> «Isis, Cérès, Cybèle, trois têtes sous le même voile.» (*Isis, Ceres, Cybele, three heads under the same veil.*) [INTRO.8 ¶15]

> «la déesse Isis est la mère de toutes choses, qu’elle les porte toutes dans son sein, et qu’elle seule est la dispensatrice de la Révélation et de l’Initiation.» (*the goddess Isis is the mother of all things, she bears them all in her womb, and she alone is the dispenser of Revelation and of Initiation.*) [AMIENS ¶39]

Identified in the text with: [Ceres](#e229), [Cybele](#e231)

## J

### J. F. Golfs — *J. F. Golfs* {#e107}

*person; Name as spelled in the text: both mentions read 'Golfs' [INTRO.1 ¶5, INTRO.1 n1], so the proposed 'Colfs' is not the book's spelling.*

Author of La Filiation généalogique de toutes les Ecoles gothiques (Paris, Baudry, 1884) [INTRO.1 n1]. Fulcanelli quotes him with approval ('avec beaucoup de vérité') on the language of stones of Gothic art, clear and sublime, speaking to the humblest and the most cultivated [INTRO.1 ¶5].

> «« La langue de pierres que parle cet art nouveau, dit avec beaucoup de vérité J. F. Golfs, est à la fois claire et sublime.» (*'The language of stones that this new art speaks,' says J. F. Golfs with much truth, 'is at once clear and sublime.'*) [INTRO.1 ¶5]

### Jacobus de Voragine — *Jacques de Voragine* {#e244}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; English uses the usual Latin form.*

Reveals Saint Christopher's primitive name, Offerus [INTRO.9 ¶15]. His text and the Lallemant loggia bas-relief of Saint Christopher are said to contain the same hermetic sense; the legend is transcribed as reported by Amédée de Ponthieu after him [BOURGES.2 ¶1; BOURGES.2 ¶8; BOURGES.2 ¶9].

> «Il semble donc hors de doute que le sujet de ce chef-d’œuvre et le texte de Jacques de Voragine contiennent le même sens hermétique, avec, en plus, certain détail qu’on ne saurait trouver ailleurs.» (*It therefore seems beyond doubt that the subject of this masterpiece and the text of Jacobus de Voragine contain the same hermetic meaning, with, in addition, a certain detail found nowhere else.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶9]

### Jacobus Tollius — *Jacobus Tollius* {#e034}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also Tollius, Jacob Tollius, J. Tollius).*

Author of Le Chemin du Ciel Chymique, translation of the Manuductio ad Coelum Chemicum (1688) [PARIS.3 n15, BOURGES.2 n11]. He mocks the puffers who seek their colours (black raven, white dove, yellow eagle, red pheasant) in their glass vessels; for him a fixed thing will not penetrate metals; foul odour denotes fixation, pleasant odour volatility [PARIS.3 ¶36, PARIS.3 ¶37, PARIS.3 ¶38]. He says the cock is the attribute of Mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶22]; Fulcanelli takes his advice to seek the magistery in a volatile thing [BOURGES.2 ¶36]. Canseliet quotes his commentary 'opening' the chemical Heaven [PREF2 ¶27, PREF2 ¶28].

> «Allez, et vous retirez présentement, vous qui cherchez avec une application extrême vos diverses couleurs dans vos vaisseaux de verre.» (*Go, and withdraw at once, you who seek with extreme diligence your various colours in your glass vessels.*) [PARIS.3 ¶36]

> «Le coq est l’emblème de la Gaule et l’attribut de Mercure, ainsi que le dit expressément Jacob Tollius» (*The cock is the emblem of Gaul and the attribute of Mercury, as Jacob Tollius expressly says*) [BOURGES.2 ¶22]

### Jacques Cœur — *Jacques Cœur* {#e473}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Grand argentier of Charles VII, builder of the Bourges palace in the 15th century, reputed a proven Adept; David de Planis-Campy credits him with the 'pierre au blanc', perhaps the origin of his title argentier [BOURGES.1 ¶3]. He multiplied symbols to accredit his quality of 'philosophe par le feu' [BOURGES.1 ¶3]. Fulcanelli reads his motto cabalistically and concludes that he practised alchemy or saw the white stone made [BOURGES.1 ¶4]; scallop and heart, paired on his house, may be a rebus or steganographic signature of his name [BOURGES.1 ¶5].

> «Notre conviction est donc que Jacques Cœur a pratiqué lui-même l’alchimie, ou du moins qu’il a vu élaborer sous ses yeux la pierre au blanc par le fer « essencifié » et trois fois cuit.» (*Our conviction, then, is that Jacques Cœur practised alchemy himself, or at least saw the stone to the white made before his eyes from 'essencified' iron cooked three times.*) [BOURGES.1 ¶4]

### Jason — *Jason* {#e393}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

The twelfth Paris medallion is a replica of the Argonauts' expedition: Jason beside the ram with the golden fleece and the tree with precious fruits of the Garden of the Hesperides [PARIS.5 ¶15]. Jason killing the dragon of Colchis is one of the fables of the combat of the chemical bodies that yield the secret solvent [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. In the Golden Fleece relief of the Hôtel Lallemant, Jason was shown at the foot of an oak, a part now detached [BOURGES.2 ¶19].

> «nous y voyons Jason auprès du bélier à la toison d’or et de l’arbre aux fruits précieux du Jardin des Hespérides.» (*we see Jason there beside the ram with the golden fleece and the tree with the precious fruits of the Garden of the Hesperides.*) [PARIS.5 ¶15]

### Jean Lallemant — *Jean Lallemant* {#e521}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Documents in the Hôtel Lallemant archives show he belonged to the alchemical Fraternity of the Knights of the Round Table [BOURGES.2 n4]. Interpreted: he is the learned hermetist who commissioned the Saint Christopher relief [BOURGES.2 ¶13 with n4] and the author of the Hôtel's emblems, who left no known book but entrusted his science to stone [BOURGES.2 ¶37]. Fulcanelli places him with Geber and Roger Bacon around the Holy Grail, equal to Basile Valentin and Flamel in knowledge and above them in modesty and sincerity [BOURGES.2 ¶38].

> «Jean Lallemant, alchimiste et chevalier de la Table Ronde, mérite de prendre place autour de saint Graal, d’y communier avec Geber (Magister magistrorum), avec Roger Bacon (Doctor admirabilis).» (*Jean Lallemant, alchemist and knight of the Round Table, deserves to take his place around the Holy Grail, to commune there with Geber (Magister magistrorum) and Roger Bacon (Doctor admirabilis).*) [BOURGES.2 ¶38]

> «il leur est supérieur par l’expression de deux qualités, éminemment scientifiques et philosophiques, qu’il porta au plus haut degré de perfection : la modestie et la sincérité.» (*he surpasses them in the expression of two qualities, eminently scientific and philosophical, which he carried to the highest degree of perfection: modesty and sincerity.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶38]

### Jean Liébaut — *Jean Liébaut* {#e453}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

The woodcuts of the Pyrotechnie that he put in his treatise show furnaces as keeps with glacis, battlements and loopholes, supporting the reading of the athanor as a tower [AMIENS ¶13]. His treatise is the Quatre Livres des Secrets de Médecine et de la Philosophie Chimique [AMIENS n4].

> «en particulier les bois de la Pyrotechnie que Jean Liébaut inséra dans son traité, — ne s’y tromperont point.» (*in particular the woodcuts of the Pyrotechnie that Jean Liébaut put in his treatise, will not be mistaken.*) [AMIENS ¶13]

### Jean Reyor (Marcel Clavelle) — *Jean Reyor* {#e093}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; the text gives Marcel Clavelle as his real name and Jean Reyor as his pseudonym [PREF3 ¶34, ¶47].*

Named in Canseliet's third preface (voice CANSELIET). Marcel Clavelle, alias Jean Reyor, published in the Voile d'Isis of November 1932 an opinion that Canseliet calls presumptuous [PREF3 ¶34]. Quoted (QUOTED: Jean Reyor): Fulcanelli was hasty in accusing Cambriel; a third, unfaithful Saint-Marcel pillar existed, replaced about 1860, and Cambriel described it in good faith [PREF3 ¶35, ¶36]. Reyor said Grillot de Givry ignored Fulcanelli's correction 'innocently' [PREF3 ¶33]; the 'known occultist' he cites is Grillot de Givry [PREF3 ¶37]. To escape the 1640 testimony he had to date the statue to Louis XIV's reign [PREF3 ¶47]. Canseliet rejects his view [PREF3 ¶49, ¶50].

> «C’est qu’il existe un troisième pilier Saint-Marcel, reproduction infidèle du pilier primitif et c’est ce pilier qui fut remplacé vers 1860 par la copie plus honnête que nous voyons actuellement.» (*The fact is that there exists a third Saint-Marcel pillar, an unfaithful reproduction of the original pillar, and it is this pillar that was replaced around 1860 by the more honest copy we see today.*) [PREF3 ¶36]

### Jean-Frédéric Helvetius — *Jean-Frédéric Helvetius* {#e048}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also Helvétius).*

Author of the Veau d'Or (Vitulus Aureus) [PREF2 ¶40]. Henckel cites the artist Elias, quoted by Helvétius, who claimed to make the stone in four days and showed it stuck to crucible shards [PARIS.7 ¶10]. Canseliet translates Helvetius's account of the goldsmith Gril's star of silver on spirit of salt and lead [PREF2 ¶40, PREF2 ¶41], and warns that it omits two essential data [PREF2 ¶42].

> «Quand je pense, écrit Henckel, que l’artiste Elias, cité par Helvétius, prétend que la préparation de la pierre philosophale se commence et s’achève en quatre jours de temps» (*When I think, writes Henckel, that the artist Elias, cited by Helvetius, claims that the preparation of the philosophers' stone is begun and finished in four days*) [PARIS.7 ¶10]

### Jean-Gaspard Knôttner — *Jean-Gaspard Knôttner* {#e052}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also 'Knottner'); the proposed 'Knöttner' does not occur.*

In Helvetius's account: his greatest friend, a cloth dyer, from whom the goldsmith Gril asked a specially prepared spirit of salt; Knottner asked whether it would be used on metals [PREF2 ¶41].

> «demandait à mon plus grand ami, c’est-à-dire à Jean-Gaspard Knôttner, teinturier en draperies, de l’esprit de sel préparé de manière non vulgaire.» (*asked my greatest friend, that is Jean-Gaspard Knôttner, dyer of cloth, for spirit of salt prepared in no common way.*) [PREF2 ¶41]

### Jean-Jacques Pauvert — *Jean-Jacques Pauvert* {#e067}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also 'J.-J. Pauvert').*

Canseliet: the publisher who reissued Le Mystère des Cathédrales, with care for both quality and price, and illustrated it with photographs of the sculptures drawn by Julien Champagne [PREF3 ¶10]; publisher of Alchimie [PREF2 n1].

> «Par Jean-Jacques Pauvert, c’est une chose faite» (*Through Jean-Jacques Pauvert, it is done*) [PREF3 ¶10]

### Jesse — *Jessé* {#e259}

*term; The usual English form; the text glosses the Hebrew Jes as fire, sun, divinity [PARIS.1 ¶10].*

Mary was of the stem of Jesse; the text states that Hebrew Jes means fire, sun, divinity, so to be of Jesse's stem is to be of the race of the sun, of fire; hence the name Jesus means fire, sun, God [PARIS.1 ¶10]. At Bourges the central tree rises from the stone as the tree of Jesse rises from the Patriarch's breast [BOURGES.1 ¶10].

> «Or, le mot hébreu Jes signifie le feu, le soleil, la divinité. Etre de la tige de Jessé, c’est donc être de la race du soleil, du feu.» (*Now the Hebrew word Jes means fire, the sun, divinity. To be of the stem of Jesse is thus to be of the race of the sun, of fire.*) [PARIS.1 ¶10]

### Jesus — *Jésus* {#e260}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

From the union of the Virgin (form) and the solar spirit comes living matter: Jesus, the incarnate spirit, fire corporified in things [PARIS.1 ¶8]; his name read as fire, sun, God [PARIS.1 ¶10]. In the crucible the first matter, like Christ, suffers the Passion, dies and rises purified [INTRO.4 ¶2]; the cross bears the three nails that sacrificed the Christ-matter [INTRO.4 ¶3]; Jesus built his Church on the stone [INTRO.4 ¶5]. The Star of the Magi shines above the crib where Jesus, the Child-King, lies [INTRO.6 ¶13]. Saint Christopher is the hieroglyph of solar sulphur (Jesus) [INTRO.9 ¶15]. The Child Jesus carried by Offerus is 'gold in its bath' [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Jesus spoke only in parables and revealed the Language of the Birds to his apostles [INTRO.3 ¶7; INTRO.3 ¶8].

> «C’est alors Jésus, l’esprit incarné, le feu corporifié dans les choses telles que nous les connaissons ici-bas» (*It is then Jesus, the incarnate spirit, the fire corporified in things as we know them here below*) [PARIS.1 ¶8]

> «C’est en effet dans le creuset que la matière première, comme le Christ lui-même, souffre la Passion» (*It is indeed in the crucible that the first matter, like Christ himself, suffers the Passion*) [INTRO.4 ¶2]

### Jonathan Swift — *Jonathan Swift* {#e062}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*
