Canseliet: 'the singular Dean of Saint-Patrick' knew thoroughly the Gaye Science, the Language of the Gods or of the Birds, and practised it in his own way [PREF2 ¶45].

> «Celles-là mêmes que Jonathan Swift, le singulier Doyen de Saint-Patrick, connaissait à fond et pratiquait à sa manière, avec tant de science et de virtuosité.» (*The very ones that Jonathan Swift, the singular Dean of Saint Patrick's, knew thoroughly and practised in his own way, with such learning and virtuosity.*) [PREF2 ¶45]

### Jourdain and Duval — *Jourdain et Duval* {#e441}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named among those who read the Amiens wheel-fire as Ezekiel's vision [AMIENS ¶4]. Durand reports they called a relief 'Inconstance' [AMIENS ¶11]. They see in the cock and fox the prototype of the fable of the crow and the fox [AMIENS ¶14]. Fulcanelli rejects these readings [AMIENS ¶5; AMIENS ¶12; AMIENS ¶14].

> «MM. Jourdain et Duval avaient appelé Inconstance ce vice opposé à la Persévérance» (*Messrs Jourdain and Duval had called Inconstancy this vice opposed to Perseverance*) [AMIENS ¶11]

### Julien Champagne — *Julien Champagne* {#e012}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

The painter to whom Fulcanelli entrusted the illustration of the book; Canseliet credits him with its original plates [PREF1 ¶12]. Three more drawings of his were added to the second edition [PREF2 ¶44]. He knew Fulcanelli from 1905, ten years before Canseliet; the third edition prints photographs of the sculptures he drew, which Canseliet says prove his conscientiousness [PREF3 ¶10].

> «C’est, en effet, au talent sincère et minutieux du peintre Julien Champagne que Le Mystère des Cathédrales doit d’envelopper son ésotérisme austère d’un superbe manteau de planches originales.» (*It is indeed to the sincere and meticulous talent of the painter Julien Champagne that The Mystery of the Cathedrals owes the superb mantle of original plates wrapping its austere esotericism.*) [PREF1 ¶12]

### Julius Africanus — *Jules Africain* {#e201}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Jules Africain; Julius Africanus in the footnote).*

A 3rd-century chronographer to whom the legend Des choses qui arrivèrent en Perse, lors de la naissance du Christ is attributed, though its real date is unknown [INTRO.7 ¶12]. Cited as Julius Africanus in the Patr. grecque, t. X [INTRO.7 n5].

> «La légende suivante, qui a pour titre Des choses qui arrivèrent en Perse, lors de la naissance du Christ, est attribué à Jules Africain, chronographe du IIIe siècle» (*The following legend, titled Of the things that happened in Persia at the birth of Christ, is attributed to Julius Africanus, a chronographer of the 3rd century*) [INTRO.7 ¶12]

### Jupiter — *Jupiter* {#e397}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

In the verse on the planetary metals Jupiter is the figure of tin [PARIS.6 ¶5]. The Cosmopolite places Jupiter second below Saturn and says Jupiter turns easily into Mercury, being higher [PARIS.6 ¶7]. On the Notre-Dame sarcophagus Jupiter and Mercury follow Saturn and the Moon from the ends inward [PARIS.6 ¶8].

> «Ainsi le Jupiter se transmue facilement en Mercure, pource que Jupiter est plus haut que Mercure» (*So Jupiter is easily transmuted into Mercury, because Jupiter is higher than Mercury*) [PARIS.6 ¶7]

## K

### Kabbalah — *kabbale* {#e057}

*term; Usual English term.*

Fulcanelli dismisses 'doctors in kabbalah', so-called Jewish or Christian cabalists whose illusory combinations lead to nothing positive [BOURGES.2 ¶21]. Canseliet: the Jewish kabbale works by transpositions, inversions, substitutions and arbitrary calculations; the word comes from Hebrew Kabbalah, 'tradition', and must not be confused with cabale, the phonetic cabala [PREF2 ¶45].

> «Laissons donc ces docteurs en kabbale dans leur ignorance et revenons à notre sujet, caractérisé hermétiquement par le chêne.» (*Let us then leave these doctors in kabbalah to their ignorance and return to our subject, characterised hermetically by the oak.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶21]

> «le deuxième, de l’hébreu Kabbalah qui signifie tradition.» (*the second, from the Hebrew Kabbalah, which means tradition.*) [PREF2 ¶45]

### Kermes — *Kermès* {#e508}

*term; Usual English term; the text glosses it through Arabic girmiz, 'which dyes scarlet' (BOURGES.2 ¶22).*

The oak also gives kermès, which in the Gaye Science means the same as Hermès, the initial consonants being interchangeable; both mean Mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. Stated: kermès names the prepared mercurial substance, as galle names the raw one; the Philosophers' mercury must have the power to dye, gained only by first preparations [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. Isolating the pure part of the subject gives 'le Kermès, l’Hermès, le mercure teingeant' that carries mystic gold [BOURGES.2 ¶23].

> «Non seulement le chêne fournit la galle, mais il donne encore le Kermès, qui a, dans la Gaye Science, la même signification que Hermès, les consonnes initiales étant permutantes.» (*Not only does the oak supply the gall, it also gives Kermes, which in the Gay Science has the same meaning as Hermes, the initial consonants being interchangeable.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶22]

> «et vous aurez le Kermès, l’Hermès, le mercure teingeant qui porte en soi l’or mystique, de même que saint Christophe porte Jésus et le bélier sa propre toison.» (*and you will have the Kermes, the Hermes, the tinging mercury that carries the mystic gold within it, just as Saint Christopher carries Jesus and the ram its own fleece.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶23]

Identified in the text with: [Mercury](#e028)

### Key — *Clef* {#e303}

*term; Literal translation; the technical sense is the text's own gloss [PARIS.3 n6].*

Stated: 'Clef' is the name given to any radical (i.e. irreducible) alchemical dissolution, sometimes extended to the menstrua or solvents able to effect it [PARIS.3 n6]. In Trévisan's text (quoted) the blackened mass is 'la clef', the beginning and sign of the method of the second régime [PARIS.3 ¶14]. The text also uses the word in its ordinary sense (e.g. the key that opens the doors, held by Cybele) [INTRO.8 ¶15; BOURGES.2 ¶21].

> «On donne le nom de Clef à toute dissolution alchimique radicale (c’est-à-dire irréductible), et l’on étend parfois ce terme aux menstrues ou dissolvants capable de l’effectuer.» (*The name Key is given to any radical (that is, irreducible) alchemical dissolution, and the term is sometimes extended to the menstrua or solvents able to effect it.*) [PARIS.3 n6]

### Khunrath — *Khunrath* {#e458}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named once: the Amiens citadel quatrefoil of 1225 may be the mother idea of the 'Forteresse alchimique' that Khunrath took up and modified in 1609 [AMIENS ¶17].

> «Ou bien devons-nous voir, en ce motif de l’an 1225, l’idée mère de la Forteresse alchimique, reprise et modifiée par Khunrath en 1609 ?» (*Or should we see, in this motif of the year 1225, the mother idea of the Alchemical Fortress, taken up and modified by Khunrath in 1609?*) [AMIENS ¶17]

### King Mark — *roi Marc* {#e481}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; English the usual form.*

On the corbel his crowned head is hidden in the foliage of the central tree, which rises from the stone as the tree of Jesse from the Patriarch's breast [BOURGES.1 ¶8, ¶10]. Uncle and nephew (Tristan) are chemically one thing; gold loses its crown with its colour for a time and later inherits a second, nobler one — hence the old king stays hidden while Tristan and Yseult stand out [BOURGES.1 ¶10].

> «En fait, l’oncle et le neveu ne sont, chimiquement parlant, qu’une même chose, de même genre et d’origine semblable.» (*In fact, uncle and nephew are, chemically speaking, but one same thing, of the same kind and of similar origin.*) [BOURGES.1 ¶10]

### Kings' Cake — *Gâteau des Rois* {#e491}

*term; The usual English term for the Epiphany cake.*

The cake shared on Epiphany is the secret version of the truth that mercury carries the reborn gold-child [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Its galette is 'signed' like the matter itself with crossed lines (the mesh of the net) and holds the baigneur [BOURGES.2 ¶14]; its dough is flaky (feuilletée), like the foliated prepared mercury, the baigneur inserted like a bookmark [BOURGES.2 ¶16]. Attending to the galette des Rois points to the subject to choose [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. 'Avoir de la galette' means being rich; who finds the bean is doubly king, by science and fortune [BOURGES.2 n7]. The cake sometimes holds a sabot instead of a bean [INTRO.1 n4]; the swaddled mineral child is like its porcelain baigneur [PARIS.7 n9]. Once Fulcanelli saw an oak drawn on its crust and a porcelain sole in place of the baigneur [BOURGES.2 ¶14].

> «Or, nous retrouvons la version secrète de cette vérité positive dans le Gâteau des Rois, qu’il est d’usage de partager en famille le jour de l’Epiphanie» (*Now, we find the secret version of this positive truth in the Kings' Cake, which it is customary to share in the family on Epiphany*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

> «Notre galette est signée comme la matière elle-même et contient dans sa pâte le petit enfant populairement dénommé baigneur.» (*Our galette is signed like the matter itself and holds in its dough the little child popularly called the 'bather'.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

## L

### L'Art profane à l'Eglise — Profane Art in the Church — *L’Art profane à l’Eglise* {#e115}

*work; Title as spelled in the text (once 'L’Art du profane à l’Eglise' [INTRO.1 n3]), with a literal English rendering.*

Work by G. J. Witkowski, Paris, Schemit, 1908, cited in two volumes, 'Etranger' [INTRO.1 n3, INTRO.8 n4, PARIS.2 n5] and 'France' [INTRO.7 n11].

> «G. J. Witkowski, L’Art profane à l’Eglise. France. Paris, Schemit, 1908, p. 382.» (*G. J. Witkowski, Profane Art in the Church. France. Paris, Schemit, 1908, p. 382.*) [INTRO.7 n11]

### L'Atmosphère — The Atmosphere — *L’Atmosphère* {#e233}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.*

Work of Camille Flammarion (Paris, Hachette, 1888, p. 362), source of the account of the Virgin in the Observatory cellars [INTRO.8 n3; INTRO.8 ¶11].

> «Le colossal édifice de Louis XIV, écrit-il, qui élève la balustrade de sa terrasse à vingt-huit mètres au dessus du sol, descend au-dessous en des fondations qui ont la même profondeur : vingt-huit mètres.» (*The colossal building of Louis XIV, he writes, which raises the balustrade of its terrace twenty-eight metres above the ground, goes down below in foundations of the same depth: twenty-eight metres.*) [INTRO.8 ¶11]

### L'Aurore — The Dawn — *L'Aurore* {#e413}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of H. de Lintaut, manuscript of the Arsenal library, S. A. F. 169, no. 3020 [PARIS.7 n6]; the source of the passage on the dry path [PARIS.7 ¶10].

> «H. de Lintaut, L’Aurore. Mss. bibl. de l’Arsenal, S. A. F. 169, n° 3020.» (*H. de Lintaut, The Dawn. Manuscript, Arsenal library, S. A. F. 169, no. 3020.*) [PARIS.7 n6]

### La Cabale Intellective — The Intellective Cabala — *La Cabale Intellective* {#e396}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Manuscript of the Bibliothèque de l'Arsenal, S. et A. 72, p. 15, the source of the verse on the seven planetary metals [PARIS.6 n1; PARIS.6 ¶3-6].

> «La Cabale Intellective. Mss. de la biblioth. de l’Arsenal, S. et A. 72, p. 15.» (*The Intellective Cabala. Manuscript of the Arsenal library, S. et A. 72, p. 15.*) [PARIS.6 n1]

### La Clef du Cabinet hermétique — The Key of the Hermetic Cabinet — *La Clef du Cabinet hermétique* {#e299}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

An anonymous 18th-century manuscript [PARIS.3 n3], called 'précieux' [PARIS.3 ¶10]. Quoted on the first putrefaction and its signs: acidity and a smell of the sepulchre (Morien), 'fish eyes' (Philalèthe), fermentation by the secret fire [PARIS.3 ¶11]. Quoted at length on the water that holds all the virtues of heaven and earth, the 'Dissolvant général de toute la Nature', the bath of King and Queen, the Fountain of Trévisan, the steel and magnet in Aries, Flamel's hollow oak, and a vessel of old oak like a barrel; the water is 'notre vinaigre mercuriel' [PARIS.4 ¶14]. Fulcanelli adds that it is useful if read with prudence: the spirit gives life, the letter kills [PARIS.4 ¶15]. Also quoted on why philosophers hid the Matter [AMIENS ¶32; AMIENS n11].

> «L’eau dont nous nous servons, écrit l’auteur anonyme de la Clef du Cabinet Hermétique, est une eau qui renferme toutes les vertus du ciel et de la terre» (*The water we use, writes the anonymous author of the Key of the Hermetic Cabinet, is a water that contains all the virtues of heaven and earth*) [PARIS.4 ¶14]

### La Croix avant Jésus-Christ — The Cross before Jesus Christ — *La Croix avant Jésus-Christ* {#e155}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with a literal English rendering.*

Work by abbé Ansault, Paris, V. Rétaux, 1894, on the cross as a pre-Christian symbol [INTRO.4 n1, INTRO.4 ¶4].

> «M. l’abbé Ansault, La Croix avant Jésus-Christ. Paris, V. Rétaux, 1894.» (*Abbé Ansault, The Cross before Jesus Christ. Paris, V. Rétaux, 1894.*) [INTRO.4 n1]

### La Filiation généalogique de toutes les Ecoles gothiques — The Genealogical Filiation of All the Gothic Schools — *La Filiation généalogique de toutes les Ecoles gothiques* {#e108}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with a literal English rendering.*

Work by J. F. Golfs, Paris, Baudry, 1884, source of the quotation on the language of stones [INTRO.1 n1, INTRO.1 ¶5].

> «J. F. Golfs, La Filiation généalogique de toutes les Ecoles gothiques. Paris, Baudry, 1884.» (*J. F. Golfs, The Genealogical Filiation of All the Gothic Schools. Paris, Baudry, 1884.*) [INTRO.1 n1]

### La Grande Encyclopédie — The Great Encyclopedia — *La Grande Encyclopédie* {#e172}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with a literal English rendering.*

Source of Berthelot's definition of the labyrinth: article Labyrinthe, vol. XXI, p. 703 [INTRO.5 n1, INTRO.5 ¶2].

> «La Grande Encyclopédie. Art. Labyrinthe. T. XXI, p. 703.» (*The Great Encyclopedia. Article Labyrinth. Vol. XXI, p. 703.*) [INTRO.5 n1]

### La Lumière sortant par soy-mesme des Ténèbres — Light Coming Forth by Itself from the Darkness — *La Lumière sortant par soy-mesme des Ténèbres* {#e451}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

A classic work, Paris, d'Houry, 1687 [AMIENS n2]. Its anonymous commentator is cited on the coction: the philosopher cooks in a single vessel a single vapour that thickens little by little [AMIENS ¶8]. The commentator is also cited on the essence in which the spirit dwells and on seeking the subject in the mineral kingdom alone [AMIENS ¶28].

> «c’est donc dans le règne minéral seulement, où réside la semence métallique, que nous devons chercher le sujet propre à notre art» (*it is therefore in the mineral kingdom only, where the metallic seed dwells, that we must seek the subject proper to our art*) [AMIENS ¶28]

### La Nature à découvert — Nature Uncovered — *La Nature à découvert* {#e297}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

Book by the Chevalier Inconnu, Aix, 1669, source of the quotation on the second operation and the Raven's Head [PARIS.3 n2; PARIS.3 ¶8].

> «La Nature à découvert, par le Chevalier Inconnu. Aix, 1669.» (*Nature Uncovered, by the Unknown Knight. Aix, 1669.*) [PARIS.3 n2]

### La Parole délaissée — The Forsaken Word — *La Parole délaissée* {#e302}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

Book of Bernard Trévisan, Paris, Jean Sara, 1618, p. 39 [PARIS.3 n4], source of the long description of the black compound ('poix noire', 'Merle de Jean', black cloud in the vessel, the black mass as 'clef' and sign of the second régime) [PARIS.3 ¶13–14].

> «Bernard Trévisan, La Parole délaissée. Paris, Jean Sara, 1618, p. 39.» (*Bernard Trévisan, The Forsaken Word. Paris, Jean Sara, 1618, p. 39.*) [PARIS.3 n4]

### La Toyson d'Or — The Golden Fleece — *La Toyson d'Or* {#e281}

*work; Title as spelled in the text (old spelling 'Toyson'), with our literal English.*

Salomon Trismosin's book, Paris, Ch. Sevestre, 1612 [PARIS.2 n8; PARIS.7 n4]. Cited for the figure of the crowned oak and the raven [PARIS.2 ¶20], and for the short way (Régime de Saturne) using an earthen vessel instead of glass [PARIS.7 ¶7]. Interpreted: the 'Toison d'Or' whose third figure shows a philosopher in red under a purple cloak and green cap holding a glass matras with a green liquid, nearly identical to G. Aurach's image of the Lion vert, is probably this book; the text does not name its author there [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. It comments the ludus puerorum, figured identically on the Lallemant ceiling (a child on a wooden horse) [BOURGES.2 ¶28]. Not to be confused with Canseliet's study on the Golden Fleece [PREF2 ¶20] or the myth of the Toison d'or [BOURGES.2 ¶20].

> «Je brouilleray ton corps dans un vase de terre où je l’enseveliray» (*I will stir up your body in an earthen vessel where I will bury it*) [PARIS.7 ¶7]

> «La troisième figure de la Toison d’Or est presque identique à l’image de G. Aurach.» (*The third figure of the Golden Fleece is almost identical to G. Aurach's image.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶5]

### La Vie de Gargantua et de Pantagruel — The Life of Gargantua and Pantagruel — *La Vie de Gargantua et de Pantagruel* {#e139}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with a literal English rendering.*

Rabelais's work, called esoteric and a novel in argot; the note is attached to 'Dive-Bouteille', one of the medieval names of the Language of the Birds [INTRO.3 ¶8, INTRO.3 n1].

> «La Vie de Gargantua et de Pantagruel, par François Rabelais, est une œuvre ésotérique, un roman d’argot.» (*The Life of Gargantua and Pantagruel, by François Rabelais, is an esoteric work, a novel in argot.*) [INTRO.3 n1]

### Labour of Hercules — *travail d'Hercule* {#e392}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

The text names 'our preparation' a labour of Hercules: the warrior of the twelfth Paris medallion is the artist who has just finished it [PARIS.5 ¶15]. Once the hard labours of Hercules are done, the work is reduced to the ludus puerorum, the keeping of the fire [AMIENS ¶6].

> «Quant au guerrier, il représente l’artiste qui vient d’accomplir le travail d’Hercule qu’est (132) notre préparation.» (*As for the warrior, he represents the artist who has just accomplished the labour of Hercules that our preparation is.*) [PARIS.5 ¶15]

### Labyrinth — *labyrinthe* {#e165}

*term; Literal translation.*

Traced on cathedral floors at the crossing of nave and transepts (Sens, Reims, Auxerre, Saint-Quentin, Poitiers, Bayeux); Amiens's had a gold rising sun at its centre; Chartres's, called 'la lieue' (for 'le lieu'), showed Theseus and the Minotaur, proof of a mytho-hermetic sense [INTRO.5 ¶1]. Berthelot calls the 'labyrinth of Solomon' a cabalistic figure at the head of alchemical manuscripts [INTRO.5 ¶2]. It is emblematic of the whole Work, with two difficulties: reaching the centre where the two natures fight, and getting out, for which Ariadne's thread is needed [INTRO.5 ¶3]. The Cnossos labyrinth was called Absolum [INTRO.5 ¶7]. The black phases form a 'ténébreux labyrinthe' [PARIS.3 ¶7].

> «L’image du labyrinthe s’offre donc à nous comme emblématique du travail entier de l’Œuvre, avec ses deux difficultés majeures : celle de la voie qu’il convient de suivre pour atteindre le centre, — où se livre le rude combat des deux natures» (*The image of the labyrinth thus presents itself to us as emblematic of the entire labour of the Work, with its two major difficulties: that of the way one must follow to reach the centre — where the hard combat of the two natures is fought*) [INTRO.5 ¶3]

> «Quant au labyrinthe de Chartres, vulgairement appelé la lieue (pour le lieu), et dessiné sur le pavé de la nef, il se compose de toute une suite de cercles concentriques qui se replient les uns dans les autres avec une infinie variété.» (*As for the labyrinth of Chartres, commonly called la lieue (for le lieu, the place), drawn on the pavement of the nave, it consists of a whole series of concentric circles folding into one another with infinite variety.*) [INTRO.5 ¶1]

### Ladder of the Philosophers — *échelle des Philosophes* {#e252}

*term; Literal translation; the text gives the Latin scala philosophorum and glosses it as patience [PARIS.1 ¶3].*

On the trumeau of the central portal, the figure of alchemy holds between her knees a ladder of nine rungs, the scala philosophorum, hieroglyph of the patience her followers need through the nine successive operations of the hermetic labour (pl. II) [PARIS.1 ¶3]. Valois is quoted: patience is the Philosophers' ladder and humility the gate of their garden [PARIS.1 ¶3].

> «se dresse l’échelle aux neuf degrés, scala philosophorum, hiéroglyphe de la patience que doivent posséder ses fidèles, au cours des neufs opérations successives du labeur hermétique» (*stands the ladder of nine rungs, scala philosophorum, hieroglyph of the patience her faithful must have during the nine successive operations of the hermetic labour*) [PARIS.1 ¶3]

> «La patience est l’eschelle des Philosophes, nous dit Valois, et l’humilité est la porte de leur jardin» (*Patience is the ladder of the Philosophers, Valois tells us, and humility is the gate of their garden*) [PARIS.1 ¶3]

### Lagneau — *Lagneau* {#e434}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of the Harmonie Chymique, at the end of which, per Albert Poisson, the escutcheon imitated by Flamel appears [PARIS.8 ¶2]. Fulcanelli concludes Lagneau had his image copied from the arcade of the charnel house, though it would have been better to use the type escutcheon of saint Thomas [PARIS.8 ¶4].

> «Cette relation, conforme à la gravure de Lagneau, nous permet de conclure que celui-ci a fait copier son image d’après l’arcade du charnier.» (*This account, matching Lagneau's engraving, allows us to conclude that he had his image copied from the arcade of the charnel house.*) [PARIS.8 ¶4]

### Lamb — *agneau* {#e153}

*term; Literal translation.*

Quoting the Dialogue with Trypho: the paschal lamb was the figure of Christ, and roasted whole it formed a cross [INTRO.4 ¶3]. On the base of the north portal of Notre-Dame the lamb is the emblem of the sweetening (edulcoration) of the arsenical principle of the Matter [PARIS.6 ¶1, PARIS.6 ¶11, PARIS.6 ¶12]. Dujols (QUOTED): the blessed, washed in the blood of the Lamb, wear white [PARIS.3 ¶28].

> «L’agneau, emblème de l’édulcoration du principe arsenical de la Matière» (*The lamb, emblem of the edulcoration of the arsenical principle of the Matter*) [PARIS.6 ¶12]

> «« Le mystère de l’agneau que Dieu avait ordonné d’immoler à Pâque, dit-il, était la figure du Christ» (*'The mystery of the lamb that God had ordered to be sacrificed at Passover,' he says, 'was the figure of Christ*) [INTRO.4 ¶3]

### Language of colours — *langue des couleurs* {#e325}

*term; Literal translation.*

The coloured phases of the coction always served as symbolic prototype; each was given a precise meaning; thus there existed in all times, as Portal says, a language of colours closely joined to religion, which reappears in the Middle Ages in the stained glass of Gothic cathedrals [PARIS.3 ¶26].

> «C’est ainsi qu’il exista, de tous temps, une langue des couleurs, intimement unie à la religion, ainsi que le dit Portal» (*Thus there existed, in all times, a language of colours, closely joined to religion, as Portal says*) [PARIS.3 ¶26]

### Language of the Birds — *Langue des Oiseaux* {#e060}

*term; Usual English term (literal translation).*

The sacred language behind the phonetic cabala. Argot derives from it, the mother of all languages, language of philosophers and diplomats; Jesus gave it to the apostles with the Holy Spirit; it teaches the mystery of things; the Incas called it 'Langue de cour'; in the Middle Ages 'Gaie science', 'Gay sçavoir', 'Langue des dieux', 'Dive-Bouteille'; spoken before Babel; traces survive in argot, Picard, Provençal, the gypsies' dialect [INTRO.3 ¶8]. Tiresias knew it, taught by Minerva [INTRO.3 ¶9]. Initiates in the 'language of the gods' hold all keys [BOURGES.2 ¶21]; in the Gaye Science Kermès equals Hermès [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. The Hendaye inscription is to be read in it, in French, by the rules of 'Diplomatique' [HENDAYE ¶9]. Canseliet: the 'langue matrice'; Fulcanelli recovered its lost key [PREF2 ¶45].

> «Ajoutons enfin que l’argot est une des formes dérivées de la Langue des Oiseaux, mère et doyenne de toutes les autres, la langue des philosophes et des diplomates.» (*Let us add finally that argot is one of the derived forms of the Language of the Birds, mother and eldest of all the others, the language of philosophers and diplomats.*) [INTRO.3 ¶8]

> «C’est elle qui enseigne le mystère des choses et dévoile les vérités les plus cachées.» (*It is this language that teaches the mystery of things and unveils the most hidden truths.*) [INTRO.3 ¶8]

### Lassus — *Lassus* {#e084}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

One of the architects who restored Notre-Dame [INTRO.2 ¶7]. Canseliet: with Viollet-le-Duc he replaced the St Marcel statue with a copy [PREF3 ¶23]; Grillot de Givry names him in the restoration [PREF3 ¶28].

> «Et, si l’art doit quelque reconnaissance aux éminents architectes Toussaint, Geffroy Dechaume, Bœswillwald, Viollet-le-Duc et Lassus qui restaurèrent la basilique, odieusement profanée par l’Ecole, la Science ne retrouvera jamais ce qu’elle a perdu.» (*And if art owes some gratitude to the eminent architects Toussaint, Geffroy Dechaume, Bœswillwald, Viollet-le-Duc and Lassus who restored the basilica, odiously profaned by the School, Science will never recover what it has lost.*) [INTRO.2 ¶7]

### Latomi (stonemasons) — *latomi* {#e447}

*term; Literal rendering of the Latin word the text uses for the builders.*

Durand's reading of the wheel-fire shows an incomplete study of the traditional technique, the formulas the latomi respected in carrying out their symbols [AMIENS ¶5].

> «La première témoigne d’une étude incomplète de la technique traditionnelle, des formules (159) que respectaient les latomi dans l’exécution de leurs symboles.» (*The first shows an incomplete study of the traditional technique, of the formulas the latomi respected in carrying out their symbols.*) [AMIENS ¶5]

### Laurentum — *Laurente* {#e198}

*term; The usual English form of the place name; the text's own cabalistic gloss is 'or enté' [INTRO.7 n1].*

The fields of Laurentum are the end of Aeneas's journey, where the star of Venus he had seen daily ceased to appear, showing him the lands appointed by Fate [INTRO.7 ¶1–2]. A footnote reads the name cabalistically as 'or enté', grafted gold [INTRO.7 n1]; the text gives no further development of this reading.

> «Cabalistiquement, l’or enté, greffé.» (*Cabalistically, grafted gold (or enté), engrafted.*) [INTRO.7 n1]

### Le Breton — *Le Breton* {#e293}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of the Clefs de la Philosophie Spagyrique [PARIS.3 n1; AMIENS n10]. Quoted for the four putrefactions of the Work (first separation, first conjunction, second conjunction of the heavy water with its salt, fixation of sulphur), each with blackness [PARIS.3 ¶5], and for a mineral known to the true learned, hidden under various names, which contains abundantly the fixed and the volatile [AMIENS ¶31].

> «« Il y a, dit Le Breton, un minéral connu des vrais Sçavans qui le cachent dans leurs écrits sous divers noms, lequel contient abondamment le fixe et le volatil. »» (*'There is,' says Le Breton, 'a mineral known to the true Learned, who hide it in their writings under various names, which contains abundantly the fixed and the volatile.'*) [AMIENS ¶31]

### Le Chemin du Ciel Chymique (Manuductio ad Coelum Chemicum) — The Path to the Chemical Heaven — *Le Chemin du Ciel Chymique* {#e342}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with its Latin original as given and our literal English.*

J. Tollius's work, a translation of the Manuductio ad Coelum Chemicum, Amsterdam, 1688 [PARIS.3 n15; BOURGES.2 n11]. Quoted for its apostrophe to the 'souffleurs' who seek colours in their glass vessels (black raven, white dove, yellow eagle, red pheasant), and for the black Earth, white Water, and the smell marking fixation or volatility [PARIS.3 ¶36–38]. Cited for the cock as attribute of Mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶22].

> «Vous qui me fatiguez les oreilles avec votre noir corbeau, vous êtes aussi fous que cet homme de l’antiquité qui avoit coutume d’applaudir au théâtre, quoyqu’il y fust seul» (*You who weary my ears with your black raven, you are as mad as that man of antiquity who used to applaud in the theatre though he was alone there*) [PARIS.3 ¶36]

### Le Denier du Pauvre — The Poor Man's Penny — *Le Denier du Pauvre* {#e256}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.*

Work of Etteilla, cited once as in the Sept nuances de l’Oeuvre philosophique (1786), p. 57 [PARIS.1 n2], and once as le Denier du Pauvre ou la Perfection des métaux (Paris, about 1785), p. 58 [PARIS.3 n13]. Source of the passage on the Magi's vase [PARIS.1 ¶7] and of the legends of a hermetic painting [PARIS.3 ¶33].

> «un vase plein d’une liqueur céleste-astrale, composée d’un tiers de miel sauvage, d’une part d’eau terrestre et d’une part d’eau céleste» (*a vase full of a celestial-astral liquor, made of one third wild honey, one part earthly water and one part heavenly water*) [PARIS.1 ¶7]

### Le Filet d'Ariadne — Ariadne's Thread — *Le Filet d'Ariadne* {#e306}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

Paris, d'Houry, 1695 [PARIS.3 n7]; ascribed to Batsdorff or to Gaston de Claves [PARIS.3 ¶15]. Quoted on the signs of true putrefaction and its names (Occident, Darkness, Eclipse, Leprosy, Raven's Head, Death, Mortification of Mercury) [PARIS.3 ¶15]. Batsdorff in it says the philosophers called the vessel of coction 'Lion vert' without giving a reason [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. Interpreted: it is the special treatise Batsdorff wrote to teach what Ariadne's thread is [INTRO.5 ¶4].

> «Batsdorff, dans son Filet d’Ariadne, dit que les Philosophes ont appelé Lion vert le vaisseau servant à la coction, mais sans en fournir aucune raison.» (*Batsdorff, in his Ariadne's Thread, says the Philosophers called the vessel used for the coction Green Lion, but without giving any reason.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶5]

### Le Grand Œuvre — The Great Work — *Le Grand Œuvre* {#e406}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of Grillot de Givry, Paris, Chacornac, 1907, p. 27 [PARIS.7 n1]. Cited for its passage on the bishop of the right portal of Notre-Dame standing on the aludel and the door kept shut all year, the non-vulgar path [PARIS.7 ¶2].

> «Grillot de Givry, Le Grand Œuvre. Paris, Chacornac, 1907, p. 27.» (*Grillot de Givry, The Great Work. Paris, Chacornac, 1907, p. 27.*) [PARIS.7 n1]

### Le Musée des Sorciers — The Museum of Sorcerers — *Le Musée des Sorciers* {#e090}

*work; Name as spelled in the text; English title is our literal translation.*

Book of Grillot de Givry, which Canseliet says claimed a 16th-century replacement statue of St Marcel [PREF3 ¶27, PREF3 ¶28]; dated Paris 20 November 1928, it came out in February 1929, a week after its author's sudden death [PREF3 ¶33]; its p. 407 reads 'XVIe siècle' [PREF3 ¶47].

> «En effet, ce premier livre de Fulcanelli était paru depuis juin 1926, lorsque — daté a Paris du 20 novembre 1928 — Le Musée des Sorciers sortit en février 1929, une semaine après le décès subit de son auteur.» (*Indeed, this first book of Fulcanelli had been out since June 1926 when — dated Paris, 20 November 1928 — The Museum of Sorcerers came out in February 1929, a week after its author's sudden death.*) [PREF3 ¶33]

### Le Mystère des Cathédrales — The Mystery of the Cathedrals — *Le Mystère des Cathédrales* {#e001}

*work; Name as spelled in the text; English title is our literal translation.*

The book itself, presented by Canseliet as the work of his Master Fulcanelli [PREF1 ¶1]; through it the Gothic cathedral gives up its secret [PREF1 ¶6]. Written in 1922 [PREF2 ¶1]; first printing of 1926 in 300 copies; a second edition added three drawings by Julien Champagne and original notes of Fulcanelli, unchanged [PREF2 ¶44]; it had appeared by June 1926 [PREF3 ¶33]. Its plates are by Julien Champagne [PREF1 ¶12]; Jean-Jacques Pauvert reissued it with photographs of the sculptures Champagne drew [PREF3 ¶10]; Canseliet calls the edition of PREF3 the third [PREF3 ¶50]. Canseliet's claim for its merit: it brought the phonetic cabala back to light [PREF2 ¶45]. Canseliet also states that the key of the major arcanum is given by one of its figures [PREF1 ¶11].

> «ce livre a remis en pleine lumière la cabale phonétique dont les principes et leur application étaient tombés dans le plus total oubli.» (*this book has brought back into full light the phonetic cabala, whose principles and their application had fallen into the most complete oblivion.*) [PREF2 ¶45]

### Le Psautier d’Hermpophile — The Psalter of Hermpophile — *Le Psautier d’Hermpophile* {#e461}

*work; Name as spelled in the text (AMIENS n6 has 'Hermpophile').*

An anonymous 18th-century manuscript in 'Traités de la Transmutation des Métaux', strophe XXV [AMIENS n6], source of the plain statement that the metallic Chaos contains all metals and is no metal [AMIENS ¶25].

> «Le Chaos métallique produit des mains de la nature contient en soy tous les métaux et n’est point métail.» (*The metallic Chaos produced by the hands of nature contains in itself all the metals and is not metal.*) [AMIENS ¶25]

### Le Règne de Saturne changé en Siècle d'or — The Reign of Saturn Changed into a Golden Age — *Le Règne de Saturne changé en Siècle d’or* {#e205}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.*

Work of Huginus à Barma (Paris, Derieu, 1780) [INTRO.7 n7]. In its Pratique the author tells the reader to take a true earth impregnated with the rays of sun, moon and stars, which Fulcanelli relates to the star of the Nativity [INTRO.7 ¶15].

> «Huginus à Barma, dans la Pratique de son ouvrage, emploie les mêmes termes pour exprimer la matière du Grand Œuvre sur laquelle paraît l’étoile» (*Huginus à Barma, in the Practice of his work, uses the same terms to express the matter of the Great Work on which the star appears*) [INTRO.7 ¶15]

### Le Secret Livre d'Artephius — The Secret Book of Artephius — *Le Secret Livre d'Artephius* {#e315}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

Printed in 'Trois Traitez de la Philosophie naturelle', Paris, Marette, 1612; the reference given for Artephius on the secret fire [PARIS.3 n9; PARIS.3 ¶20].

> «Le Secret Livre d’Artephius, dans Trois Traitez de la Philosophie naturelle. Paris, Marette, 1612.» (*The Secret Book of Artephius, in Three Treatises of Natural Philosophy. Paris, Marette, 1612.*) [PARIS.3 n9]

### Le Testament — The Testament — *Le Testament* {#e066}

*work; Name as spelled in the text; English title is our literal translation.*

Poem of François Villon, stanzas XXXVI and XXXVII of which serve as epigraph to the third preface [PREF3 ¶1, PREF3 ¶8, PREF3 ¶9]. The text does not comment on it.

> «« Mieux vault vivre soubz gros bureaux» (*'Better to live under coarse homespun*) [PREF3 ¶1]

### Le Très précieux Don de Dieu — The Most Precious Gift of God — *Le Très précieux Don de Dieu* {#e486}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Manuscript of Georges Aurach of Strasbourg, written and painted by his own hand in 1415 [BOURGES.2 n1], cited for its image of the green-filled matras [BOURGES.2 ¶5].

> «Le Très précieux Don de Dieu. Manuscrit de Georges Aurach, de Strasbourg, escript et peint de sa propre main, l’an du Salut de l’Humanité rachetée, 1415.» (*The Most Precious Gift of God. Manuscript of Georges Aurach, of Strasbourg, written and painted by his own hand, in the year of the Salvation of redeemed Humanity, 1415.*) [BOURGES.2 n1]

### Lenglet-Dufresnoy — *Lenglet-Dufresnoy* {#e347}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of the Histoire de la Philosophie Hermétique (1742), whose volume II gives Philalèthe's Entrée au Palais Fermé du Roy and his Règles [PARIS.4 n1; AMIENS n3]. In his Remarks on chapter XIX of the Introitus he says the short way is done by the double philosophical mercury in eight days, against nearly eighteen months for the first way [PARIS.7 ¶6].

> «Par là, ajoute-t-il, l'Œuvre s’accomplit en huit jours, au lieu qu’il faut près de dix-huit mois pour la première voye» (*By this means, he adds, the Work is accomplished in eight days, whereas nearly eighteen months are needed for the first way*) [PARIS.7 ¶6]

### Les Demeures philosophales — The Philosophers' Dwellings — *Les Demeures philosophales* {#e018}

*work; Name as spelled in the text; English title is our literal translation.*

A further work, for which Canseliet wrote a Preface, where he had called Basile Valentin Fulcanelli's 'true' initiator, later corrected to 'first' [PREF2 ¶5]. Canseliet says a long-pondered question of the Master, to which the added notes relate, will be discussed apropos of it [PREF2 ¶44]. A note cites it for the sculpted 16th-century soffit at Dampierre-sur-Boutonne, comparable to the Lallemant chapel ceiling [BOURGES.2 n12]. The text does not state its author outright.

> «l’un, à Dampierre-sur-Boutonne, également sculpté, du XVIe siècle (Les Demeures Philosophales)» (*one at Dampierre-sur-Boutonne, likewise sculpted, of the 16th century (The Philosophers' Dwellings)*) [BOURGES.2 n12]

### Les Trois Livres de l’Art du Potier — The Three Books of the Potter's Art — *Les Trois Livres de l’Art du Potier* {#e520}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of the cavalier Cyprian Piccolpassi, cited under Claudius Popelin's name (Paris, Librairie Internationale, 1861) [BOURGES.2 n14], whose plates include the dove tied to a stone [BOURGES.2 ¶36].

> «Claudius Popelin, Les Trois Livres de l’Art du Potier, du cavalier Cyprian Piccolpassi. Paris, Librairie Internationale, 1861.» (*Claudius Popelin, The Three Books of the Potter's Art, by the cavalier Cyprian Piccolpassi. Paris, Librairie Internationale, 1861.*) [BOURGES.2 n14]

## T

### The letter S — *lettre S* {#e528}

*term; Literal translation.*

On the Hendaye cross, the S, serpent-shaped, corresponds to the Greek khi (X) and takes its esoteric meaning: the helical track of the sun at the zenith of its curve at the cyclic catastrophe, a theoretical image of the beast of the Apocalypse [HENDAYE ¶9]. Its deliberate displacement shows the inscription must be read in the language of the gods or of the birds [HENDAYE ¶9].

> «La lettre S qui emprunte la forme sinueuse du serpent, correspond au khi (X) de la langue grecque et en prend la signification ésotérique.» (*The letter S, which borrows the sinuous form of the serpent, corresponds to the khi (X) of the Greek language and takes on its esoteric meaning.*) [HENDAYE ¶9]

## L

### Libethra — *Libéthra* {#e267}

*term; Name as spelled in the text.*

Mythology's name for the Mysterious Fountain: a fountain of Magnesia with a neighbouring spring called the Rock, both issuing from a great rock shaped like a woman's breast, so that the water seemed to flow like milk from two breasts [PARIS.2 ¶7]. Since the old authors call the matter of the Work 'our Magnesia' and its liquor Virgin's Milk, Fulcanelli sees 'an indication' here [PARIS.2 ¶7]. Source: Noël, Dictionnaire de la Fable [PARIS.2 n3].

> «La mythologie la nomme Libéthra et nous raconte que c’était une fontaine de Magnésie, laquelle avait, dans son voisinage, une autre source nommée la Roche.» (*Mythology names it Libethra and tells us it was a fountain of Magnesia, which had near it another spring named the Rock.*) [PARIS.2 ¶7]

### Limojon de Saint-Didier — *Limojon de Saint-Didier* {#e064}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (epigraph spells 'LIMONJON').*

Cited author; commentator of the Ancienne Guerre des Chevaliers in the Triomphe hermétique [PARIS.3 n8]. The secret fire is prepared by the artist; it is not actually hot but an igneous spirit put into a subject of the Stone's nature, which calcines, dissolves, sublimes and resolves it into dry water [PARIS.3 ¶22]. The reborn gold receives a second crown 'infinitely nobler than the first' [BOURGES.1 ¶10]. He advises steering 'by the sight of the north star' [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. His epigraph: knowledge of the magistery comes from Heaven's inspiration [PREF2 ¶49, PREF2 ¶50].

> «Ce feu n’est pas actuellement chaud, mais il est un esprit igné introduit dans un sujet d’une même nature que la Pierre» (*This fire is not actually hot, but it is an igneous spirit introduced into a subject of the same nature as the Stone*) [PARIS.3 ¶22]

> «C’est pourquoi Limojon de Saint-Didier recommande aux investigateurs de régler leur route « par la vue de l’étoile du nord ».» (*That is why Limojon de Saint-Didier advises seekers to set their course 'by the sight of the north star'.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

### Lion — *Lion* {#e351}

*term; Literal translation.*

A complex symbol on the Paris stylobate, whose original colour is lost, so Fulcanelli cannot settle on one reading [PARIS.4 ¶16, ¶19]. In the griffin it is the fixed, basic part of a compound [PARIS.4 ¶16]; with the eagle it figures the earthly, fixed force of one of the two natures [PARIS.4 ¶11]. Stated: in general the Lion is the sign of gold, alchemical and natural; texts also give the name to the matter that receives the Universal Spirit, the secret fire, in making the dissolvent; in both cases it means power, incorruptibility, perfection [PARIS.4 ¶16]. On the Paris zodiac Leo, put in place of Cancer, is 'the philosophical ferment' to be joined with the mercurial compound [PARIS.6 ¶21]. The Sages lead their eagles to devour the lion (Philalèthe, quoted) [PARIS.4 ¶4].

> «En général, le Lion est le signe de l’or, tant alchimique que naturel ; il traduit donc les propriétés physico-chimiques de ces corps.» (*In general, the Lion is the sign of gold, both alchemical and natural; it therefore expresses the physico-chemical properties of these bodies.*) [PARIS.4 ¶16]

### Livre de Seth — Book of Seth — *Livre de Seth* {#e199}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.*

A legend drawn from a work titled Livre de Seth is related by a 6th-century author (cited as the Opus imperfectum in Mattheum, joined to the works of Saint John Chrysostom) [INTRO.7 ¶3; INTRO.7 n3]. A people of the Far East on the shores of the Ocean had a Book attributed to Seth foretelling the star and the gifts for the Child; twelve of the wisest, called Magi, climbed each year the Mount of Victory, with a cave cut in the rock, prayed in silence three days, until the star appeared there as a small child bearing the figure of a cross, spoke, instructed them and sent them to Judea [INTRO.7 ¶4–7]. The star led them two years [INTRO.7 ¶8].

> «il y avait un Livre attribué à Seth, qui parlait de l’apparition future de cette étoile et des présents qu’on devait apporter à l’Enfant» (*there was a Book attributed to Seth, which spoke of the future appearance of this star and of the gifts that were to be brought to the Child*) [INTRO.7 ¶4]

### Livre des Figures hiéroglyphiques — Book of Hieroglyphic Figures — *Livre des Figures hiéroglyphiques* {#e279}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

A work of Nicolas Flamel. Of the hollow oak from which the fountain springs, Flamel says there only 'Note ce chêne' [PARIS.2 n2]. In his 'Figures Hiéroglyphiques' Flamel describes the parabolic pilgrimage he made to ask 'Monsieur Jacques de Galice' for help, light and protection, which Fulcanelli takes as the journey every alchemist begins with [BOURGES.1 ¶5].

> «« Note ce chêne », dit simplement Flamel au Livre des Figures hiéroglyphiques.» (*'Note this oak,' Flamel simply says in the Book of Hieroglyphic Figures.*) [PARIS.2 n2]

### Livre des Images — Book of Images — *Livre des Images* {#e335}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

Work of Paracelsus quoted on the successive colorations of the Work and on tincture [PARIS.3 ¶30].

> «Paracelse, au Livre des Images, parle ainsi des colorations successives de l’Œuvre» (*Paracelsus, in the Book of Images, speaks thus of the successive colorations of the Work*) [PARIS.3 ¶30]

### Lorraine glass — *verre de Lorraine* {#e488}

*term; Literal translation; the text glosses it as moulded glass (BOURGES.2 n2).*

Some authors advise it for the egg's walls, which must be thick to resist internal pressure [BOURGES.2 ¶6]. Stated: the term once distinguished moulded from blown glass; moulding allowed very thick, regular walls [BOURGES.2 n2].

> «Le vocable verre de Lorraine servait autrefois à distinguer le verre moulé du verre soufflé.» (*The term Lorraine glass once served to distinguish moulded glass from blown glass.*) [BOURGES.2 n2]

### Lost Word — *parole perdue* {#e050}

*term; Literal translation; the text gives it as the equivalent of Verbum dimissum [PARIS.6 ¶25].*

The 'Lost Word' of the medieval freemasons, which all hermetic fraternities hoped to recover; Fulcanelli equates it with the Verbum dimissum of the Trévisan and with the highest secret, the extraction of May Dew [PARIS.6 ¶25]. Canseliet: Freemasonry still seeks it, while the Church, which possesses this Word, is abandoning it [PREF3 ¶14]. The text identifies it with Verbum dimissum (e003).

> «C’est là le Verbum dimissum du Trévisan, la Parole perdue des francs-maçons médiévaux, celle que toutes les Fraternités hermétiques espéraient retrouver, et dont la recherche constituait le but de leurs travaux et la raison d’être de leur existence.» (*That is the Verbum dimissum of the Trévisan, the Lost Word of the medieval freemasons, the one all hermetic Fraternities hoped to recover, and whose search was the aim of their labours and the reason for their existence.*) [PARIS.6 ¶25]

> «Quand la Franc-Maçonnerie recherche toujours la parole perdue (verbum dimissum), l’Eglise universelle (χαθολιχή katholiké) qui possède ce Verbe, est elle-même en voie de l’abandonner dans l’œucuménisme du diable.» (*While Freemasonry still seeks the lost word (verbum dimissum), the universal Church (katholiké), which possesses this Word, is itself about to abandon it in the devil's ecumenism.*) [PREF3 ¶14]

Identified in the text with: [Verbum dimissum (the abandoned Word)](#e003)

### Louis David — *Louis David* {#e337}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named as the composer of the French flag, whose colours repeat the three of the Work: dark blue or black for the bourgeoisie, white for the people, red for royalty [PARIS.3 ¶31]. The text calls him 'le maçon écribouille Louis David' and gives no gloss of 'écribouille'.

> «Ce sont là les couleurs nationales du drapeau français, lequel, d’ailleurs fut composé par le maçon écribouille Louis David.» (*These are the national colours of the French flag, which, moreover, was composed by the mason 'écribouille' Louis David.*) [PARIS.3 ¶31]

### Lucifer — *Lucifer* {#e112}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; the text glosses it as 'qui porte la lumière, — l’étoile du matin' [INTRO.4 ¶6].*

The devil figure called Maistre Pierre du Coignet at Notre-Dame represented the initial matter of the Work humanized as Lucifer, the light-bearer or morning star, and was the symbol of the cornerstone [INTRO.4 ¶6]. Before being cut, the rough stone was often given the image of the devil [INTRO.4 ¶5].

> «Or, cette figure, destinée à représenter la matière initiale de l’Œuvre, humanisée sous l’aspect de Lucifer (qui porte la lumière, — l’étoile du matin), était le symbole de notre pierre angulaire» (*Now this figure, meant to represent the initial matter of the Work, humanized in the guise of Lucifer (who bears the light — the morning star), was the symbol of our cornerstone*) [INTRO.4 ¶6]

Identified in the text with: [Morning star](#e021)

### Ludus puerorum (children's play) — *ludus puerorum* {#e448}

*term; Literal translation of the Latin; the text glosses it as the keeping of the fire [AMIENS ¶6].*

The text states its meaning: once the labours of Hercules are done, the artist's work is reduced to the ludus puerorum of the texts, that is, maintaining the fire, which a woman spinning could do [AMIENS ¶6]. On the chapel ceiling of the Hôtel Lallemant it appears as a child riding a wooden horse, whip raised, as in Trismosin's Toison d'Or [BOURGES.2 ¶28].

> «son labeur se réduit au ludus puerorum des textes, c’est-à-dire à l’entretien du feu, ce qu’une femme filant quenouille peut facilement entreprendre et mener à bien.» (*his labour is reduced to the ludus puerorum of the texts, that is, to the keeping of the fire, which a woman spinning at her distaff can easily undertake and carry through.*) [AMIENS ¶6]

### Légendes du Vieux Paris — Legends of Old Paris — *Légendes du Vieux Paris* {#e240}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.*

Work of Amédée de Ponthieu (Bachelin-Deflorenne, 1867), cited at p. 91 for the Parvis monolith [INTRO.9 n1] and at p. 106 for the legend of Saint Christopher after Jacobus de Voragine [BOURGES.2 n3; BOURGES.2 ¶10–12].

> «Avant d’être chrétien, Christophe se nommait Offerus ; c’était une espèce de géant, esprit très épais.» (*Before becoming a Christian, Christopher was named Offerus; he was a kind of giant, a very thick-witted fellow.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶10]

## M

### Magi — *Mages* {#e200}

*term; The usual English term.*

In the legend from the Book of Seth, the twelve wise men awaiting the star were called Magi in their tongue because they glorified God in silence and in a low voice; the star appeared to them on the Mount of Victory as a small child with a cross [INTRO.7 ¶6–7]. Other legends: a thirteen-day journey with a star in the form of an eagle surmounted by a cross [INTRO.7 ¶11]; in Persia the Magi announce the Child born at Bethlehem [INTRO.7 ¶13]; Chalcidius speaks of the most learned Chaldeans who saw the star [INTRO.7 ¶17]; Matthew's Gospel of Magi from the East following his star [INTRO.7 ¶24–28]. Tradition says a star guided them to the Saviour's cradle, the announcing sign; the Epiphany cake keeps a hidden version of this [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Canseliet cites Saint Chrysostom that the sign that led the Magi rested on the Saviour's head [PREF2 ¶26].

> «On les appelait, dans leur langue, Mages, parce qu’ils glorifiaient Dieu dans le silence et à voix basse.» (*In their language they were called Magi, because they glorified God in silence and in a low voice.*) [INTRO.7 ¶6]

> «La Tradition veut que les Mages aient été guidés jusqu’au berceau du Sauveur par une étoile, laquelle fut, pour eux, le signe annonciateur, la Bonne Nouvelle de sa naissance.» (*Tradition holds that the Magi were guided to the Saviour's cradle by a star, which was for them the announcing sign, the Good News of his birth.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

### Magistery — *Magistère* {#e023}

*term; Usual English term.*

The accomplished Work, or its object. Read cabalistically, Jacques Cœur's motto yields 'the three reiterations indispensable to the total perfection of the two Magisteries' [BOURGES.1 ¶4]. Ripley: one unclean body, the Green Lion, enters 'our magistery' [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. Following Tollius, the magistery is to be sought in a volatile thing, hence the need for a vessel [BOURGES.2 ¶36]. The letter to Fulcanelli: to make the Work by mercury alone is to have accomplished the Magistery [PREF2 ¶17]; Limojon's epigraph: knowledge of the magistery comes from Heaven's inspiration [PREF2 ¶49]. The text does not say what the two Magisteries are.

> «3° les trois réitérations indispensables à la perfection totale des deux Magistères (les trois cuers).» (*3rd, the three reiterations indispensable to the total perfection of the two Magisteries (the three cuers).*) [BOURGES.1 ¶4]

> «Ne devez-vous pas, d’après l’excellent avis de Tollius, chercher et trouver le magistère dans une chose volatile ?» (*Must you not, following the excellent advice of Tollius, seek and find the magistery in a volatile thing?*) [BOURGES.2 ¶36]

### Magnesia — *Magnésie* {#e177}

*term; The usual English term; literal.*

A name for the matter of the Work: old authors call it 'notre Magnésie', and the liquor drawn from it the Virgin's Milk [PARIS.2 ¶7]. The magnet (αιρην) is the virtue in the body the Sages call their magnesia [INTRO.5 ¶5]. Bernard Trévisan (QUOTED) names the blackened compost Magnesia [PARIS.3 ¶13, PARIS.3 ¶14]. At Amiens the dew falls on a mass that may be the mineral called Magnesia or philosophical Magnet, showing its attractive virtue [AMIENS ¶34]; the nostoc symbolizes the Sages' mineral Magnesia [AMIENS ¶35]. Some call the raw subject Magnesia lunarii [BOURGES.2 ¶23].

> «Or, nous avons vu que les anciens auteurs appellent la matière de l’Œuvre notre Magnésie et que la liqueur extraite de cette magnésie est dite Lait de la Vierge.» (*Now we have seen that the ancient authors call the matter of the Work our Magnesia, and that the liquor extracted from this magnesia is called Virgin's Milk.*) [PARIS.2 ¶7]

> «Donc, αιρην est l’aimant, la vertu renfermée dans le corps que les Sages nomment leur magnésie.» (*So αιρην is the magnet, the virtue enclosed in the body that the Sages call their magnesia.*) [INTRO.5 ¶5]

### Magnet — *Aimant* {#e043}

*term; Literal translation.*

The attractive virtue in the matter. By Greek etymology, Ariadne's name leads to αιρην, 'what takes, attracts', the magnet, the virtue enclosed in the body the Sages call their magnesia [INTRO.5 ¶5]. Adepts call their steel from Aries their magnet [PARIS.3 ¶23]. The spirit spread over the globe needs an attractive receptacle: the magnet in the belly of Aries, to be taken at its birth [PARIS.4 ¶13]; the water of the Work is a magnet drawing all celestial influences [PARIS.4 ¶14]. The first magnetic agent for the dissolvent is the Green Lion [PARIS.4 ¶17]. At Amiens the dew falls on a body that may be Magnesia or philosophical Magnet, showing an attractive virtue [AMIENS ¶34]. Philalèthe's recipe: nine parts of 'our Magnet' [PREF2 ¶37].

> «Donc, αιρην est l’aimant, la vertu renfermée dans le corps que les Sages nomment leur magnésie.» (*So αιρην is the magnet, the virtue enclosed in the body the Sages call their magnesia.*) [INTRO.5 ¶5]

> «C’est là cet aimant enfermé au ventre d’Ariès, qu’il faut prendre au moment de sa naissance, avec autant d’adresse que d’habileté.» (*That is this magnet shut in the belly of Aries, which must be taken at the moment of its birth, with as much dexterity as skill.*) [PARIS.4 ¶13]

### Marcellin Berthelot — *Marcellin Berthelot* {#e171}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Quoted from La Grande Encyclopédie on the 'labyrinth of Solomon': a cabalistic figure at the head of some alchemical manuscripts, part of the magical traditions attributed to Solomon, a series of broken concentric circles [INTRO.5 ¶2, INTRO.5 n1].

> «« une figure cabalistique qui se trouve en tête de certains manuscrits alchimiques, et qui fait partie des traditions magiques attribuées au nom de Salomon.» (*'a cabalistic figure found at the head of certain alchemical manuscripts, and which forms part of the magical traditions attributed to the name of Solomon.*) [INTRO.5 ¶2]

### Marie-Guillame Devergie — *Marie-Guillame Devergie* {#e076}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; the proposed 'Guillaume' does not occur.*

Canseliet: physician and toxicologist who, with Orfila, observed the slow decomposition of the human body, whose stench finally turns almost aromatic [PREF3 ¶20, PREF3 ¶21].

> «les célèbres médecins, toxicologues de surcroît, Mathieu-Joseph Orfila et Marie-Guillame Devergie, sur la lente et progressive décomposition du corps humain.» (*the famous physicians, toxicologists besides, Mathieu-Joseph Orfila and Marie-Guillame Devergie, on the slow and progressive decomposition of the human body.*) [PREF3 ¶20]

### Mars — *Mars* {#e045}

*term; Usual English term.*

Planetary name of iron: 'And of iron Mars is the figure' [PARIS.6 ¶6]. The Cosmopolite: Mars converts easily into Venus but not the reverse, the virtues of planets descending [PARIS.6 ¶7]. On the sarcophagus of the north portal, Mars and Venus stand on either side of the Sun [PARIS.6 ¶1, PARIS.6 ¶2, PARIS.6 ¶8]. Canseliet quotes Philalèthe: the purgation succeeds 'if Saturn has seen his image in the mirror of Mars' [PREF2 ¶37]; the text does not explain this.

> «Et Mars du fer sont la figure.» (*And Mars of iron [is] the figure.*) [PARIS.6 ¶6]

> «mesme l’expérience nous apprend que le Mars se convertit facilement en Vénus, et non le Vénus en Mars, comme plus basse d’une sphère.» (*even experience teaches us that Mars is easily converted into Venus, and not Venus into Mars, as being lower by one sphere.*) [PARIS.6 ¶7]

### Martigny — *Martigny* {#e498}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of the Dictionnaire des Antiquités chrétiennes (art. Eucharistie, 2nd ed., p. 291) [BOURGES.2 n8], who reproduces a Catacomb painting of a fish carrying a basket of loaves and a red object; the basket is the same hieroglyph as the galette [BOURGES.2 ¶15].

> «Martigny reproduit, en effet, une curieuse peinture des Catacombes qui représente un poisson, nageant dans les flots et portant sur son dos une corbeille dans laquelle sont des pains et un objet rouge» (*Martigny indeed reproduces a curious painting from the Catacombs showing a fish swimming in the waves and carrying on its back a basket holding loaves and a red object*) [BOURGES.2 ¶15]

### Martyrius — *Martyrius* {#e125}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

An Armenian bishop and traveller of the 15th century whose text attests that cathedrals were gilded and painted; he said the porch of Notre-Dame de Paris shone like the entrance to paradise, with purple, rose, azure, silver and gold [INTRO.2 n2].

> «Cet auteur dit que le porche de Notre-Dame de Paris resplendissait comme l’entrée du paradis.» (*This author says that the porch of Notre-Dame de Paris shone like the entrance to paradise.*) [INTRO.2 n2]

### Massacre of the Innocents — *Massacre des Innocents* {#e402}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

Abraham the Jew's fourth figure shows soldiers killing children of two mothers, the blood gathered in a vat where sun and moon come to bathe; there are seven innocents dead [PARIS.2 ¶16; PARIS.2 ¶19]. Fulcanelli recognises in the Bath of the stars relief the simplified allegory of the Massacre of the Innocents dear to Flamel [PARIS.6 ¶22]. Its meaning: the universal Spirit, as Sulphur, is the agent of metallic tinctures; this red 'blood' of the children is had only by breaking down what nature joined, so the body must die to release its soul [PARIS.6 ¶23]. A window of the Sainte-Chapelle on this subject is given as a specimen [PARIS.8 ¶8].

> «Mais on ne peut obtenir cet Esprit, ce sang rouge des enfants qu’en décomposant ce que la nature avait d’abord assemblé en eux.» (*But this Spirit, this red blood of the children, can only be obtained by decomposing what nature had first assembled in them.*) [PARIS.6 ¶23]

### Master Pierre du Coignet — *Maistre Pierre du Coignet* {#e162}

*term; Name as spelled in the text; the popular name of a sculpted figure.*

A devil figure with a huge mouth, under the rood-screen at the corner of the choir enclosure of Notre-Dame, where the faithful put out their candles; the people's name for it puzzled archaeologists [INTRO.4 ¶6]. It represented the initial matter as Lucifer and was the symbol of the cornerstone, 'maîtresse pierre du coignet' [INTRO.4 ¶6].

> «Le peuple appelait cette image Maistre Pierre du Coignet, ce qui ne laissait pas d’embarrasser les archéologues.» (*The people called this image Master Pierre du Coignet, which never failed to puzzle the archaeologists.*) [INTRO.4 ¶6]

### Mathieu-Joseph Orfila — *Mathieu-Joseph Orfila* {#e075}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Canseliet: famous physician and toxicologist who, with Devergie, at the start of the Second Empire, observed the slow decomposition of the human body, ending in a residue with an almost aromatic smell; Canseliet compares this with the old masters' passage from stench to perfume [PREF3 ¶20, PREF3 ¶21, PREF3 ¶22].

> «les célèbres médecins, toxicologues de surcroît, Mathieu-Joseph Orfila et Marie-Guillame Devergie, sur la lente et progressive décomposition du corps humain.» (*the famous physicians, toxicologists besides, Mathieu-Joseph Orfila and Marie-Guillame Devergie, on the slow and progressive decomposition of the human body.*) [PREF3 ¶20]

### Matrass — *matras* {#e437}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

At the Hôtel Lallemant, what the guide calls a pestle held by the figure in the 'kitchen' is in fact a long-necked matrass, like chemists' balloons [BOURGES.2 ¶4]. This secret vessel is called philosophical egg and Green Lion; G. Aurach's manuscript and the third figure of the Toison d'Or show a glass matrass half full of green liquor [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. An inverted gold matrass, dropping silver tears, crowns the escutcheon of saint Thomas Aquinas [PARIS.8 ¶6].

> «ce que tient le marmouset en question est en (184) réalité un matras à long col, semblable à ceux qu’emploient nos chimistes» (*what the figure in question holds is in reality a long-necked matrass, like those our chemists use*) [BOURGES.2 ¶4]

### Matter — *Matière* {#e253}

*term; Literal translation; the text derives the word from mater, mother [PARIS.1 ¶5].*

The cathedral rests on alchemy, which studies the transformations of the original substance, elementary Matter, a word the text derives from Latin materea, root mater, mother [PARIS.1 ¶5]. The Virgin-Mother, without her veil, personifies the primitive substance used by the creative Principle [PARIS.1 ¶5]. An anonymous author quoted says the Philosophers were right to hide this mystery from those who value things only by their uses, for if they knew the Matter, which God took pleasure in hiding in things that seem useful to them, they would no longer esteem it [AMIENS ¶32].

> «Car la Vierge-Mère, dépouillée de son voile symbolique, n’est autre chose que la personnification de la substance primitive dont se servit pour réaliser ses desseins, le Principe créateur de tout ce qui est.» (*For the Virgin-Mother, stripped of her symbolic veil, is nothing other than the personification of the primitive substance which the creative Principle of all that is used to carry out its designs.*) [PARIS.1 ¶5]

### May Dew — *Rosée de Mai* {#e359}

*term; Literal translation.*

Stated: the name the Sages gave to the condensation of the universal spirit, the mineral blood needed to animate the fixed body of gold, because it forms in humid form only at night, under a clear sky and calm air, and most abundantly in the earth's spring [PARIS.6 ¶24]. The text adds that Maia was mother of Hermes and May the month of Mary, and that this dew is easily extracted from a particular, abject, despised body; this is the highest secret, the Verbum dimissum of the Trevisan, the Lost Word of the medieval freemasons [PARIS.6 ¶25]. The soul extracted from the dying body is called 'vie métallique et Rosée céleste' [PARIS.6 ¶23]. Thomas Corneille says the Rose-Croix masters were called Brothers of the Cooked Dew [PARIS.6 ¶24]. May Dew is one name of the Green Lion [PARIS.4 ¶17]. At Amiens an Adept watches the celestial dew fall on a mass (fleece, or Magnesia, the philosophical Magnet) [AMIENS ¶34]. Identified with the Green Lion (e357).

> «les Sages, pour ces raisons combinées, lui donnèrent le nom de Rosée de Mai.» (*the Sages, for these combined reasons, gave it the name of May Dew.*) [PARIS.6 ¶24]

> «le sang minéral dont ils avaient besoin pour animer le corps fixe et inerte de l’or n’était qu’une condensation de l’esprit universel, âme de toute chose» (*the mineral blood they needed to animate the fixed and inert body of gold was only a condensation of the universal spirit, soul of all things*) [PARIS.6 ¶24]

Identified in the text with: [Green Lion](#e357)

### Melampus — *Melampus* {#e142}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Mélampus in BOURGES.2 ¶21).*

Said to have shared the Language of the Birds with Tiresias; called a fictitious person whose name speaks eloquently [INTRO.3 ¶9]. Named again among the few able to understand [BOURGES.2 ¶21].

> «Mais, pour quelques prétendus cabalistes, juifs ou chrétiens, plus riches de prétention que de savoir, combien y a-t-il de Tirésias, de Thalès ou de Mélampus capables de comprendre ces choses ?» (*But for some self-styled cabalists, Jewish or Christian, richer in pretension than in knowledge, how many Tiresiases, Thaleses or Melampuses are there able to understand these things?*) [BOURGES.2 ¶21]

### Mercury — *Mercure* {#e028}

*term; Usual English term.*

The volatile principle and, prepared, the philosophers' water. The serpent on the golden rod figures Mercury 'in its first state' absorbing the bodily sulphur; the result, keeping the name Philosophical Mercury, needs only graduated cooking to become red sulphur, Elixir, universal Medicine [PARIS.3 ¶17, PARIS.3 ¶18]. Mixing two waters yields a third that does not wet the hands, called Mercury or Sulphur [PARIS.2 ¶7]. The cock is the volatile portion drawn from the subject (the oak), named Mercury though unlike common quicksilver; a dry water [AMIENS ¶15]. 'Our' mercury differs from vulgar quicksilver [PARIS.5 ¶7]; the old man of the seventh medallion is 'our Mercury', the secret agent, identified with Saturn [PARIS.5 ¶11]. Kermès equals Hermès, i.e. Mercury; the prepared mercury must have the power of tinting [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. The scallop designates the principle Mercury, called Traveller or Pilgrim [BOURGES.1 ¶5]. Canseliet: star first seen in the 'mirror of the art or mercury' [PREF2 ¶20].

> «C’est là notre source fameuse dont l’onde claire coule à la base de l’arbre sacré, si vénéré des Druides, et que les anciens philosophes ont nommée Mercure, quoiqu’elle n’ait aucune apparence du vif-argent vulgaire.» (*That is our famous spring whose clear water flows at the foot of the sacred tree so venerated by the Druids, and which the ancient philosophers named Mercury, though it has no appearance of common quicksilver.*) [AMIENS ¶15]

> «Ce reptile est le type du Mercure dans son premier état, et la verge d’or, le soufre corporel qui lui est ajouté.» (*This reptile is the type of Mercury in its first state, and the golden rod the bodily sulphur added to it.*) [PARIS.3 ¶18]

Identified in the text with: [Fountain of Life](#e276), [Kermes](#e508)

### Mercury alone — *seul mercure* {#e022}

*term; Literal translation.*

Maxim that the Work can be done by mercury alone. 'In our Work, the Philosophers affirm, Mercury alone suffices' [PARIS.3 ¶18]. Mercury contains in itself everything needed for the work and is the only matter required [AMIENS ¶15]. On a Lallemant ceiling panel (mercury below, the Absolute above) Fulcanelli reads a simple procedure of one way, one matter, one operation, and cites 'He who knows how to make the Work by mercury alone has found what is most perfect' as the claim of the most famous authors [BOURGES.2 ¶28]. The same sentence closes the letter to Fulcanelli, adding 'that is, has received the light and accomplished the Magistery' [PREF2 ¶17].

> «« Dans notre Oeuvre, affirment les Philosophes, le Mercure seul suffit. »» (*'In our Work,' the Philosophers affirm, 'Mercury alone suffices.'*) [PARIS.3 ¶18]

> «Il est important, avant d’entreprendre la pratique, de savoir que le mercure contient en soi tout ce qui est nécessaire au travail.» (*It is important, before undertaking the practice, to know that mercury contains in itself all that is necessary for the work.*) [AMIENS ¶15]

### Michael Maier — *Michel Maier* {#e101}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Michel Maier); the proposed English form is the usual English spelling.*

Canseliet says the Saint Marcel legend corresponds exactly to the 50th emblem of Maier's Atalanta Fugiens, where the dragon entwines a mature clothed woman lying inert in her violated grave [PREF3 ¶43].

> «Elle répond exactement au 50e emblème de Michel Maier, en son Atalanta Fugiens» (*It corresponds exactly to the 50th emblem of Michel Maier, in his Atalanta Fugiens*) [PREF3 ¶43]

### Microcosm — *microcosme* {#e135}

*term; The usual English term; literal.*

Our microcosm is a tiny, imperfect particle of the macrocosm; we invent nothing, all is in all [INTRO.3 ¶7]. Canseliet places the 'family drama' of mother and child in the alchemical-physical microcosm [PREF3 ¶13] and speaks of the artist separating light from darkness in his 'petit monde' [PREF2 ¶32]. The solar-cycle diagram at Hendaye joins the signs of macrocosm and microcosm [HENDAYE ¶11].

> «Notre microcosme n’est qu’une particule infime, animée, pensante, plus ou moins imparfaite, du macrocosme.» (*Our microcosm is only a tiny particle of the macrocosm, animate, thinking, more or less imperfect.*) [INTRO.3 ¶7]

### Millenarianism — *millénarisme* {#e526}

*term; The usual English term; chiliasme is given as its equivalent in the text.*

The Hendaye cross is called the most singular monument of primitive millenarianism, the rarest symbolic rendering of chiliasm [HENDAYE ¶2]. The doctrine, first accepted then opposed by Origen, saint Denys of Alexandria and saint Jerome, though never condemned by the Church, belonged to the esoteric traditions of the ancient philosophy of Hermes [HENDAYE ¶2].

> «On sait que cette doctrine, acceptée tout d’abord puis combattue par Origène, saint Denys d’Alexandrie et saint Jérôme, bien que l’église ne l’eût point condamnée, faisait partie des traditions ésotériques de l’antique philosophie d’Hermès.» (*It is known that this doctrine, first accepted then opposed by Origen, Saint Denys of Alexandria and Saint Jerome, although the Church had not condemned it, was part of the esoteric traditions of the ancient philosophy of Hermes.*) [HENDAYE ¶2]

### Minotaur — *Minotaure* {#e169}

*person; Name as spelled in the text, with the usual English form.*

Theseus's combat with it was once shown at the centre of the Chartres labyrinth [INTRO.5 ¶1]. Tristan's killing of the Morhout matches Theseus's killing of the Minotaur, a hieroglyph of making the Green Lion [BOURGES.1 ¶9].

> «Au centre de cette figure se voyait autrefois le combat de Thésée et du Minotaure.» (*At the centre of this figure one formerly saw the combat of Theseus and the Minotaur.*) [INTRO.5 ¶1]

> «Tristan combat et tue le Morhout, Thésée le Minotaure.» (*Tristan fights and kills the Morhout, Theseus the Minotaur.*) [BOURGES.1 ¶9]

### Mirror of the Art — *Miroir de l'Art* {#e026}

*term; Literal translation.*

Name of the first matter the artist must choose [PARIS.5 ¶4]. On the second Notre-Dame medallion the Initiator holds a mirror: the mirror is the beginning of the work, the Tree of Life its end [PARIS.5 ¶3]. Moras de Respour: through it one learned how metals are composed in the earth; the Cosmopolite: a mirror in which the whole world is seen; Basile Valentin: the body of Vitriol is a 'Mirror of philosophical Science' where Mercury, Sun and Moon shine [PARIS.5 ¶4]. The sixth medallion's bust of Nature reflected in a mirror is the hieroglyph of the subject of the Sages [PARIS.5 ¶10]. Canseliet: the hermetic star is first seen in the 'mirror of the art or mercury' [PREF2 ¶20]; the star on the Virgin is an image reflected by the 'mirror of Wisdom' [PREF2 ¶26].

> «Alchimiquement, la matière première, celle que l’artiste doit élire pour commencer l’Œuvre, est dénommée Miroir de l’Art.» (*Alchemically, the first matter, the one the artist must choose to begin the Work, is called the Mirror of the Art.*) [PARIS.5 ¶4]

> «Le miroir symbolise le début de l’ouvrage, l’arbre de vie en marque la fin, et la corne d’abondance le résultat.» (*The mirror symbolises the beginning of the work, the tree of life marks its end, and the horn of plenty its result.*) [PARIS.5 ¶3]

Identified in the text with: [First matter](#e094)

### Modhallan — *Modhallan* {#e273}

*term; Name kept; the text's own gloss of its root is 'dark and gloomy Sea' [PARIS.2 ¶9].*

Where the Arab authors place the fountain Holmat; the text says the root of the term means 'dark and gloomy Sea', which marks the elemental confusion the Sages attribute to their Chaos or first matter [PARIS.2 ¶9].

> «Ils placent la source fameuse dans le Modhallan, terme dont la racine signifie Mer obscure et ténébreuse, ce qui marque bien la confusion élémentaire que les Sages attribuent à leur Chaos ou matière première.» (*They place the famous spring in the Modhallan, a term whose root means dark and gloomy Sea, which well marks the elemental confusion the Sages attribute to their Chaos or first matter.*) [PARIS.2 ¶9]

### Monographie de l'Eglise Cathédrale d'Amiens — Monograph on the Cathedral Church of Amiens — *Monographie de l'Eglise Cathédrale d'Amiens* {#e445}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of G. Durand, Paris, A. Picard, 1901 [AMIENS n1], source of the readings of the Amiens reliefs that Fulcanelli disputes [AMIENS ¶4; AMIENS ¶10].

> «G. Durand, Monographie de l’Eglise Cathédrale d’Amiens. Paris, A. Picard, 1901.» (*G. Durand, Monograph on the Cathedral Church of Amiens. Paris, A. Picard, 1901.*) [AMIENS n1]

### Monsieur Legris — *Monsieur Legris* {#e184}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Popular name of a tall, narrow stone statue that stood on the Parvis of Notre-Dame, holding a book in one hand and a serpent in the other, part of a monumental fountain bearing a Latin distich on the Goddess who prepared the eternal waters [INTRO.9 ¶4–8]. The people also called it Vendeur de gris and Grand Jeûneur de Notre-Dame [INTRO.9 ¶9]. Fulcanelli calls Amédée de Ponthieu's explanation the best [INTRO.9 ¶10]: a sacred monolith which the ancients named Phœbigène, son of Apollo (glossed 'engendered of the sun or of gold'), which the people later called Maître Pierre, meaning master stone, stone of power (glossed as the cornerstone already discussed), and messire Legris when gris meant fire, particularly firedamp, will-o'-the-wisp [INTRO.9 ¶11; INTRO.9 n2; INTRO.9 n3]. Its features were variously read as Aesculapius, Mercury, the god Terminus, Archambaud, Guillaume de Paris, Jesus Christ or Saint Genevieve [INTRO.9 ¶12]. It was removed in 1748 when the Parvis was enlarged [INTRO.9 ¶13]. Fulcanelli later recalls having held back the esoteric sense of Saint Christopher when speaking of his statues and of this monolith [BOURGES.2 ¶9].

> «le peuple le nomma plus tard Maître Pierre, voulant dire Pierre maîtresse, pierre de pouvoir» (*the people later named it Master Peter, meaning master stone, stone of power*) [INTRO.9 ¶11]

> «il se nommait aussi messire Legris, alors que gris signifiait feu, et particulièrement feu grisou, feu follet» (*it was also named Messire Legris, at a time when gris meant fire, and particularly firedamp, will-o'-the-wisp*) [INTRO.9 ¶11]

Identified in the text with: [Cornerstone](#e161)

### Moon — *Lune* {#e257}

*term; The usual English term.*

In the symbolism of planetary metals the Virgin is the Moon, receiving the Sun's rays and keeping them secretly; she dispenses the passive substance that the solar spirit animates [PARIS.1 ¶8]. On the disk of Philosophy, sun and moon (the moon hammered away) are sulphur and mercury, parents of the stone according to Hermes [PARIS.3 ¶39]. The cow is consecrated to the moon; bull and cow, sun and moon, sulphur and mercury denote the contrary primitive natures before conjunction [PARIS.4 ¶21]. The Moon stands for silver [PARIS.6 ¶5]; in the Cosmopolite's scheme it is the lowest planet, corresponding to Saturn, the highest [PARIS.6 ¶7]. The Virgin stands on the lunar crescent, always in blue, the colour of the night star [AMIENS ¶39]. In Ceres's cult the Minister of the Altar represented the Moon [INTRO.8 ¶14].

> «Dans le symbolisme des métaux planétaires, c’est la Lune, qui reçoit les rayons du Soleil et les conserve secrètement dans son sein.» (*In the symbolism of the planetary metals, she is the Moon, which receives the rays of the Sun and keeps them secretly in her bosom.*) [PARIS.1 ¶8]

> «Le taureau et la vache, le soleil et la lune, le soufre et le mercure sont donc les hiéroglyphes de sens identique» (*The bull and the cow, the sun and the moon, sulphur and mercury are therefore hieroglyphs of identical meaning*) [PARIS.4 ¶21]

### Moras de Respour — *Moras de Respour* {#e372}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited for the name Mirror of Art given to the first matter: philosophers understand it so because through it one learned how the metals are composed in the veins of the earth [PARIS.5 ¶4]. The footnote gives his work as Rares Expériences sur l'Esprit minéral, Paris, Langlois et Barbin, 1668, under the name De Respour [PARIS.5 n1].

> «Communément entre les Philosophes, dit Moras de Respour, elle est entendue par le Miroir de l’Art, parce que c’est principalement par elle que l’on a appris la composition des métaux dans les veines de la terre» (*Commonly among the Philosophers, says Moras de Respour, it is meant by the Mirror of Art, because it is chiefly through it that one has learned the composition of the metals in the veins of the earth*) [PARIS.5 ¶4]

### Morien — *Morien* {#e077}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited master. Per an anonymous 18th-century manuscript Fulcanelli quotes, Morien says the corrupted matter must show some acidity and a smell of the sepulchre [PARIS.3 ¶10, PARIS.3 ¶11]. Flamel quotes 'the wise Morien': this stone that is not a stone is animate, with power to procreate [AMIENS ¶30]. Canseliet: with Raymond Lulle, he says the foul odour of the dark dissolution is followed by the sweetest perfume [PREF3 ¶22].

> «Morien dit : il faut qu’on y remarque quelque acidité, et qu’elle ait quelque odeur de sépulcre.» (*Morien says: one must notice some acidity in it, and it must have some smell of the sepulchre.*) [PARIS.3 ¶11]

> «Parquoy, dict le sage Morien, ceste pierre non pierre est animée, ayant vertu de procréer et engendrer.» (*Wherefore, says the wise Morien, this stone that is no stone is animate, having the power to procreate and engender.*) [AMIENS ¶30]

### Morning star — *étoile du matin* {#e021}

*term; Literal translation.*

Fulcanelli glosses Lucifer as 'who bears the light, — the morning star', the human form given to the initial matter of the Work in the devil figure Maistre Pierre du Coignet, symbol of the cornerstone [INTRO.4 ¶6]. The letter to Fulcanelli says whoever has saluted the morning star is fascinated by 'this false light' [PREF2 ¶13]. Canseliet, noting the apparent contradiction [PREF2 ¶19, PREF2 ¶20], explains: the star is first seen in the 'mirror of the art' or mercury before the chemical heaven; the celestial Virgin is called stella matutina, but that star is only an image reflected in the mirror of Wisdom, attesting the other [PREF2 ¶26]. Limojon de Saint-Didier tells seekers to steer their route 'by the sight of the étoile du nord' [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. See also Star (e024).

> «Or, cette figure, destinée à représenter la matière initiale de l’Œuvre, humanisée sous l’aspect de Lucifer (qui porte la lumière, — l’étoile du matin), était le symbole de notre pierre angulaire» (*Now this figure, meant to represent the initial matter of the Work, humanised in the guise of Lucifer (who bears the light, — the morning star), was the symbol of our cornerstone*) [INTRO.4 ¶6]
