> «L’on peut presque dire que celui qui a salué l’étoile du matin a perdu pour jamais l’usage de la vue et de la raison, car il est fasciné par cette fausse lumière et précipité dans l’abîme…» (*One may almost say that whoever has saluted the morning star has lost forever the use of sight and reason, for he is fascinated by this false light and hurled into the abyss…*) [PREF2 ¶13]

Identified in the text with: [Lucifer](#e112)

### Mullachius — *Mullachius* {#e207}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named as the last editor of Chalcidius, and the source for the statement that Chalcidius professed the worship of the Greek, Roman and foreign gods [INTRO.7 ¶16].

> «le philosophe Chalcidius, qui, comme le dit Mullachius, le dernier de ses éditeurs, professait qu’il fallait adorer les dieux de la Grèce, les dieux de Rome et les Dieux étrangers» (*the philosopher Chalcidius, who, as Mullachius, the last of his editors, says, professed that one should worship the gods of Greece, the gods of Rome and foreign Gods*) [INTRO.7 ¶16]

### Multiplication — *multiplication* {#e424}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

Cambriel says the multiplication of the white or red Sulphur is not shown on the Saint Anne pillar; Fulcanelli disagrees: multiplication is done only with mercury, the patient in the Work, by successive cookings or fixations, so its sign should be sought on the dragon, image of mercury [PARIS.7 ¶23]. The solar circles covering the dragon mean it can transmute in quantity, a power gained by later cookings with the Sulphur or philosophical Gold, which make up the multiplications [PARIS.7 ¶27]. The Chevalier Inconnu speaks of a third multiplication by adding the soul of the world [PARIS.3 ¶8].

> «La multiplication, en effet, ne peut se réaliser qu’à l’aide du mercure, qui joue le rôle de patient dans l’Œuvre, et par coctions ou fixations successives.» (*Multiplication, indeed, can only be achieved with the help of mercury, which plays the part of patient in the Work, and by successive cookings or fixations.*) [PARIS.7 ¶23]

### Musée de Cluny — *Musée de Cluny* {#e079}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Where the original St Marcel pillar of Notre-Dame was kept, near the stair to the frigidarium of the Thermes de Julien [PARIS.7 ¶16]. A later note says the itinerary is no longer valid: for about six years the pillar has been back in Notre-Dame's north tower, with most of the dragon amputated [PARIS.7 n9]. Canseliet: Fulcanelli studied the statue there [PREF3 ¶23]; it now sits in the north tower [PREF3 ¶44]; the museum did not exist in the 16th century and has held it for about a century [PREF3 ¶48].

> «Traversez la Seine, entrez au musée de Cluny et vous aurez la satisfaction de l’y trouver, auprès de l’escalier d’accès au frigidarium des Thermes de Julien.» (*Cross the Seine, enter the Cluny museum and you will have the satisfaction of finding it there, near the stair leading to the frigidarium of the Baths of Julian.*) [PARIS.7 ¶16]

### Mutus Liber — The Silent Book — *Mutus Liber* {#e087}

*work; Latin title kept; English is our literal translation.*

Fulcanelli calls the Gothic temple itself a 'mutus Liber', of which the alchemy figure is the title-page [PARIS.1 ¶4]. The gesture of silence of the figures of the Mutus Liber (and of Harpocrates) is not on the St Marcel statue [PARIS.7 ¶32]. Its fishermen in a boat try to catch a dolphin with net and hook [BOURGES.2 ¶15].

> «Tel est le titre du chapitre de ce mutus Liber qu’est le temple gothique ; le frontispice de cette Bible occulte aux massifs feuillets de pierre» (*Such is the chapter heading of this mutus Liber that is the Gothic temple; the frontispiece of this occult Bible with massive leaves of stone*) [PARIS.1 ¶4]

> «C’est, d’ailleurs, un dauphin que les pêcheurs en barque du Mutus Liber cherchent à capturer au filet et à l’hameçon.» (*It is, moreover, a dolphin that the fishermen in their boat in the Mutus Liber try to catch with net and hook.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶15]

### Mysterious Fountain — *Fontaine mystérieuse* {#e263}

*term; Literal translation.*

A bas-relief on the left buttress of the central portal shows the alchemist discovering the Mysterious Fountain described by the Trévisan: a stream of living water bubbling from an old hollow oak, its dissolving virtue and volatile essence attested by a bird (pl. IV) [PARIS.2 ¶4–5]. It is the powerful dissolvent able to penetrate all metals, gold above all, and to accomplish the whole work with the dissolved body [PARIS.2 ¶6]. Mythology calls it Libethra; the Arab authors Holmat [PARIS.2 ¶7; PARIS.2 ¶9]. Abraham the Jew's third figure shows it at the foot of a hollow oak [PARIS.2 ¶17–18]. The tradition of the Fountain of Life or Youth is materialized in church wells [PARIS.2 ¶21]. A quoted text calls it the Fountain of Trévisan, bath of King and Queen [PARIS.4 ¶14]. Fulcanelli identifies the source at the foot of the sacred oak with what the philosophers named Mercury [AMIENS ¶15]; the mercury is called Fountain of Youth [BOURGES.2 ¶14].

> «De quelle nature est ce puissant dissolvant capable de pénétrer tous les métaux, — l’or en particulier, — et d’accomplir, avec l’aide du corps dissous, le grand ouvrage en entier ?» (*Of what nature is this powerful dissolvent, able to penetrate all metals, gold in particular, and to accomplish, with the help of the dissolved body, the whole great work?*) [PARIS.2 ¶6]

> «C’est là notre source fameuse dont l’onde claire coule à la base de l’arbre sacré, si vénéré des Druides, et que les anciens philosophes ont nommée Mercure» (*That is our famous spring whose clear water flows at the foot of the sacred tree so venerated by the Druids, and which the old philosophers named Mercury*) [AMIENS ¶15]

### Mystic almond — *Amande mystique* {#e534}

*term; Literal translation.*

On Romanesque tympana Jesus sits, left hand on a book, right hand blessing, separated from the four animals by the ellipse called the mystic almond [HENDAYE ¶12]; this is the medieval form of the four phases of the cycle [HENDAYE ¶12].

> «Jésus y est exposé assis, la main gauche appuyée sur un livre, la droite levée dans le geste de bénédiction, et séparé des quatre animaux qui lui font cortège par l’ellipse dite Amande mystique.» (*There Jesus is shown seated, his left hand resting on a book, the right raised in blessing, and separated from the four animals escorting him by the ellipse called the mystic almond.*) [HENDAYE ¶12]

## N

### Natures — *natures* {#e183}

*term; Literal translation of the French plural.*

The two contrary substances the alchemist works and joins. On the cubic socle of the Saint-Marcel pillar two raised bezants are the metallic matters or natures, subject and dissolvent, with which the Work must begin [PARIS.7 ¶11, PARIS.7 ¶18]; the pair of natures, once united, yields the Saturn of the Sages, which the text calls an anagram of natures [PARIS.7 ¶22]. The centre of the labyrinth is where the hard combat of the two natures takes place [INTRO.5 ¶3]. The Sages figured the two natures by the Eagle (aerial, volatile force) and the Lion (earthly, fixed force), which fight until they become one body, the animated Mercury [PARIS.4 ¶11]. At Notre-Dame they are shown as two quarrelling children, one dropping a pot, the other a stone [PARIS.5 ¶13]. Bull and cow, sun and moon, sulphur and mercury all designate the contrary primitive natures before their conjunction [PARIS.4 ¶21]. The two wheels of Amiens may also be read as the two natures in the conversion, a thesis the text says Pernety adopts [AMIENS ¶6].

> «ce sont les matières ou natures métalliques, — sujet et dissolvant, — avec lesquelles on doit commencer l’Œuvre» (*these are the metallic matters or natures, subject and dissolvent, with which the Work must be begun*) [PARIS.7 ¶18]

> «le duo des natures que l’alchimiste travaille et dont la réunion fournit le Saturne des Sages, dénomination anagrammatique de natures» (*the pair of natures the alchemist works, whose union yields the Saturn of the Sages, an anagrammatic name for natures*) [PARIS.7 ¶22]

Identified in the text with: [Saturn](#e044)

### Naxagoras — *Naxagoras* {#e054}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Canseliet: with Blaise de Vigenère, he wrote at length on the value of a long preliminary coction of lead [PREF2 ¶43].

> «Blaise de Vigenère et Naxagoras, par exemple, se sont étendus sur l’opportunité d’une longue coction préalable.» (*Blaise de Vigenère and Naxagoras, for instance, dwelt on the advisability of a long preliminary coction.*) [PREF2 ¶43]

### Nicolas Flamel — *Nicolas Flamel* {#e238}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Adept to whom the book of the Figures of Abraham the Jew is said to have belonged, displayed in his scribe's shop; its third figure shows the hollow oak and the fountain [PARIS.2 ¶16–17]; he says 'Note this oak' [PARIS.2 n2]. Abraham's serpent on a golden rod is in that book [PARIS.3 ¶17]. He figured the combat of the two natures as eagle and lion [PARIS.5 ¶12] and favoured the allegory of the Massacre of the Innocents [PARIS.6 ¶22]. He covered his buildings with emblems: the portal of Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie (1389), houses for the poor (1407, 1410), the Saint-Gervais chapel, arcades at the Innocents' charnel (1389, 1407) [PARIS.8 ¶2]. Quoted on the hidden, despised stone [AMIENS ¶30]; his parabolic pilgrimage to 'Monsieur Jacques de Galice' [BOURGES.1 ¶5]. Called 'the charitable Flamel' [BOURGES.2 ¶38].

> «Flamel se plaisait à revêtir d’emblèmes et d’hiéroglyphes les constructions qu’il élevait de tous côtés.» (*Flamel liked to cover with emblems and hieroglyphs the buildings he raised on every side.*) [PARIS.8 ¶2]

> «Il y a une pierre occulte, absconsée et ensevelie au plus profond d’une fontaine, laquelle est vile, abjecte et nullement prisée» (*There is a hidden stone, concealed and buried in the depth of a fountain, which is vile, abject and not at all prized*) [AMIENS ¶30]

### Nicolas Rollin — *Nicolas Rollin* {#e470}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Chancellor of Philippe le Bon, founder of the hospital of Beaune, on whose tiled floor his motto was painted in 1477 [AMIENS ¶40]. The motto, given as a rebus ('Seule'), showed his science by the characteristic sign of the Work, the single star [AMIENS ¶40].

> «Cette devise, présentée à la manière d’un rébus, — Seule ✮, — manifestait la science de son possesseur par le *signe* caractéristique de l'Œuvre, l’unique, la *seule étoile*.» (*This motto, presented in the manner of a rebus — Seule ('alone') — showed its owner's science by the characteristic sign of the Work, the unique, the single star.*) [AMIENS ¶40]

### Nicolas Valois — *Nicolas Valois* {#e063}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited author (works with Nicolas Grosparmy, Arsenal manuscript) [PARIS.1 n1, AMIENS n8]. On the ladder held by Alchemy at Notre-Dame: patience is the ladder of the Philosophers, humility the door of their garden [PARIS.1 ¶3]. On the subject: a stone that is a stone and not a stone, mineral, vegetable and animal, found everywhere [AMIENS ¶29]. His epigraph opens the 1957 preface: the first who transmuted had no book but followed nature [PREF2 ¶47, PREF2 ¶48].

> «« La patience est l’eschelle des Philosophes, nous dit Valois, et l’humilité est la porte de leur jardin ; car quiconque persévérera sans orgueil et sans envie, Dieu lui fera miséricorde. »» (*'Patience is the ladder of the Philosophers,' Valois tells us, 'and humility is the door of their garden; for whoever perseveres without pride and without envy, God will show him mercy.'*) [PARIS.1 ¶3]

> «Il est une pierre de grande vertu, dit à son tour Nicolas Valois et est dite pierre et n’est pas pierre, et est minérale, végétale et animale, qui est trouvée en tous lieux et tout temps, et chez toutes personnes.» (*There is a stone of great virtue, says Nicolas Valois in turn, and it is called stone and is not stone, and is mineral, vegetable and animal, which is found in all places and at all times, and with all persons.*) [AMIENS ¶29]

### Nostoc — *Nostoc* {#e469}

*term; The plant name kept; its meaning 'born of night' is the text's own etymology (AMIENS ¶36).*

A gelatinous green cryptogam found in the countryside, swollen with night dew in spring mornings and vanishing in the sun; hence also called trémelles [AMIENS ¶35]. Its sudden appearance, absorption of water, green colour and soft consistency let the Philosophers take it as hieroglyph of their matter; the mass absorbing celestial dew in an Amiens quatrefoil is such a heap, symbol of the Sages' mineral Magnesia [AMIENS ¶34–35]. Its other names (Archée céleste, Crachat de Lune, Beurre de terre, Graisse de rosée, Vitriol végétal, Flos coeli) designated only their mineral principle [AMIENS ¶35]; taken literally by ignorant puffers it discredited alchemy [AMIENS ¶35]. The text derives the word from Greek νυξ, Latin nox: a thing born at night, needing night to develop and workable only at night [AMIENS ¶36]; the following paragraphs argue that generation requires the total absence of sunlight [AMIENS ¶37–39].

> «Ce mot vient du grec νυξ, νυχτος, correspondant au latin nox, noctis, nuit. C’est donc une chose qui naît la nuit, a besoin de la nuit pour se développer et ne peut se travailler que la nuit.» (*This word comes from the Greek νυξ, νυχτος, corresponding to the Latin nox, noctis, night. It is therefore a thing that is born at night, needs the night to develop, and can be worked only at night.*) [AMIENS ¶36]

> «Tous ces caractères combinés, — apparition soudaine, absorption d’eau et gonflement, coloration verte, consistance molle et gluante, — ont permis aux Philosophes de prendre cette algue comme type hiéroglyphique de leur matière.» (*All these features combined — sudden appearance, absorption of water and swelling, green colour, soft and sticky consistency — allowed the Philosophers to take this alga as the hieroglyphic type of their matter.*) [AMIENS ¶35]

### Nostre-Dame de dessoubs terre — *Nostre-Dame de dessoubs terre* {#e230}

*place; Name as spelled in the text (old French spelling preserved).*

A statuette of the Virgin in an underground gallery of the Paris Observatory, placed there in 1671 and invoked by this name in verses carved at its feet, as reported by Camille Flammarion [INTRO.8 ¶11]. Fulcanelli says this little-known Paris Virgin personifies in the capital the mysterious subject of Hermes and seems a replica of the Chartres Virgin, 'la benoiste Dame souterraine' [INTRO.8 ¶11].

> «Cette Vierge parisienne peu connue, qui personnifie dans la capitale le mystérieux sujet d’Hermès, paraît être une réplique de celle de Chartres, la benoiste Dame souterraine.» (*This little-known Parisian Virgin, who personifies in the capital the mysterious subject of Hermes, appears to be a replica of the Chartres one, the blessed Lady beneath the earth.*) [INTRO.8 ¶11]

### Notre-Dame d'Amiens — *Notre-Dame d’Amiens* {#e167}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Its central porch, the porche du Sauveur, nearly reproduces the hermetic motifs of the Paris portal and their order, with shields instead of discs; Guillaume le Parisien's work influenced it [AMIENS ¶1]. Built by Robert de Luzarches, Thomas and Renault de Cormont, and well preserved [AMIENS ¶2]. Its labyrinth had a gold bar and half-circle figuring sunrise, later copper, then lost [INTRO.5 ¶1]; 'Ariadne, the mystic spider, escaped from Amiens' left only the trace of her web [INTRO.5 ¶6]. It keeps a bas-relief Saint Christopher [INTRO.9 ¶14].

> «Le fait singulier, et qu’il convient de noter, c’est que le porche central de Notre-Dame d’Amiens, — porche du Sauveur, — est la reproduction à peu près fidèle, non seulement des motifs qui ornent le portail de Paris» (*The singular fact, worth noting, is that the central porch of Notre-Dame d'Amiens — porch of the Saviour — is an almost faithful reproduction not only of the motifs that adorn the Paris portal*) [AMIENS ¶1]

> «Ariane, l’araignée mystique, échappée d’Amiens, a seulement laissé sur le pavé du chœur la trace de sa toile…» (*Ariadne, the mystic spider, escaped from Amiens, has left only the trace of her web on the pavement of the choir…*) [INTRO.5 ¶6]

### Notre-Dame de l'Epine — *Notre-Dame de l'Epine* {#e287}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

A Gothic basilica in the Marne which, the text says, still has inside it a miraculous well called the Well of the Holy Virgin, an example of the sacred wells [PARIS.2 ¶21].

> «Il existe encore aujourd’hui à l’intérieur de la basilique ogivale Notre-Dame de l’Epine (Marne), un puits miraculeux, dit Puits de la Sainte-Vierge» (*There still exists today inside the Gothic basilica of Notre-Dame de l'Epine (Marne) a miraculous well, called the Well of the Holy Virgin*) [PARIS.2 ¶21]

### Notre-Dame de Limoux — *Notre-Dame de Limoux* {#e288}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Church in the Aude with a well in the middle of the choir whose water, it is said, heals all illnesses [PARIS.2 ¶21]. It bears the inscription 'Omnis qui bibit hanc aquam, si fidem addit, savus erit' [PARIS.2 ¶22], which the book renders: whoever drinks this water, if he adds faith, will be well [PARIS.2 ¶23].

> «Quiconque boit cette eau, s’il y joint la foi, sera bien portant.» (*Whoever drinks this water, if he joins faith to it, will be healthy. (the book's gloss of the Latin)*) [PARIS.2 ¶23]

### Notre-Dame de Paris — *Notre-Dame de Paris* {#e082}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

The type chosen for the hermetic study of the cathedral [INTRO.9 ¶1]. A 'philosophical church', one of the most perfect specimens; Victor Hugo called it the most satisfying summary of the hermetic science [INTRO.2 ¶4]. Fourteenth-century alchemists met there weekly on Saturn's day [INTRO.2 ¶5]. Dedicated to the Virgin-Mother [PARIS.1 ¶1]; it keeps its alchemist on the gallery [PARIS.1 ¶12, PARIS.1 ¶13]; its rose window keeps the original colours of the porch medallions, thanks to Guillaume de Paris [PARIS.5 ¶16, PARIS.5 ¶17]. Its porches are read in PARIS.1–PARIS.7. Canseliet: setting of the St Marcel statue controversy [PREF3 ¶23].

> «A cet égard, Notre-Dame de Paris, église philosophale, est sans contredit l’un des plus parfaits spécimens, et, comme l’a dit Victor Hugo, « l’abrégé le plus satisfaisant de la science hermétique» (*In this respect Notre-Dame de Paris, a philosophical church, is beyond question one of the most perfect specimens, and, as Victor Hugo said, 'the most satisfying summary of the hermetic science*) [INTRO.2 ¶4]

> «Notre-Dame de Paris, en effet, garde son alchimiste.» (*Notre-Dame de Paris, indeed, keeps its alchemist.*) [PARIS.1 ¶12]

### Notre-Dame de Rocamadour — *Notre-Dame de Rocamadour* {#e228}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Miraculous madonna and goal of a famous pilgrimage, already visited in 1166, traced by tradition to Zacchaeus; a wooden statuette blackened by time, clad in a robe of silver strips, over the altar of a chapel built in 1479; a quoted source links it to the hermit Saint Amateur or Amadour [INTRO.8 ¶8]. At Rocamadour also stand a giant Saint Christopher, an old iron-bound coffer and a sword fragment said to be from Durandal [INTRO.9 ¶16].

> «C’est une statuette de bois, noircie par le temps, enveloppée dans une robe de lamelles d’argent qui en consolide les débris vermoulus.» (*It is a wooden statuette, blackened by time, wrapped in a robe of silver strips that holds together its worm-eaten remains.*) [INTRO.8 ¶8]

### Notre-Dame de Strasbourg — *Notre-Dame de Strasbourg* {#e116}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

In its nave, per Witkowski, a capital shows a satirical procession of a pig with a holy-water stoup, asses in priestly robes, monkeys and a fox in a shrine: the Procession of the Fox or Feast of the Ass [INTRO.1 ¶14].

> «le bas-relief d’un des chapiteaux des grands piliers reproduit une procession satirique où l’on distingue un pourceau, porteur d’un bénitier, suivi d’ânes revêtus d’habits sacerdotaux et de singes munis de divers attributs de la religion» (*the bas-relief of one of the capitals of the great pillars shows a satirical procession in which one makes out a pig carrying a holy-water stoup, followed by asses in priestly vestments and monkeys bearing various attributes of religion*) [INTRO.1 ¶14]

### Notre-Dame du Puy — *Notre-Dame du Puy* {#e226}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Black Virgin whose limbs are not visible; her robe, fastened at the neck and widening without a fold to the foot, gives her the figure of a triangle; it is decorated with vine stocks and ears of wheat, allegories of the eucharistic bread and wine, and at the level of the navel shows the Child's head, crowned as richly as the mother's [INTRO.8 ¶6].

> «elle affecte la figure d’un triangle, par sa robe qui la ceint au col et s’évase sans un pli jusqu’au pied.» (*she takes the figure of a triangle, through her robe which girds her at the neck and flares without a fold down to the foot.*) [INTRO.8 ¶6]

### Notre-Dame-de-Confession — *Notre-Dame-de-Confession* {#e227}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Celebrated Black Virgin of the Saint-Victor crypts at Marseille, a fine specimen of ancient statuary, holding a sceptre in the right hand and crowned with a triple-fleuron crown (pl. I) [INTRO.8 ¶7].

> «Cette figure, pleine de noblesse, tient un sceptre de la main droite et a le front ceint d’une couronne à triple fleuron» (*This figure, full of nobility, holds a sceptre in her right hand, and her brow is girt with a crown of three fleurons*) [INTRO.8 ¶7]

### Notre-Dame-sous-Terre — *Notre-Dame-sous-Terre* {#e225}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Black Virgin in the crypt of Chartres, seated on a throne whose socle bears Virgini parituræ [INTRO.8 ¶5]. With her, Chartres passes for the oldest pilgrimage; originally an antique statuette of Isis 'carved before Jesus Christ', later replaced by a wooden statue burnt in 1793; the present image dates only from the end of the 18th century [INTRO.8 ¶5]. The Paris Virgin of the Observatory seems a replica of her, 'la benoiste Dame souterraine' [INTRO.8 ¶11].

> «l’une désignée sous le vocable expressif de Notre-Dame-sous-Terre, dans la crypte, est assise sur un trône dont le socle porte l’inscription déjà relevée : Virgini parituræ» (*one, designated by the expressive title of Our Lady under the Earth, in the crypt, sits on a throne whose socle bears the inscription already noted: Virgini parituræ*) [INTRO.8 ¶5]

### Nouvelle Lumière chymique — New Chemical Light — *Nouvelle Lumière chymique* {#e374}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work given in a footnote under the title Cosmopolite ou Nouvelle Lumière chymique [BOURGES.2 n5]. Its parts are cited: the Traité du Soufre (Paris, d'Houry, 1649) for the mirror in which the whole world is seen [PARIS.5 n2; PARIS.5 ¶4]; the Traité du Mercure (Paris, Jean d'Houry, 1649) for the order of the planets and their mutations [PARIS.6 n2; PARIS.6 ¶7]; and the Traité du Sel for the weights of water and earth [PARIS.5 ¶6] and for the fish swimming in the philosophical sea (Paris, J. D'Houry, 1669) [BOURGES.2 n5; BOURGES.2 ¶14].

> «Cosmopolite ou Nouvelle Lumière chymique. Traité du Sel, p. 76. Paris, J. D’Houry, 1669.» (*Cosmopolite or New Chemical Light. Treatise on Salt, p. 76. Paris, J. D'Houry, 1669.*) [BOURGES.2 n5]

### Noël du Fail — *Noël du Fail* {#e123}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Quoted: Notre-Dame de Paris was the great meeting place of such 'academicians', the alchemists [INTRO.2 ¶5]; source: Propos rustiques…, ch. X [INTRO.2 n1].

> «et Noël du Fail dit que « le grand rendez-vous de tels académiques estoit à Nostre-Dame de Paris »» (*and Noël du Fail says that 'the great meeting place of such academicians was at Notre-Dame de Paris'*) [INTRO.2 ¶5]

## O

### O crux ave spes unica — Hail, O Cross, our only hope — *O crux ave spes unica* {#e527}

*work; Inscription as restored in the text; English ours.*

Carved on the Hendaye cross as OCRUXAVES / PESUNICA [HENDAYE ¶4–6]. Read as written, 'pes' (foot) with feminine 'unica' breaks grammar; the careful cutting excludes a slip, so the apparent error is a deliberate sign [HENDAYE ¶7]. The S, displaced on purpose, shows that the line must be read in the language of the gods or birds, by the rules of 'Diplomatique': read the Latin in French, with interchangeable vowels [HENDAYE ¶9]. The result: 'Il est écrit que la vie se réfugie en un seul espace' — there is a land where death will not reach man at the time of the double cataclysm [HENDAYE ¶9]. The text gives the result, not the step-by-step reading.

> «Ainsi, nous recevons ce singulier avertissement : Il est écrit que la vie se réfugie en un seul espace, et nous apprenons qu’il existe une contrée où la mort n’atteindra point l’homme, à l’époque terrible du double cataclysme.» (*Thus we receive this singular warning: It is written that life takes refuge in a single space, and we learn that there is a land where death will not reach man, at the terrible time of the double cataclysm.*) [HENDAYE ¶9]

### Oak — *chêne* {#e265}

*term; Literal translation.*

The water of the Mysterious Fountain springs from an old hollow oak [PARIS.2 ¶5]; Flamel says 'Note this oak' [PARIS.2 n2]; Abraham's third figure shows a fountain at the foot of a hollow oak [PARIS.2 ¶17]. An oak branch on a Notre-Dame escutcheon reveals the material of the hermetic vessel [PARIS.4 ¶13]; a quoted text reads the hollow oak as the oaken barrel in which the water is corrupted, and the vessel as of old oak [PARIS.4 ¶14]. At Amiens the oak is the emblem of the subject from which the volatile cock is drawn [AMIENS ¶14–15]. The old authors used the oak, by an à-peu-près, for the common name of the initial subject as found in the mine [BOURGES.2 ¶21]. Its gall leads via galla, Gallia, gallus to the cock, attribute of Mercury, and its kermès to Hermès [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. The old hermetic oak serves as mother to the secret mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶23]; a crown of oak leaves is the graphic sign of the subject's common name [BOURGES.2 ¶27].

> «vous pourrez dire, sans offenser la vérité, que le vieux chêne hermétique sert de mère au mercure secret.» (*you may say, without offending truth, that the old hermetic oak serves as mother to the secret mercury.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶23]

> «Or, si nous rapprochons trois mots de la même famille latine : galla, Gallia, gallus, nous obtenons galle, Gaule, coq.» (*Now if we bring together three words of the same Latin family, galla, Gallia, gallus, we get gall, Gaul, cock.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶22]

### Orient (East) — *Orient* {#e180}

*term; Literal translation.*

Through Greek αρυω, rising of a star from the sea, αρυαν or ariane is the Orient, the rising sun, and also the magnet [INTRO.5 ¶5]. Churches are oriented so that people entering from the West walk toward the side where the sun rises, the Orient, leaving darkness for light [INTRO.6 ¶1].

> «marchent droit au sanctuaire, la face portée du côté où le soleil se lève, vers l’Orient, la Palestine, berceau du christianisme. Ils quittent les ténèbres et vont vers la lumière.» (*walk straight to the sanctuary, facing the side where the sun rises, toward the Orient, Palestine, cradle of Christianity. They leave darkness and go toward the light.*) [INTRO.6 ¶1]

### Original du Désir désiré — The Original of the Desired Desire — *Original du Désir désiré* {#e467}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of Nicolas Flamel, also titled Thrésor de Philosophie (Paris, Hulpeau, 1629, p. 144) [AMIENS n9]; quoted among descriptions of the subject or first matter: a hidden, vile, despised stone in the depth of a fountain, given every name [AMIENS ¶27, ¶30].

> «Il y a une pierre occulte, absconsée et ensevelie au plus profond d’une fontaine, laquelle est vile, abjecte et nullement prisée ; et si est couverte de fiens et excrémens» (*There is an occult stone, hidden and buried in the depth of a fountain, which is vile, abject and not at all prized; and it is covered with dung and excrement*) [AMIENS ¶30]

### Osiris — *Osiris* {#e329}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

In Egyptian temples, a priest whispered to the candidate about to undergo the initiatory trials: 'Remember that Osiris is a black god!' [PARIS.3 ¶27]. Portal, quoted by Dujols, says the black Osiris at the beginning of the funerary Ritual represents the soul's passage from night to day, from death to life [PARIS.3 ¶28]. Horus fighting Typhon in the Osirian myth is listed among the combats figuring the making of the dissolvent [BOURGES.1 ¶9].

> «« Souviens-toi qu’Osiris est un dieu noir ! »» (*'Remember that Osiris is a black god!'*) [PARIS.3 ¶27]

### Our Lady — *Notre-Dame* {#e250}

*term; The usual English rendering; the text derives the name from Mater, Matrone, Madone [PARIS.1 ¶1].*

Metropolitan cathedrals, dedicated to the Virgin Mary or Virgin-Mother, are popularly called Notre-Dame in France and Matrices in Sicily; they are temples to the Mother (Latin mater), the Matrone, a word corrupted into Madone (ma donna), my Lady, and by extension Our Lady [PARIS.1 ¶1]. The Virgin-Mother, stripped of her symbolic veil, personifies the primitive substance [PARIS.1 ¶5].

> «Ce sont donc bien des temples dédiés à la Mère (lat. mater, matris), à la Matrone dans le sens primitif, mot qui, par corruption, est devenu la Madone (lat. ma donna), ma Dame, et, par extension, Notre-Dame.» (*They are thus indeed temples dedicated to the Mother (Lat. mater, matris), to the Matron in the primitive sense, a word that by corruption became the Madonna (Lat. ma donna), my Lady, and by extension Our Lady.*) [PARIS.1 ¶1]

## P

### P. H. Pfau — *P. H. Pfau* {#e494}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Maker in 1702 of the alchemical stove whose paintings include an angler and a fine fish [BOURGES.2 ¶14]; the stove is kept at the Winterthur museum [BOURGES.2 n6].

> «Parmi les peintures du poêle alchimique exécuté en 1702 par P. H. Pfau, on voit un pêcheur à la ligne sortant de l’eau et un beau poisson.» (*Among the paintings of the alchemical stove made in 1702 by P. H. Pfau, one sees an angler coming out of the water and a fine fish.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

### Palace — *Palais* {#e386}

*term; Literal translation.*

On the seventh lower medallion of Paris an old man, who is our Mercury, the secret agent, is about to cross the threshold of the mysterious Palace after tearing away its veil [PARIS.5 ¶1, PARIS.5 ¶11]. The text says what the Palace stands for: living or philosophical gold, vile gold despised by the ignorant and hidden under rags [PARIS.5 ¶11]. Entering it is the first step of practice, the discovery of the agent that reincrudates the fixed body; it is Philalethes' Entrance to the King's closed Palace [PARIS.5 ¶11]. The interior of the Palace was once coloured purple, the old man green, a variant of the Green and Red Lions [PARIS.5 ¶11]. At Amiens a fortified building may be the mysterious closed Palace of the king of the Art spoken of by Basile Valentin and Philalethes [AMIENS ¶17].

> «Quant au Palais, il représente l’or vif, ou philosophique, or vil, méprisé de l’ignorant, et caché sous des haillons qui le dérobent aux yeux» (*As for the Palace, it represents living or philosophical gold, vile gold, despised by the ignorant, and hidden under rags that conceal it from the eyes*) [PARIS.5 ¶11]

### Palais Jacques-Cœur — *palais Jacques-Cœur* {#e471}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

One of the two jewels of Bourges with the Hôtel Lallemant [BOURGES.1 ¶1]; once a true museum of hermetic emblems, its interior ruined by vandalism, only the façade kept in its original state [BOURGES.1 ¶2]. Built in the 15th century by Jacques Cœur [BOURGES.1 ¶3]; the scallop and heart recur on its decoration [BOURGES.1 ¶5]; the restored chapel is of little interest, and its most curious piece is the Tristan and Yseult corbel in the 'Trésor' room [BOURGES.1 ¶6–7].

> «Du premier, qui fut jadis un véritable musée d’emblèmes hermétiques, nous dirons peu de choses.» (*Of the first, which was once a true museum of hermetic emblems, we shall say little.*) [BOURGES.1 ¶2]

### Paracelsus — *Paracelse* {#e334}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; Paracelsus is the usual English form.*

Quoted from his Livre des Images on the colours of the Work: black is the root of the other colours; white follows black, yellow white, red yellow; matter reaching the fourth colour by reverberation is the tincture of things of its kind [PARIS.3 ¶30]. Huginus à Barma says the old masters, Geber, Lulle, Paracelsus, each had his own process [PARIS.7 ¶3].

> «Or, le noir est la racine et l’origine des autres couleurs» (*Now, black is the root and origin of the other colours*) [PARIS.3 ¶30]

### Paraphrase de la Première Epître de saint Pierre — Paraphrase of the First Epistle of Saint Peter — *Paraphrase de la Première Epître de saint Pierre* {#e164}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with a literal English rendering.*

Work by Amyraut (ch. II, v. 7), Saumur, Jean Lesnier, 1646, p. 27, source of the quotation on the corner stone [INTRO.4 n2, INTRO.4 ¶6].

> «M. Amyraut, Paraphrase de la Première Epître de saint Pierre (ch. II, v. 7). Saumur, Jean Lesnier, 1646, p. 27.» (*M. Amyraut, Paraphrase of the First Epistle of Saint Peter (ch. II, v. 7). Saumur, Jean Lesnier, 1646, p. 27.*) [INTRO.4 n2]

### Pegasus — *Pégase* {#e368}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

The text identifies the horse of the first lower medallion of Notre-Dame with the winged Pegasus of the fable, giving the root πηγη, 'spring' [PARIS.5 ¶1, PARIS.5 ¶2]. Like it, Pegasus throws his riders, Perseus or Bellerophon, carries Perseus through the air to the Hesperides, and with a kick makes the Hippocrene spring gush on Mount Helicon [PARIS.5 ¶2]. Perseus, mounted on Pegasus and killing the monster guarding Andromeda, is among the fables of the combat of the chemical bodies that yield the secret solvent [BOURGES.1 ¶9].

> «Le coursier de Notre-Dame est le même que le Pégase ailé de la fable (racine πηγη, source).» (*The steed of Notre-Dame is the same as the winged Pegasus of the fable (root πηγη, spring).*) [PARIS.5 ¶2]

### Pernety — *Pernety* {#e322}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Quoted: the Adepts say they draw their steel from the belly of Aries and also call this steel their magnet [PARIS.3 ¶23]. In his Dictionnaire Mytho-Hermétique he does not cite the term 'Miroir de l'Art', either not knowing it or omitting it on purpose [PARIS.5 ¶4]. He refuses to believe in two ways (dry and humid) [PARIS.7 ¶3]. He adopts the thesis that the two wheels of Amiens indicate the two natures in the conversion [AMIENS ¶6].

> «Les Adeptes disent qu’ils tirent leur acier du ventre d’Ariès, et ils appellent aussi cet acier leur aimant.» (*The Adepts say they draw their steel from the belly of Aries, and they also call this steel their magnet.*) [PARIS.3 ¶23]

### Perseus — *Persée* {#e369}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Perseus is named with Bellerophon among the riders Pegasus throws, and Pegasus carries him through the air to the Hesperides [PARIS.5 ¶2]. Perseus cutting off the Gorgon's head, and Perseus on Pegasus killing the monster that guarded Andromeda, belong to the many fables of the single combat of the chemical bodies whose combination gives the secret solvent; saint Michael, saint George and saint Marcel slaying the Dragon are called Christian replicas of him [BOURGES.1 ¶9].

> «saint Michel, saint Georges, saint Marcel terrassant le Dragon, répliques chrétiennes de Persée, tuant le monstre gardien d’Andromède, monté sur son cheval Pégase» (*saint Michael, saint George, saint Marcel slaying the Dragon, Christian replicas of Perseus killing the monster that guarded Andromeda, mounted on his horse Pegasus*) [BOURGES.1 ¶9]

### Phidias — *Phidias* {#e236}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited for his representation of Cybele seated on a throne between two lions, wearing a mural crown from which a veil descends [INTRO.8 ¶15].

> «Phidias représente la déesse assise sur un trône entre deux lions, ayant sur la tête une couronne murale de laquelle descend un voile.» (*Phidias represents the goddess seated on a throne between two lions, with a mural crown on her head from which a veil descends.*) [INTRO.8 ¶15]

### Philalethes — *Philalèthe* {#e040}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; English form is the usual one.*

Adept often cited. The putrefaction must show 'fish eyes' [PARIS.3 ¶11]; whoever knows the Regimen will be honoured by princes [PARIS.3 ¶35]; the preparation of the flying Eagles is the first degree; seven to nine eagles are needed; the philosophical Mercury is the bird of Hermes, goose, swan or pheasant [PARIS.4 ¶3, PARIS.4 ¶4]. In the Introitus, his way takes only a week [PARIS.7 ¶6]. His Rules: tempered heat; one must boil, else it is digestion, not cooking [AMIENS ¶8, AMIENS ¶9]. He describes the vessel by the Python fable [PARIS.4 ¶14]; read the Philosophers 'with a grain of salt' [PARIS.5 ¶7]. Canseliet: in his Entrée ouverte he dwelt most on practice and signals the hermetic star [PREF2 ¶33].

> «« Celui qui connoîtra le Régime, écrit Philalèthe, sera honoré des princes et des grands de la terre. »» (*'Whoever knows the Regimen,' writes Philalethes, 'will be honoured by the princes and great ones of the earth.'*) [PARIS.3 ¶35]

> «Car je vous assure que si nous ne faisons bouillir, nous ne pouvons jamais nommer notre ouvrage une cuisson, mais une digestion.» (*For I assure you that if we do not make it boil, we can never call our work a cooking, but a digestion.*) [AMIENS ¶9]

### Philosopher by fire — *philosophe par le feu* {#e476}

*term; Literal translation.*

Jacques Cœur used a profusion of chosen symbols to accredit his 'true or supposed' quality of philosophe par le feu [BOURGES.1 ¶3]. Interpreted: an alchemist who practises the Work, as the next paragraph concludes he did [BOURGES.1 ¶4].

> «nous devons reconnaître que Jacques Cœur mit tout en œuvre pour accréditer, par une profusion de symboles choisis, sa qualité vraie ou supposée de philosophe par le feu.» (*we must recognize that Jacques Cœur did everything to accredit, by a profusion of chosen symbols, his true or supposed quality of philosopher by fire.*) [BOURGES.1 ¶3]

### Philosophers (Sages) — *Philosophes* {#e543}

*term; Literal translation; the text uses Philosophes and Sages interchangeably for the alchemical masters.*

The text's usual name for the alchemical masters and authors, also called Sages. They speak plainly only to turn the profane away, so their clear descriptions of colours are to be distrusted [PARIS.3 ¶34]. The Sibyl defines a Philosopher as one who knows how to make glass [BOURGES.2 ¶36]. The disciple should not despise the works of the old Philosophers but study the classic texts [CONCL ¶4]. In the first preface Canseliet says no Philosopher, to his knowledge, has noted the colour that is the key [PREF1 ¶11].

> «Vous n’êtes pas sans avoir lu, en plusieurs endroits de vos ouvrages, que les Philosophes ne parlent clairement que lorsqu’ils veulent écarter les profanes de leur Table ronde.» (*You will have read, in several places in your books, that the Philosophers speak clearly only when they wish to keep the profane away from their Round Table.*) [PARIS.3 ¶34]

> «La Sibylle, interrogée sur ce qu’était un Philosophe, répondit : « C’est celui qui sait faire le verre. »» (*The Sibyl, asked what a Philosopher was, answered: 'It is he who knows how to make glass.'*) [BOURGES.2 ¶36]

### Philosophers' stone — *pierre philosophale* {#e215}

*term; The usual English term.*

The end product of the Work. The ancient alchemists called it the Absolute [INTRO.5 ¶7]. Obtained as a red crystal or powder, volatile and fusible, it becomes penetrating and able to 'heal lepers', that is, transmute base metals into gold [PARIS.3 ¶29]. The despised subject becomes the Tree of Life, Elixir or philosophers' stone, giving knowledge, fortune and health [PARIS.5 ¶4]. Stone of the Philosophers and Philosophers' Stone are alike in kind and origin, the first raw, the second fully cooked and digested; the Golden Fleece names both the prepared matter and the result [BOURGES.2 ¶20]. The third repetition of one operation gives it as medicine of the third order, containing the virtues of Sulphur and Elixir multiplied [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. A legend says Guillaume de Paris hid it in a pillar of Notre-Dame [PARIS.6 ¶18].

> «Pierre des Philosophes et Pierre Philosophale sont donc deux choses semblables, en espèce et en origine, mais la première est crue, tandis que la seconde, qui en dérive, est parfaitement cuite et digérée.» (*Stone of the Philosophers and Philosophers' Stone are therefore two similar things, in kind and in origin, but the first is raw, while the second, derived from it, is perfectly cooked and digested.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶20]

> «la pierre philosophale devient pénétrante et idoine à guérir les lépreux, c’est-à-dire à transmuer en or les métaux vulgaires» (*the philosophers' stone becomes penetrating and fit to heal lepers, that is, to transmute common metals into gold*) [PARIS.3 ¶29]

Identified in the text with: [Absolute](#e181), [Elixir](#e247), [Tree of Life](#e371)

### Philosophical earth — *terre philosophale* {#e036}

*term; Literal translation.*

At the start of the second work, while the Rebis decomposes in the Athanor, the seeds enclosed in the philosophical earth undergo the fermenting influence of moisture; this is the reign of Saturn [PARIS.5 ¶9]. Canseliet: the alchemical earth, chaotic and inert, contains the philosophical heaven; separating the pure from the gross lets one know what the philosophical earth is [PREF2 ¶32].

> «période embryonnaire où les semences, encloses au sein de la terre philosophale, subissent l’influence fermentative de l’humidité.» (*embryonic period in which the seeds, enclosed in the bosom of the philosophical earth, undergo the fermenting influence of moisture.*) [PARIS.5 ¶9]

### Philosophical Egg — *Œuf philosophique* {#e292}

*term; The usual English term; literal translation.*

Stated: by 'œuf' the Sages mean their compound placed in its proper vessel, ready to undergo the transformations the fire will cause; it is an egg because its shell holds the Rebis, made of white and red in a proportion like that of a bird's egg [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. The secret vessel is generally called 'œuf philosophique' and 'Lion vert' [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. As to form, the egg may be spherical or ovoid, of clear transparent glass without bubbles, thick-walled (Lorraine glass is recommended), with a neck that can be sealed at the enameller's lamp [BOURGES.2 ¶6]. The Paris relief shows a section of the Athanor with the inner apparatus that supports the philosophical egg [PARIS.4 ¶2]. The black colour follows the perfect mixing of 'les matières de l'Œuf' [PARIS.3 ¶3]. The Philosophical Mercury is called 'l'oeuf vitriolé', needing only graduated cooking [PARIS.3 ¶18]. At Amiens the tower of the 'church' is the secret furnace holding the philosophical egg [AMIENS ¶12]; its contents end calcined, granular or powdery [AMIENS ¶7]. The masters called the shell of the egg 'secretum secretorum' [BOURGES.2 ¶34].

> «Par le terme d’œuf, les Sages entendent leur composé, disposé dans son vase propre, et prêt à subir les transformations que l’action du feu y provoquera.» (*By the term egg, the Sages mean their compound, placed in its proper vessel, and ready to undergo the transformations that the action of fire will cause in it.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶5]

### Philosophical gold — *or philosophique* {#e425}

*term; Literal translation.*

The dragon gains its power of transmuting in quantity by cookings with the Sulphur or philosophical Gold, the multiplications [PARIS.7 ¶27]. The Palace of the seventh Paris medallion stands for living or philosophical gold, vile and despised [PARIS.5 ¶11]. The Red Lion is the Green Lion brought to the quality of hermetic gold [PARIS.4 ¶18]. The pure part of the subject is the dyeing Hermes that carries the mystic gold [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. In the RERE enigma, one RE is a dry matter, philosophical gold, the other a wet matter, philosophical mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶33]. The text distinguishes common gold, which has suffered neither exaltation nor transfusion [PARIS.5 ¶6].

> «RE, une matière sèche, or philosophique ; RE, une matière humide, mercure philosophique ; RERE ou REBIS, une matière double» (*RE, a dry matter, philosophical gold; RE, a wet matter, philosophical mercury; RERE or REBIS, a double matter*) [BOURGES.2 ¶33]

### Philosophical Mercury — *Mercure philosophique* {#e307}

*term; The usual English term; literal translation.*

Shown on the Paris stylobate as a serpent coiled on a golden rod, the caduceus [PARIS.3 ¶17–18]. The serpent is Mercury in its first state, dissolving and absorbing the metallic sulphur; the golden rod is the corporeal sulphur added; the resulting homogeneous body keeps the name Philosophical Mercury and the image of the caduceus [PARIS.3 ¶18]. Stated: it is the matter or compound of the first order, 'l'oeuf vitriolé', which needs only graduated cooking to become red sulphur, then Elixir, then Universal Medicine [PARIS.3 ¶18]. Philalèthe (quoted) calls it the bird of Hermes, Goose, Swan or Pheasant [PARIS.4 ¶4]. It results from the absorption of a certain part of sulphur by a determined quantity of mercury, so the proportions must be known if one works by the old way [PARIS.5 ¶6]. Sabine Stuart de Chevalier's claim to get it by dissolving common mercury is rejected [PARIS.5 ¶7]. Lenglet-Dufresnoy (quoted) says the short way is done by the 'double mercure philosophique' in eight days [PARIS.7 ¶6]. In the RERE reading, one RE is the humid matter, philosophical mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶33]. 'Celui qui sait faire l'Œuvre par le seul mercure a trouvé ce qu'il y a de plus parfait' [BOURGES.2 ¶28]. Identified with the Bird of Hermes (e317).

> «mais le corps résultant, homogène et parfaitement préparé, conserve le nom de Mercure philosophique et l’image du caducée.» (*but the resulting body, homogeneous and perfectly prepared, keeps the name of Philosophical Mercury and the image of the caduceus.*) [PARIS.3 ¶18]

> «C’est la matière ou le composé du premier ordre, l’oeuf vitriolé qui n’exige plus qu’une cuisson graduée pour se transformer d’abord en soufre rouge, ensuite en Elixir» (*It is the matter or compound of the first order, the vitriolated egg, which now needs only a graduated cooking to change first into red sulphur, then into Elixir*) [PARIS.3 ¶18]

Identified in the text with: [Bird of Hermes](#e317)

### Philostratus — *Philostrate* {#e147}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Philostrate); the proposed form is the usual English spelling.*

Teller of the wonders of Apollonius of Tyana, which Fulcanelli says are of a chemical order [INTRO.3 n4].

> «Dès lors, les merveilles racontées par Philostrate, étant d’ordre chimique, ne sauraient nous surprendre.» (*Hence the wonders told by Philostratus, being of a chemical order, cannot surprise us.*) [INTRO.3 n4]

### Phoenix — *phénix* {#e004}

*term; Literal translation; usual English term.*

In the Work: the cock, the volatile and living portion (Mercury) extracted from the subject, 'can become the Phoenix', but must first take the provisional fixity figured by the fox [AMIENS ¶15]. Canseliet uses the 'phoenix of the poets' for the philosopher whose old self is consumed by the flame of Revelation and from whose ashes a new personality is reborn, 'so at least the philosophical Tradition will have it' [PREF1 ¶3].

> «Ce coq, tout volatil qu’il soit, peut devenir le Phénix. Encore doit-il, auparavant, prendre l’état de fixité provisoire que caractérise le symbole du goupil, notre renard hermétique.» (*This cock, volatile as it is, can become the Phoenix. Yet it must first take on the state of provisional fixity characterised by the symbol of the goupil, our hermetic fox.*) [AMIENS ¶15]

> «Et de ce cendres comme le phénix des poètes, une personnalité nouvelle renaît.» (*And from these ashes, like the phoenix of the poets, a new personality is reborn.*) [PREF1 ¶3]

### Phrygian cap — *bonnet phrygien* {#e262}

*term; The usual English term.*

The alchemist of Notre-Dame wears it as the attribute of Adeptship [PARIS.1 ¶14]. A footnote says it was worn by the sans-culottes as a protective talisman and was the distinctive sign of Initiates; Pierre Dujols is quoted that in the Eleusinian grade of Epopt a red cap was set on the candidate's head, that this headgear, called libéria in the Mithraic rites, once marked freed slaves, and was a masonic symbol and supreme mark of Initiation [PARIS.1 n4].

> «Le bonnet phrygien, qui coiffait les sans-culottes et constituait une sorte de talisman protecteur, au milieu des hécatombes révolutionnaires, était le signe distinctif des Initiés.» (*The Phrygian cap, worn by the sans-culottes and serving as a kind of protective talisman amid the revolutionary slaughters, was the distinctive sign of the Initiates.*) [PARIS.1 n4]

> «Couvre-toi de ce bonnet, il vaut mieux que la couronne d’un roi.» (*Cover yourself with this cap; it is worth more than a king's crown.*) [PARIS.1 n4]

### Phylosophie naturelle de Métaux — Natural Philosophy of Metals — *Phylosophie naturelle de Métaux* {#e264}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.*

Book of Bernard Trévisan, whose final Parable describes the Mysterious Fountain carved at Notre-Dame [PARIS.2 ¶4]; cited from Mangin de Richebourg's Bibliothèque des Philosophes Chimiques (1741, t. II, traité VII) [PARIS.2 n1]. In the parable an 'old priest' knows the fountain's action on the 'king of the country', whom it loves, attracts and engulfs; a Notre-Dame medallion is compared to it [PARIS.5 ¶12].

> «où le « prestre ancien et de vieil âge » se montre si bien instruit des propriétés de la fontaine occulte, de son action sur le « roy du pays » qu’elle aime, attire et engloutit.» (*where the 'ancient priest of old age' shows himself so well versed in the properties of the hidden fountain and its action on the 'king of the country', whom it loves, attracts and engulfs.*) [PARIS.5 ¶12]

### PIERRE BELLE (house at Tousson) — *PIERRE BELLE* {#e354}

*place; Name as carved in the inscription quoted by the text.*

An old 18th-century house at Tousson, near Malesherbe (Seine-et-Oise), bearing on its façade an inscription, reproduced with its spelling, saying it was built by a 'Laboureur' who named it PIERRE BELLE, 1762 [PARIS.4 n2]. The note adds that alchemy was also called Celestial Agriculture and its Adepts 'Laboureurs' [PARIS.4 n2]. The text makes no further reading of the name.

> «Par un Laboureur je fus construite. sans intérêt et d’un don zellé, il m’a nommée PIERRE BELLE. 1762.» (*By a Ploughman I was built. without self-interest and with zealous gift, he named me PIERRE BELLE (Fair Stone). 1762.*) [PARIS.4 n2]

### Pierre de Corbeil — *Pierre de Corbeil* {#e110}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Called 'the initiate', archbishop of Sens; he composed the mass of the Feast of Fools according to pagan ritual, at which the flock of the year 1220 cried Evohé [INTRO.1 ¶11].

> «Composée par l’initié Pierre de Corbeil, archevêque de Sens, selon le rituel païen, et où les ouailles de l’an 1220 poussaient le cri de joie des bacchanales : Evohé ! Evohé !» (*Composed by the initiate Pierre de Corbeil, archbishop of Sens, according to the pagan ritual, and at which the flock of the year 1220 raised the joyful cry of the bacchanals: Evohe! Evohe!*) [INTRO.1 ¶11]

### Pierre de Montereau — *Pierre de Montereau* {#e438}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named as the maker of the Sainte-Chapelle, a stone shrine built 1245-1248 for the relics of the Passion, whose south windows hold a large alchemical collection [PARIS.8 ¶8].

> «La Sainte-Chapelle, chef-d’oeuvre de Pierre de Montereau, merveilleuse châsse de pierre élevée, de 1245 à 1248, pour recevoir les reliques de la Passion» (*The Sainte-Chapelle, masterpiece of Pierre de Montereau, a marvellous stone shrine raised from 1245 to 1248 to receive the relics of the Passion*) [PARIS.8 ¶8]

### Pierre Dujols — *Pierre Dujols* {#e220}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Called 'the erudite' and 'the learned' [INTRO.8 ¶4; PARIS.1 n4]. In his Bibliographie générale de l’Occulte he reports Elias Schadius's inscription to Isis and Bigarne's account of the Virgo paritura [INTRO.8 ¶4]. Analysing Lombard's Histoire des Jacobins, he writes that in the Eleusinian grade of Epopt a red cap was placed on the candidate, and that this cap was a masonic symbol and supreme mark of Initiation [PARIS.1 n4]. On the colour white he traces Hebrew hur, heur to heureux and bienheureux, equivalent of Initiate, noble, pure [PARIS.3 ¶28]; Fulcanelli quotes him in support of his own reading.

> «Ce terme de Blanc, dit Pierre Dujols, avait été choisi pour des raisons philosophiques très profondes.» (*This term White, says Pierre Dujols, had been chosen for very deep philosophical reasons.*) [PARIS.3 ¶28]

### Planetary metals — *métaux planétaires* {#e395}

*term; Literal translation.*

The seven circles on the sarcophagus of the Portal of the Virgin are the symbols of the seven planetary metals; a verse gives the pairs: sun gold, Mercury quicksilver, Saturn lead, Venus brass, Moon silver, Jupiter tin, Mars iron [PARIS.6 ¶2-6]. The Cosmopolite teaches that planetary virtues descend, not ascend, so Mars turns easily into Venus and Jupiter into Mercury [PARIS.6 ¶7]. The portal shows their correspondence of mutation: Sun in the centre, Saturn and Moon at the ends, then Jupiter and Mercury, then Mars and Venus [PARIS.6 ¶8]. In the symbolism of the planetary metals the Virgin is the Moon, which keeps the sun's rays in her womb [PARIS.1 ¶8]. At Amiens, dead trees under a faded sky refer to the first matters, planetary metals killed by fire and made inert by fusion, like trees in winter [AMIENS ¶19; AMIENS ¶20].

> «La correspondance de mutation des planètes métalliques entre elles est donc indiquée, sur le porche de Notre-Dame, de la manière la plus formelle.» (*The correspondence of mutation of the metallic planets among themselves is thus indicated, on the porch of Notre-Dame, in the most formal manner.*) [PARIS.6 ¶8]

### Plessis-Bourré — *Plessis-Bourré* {#e058}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

A note cites its 15th-century painted soffit with initiatic subjects, comparable to the Lallemant chapel ceiling, referring to Deux Logis Alchimiques [BOURGES.2 n12]. Canseliet discussed its centaur, the 'man-horse', in Deux Logis alchimiques [PREF2 ¶45].

> «l’autre, au Plessis-Bourré, composé de peintures, du XVe siècle (Deux Logis Alchimiques).» (*the other, at Plessis-Bourré, made of paintings, of the 15th century (Two Alchemical Dwellings).*) [BOURGES.2 n12]

### Pomegranate — *grenade* {#e514}

*term; Literal translation.*

On the Lallemant ceiling, philosophic calcination is symbolized by a pomegranate under fire in a goldsmith's vase, with '3 R' above marking the three reiterations [BOURGES.2 ¶28]. Three flaming pomegranates crown the credence [BOURGES.2 ¶30]; stated: they represent fire corporified in the red salt that is philosophic Sulphur, and confirm that its calcination is reiterated three times [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. For how the triple operation is done, the text refers to the pomegranate roasting 'in a certain vase' [BOURGES.2 ¶32].

> «la calcination philosophique, symbolisée par une grenade soumise à l’action du feu dans un vase d’orfèvrerie» (*philosophic calcination, symbolized by a pomegranate subjected to the action of fire in a goldsmith's vessel*) [BOURGES.2 ¶28]

> «comme elles représentent le feu corporifié dans ce sel rouge qu’est le Soufre philosophal, nous comprendrons aisément qu’il faille réitérer trois fois la calcination de ce corps» (*since they represent fire corporified in that red salt which is the philosophic Sulphur, we shall easily understand that the calcination of this body must be reiterated three times*) [BOURGES.2 ¶32]

### Pontanus — *Pontanus* {#e316}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of De Lapide Philosphico (1614) [PARIS.3 n10], recommended for the nature of the secret fire [PARIS.3 ¶20]. He admits that before knowing the secret vessel he had begun the same work more than two hundred times without success, though working on the proper matters and by the regular method [BOURGES.2 ¶34].

> «Pontanus avoue qu’avant de connaître ce vaisseau secret il avait recommencé, sans succès, plus de deux cent fois le même travail» (*Pontanus admits that before knowing this secret vessel he had begun the same work over again, without success, more than two hundred times*) [BOURGES.2 ¶34]

### Pontic water — *eau pontique* {#e289}

*term; Literal rendering of 'eau pontique'; the text gives no gloss of 'pontique'.*

After the sacred wells, the text promises to return to 'cette eau pontique' to which the philosophers gave many suggestive epithets [PARIS.2 ¶24]. On the Paris portal the action of the pontic water on the heavy matter is shown by two quarrelling children, one dropping a pot, the other a stone [PARIS.5 ¶13]. Interpreted: 'cette' points back to the water of the fountain and wells, i.e. the dissolvent of the Work [PARIS.2 ¶6, ¶21, ¶24]; the medallion shows its action on the 'matière grave' in the Solution [PARIS.5 ¶13–14].

> «Nous aurons bientôt l’occasion de revenir sur cette eau pontique, à laquelle les Philosophes ont donné une foule d’épithètes plus ou moins suggestives.» (*We shall soon have occasion to return to this pontic water, to which the Philosophers have given a host of more or less suggestive epithets.*) [PARIS.2 ¶24]

> «Il n’est guère possible d’écrire avec plus de clarté ni de simplicité l’action de l’eau pontique sur la matière grave» (*It is hardly possible to write with more clarity or simplicity the action of the pontic water on the heavy matter*) [PARIS.5 ¶13]

### Portal of Saint Anne — *porche de Sainte-Anne* {#e408}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

The south portal of Notre-Dame de Paris; it offers a single motif, of great interest because it describes the shortest practice of the Science [PARIS.7 ¶1]. Grillot de Givry reads its door, kept shut all year, as the non-vulgar path [PARIS.7 ¶2]. Its trumeau bears the statue of saint Marcel, which Fulcanelli holds to have no secret meaning; only the dado and pedestal with the dragon, besants, king in the flames and athanor carry hermetic value [PARIS.7 ¶11; PARIS.7 ¶18-22]. The pillar was remade and modified; the original went to the Cluny museum [PARIS.7 ¶12; PARIS.7 ¶16]. Canseliet returns to the controversy over this statue in his third preface [PREF3 ¶23].

> «Revenons sur nos pas et arrêtons-nous au portail sud, appelé encore porche de Sainte-Anne. Il ne nous offre qu’un seul motif, mais l’intérêt en est considérable, parce qu’il décrit la pratique la plus courte de notre Science» (*Let us retrace our steps and stop at the south portal, also called the porch of Saint Anne. It offers us only one motif, but its interest is considerable, because it describes the shortest practice of our Science*) [PARIS.7 ¶1]

### Portal of the Judgement — *porche du Jugement* {#e251}

*place; Name of the central portal of Notre-Dame as the text gives it, rendered in English.*

The great central portal of Notre-Dame de Paris [PARIS.1 ¶2]. Its trumeau shows alchemy enthroned with sceptre, two books and the nine-rung ladder [PARIS.1 ¶3]. The stylobate under the three porches is wholly devoted to 'our science' [PARIS.2 ¶1]; two bas-reliefs on the buttresses show the Fountain and the Athanor [PARIS.2 ¶4; PARIS.2 ¶25]. The hermetic subjects stand in two rows, twelve figures above and twelve medallions below [PARIS.3 ¶1; PARIS.5 ¶1]; their original colours survive in the central rose [PARIS.5 ¶16–17]. The central porch of Amiens reproduces it almost exactly [AMIENS ¶1]. In the 14th century alchemists met weekly at the great porch [INTRO.2 ¶5].

> «Le stylobate de la façade, qui se développe et s’étend sous les trois porches, est tout entier consacré à notre science» (*The stylobate of the façade, which runs beneath the three porches, is entirely devoted to our science*) [PARIS.2 ¶1]

> «Les sujets hermétiques du stylobate se développent sur deux rangs superposés, à droite et à gauche du porche.» (*The hermetic subjects of the stylobate unfold in two superposed rows, to the right and left of the porch.*) [PARIS.3 ¶1]

### Portal of the Virgin — *portail de la Vierge* {#e394}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

The north portal of Notre-Dame de Paris [PARIS.6 ¶1]. On its tympanum a sarcophagus bears seven circles, the symbols of the seven planetary metals [PARIS.6 ¶2]. Its base carries the dog and doves, the lamb and the upside-down man; the Raven was once carved there [PARIS.6 ¶11; PARIS.6 ¶12; PARIS.6 ¶17]. Its piers carry the zodiac, Aries, Taurus, Gemini, with Leo moved into Cancer's place [PARIS.6 ¶20; PARIS.6 ¶21]. A small relief under the porch shows the Bath of the stars as a simplified Massacre of the Innocents [PARIS.6 ¶22].

> «Quittons le grand porche et venons au portail nord ou de la Vierge.» (*Let us leave the great porch and come to the north portal, or portal of the Virgin.*) [PARIS.6 ¶1]

### Poème philosophic de la Vérité de la Phisique Minéralle — Philosophical Poem on the Truth of Mineral Physics — *Poème philosophic de la Vérité de la Phisique Minéralle* {#e190}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our English rendering.*

Poem by De Nuysement, printed in Traitez de l’Harmonie et Constitution generalle du Vray Sel (Paris, Périer et Buisard, 1620 and 1621, p. 254) [INTRO.6 n1]. The text cites from it eight lines on the wheel: the artist is to follow the tracks of the wheel and give an even heat, since too high burns the heaven and too low destroys the earth, while the middle course is surer [INTRO.6 ¶4–11].

> «Car en montant trop haut le ciel tu brusleras,» (*For by climbing too high you will burn the heaven,*) [INTRO.6 ¶8]

### Propos rustiques, baliverneries, contes et discours d'Eutrapel — Rustic Talk, Nonsense, Tales and Discourses of Eutrapel — *Propos rustiques, baliverneries, contes et discours d’Eutrapel* {#e124}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with a literal English rendering.*

Work by Noël du Fail (ch. X, Paris, 1842), source of the statement that Notre-Dame was the alchemists' meeting place [INTRO.2 n1, INTRO.2 ¶5].

> «Noël du Fail, Propos rustiques, baliverneries, contes et discours d’Eutrapel (ch. X). Paris, 1842.» (*Noël du Fail, Rustic Talk, Nonsense, Tales and Discourses of Eutrapel (ch. X). Paris, 1842.*) [INTRO.2 n1]

### Prudentius — *Prudence* {#e103}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Prudence); the proposed English form renders it.*

Saint Marcel was his pupil, disciple and successor [PARIS.7 ¶31, PREF3 ¶44]. Fulcanelli mocks Cambriel's engraving that shows Prudence's pupil bearded [PARIS.7 ¶30, PARIS.7 ¶31].

> «et, superbe anachronisme, l’élève de Prudence est barbu !» (*and, superb anachronism, Prudentius's pupil is bearded!*) [PARIS.7 ¶31]

### Préceptes du Père Abraham — Precepts of Father Abraham — *Préceptes du Père Abraham* {#e320}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

Work of an anonymous author quoted on the primitive, celestial water to be drawn from the body where it is, expressed by seven letters, the first seed of all beings; it is the universal solvent, in which sun and moon bathe and resolve into water, their first origin [PARIS.3 ¶21].

> «C’est à cette eau que les Philosophes ont donné tant de noms, et c’est le dissolvant universel, la vie et la santé de toute chose.» (*It is to this water that the Philosophers have given so many names, and it is the universal solvent, the life and health of all things.*) [PARIS.3 ¶21]

### Puffer — *souffleur* {#e127}

*term; The usual English term for the bellows-blowing pseudo-alchemist; literally 'blower'.*

Mostly the alchemist who works by the letter: Tollius addresses the souffleurs, slaves of the letter, who look for colours in their glass vessels [PARIS.3 ¶36]; the 'tourbe des souffleurs' was drawn by the legend of the raven and its hidden treasure [PARIS.6 ¶18]; ignorant souffleurs took the nostoc literally and brought ridicule on alchemy [AMIENS ¶35]; the frauds of greedy souffleurs discredited hermetic truth [PARIS.7 ¶29]. The word is also used neutrally: the 16th-century souffleur at Notre-Dame [INTRO.2 ¶7], the pilgrim's emblem popular among medieval souffleurs [BOURGES.1 ¶5], the lightly clad 'souffleur chartrain' at the furnace [AMIENS ¶12].

> «Ainsi, vous saisirez mieux l’apostrophe que Tollius adresse aux souffleurs, esclaves de la lettre» (*Thus you will better grasp the rebuke that Tollius addresses to the puffers, slaves of the letter*) [PARIS.3 ¶36]

> «Souvenons-nous que ce sont les friponneries des souffleurs avides, les pratiques insensées des charlatans, les inepties d’écrivains ignares et sans scrupule qui ont jeté le discrédit sur la vérité hermétique.» (*Let us remember that it is the trickery of greedy puffers, the senseless practices of charlatans, the inanities of ignorant and unscrupulous writers that have brought discredit on hermetic truth.*) [PARIS.7 ¶29]

### Putrefaction — *putréfaction* {#e073}

*term; Literal translation.*

Decomposition of the matter, marked by black. The woman holding the raven personifies Putrefaction [PARIS.3 ¶2]; the black colour is the first sign of decomposition after the perfect mixing of the matters of the Egg, the certain mark of success [PARIS.3 ¶3]. Le Breton counts four putrefactions in the Work, each with its blackness [PARIS.3 ¶5]; in the first putrefaction the corrupted matter must show acidity, a sepulchral smell, 'fish eyes' [PARIS.3 ¶10, PARIS.3 ¶11]. Batsdorff: it separates pure from impure; its names include Occident, Darkness, Raven's Head, Death; its stench is sensible only to understanding [PARIS.3 ¶15]. A winged skull at Bourges expresses a new generation issued from putrefaction [BOURGES.2 ¶25]. Canseliet: fertile putrefaction is necessary for life to continue in any matter [PREF3 ¶15].

> «Il appert donc que par cette putréfaction on fait la séparation du pur et de l’impur.» (*It is clear then that by this putrefaction the pure is separated from the impure.*) [PARIS.3 ¶15]

> «il y a quatre putréfactions dans l’Œuvre philosophique.» (*there are four putrefactions in the philosophical Work.*) [PARIS.3 ¶5]

Identified in the text with: [Raven's Head](#e174)

### Python — *Python* {#e484}

*term; Name as spelled in the text.*

The anonymous author of the Clef du Cabinet Hermétique says Philalèthe describes the oak vessel needed for putrefying the water by the fable of the serpent Python that Cadmus pierced against an oak [PARIS.4 ¶14]. Apollo's killing of Python is listed among fables of the combat that yields the secret dissolvent (and the vessel of the compound) [BOURGES.1 ¶9].

> «Philalèthe le décrit par la fable du serpent Python, que Cadmus perça d’outre en outre contre un chesne.» (*Philalèthe describes it by the fable of the serpent Python, which Cadmus pierced right through against an oak.*) [PARIS.4 ¶14]

## Q

### Quatre Livres des Secrets de Médecine et de la Philosophie Chimique — Four Books of the Secrets of Medicine and Chemical Philosophy — *Quatre Livres des Secrets de Médecine et de la Philosophie Chimique* {#e454}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Treatise of Jean Liébaut, Paris, Jacques du Puys [AMIENS n4], containing the woodcuts of the Pyrotechnie that show furnaces as fortresses [AMIENS ¶13].

> «Cf. Jean Liébaut, Quatre Livres des Secrets de Médecine et de la Philosophie Chimique. Paris, Jacques du Puys, p. 17a et 19 a.» (*Cf. Jean Liébaut, Four Books of the Secrets of Medicine and Chemical Philosophy. Paris, Jacques du Puys, pp. 17a and 19a.*) [AMIENS n4]

### Quicksilver — *vif-argent* {#e381}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

Common quicksilver (mercure vulgaire) is distinguished from the Philosophers' mercury: some authors say that, purified and exalted, it would become a solvent, which Fulcanelli doubts, holding from experience that without its own agent it cannot become a water useful to the Work [PARIS.5 ¶7]. In the verse on the planetary metals, quicksilver is marked by Mercury [PARIS.6 ¶3]. The spring the old philosophers called Mercury has no likeness to common quicksilver: the water needed is dry and does not wet the hands [AMIENS ¶15].

> «que les anciens philosophes ont nommée Mercure, quoiqu’elle n’ait aucune apparence du vif-argent vulgaire. Car l’eau dont nous avons besoin est sèche, ne mouille pas les mains» (*which the ancient philosophers named Mercury, although it has no appearance of common quicksilver. For the water we need is dry, does not wet the hands*) [AMIENS ¶15]

### Quintessence — *quintessence* {#e423}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

On the Saint Anne dado, beside the athanor and under the dragon's claws, five units are the quintessence, made of the three principles and the two natures, totalled under the number ten 'in which all ends' [PARIS.7 ¶22]. Elsewhere the soul, metallic life and celestial Dew, freed by the death of the body, is called a quintessence which, put into a pure fixed body, gives birth to a new creature [PARIS.6 ¶23].

> «les cinq unités de la quintessence, comprenant les trois principes et les deux natures, puis leur totalisation sous le nombre dix « auquel tout finit et se termine ».» (*the five units of the quintessence, comprising the three principles and the two natures, then their totalling under the number ten 'in which all finishes and ends'.*) [PARIS.7 ¶22]

## R

### Ram (Aries) — *Bélier* {#e179}

*term; Literal translation, with the Latin zodiac name the text also uses.*

In the fourth motif of the Notre-Dame stylobate a man shows the Ram: the matter from which the dissolvent is prepared; per Pernety (QUOTED), Adepts draw their steel, their magnet, from the belly of Aries [PARIS.3 ¶1, PARIS.3 ¶23]. That magnet must be taken at its birth [PARIS.4 ¶13]; the steel is found in Aries, the beginning of spring [PARIS.4 ¶14]. In the last medallion the ram shows the right season and substance were chosen [PARIS.5 ¶15]. Aries opens the zodiac on the north portal, the spring months to begin work [PARIS.6 ¶1, PARIS.6 ¶20]. Oak and ram are one thing under two aspects [BOURGES.2 ¶21]; the raw subject has the Ram as celestial hieroglyph [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. Hiram is the divine Ram [INTRO.5 ¶5].

> «« Les Adeptes disent qu’ils tirent leur acier du ventre d’Ariès, et ils appellent aussi cet acier leur aimant. »» (*'The Adepts say they draw their steel from the belly of Aries, and they also call this steel their magnet.'*) [PARIS.3 ¶23]

> «enfin, ce minéral a pour hiéroglyphe céleste le signe astronomique du Bélier (Aries).» (*finally, this mineral has as its celestial hieroglyph the astronomical sign of the Ram (Aries).*) [BOURGES.2 ¶23]

### Rares Expériences sur l'Esprit minéral — Rare Experiments on the Mineral Spirit — *Rares Expériences sur l'Esprit minéral* {#e373}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of De Respour, Paris, Langlois et Barbin, 1668, the source of the passage on the Mirror of Art [PARIS.5 n1; PARIS.5 ¶4].

> «De Respour, Rares Expériences sur l’Esprit minéral. Paris, Langlois et Barbin, 1668.» (*De Respour, Rare Experiments on the Mineral Spirit. Paris, Langlois and Barbin, 1668.*) [PARIS.5 n1]

### Raven — *Corbeau* {#e282}

*term; Literal translation; the text glosses it as 'symbole de la couleur noire' [PARIS.3 ¶2].*

Stated: the raven is the symbol of the black colour; on the Paris stylobate it is held by a woman personifying Putrefaction [PARIS.3 ¶2]. In the cooking of the Rebis it expresses the black colour, first appearance of the decomposition that follows the perfect mixing of the matters in the Egg; the philosophers call it the sure mark of success, and the text calls it the 'canonical seal' of the Work, as the star is the signature of the initial subject [PARIS.3 ¶3]. The black bird appears several times, which lets authors confuse the order of operations [PARIS.3 ¶4]; the Chevalier Inconnu calls the long putrefaction of the second operation 'Tête de Corbeau' [PARIS.3 ¶8], and Batsdorff lists 'Teste de Corbeau' among its names [PARIS.3 ¶15]. Descriptions of the raven of the coction are the most numerous [PARIS.3 ¶12]. Tollius (quoted) mocks those who look for their 'noir corbeau' in glass vessels [PARIS.3 ¶36]. A raven once sculpted on the north porch of Notre-Dame was 'l'hiéroglyphe majeur de notre pratique'; legend said its line of sight marked the pillar where Guillaume de Paris hid the philosophers' stone [PARIS.6 ¶17–19]. At Bourges the dark blue or black colour of the dead body is assigned to the Raven, hieroglyph of the caput mortuum [BOURGES.2 ¶25]; a fiery raven pecks a skull on the chapel ceiling [BOURGES.2 ¶28]. Identified with the black colour (e291).

> «Le Corbeau est, en quelque sorte, le sceau canonique de l'Œuvre, comme l’étoile est la signature du sujet initial.» (*The Raven is, in a way, the canonical seal of the Work, as the star is the signature of the initial subject.*) [PARIS.3 ¶3]

> «C’est dans cette partie du porche que se trouvait sculpté autrefois l’hiéroglyphe majeur de notre pratique : le Corbeau.» (*It is in this part of the porch that the major hieroglyph of our practice was once carved: the Raven.*) [PARIS.6 ¶17]

Identified in the text with: [Black colour](#e291)

### Raven's Head — *Tête de Corbeau* {#e174}

*term; The usual English term; literal.*

A name for the putrefaction marked by blackness. Per the Chevalier Inconnu (QUOTED), in the second operation the putrefaction called the Raven's Head is very long [PARIS.3 ¶8]. Batsdorff (QUOTED) lists 'Teste de Corbeau' among the Philosophers' names for putrefaction, which separates pure from impure [PARIS.3 ¶15].

> «Dans cette dite opération, la putréfaction, qu’ils appellent la Tête de Corbeau, est très longue.» (*In this said operation the putrefaction, which they call the Raven's Head, is very long.*) [PARIS.3 ¶8]

> «Les Philosophes lui ont donné divers noms et l’ont appelée Occident, Ténèbres, Eclypse, Lèpre, Teste de Corbeau, Mort, Mortification du Mercure…» (*The Philosophers have given it various names and called it West, Darkness, Eclipse, Leprosy, Raven's Head, Death, Mortification of Mercury…*) [PARIS.3 ¶15]

Identified in the text with: [Putrefaction](#e073)

### Raymond Lull — *Raymond Lulle* {#e078}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; English form is the usual one.*

A note explains that 'Raymond' alone means Raymond Lulle, 'Docteur Illuminatus' [PARIS.3 n5]; Bernard Trévisan (QUOTED) cites his 'eclipse of the sun' for the blackening [PARIS.3 ¶13, PARIS.3 ¶14]. He spoke of weights [PARIS.5 ¶6]; Huginus à Barma counts him among old masters each with his own process [PARIS.7 ¶3]. Canseliet: with Morien, he says the foul odour of dissolution gives way to the sweetest perfume [PREF3 ¶22].

> «L’auteur, sous ce seul prénom, entend parler de Raymond Lulle (Docteur Illuminatus).» (*By this first name alone the author means Raymond Lull (Doctor Illuminatus).*) [PARIS.3 n5]

> «Là est l’éclipse du soleil, dont parle Raymond.» (*There is the eclipse of the sun of which Raymond speaks.*) [PARIS.3 ¶14]

### Realgar — *réalgar* {#e517}

*term; The usual English term; the text glosses it as natural sulphide of arsenic (BOURGES.2 ¶35).*

Offered only as a supposed example to explain RER: if RE were realgar, 'natural sulphide of arsenic', R could be its sulphur or its arsenic, and RER realgar increased by one of them [BOURGES.2 ¶35]. The text presents this as an illustration ('supposons'), not a statement of the matter.

> «prenons un exemple et supposons que la matière représentée par RE soit le réalgar ou sulfure naturel d’arsenic.» (*let us take an example and suppose that the matter represented by RE is realgar, or natural sulphide of arsenic.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶35]

### Rebis — *Rebis* {#e144}

*term; The established term, kept as is; the text decodes it as RE BIS, 'a double thing' [BOURGES.2 ¶33].*

The double matter of the Work, the compost. In its cooking, the black colour is the first sign of decomposition after the perfect mixture of the matters in the Egg [PARIS.3 ¶3]. The Rebis enclosed in the athanor suffers dislocation at the first phase of the second work [PARIS.5 ¶9]. The Egg's shell holds the rebis formed of white and red [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. Apollonius's name is an image of the 'rebis philosophal', union of brother and sister [INTRO.3 n4]. At the Hôtel Lallemant RERE equals RE BIS: RE a dry matter, philosophical gold; RE a wet matter, philosophical mercury; together a double matter, amalgam of both [BOURGES.2 ¶30, BOURGES.2 ¶31, BOURGES.2 ¶33]. The text identifies it with the compost.

> «Résumons. RE, une matière sèche, or philosophique ; RE, une matière humide, mercure philosophique ; RERE ou REBIS, une matière double, à la fois humide et sèche, amalgame d’or et de mercure philosophiques» (*To sum up. RE, a dry matter, philosophical gold; RE, a wet matter, philosophical mercury; RERE or REBIS, a double matter, at once wet and dry, an amalgam of philosophical gold and mercury*) [BOURGES.2 ¶33]

> «C’est, dans ce sens, positivement un œuf, puisque son enveloppe, ou sa coque, renferme le rebis philosophal, formé de blanc et de rouge dans une proportion analogue à celle de l’oeuf des oiseaux.» (*It is in this sense positively an egg, since its envelope or shell contains the philosophical rebis, formed of white and red in a proportion like that of a bird's egg.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶5]

Identified in the text with: [Compost](#e170), [RERE](#e301)

### Red — *rouge* {#e333}

*term; Literal translation.*

The third and last principal colour [PARIS.3 ¶25]. Stated: symbol of fire, it marks exaltation, the predominance of spirit over matter, sovereignty, power and apostolate [PARIS.3 ¶29]. Obtained as red crystal or powder, volatile and fusible, the philosophers' stone becomes penetrating and able to 'heal lepers', i.e. to transmute common metals into gold [PARIS.3 ¶29]. On the cathedral roses the colours end in 'la perfection de la lumière rubiconde' [INTRO.6 ¶2]. In Paracelsus (quoted) red follows yellow and matter reaching it is the tincture [PARIS.3 ¶30]. Christ is shown in red [PARIS.3 ¶31].

> «Quant au rouge, symbole du feu, il marque l’exaltation, la prédominance de l’esprit sur la matière, la souveraineté, la puissance et l’apostolat.» (*As for red, symbol of fire, it marks exaltation, the predominance of spirit over matter, sovereignty, power and apostolate.*) [PARIS.3 ¶29]

> «la pierre philosophale devient pénétrante et idoine à guérir les lépreux, c’est-à-dire à transmuer en or les métaux vulgaires» (*the philosophers' stone becomes penetrating and fit to heal lepers, that is, to transmute common metals into gold*) [PARIS.3 ¶29]

### Red Lion — *Lion rouge* {#e362}

*term; The usual English term; literal translation.*

Stated (according to the philosophers): nothing other than the same matter, the Green Lion, brought by certain processes to the quality of hermetic gold [PARIS.4 ¶18]. Basile Valentin (quoted) advises dissolving and feeding the true Lion with the blood of the Green Lion, both being of one nature [PARIS.4 ¶18]. The occult salt of the Red Lion is extracted by the spirit of the Green Lion through repeated cohobation [PARIS.5 ¶2]. The Palace and old man of the seventh medallion are a variant of the Green and Red Lions, dissolvent and body to dissolve [PARIS.5 ¶11].
