> «Quant au Lion rouge, ce n’est autre chose, selon les Philosophes, que la même matière, ou Lion vert, amené par certains procédés à cette qualité spéciale qui caractérise l’or hermétique ou Lion rouge.» (*As for the Red Lion, it is nothing other, according to the Philosophers, than the same matter, or Green Lion, brought by certain processes to that special quality which characterises hermetic gold or Red Lion.*) [PARIS.4 ¶18]

> «Dissous et nourris le vray Lion du sang du Lion vert, parquoy ils sont tous deux de même nature.» (*Dissolve and feed the true Lion with the blood of the Green Lion, for they are both of the same nature.*) [PARIS.4 ¶18]

### Regimen — *régime* {#e341}

*term; Usual English term; the text glosses it itself as a modus operandi [PARIS.3 ¶35].*

Stated: a régime is simply the way to make the stone vegetate, to maintain and increase the life it received at birth; a modus operandi, not necessarily shown by a succession of colours [PARIS.3 ¶35]. The clear descriptions of régimes with emblematic colours are false; one must learn how one régime differs from the next [PARIS.3 ¶34–35]. The Régime of the stone, i.e. its coction, contains several others, meaning repetitions of one way of working [PARIS.3 ¶35]. Like a living body it needs food, graduated and changed (milk first, then meat), rest, and removal of excrements after each digestion [PARIS.3 ¶35–36]. Philalèthe (quoted): who knows the Régime will be honoured by princes [PARIS.3 ¶35]. The Bull is the first régime of elemental fire in the second work [PARIS.4 ¶21]. Trévisan's 'second régime' begins with the blackness [PARIS.3 ¶14]. The short way is called the 'Régime de Saturne' [PARIS.7 ¶7].

> «Tout simplement la manière de faire végéter, d’entretenir et d’accroître la vie que votre pierre a reçue dès sa naissance.» (*Simply the way to make vegetate, to maintain and to increase the life your stone received at its birth.*) [PARIS.3 ¶35]

> «le Régime de la pierre, c’est-à-dire sa coction, en contient plusieurs autres, entendez plusieurs répétitions d’une même manière d’opérer.» (*the Regimen of the stone, that is, its coction, contains several others, meaning several repetitions of one same way of working.*) [PARIS.3 ¶35]

### Regimen of Saturn — *Régime de Saturne* {#e410}

*term; Literal translation.*

The text states that the sages named the short, veiled path the Regimen of Saturn; in it the cooking needs only a simple crucible instead of a glass vessel [PARIS.7 ¶7]. This may explain the maxim: one vessel, one matter, one furnace [PARIS.7 ¶7].

> «Cette voie abrégée, mais couverte d’un voile épais, a été nommée par les Sages le Régime de Saturne.» (*This shortened path, but covered with a thick veil, has been named by the Sages the Regimen of Saturn.*) [PARIS.7 ¶7]

### Reign of Saturn — *règne de Saturne* {#e385}

*term; Literal translation.*

The old man numb with cold on the fourth lower medallion of Paris shows the first phase of the second work: the Rebis, shut in the Athanor, suffers the dislocation of its parts, at the gentle start of the wheel fire symbolised by cold and winter [PARIS.5 ¶1, PARIS.5 ¶8, PARIS.5 ¶9]. The reign of Saturn that then appears is the emblem of radical dissolution, decomposition and the black colour [PARIS.5 ¶9]. A different name, the Regimen of Saturn, is given to the short path [PARIS.7 ¶7].

> «C’est le règne de Saturne qui va paraître, emblème de la dissolution radicale, de la décomposition et de la couleur noire.» (*It is the reign of Saturn that is about to appear, emblem of radical dissolution, of decomposition and of the black colour.*) [PARIS.5 ¶9]

### Reincrudate — *réincruder* {#e387}

*term; From the text's own gloss: a technical hermetic term meaning to make raw [BOURGES.1 n2].*

The text defines it: to make raw, that is, to return to a state before maturity, to go backwards [BOURGES.1 n2]. The first step of practice is finding the agent able to reduce the fixed body, to reincrudate it into a form like its first substance; alchemists call this reanimating the corporifications, making dead metals live [PARIS.5 ¶11]. The masters recommend reincrudating the planetary metals, killed by fire, by giving them back with the fluid form the proper agent they lost in metallurgical reduction [AMIENS ¶20; AMIENS ¶21]. The rare solvent allows the reincrudation of natural gold, its softening and return to its first state as a friable, very fusible salt: the rejuvenation of the king [BOURGES.1 ¶10].

> «Terme technique hermétique qui signifie rendre cru, c'est-à-dire remettre dans un état antérieur à celui qui caractérise la maturité, rétrograder.» (*Hermetic technical term meaning to make raw, that is, to put back into a state prior to the one that characterises maturity, to go backwards.*) [BOURGES.1 n2]

### Remora — *Rémore* {#e390}

*term; Literal translation.*

In the Conjunction the authors show the violent reaction by fights of unlike animals; in Fulcanelli's view the best and most initiatic description is the duel of the Remora and the Salamander witnessed by Cyrano Bergerac [PARIS.5 ¶12]. The fight of the remora and the salamander is listed among the combats of the chemical bodies that give the secret solvent [BOURGES.1 ¶9].

> «la meilleure description, — la plus initiatique surtout, — est celle que nous laissa le grand philosophe de Cyrano Bergerac du duel effroyable que se livrèrent, sous ses yeux, la Rémore et la Salamandre.» (*the best description, the most initiatic above all, is the one left us by the great philosopher Cyrano Bergerac of the dreadful duel fought before his eyes by the Remora and the Salamander.*) [PARIS.5 ¶12]

### Renaissance — *Renaissance* {#e249}

*term; The usual English term.*

For Fulcanelli, a decadence that took under François I the paradoxical name of Renaissance; its artists, ignorant of medieval symbolism, copied antique models counterfeited in Italy [INTRO.9 ¶19–20]. Medieval builders built for Truth, anonymously; Renaissance ones for their own name: thought against fashion, genius against talent, spirit against matter [INTRO.9 ¶21]. This antagonism explains the Renaissance's contempt for all that was Gothic and the mutilations of medieval work [INTRO.9 ¶22–23]. The Classical School, 'imbued with the decadent principles of the Renaissance', derided Gothic as barbarous [INTRO.3 ¶2].

> «Entraînés par le grand courant de la décadence qui prit sous François Ier le nom paradoxal de Renaissance» (*Swept along by the great current of decadence which, under François I, took the paradoxical name of Renaissance*) [INTRO.9 ¶19]

> «Le moyen âge dut sa splendeur à l’originalité de ses créations ; la Renaissance dut sa vogue à la fidélité servile de ses copies.» (*The Middle Ages owed its splendour to the originality of its creations; the Renaissance owed its vogue to the servile fidelity of its copies.*) [INTRO.9 ¶21]

### RER — *RER* {#e516}

*term; The inscription kept as written; its meanings are the text's own reading (BOURGES.2 ¶33–35).*

With RERE, the enigma written three times in the Lallemant credence [BOURGES.2 ¶31]. RE, ablative of res, is 'a thing'; RERE is 'two things in one', i.e. RE BIS, REBIS, the compost of philosophic gold (dry) and philosophic mercury (wet) [BOURGES.2 ¶33]. RER holds a secret of capital importance concerning the vessel of the Work: it serves to cook, unite indissolubly and transform the compost RERE [BOURGES.2 ¶34]. R, half of RE, is half a thing; RER is a matter increased by half of another or of its own — a chemical combination, not a proportion; the text gives realgar plus its sulphur or arsenic as a supposed example [BOURGES.2 ¶35]. Seek RER, the vessel, first; RERE then follows [BOURGES.2 ¶36].

> «RER sert à cuire, à unir radicalement et indissolublement, à provoquer les transformations du compost RERE.» (*RER serves to cook, to unite radically and indissolubly, to bring about the transformations of the compost RERE.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶34]

> «R, qui est la moitié de RE, signifiera une moitié de chose, de matière. RER équivaut donc à une matière augmentée de la moitié d’une autre ou de la sienne propre.» (*R, which is half of RE, will mean half a thing, half a matter. RER is therefore a matter increased by the half of another, or of its own.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶35]

### RERE — *RERE* {#e301}

*term; Kept as written on the Lallemant credence [BOURGES.2 ¶30, BOURGES.2 ¶31]; the text's own reading glosses it as RE BIS, REBIS [BOURGES.2 ¶33].*

One of two words, RERE and RER, each repeated three times in the niche of the credence of the Hôtel Lallemant chapel [BOURGES.2 ¶29, BOURGES.2 ¶30, BOURGES.2 ¶31]. The triple repetition indicates three repetitions of one technique, confirmed by the three flaming pomegranates (the calcination of the Sulphur reiterated three times, giving Sulphur, Elixir, Philosophers' Stone) [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. Stated reading: RE is the Latin ablative of res, 'the thing'; RERE is 'two things in one', a double thing, and so equals RE BIS, the philosophers' REBIS or compost ready to undergo the fire; one RE is a dry matter, philosophical gold, the other a humid matter, philosophical mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶33]. The text advises seeking RER, the vessel, first; RERE will then be easily known [BOURGES.2 ¶36].

> «RERE ou REBIS, une matière double, à la fois humide et sèche, amalgame d’or et de mercure philosophiques» (*RERE or REBIS, a double matter, at once humid and dry, an amalgam of philosophical gold and mercury*) [BOURGES.2 ¶33]

> «puisque le mot RERE est l’assemblage de RE, une chose, et RE, une autre chose, nous traduirons deux choses en une, ou bien une double chose» (*since the word RERE is the joining of RE, a thing, and RE, another thing, we shall translate two things in one, or a double thing*) [BOURGES.2 ¶33]

Identified in the text with: [Rebis](#e144)

### Ripley — *Ripley* {#e388}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

The first gate of Ripley, like Basile Valentin's and Philalethes' entrance to the King's Palace, is the first step of practice that must be opened [PARIS.5 ¶11]. As 'Ripleus Anglois' he is cited at the start of his Douze Portes on the essence in which the sought spirit dwells [AMIENS ¶28]. He is said to come closer to the truth on the Green Lion: a single unclean body enters the magistery, the means of joining the tinctures between sun and moon [BOURGES.2 ¶5].

> «Il n’entre qu’un seul corps immonde dans notre magistère ; les Philosophes l’appellent communément Lion vert.» (*Only one unclean body enters our magistery; the Philosophers commonly call it Green Lion.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶5]

### Robert de Luzarches — *Robert de Luzarches* {#e439}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named with Thomas and Renault de Cormont as the genius to whom the cathedral of Amiens is due [AMIENS ¶2].

> «l’admirable temple dû au génie de Robert de Luzarches, de Thomas et Renault de Cormont» (*the admirable temple owed to the genius of Robert de Luzarches, of Thomas and Renault de Cormont*) [AMIENS ¶2]

### Roger Bacon — *Roger Bacon* {#e522}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named, with the epithet Doctor admirabilis, among those with whom Jean Lallemant deserves to commune around the Holy Grail [BOURGES.2 ¶38].

> «mérite de prendre place autour de saint Graal, d’y communier avec Geber (Magister magistrorum), avec Roger Bacon (Doctor admirabilis).» (*deserves to take his place around the Holy Grail, to commune there with Geber (Magister magistrorum) and Roger Bacon (Doctor admirabilis).*) [BOURGES.2 ¶38]

### Rose window — *rose* {#e185}

*term; The usual English term for the round window of a Gothic church.*

Because of the fixed orientation of churches, of the three roses the northern is never lit by the sun, the southern blazes at noon, and the great western rose of the portal is lit by the setting sun; thus the colours of the Work unfold on the cathedral front in a circle from darkness (black) through white to the red of perfect light [INTRO.6 ¶1; INTRO.6 ¶2]. In the Middle Ages the central rose was called Rota, the wheel, the hieroglyph of the time of coction [INTRO.6 ¶3]. The rose alone represents the action of fire and its duration, and medieval decorators tried to render in their rose windows the movements of matter excited by fire [INTRO.6 ¶12]. Some roses, emblematic of the compound, bear the six-pointed star; six-petalled star roses reproduce the Seal of Solomon [INTRO.6 ¶13; INTRO.6 ¶14]. Guillaume de Paris had the medallions of the great porch reproduced in the glass of the central rose of Notre-Dame, which keeps their original colours [PARIS.5 ¶17; PARIS.5 ¶18].

> «Au moyen âge, la rose centrale des porches se nommait Rota, la roue.» (*In the Middle Ages the central rose of porches was called Rota, the wheel.*) [INTRO.6 ¶3]

> «La rose représente donc, à elle seule, l’action du feu et sa durée.» (*The rose therefore represents, by itself alone, the action of the fire and its duration.*) [INTRO.6 ¶12]

### Rosicrucians — *Rose-Croix* {#e405}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

After explaining the May Dew as the condensation of the universal spirit, formed at night in spring, Fulcanelli says it is no surprise that, per Thomas Corneille, the grand masters of the Rose-Croix were called Brothers of the Cooked Dew, the sense they gave their initials F. R. C. [PARIS.6 ¶24].

> «signification qu’ils donnaient eux-mêmes aux initiales de leur ordre : F. R. C.» (*a meaning they themselves gave to the initials of their order: F. R. C.*) [PARIS.6 ¶24]

### Round Table — *Table ronde* {#e339}

*term; The usual English term.*

The philosophers speak clearly only when they want to keep the profane from their Round Table [PARIS.3 ¶34]. Stated: the romances of the Round Table cycle, to which Tristan and Yseult belongs, are traditional hermetic legends renewed from Greek fables, transmitting ancient science under fictions [BOURGES.1 ¶7]. Documents of the Hôtel Lallemant show Jean Lallemant belonged to the alchemical fraternity of the Knights of the Round Table [BOURGES.2 n4]; he deserves a place around the Holy Grail [BOURGES.2 ¶38]. The Knights of the Round Table are listed among initiation centres [PARIS.6 n4].

> «Le beau poème médiéval fait partie du cycle des romans de la Table Ronde, légendes hermétiques traditionnelles renouvelées des fables grecques.» (*The fine medieval poem belongs to the cycle of the romances of the Round Table, traditional hermetic legends renewed from the Greek fables.*) [BOURGES.1 ¶7]

> «les Philosophes ne parlent clairement que lorsqu’ils veulent écarter les profanes de leur Table ronde.» (*the Philosophers speak clearly only when they want to keep the profane away from their Round Table.*) [PARIS.3 ¶34]

### Ruskin — *Ruskin* {#e442}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named with his book The Bible of Amiens among those who read the wheel-fire quatrefoil as Ezekiel's vision [AMIENS ¶4]. Durand cites him for the remark that the faithless fool is always barefoot in twelfth- and thirteenth-century miniatures [AMIENS ¶11].

> «Ruskin remarque que le fou infidèle est toujours, dans les miniatures du XIIe et XIIIe siècle, représenté nu-pieds.» (*Ruskin observes that the faithless fool is always, in the miniatures of the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, shown barefoot.*) [AMIENS ¶11]

### Règles du Philalèthe — Rules of Philalethes for Conducting the Hermetic Work — *Règles du Philalèthe pour se conduire dans l'Œuvre hermétique* {#e452}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Printed in Lenglet-Dufresnoy's Histoire de la Philosophie hermétique, Paris, Coustelier, 1742, vol. II [AMIENS n3]. Cited for the 'tempered heat', which may hold lead or tin molten and more, and for the fifteenth rule: the work must boil night and day, otherwise it is a digestion, not a cooking [AMIENS ¶8; AMIENS ¶9].

> «Car je vous assure que si nous ne faisons bouillir, nous ne pouvons jamais nommer notre ouvrage une cuisson, mais une digestion.» (*For I assure you that if we do not boil, we can never call our work a cooking, but a digestion.*) [AMIENS ¶9]

## S

### Sabine Stuart de Chevalier — *Sabine Stuart de Chevalier* {#e382}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of the Discours philosophique sur les Trois Principes, or Clef du Sanctuaire philosophique, Paris, Quillau, 1781 [PARIS.5 n4]. Cited for the claim that philosophical mercury is had by dissolving common mercury without loss of weight into philosophical water, quickly [PARIS.5 ¶7]. Fulcanelli rejects this, says so from experience, and advises reading this author with the whole salt-cellar rather than a grain of salt [PARIS.5 ¶7].

> «S’il convient de lire les Philosophes, — selon le conseil de Philalèthe, — avec un grain de sel, il conviendrait (127) d’utiliser la salière entière à l’étude de Stuart de Chevalier.» (*If the Philosophers are to be read, as Philalethes advises, with a grain of salt, the whole salt-cellar should be used for the study of Stuart de Chevalier.*) [PARIS.5 ¶7]

### Sabot (top / clog) — *sabot* {#e117}

*term; Gloss: the text defines the sabot of the Alleluia custom as a top shaped like a Tau or Cross [INTRO.1 n4]; the word also means clog.*

At Langres the choirboys whipped their humming sabots, spinning tops, out of the nave [INTRO.1 ¶15]. The note calls it a top in the shape of a Tau or Cross; in cabala sabot equals cabot or chabot, Puss in Boots; the Epiphany cake sometimes holds a sabot instead of a bean [INTRO.1 n4]. The little figure in the Kings' cake is called the bean, the sabot, the cradle or the cross of honour: the gold in its bath [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. A ceiling panel of the Hôtel Lallemant chapel shows a child urinating into his sabot [BOURGES.2 ¶24, BOURGES.2 ¶28].

> «Toupie au profil de Tau ou Croix. En cabale, sabot équivaut à cabot ou chabot, le chat botté des Contes de ma Mère l’Oie.» (*A top with the profile of a Tau or Cross. In cabala, sabot is equivalent to cabot or chabot, the Puss in Boots of the Tales of Mother Goose.*) [INTRO.1 n4]

> «c’est l’or dans son bain, le baigneur ; c’est la fève, le sabot, le berceau ou la croix d’honneur» (*it is the gold in its bath, the bather; it is the bean, the sabot, the cradle or the cross of honour*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

### Sacred Wells — *Puits sacrés* {#e283}

*term; Literal translation.*

Wells possessed by most Gothic churches in the Middle Ages, in which, the text says, the esoteric tradition of the Fountain of Life or of Youth was materialised; their water often passed for curative [PARIS.2 ¶21]. Examples: the well of Saint-Germain-des-Prés (Abbo), the well of Saint-Marcel in Paris (Gregory of Tours), the 'Puits de la Sainte-Vierge' at Notre-Dame de l'Epine and the well in the choir of Notre-Dame de Limoux with its Latin inscription [PARIS.2 ¶21–23]. The text then announces it will return to this 'eau pontique' [PARIS.2 ¶24].

> «L’eau qu’on y puisait passait le plus souvent pour avoir des vertus curatives, et on l’employait dans le traitement de certaines maladies.» (*The water drawn there was most often held to have healing virtues, and it was used in treating certain illnesses.*) [PARIS.2 ¶21]

### Saint Augustine — *saint Augustin* {#e151}

*person; Name as spelled in the text, with the usual English form.*

The text attributes to him the 'Dispute avec Tryphon' (Dialogus cum Tryphone, 40), quoted on the paschal lamb as figure of Christ and, roasted whole on crossed spits, as symbol of the cross [INTRO.4 ¶3].

> «Or, cet agneau, que la loi prescrivait de faire rôtir en entier, était le symbole de la croix que le Christ devait endurer.» (*Now this lamb, which the law prescribed to be roasted whole, was the symbol of the cross that Christ was to endure.*) [INTRO.4 ¶3]

### Saint Christopher — *saint Christophe* {#e243}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; the usual English form.*

A colossal grey-painted statue at Notre-Dame, erected 1413, was destroyed in 1781; others at Auxerre and Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie perished; Amiens and Seville keep theirs [INTRO.9 ¶14]. Jacobus de Voragine gives his first name Offerus; for the people the name means 'who carries Christ', but phonetic cabala reads Chrysophe, 'who carries gold' [INTRO.9 ¶15]. He is the hieroglyph of solar sulphur (Jesus) or nascent gold, raised on the mercurial waves and carried by Mercury to the power of the Elixir [INTRO.9 ¶15]; he indicates the nature of the hidden matter [INTRO.9 ¶16]. At the Hôtel Lallemant a painted stone bas-relief in the loggia follows the legend closely [BOURGES.2 ¶1; BOURGES.2 ¶8–12]; his belt, marked with crossed lines, is the Sign of the canonically prepared mercurial dissolvent [BOURGES.2 ¶13–14]. He carries Jesus as the mercury carries the mystic gold [BOURGES.2 ¶23].

> «Christophe est mis pour Chrysophe : qui porte l’or» (*Christopher stands for Chrysophe: who carries gold*) [INTRO.9 ¶15]

> «C’est cette ceinture qui donne à saint Christophe son véritable caractère ésotérique.» (*It is this belt that gives Saint Christopher his true esoteric character.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶13]

### Saint Chrysostom — *saint Chrysostome* {#e032}

*person; Name as spelled in the text ('saint Jean Chrysostome' in the note).*

A note gives the source of the Book of Seth legend about the star awaited by the Magi as the Opus imperfectum in Mattheum, printed with the works of St John Chrysostom [INTRO.7 n3, INTRO.7 ¶3, INTRO.7 ¶4, INTRO.7 ¶6]. Canseliet: Chrysostom teaches that the sign that led the Magi came to rest on the Saviour's head before vanishing [PREF2 ¶26].

> «Opus imperfectum in Mattheum. Hom. II, joint aux Œuvres de saint Jean Chrysostome, Patr. grecque, t. LVI, p. 637.» (*Opus imperfectum in Mattheum, Homily II, printed with the Works of St John Chrysostom, Greek Patrology, vol. LVI, p. 637.*) [INTRO.7 n3]

> «Le signe qui conduisit les mages vers la caverne de Bethléem, nous apprend saint Chrysostome, vint, avant de disparaître, se poser sur la tête du Saveur et l’environner d’une gloire lumineuse.» (*The sign that led the magi to the cave of Bethlehem, St Chrysostom tells us, came before vanishing to rest on the Saviour's head and surround it with a luminous glory.*) [PREF2 ¶26]

### Saint Ignatius — *saint Ignace* {#e203}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Quoted on the star of the Nativity: its light surpassed all others, its brightness was ineffable, and sun, moon and stars formed its chorus [INTRO.7 ¶14]. Source given as the Epistle to the Ephesians, c. XIX [INTRO.7 n6]. Fulcanelli notes that Huginus à Barma uses the same terms for the matter of the Great Work on which the star appears [INTRO.7 ¶15].

> «Le soleil, la lune et les autres astres formaient le chœur de cette étoile.» (*The sun, the moon and the other stars formed the chorus of this star.*) [INTRO.7 ¶14]

### Saint John — *saint Jean* {#e074}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Fulcanelli quotes St John's vision of the four animals before the throne, matching Ezekiel's, in his account of the four ages of the world at Hendaye [HENDAYE ¶11, HENDAYE ¶12, HENDAYE ¶13]. Canseliet cites from St John the parable of the grain that must die to bear fruit, and the raising of Lazarus, against the Church's acceptance of cremation [PREF3 ¶15, PREF3 ¶16, PREF3 ¶17, PREF3 ¶18].

> «Quel horizon immense, découvre cependant, la parabole du grain confié au sol, que rapporta saint Jean» (*What an immense horizon is opened, however, by the parable of the grain entrusted to the soil, which St John reported*) [PREF3 ¶15]

### Saint Marcel — *saint Marcel* {#e081}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (also Marcel, Marcellus).*

Bishop of Paris [PREF3 ¶23], pupil of Prudence [PARIS.7 ¶31]. On the Notre-Dame trumeau he is shown blessing, beardless, his crosier touching the dragon's mouth [PARIS.7 ¶32]; the legend has him kill the dragon by touching it with his crosier [PARIS.7 ¶35]. St Michael, St George and St Marcel slaying the Dragon are Christian replicas of Perseus, figures of the combat of chemical bodies yielding the secret dissolvent [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. A well near his tomb had curative water [PARIS.2 ¶21]. Canseliet: he alone among saints touches the monster's head with his crosier; his legend of the serpent and the dead lady is a hermetic allegory of the dry and humid ways [PREF3 ¶40, PREF3 ¶41, PREF3 ¶42, PREF3 ¶43].

> «Il y verra l’évêque tuant le dragon en le touchant de sa crosse, ainsi que le rapporte la tradition.» (*He will see there the bishop killing the dragon by touching it with his crosier, as tradition reports.*) [PARIS.7 ¶35]

> «saint Michel, saint Georges, saint Marcel terrassant le Dragon, répliques chrétiennes de Persée» (*St Michael, St George, St Marcel striking down the Dragon, Christian replicas of Perseus*) [BOURGES.1 ¶9]

### Saint Marcel pillar — *pilier de saint Marcel* {#e080}

*place; Name as used in the text (trumeau of the porch of Sainte-Anne).*

The trumeau of Notre-Dame's south (Sainte-Anne) portal, which 'describes the shortest practice of our Science' [PARIS.7 ¶1]. The statue of St Marcel has no secret meaning; only the die and socle are hermetic [PARIS.7 ¶11]. It was remade and altered, losing its key [PARIS.7 ¶12, PARIS.7 ¶14]; the original went to Cluny [PARIS.7 ¶16]. Reading: the bezants are the two natures, subject and dissolvent, turning into rosettes and calyxed flowers as their roots open [PARIS.7 ¶18]; the die shows the king rising from the flames, the 'salt of the philosophers', with triple crown per Gobineau [PARIS.7 ¶19]; the residue gives the mercurial salt or 'Babylonian Dragon' [PARIS.7 ¶20]; the dragon covered with solar signs can transmute in quantity, by multiplications [PARIS.7 ¶27]. Cambriel's account is rejected as faked [PARIS.7 ¶31, PARIS.7 ¶32]. Canseliet defends this correction against Grillot de Givry [PREF3 ¶23, PREF3 ¶50].

> «Il ne nous offre qu’un seul motif, mais l’intérêt en est considérable, parce qu’il décrit la pratique la plus courte de notre Science» (*It offers us only one motif, but its interest is considerable, because it describes the shortest practice of our Science*) [PARIS.7 ¶1]

> «Dé et socle ont, seuls, une réelle valeur hermétique» (*The die and the socle alone have real hermetic value.*) [PARIS.7 ¶11]

### Saint Matthew — *saint Matthieu* {#e541}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

His Gospel is that of the iron age [HENDAYE ¶15]. The text derives Matthæus from Greek Μαθημα, 'science', making it the Gospel according to Science, last of all but first for us, teaching that all but a few elect must perish [HENDAYE ¶15]. The angel is his attribute because science alone can give man wings to rise to God [HENDAYE ¶15]. His account of the Magi and the star is quoted in the introduction [INTRO.7 ¶23–28].

> «C’est l’Evangile selon la Science, le dernier de tous, mais le premier pour nous, parce qu’il nous enseigne que, sauf un petit nombre d’élus, nous devons collectivement périr.» (*It is the Gospel according to Science, the last of all, but the first for us, because it teaches us that, save a small number of elect, we must collectively perish.*) [HENDAYE ¶15]

### Saint Paul — *saint Paul* {#e460}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited as witness that Jesus confided the Verbum dimissum to his Apostles [AMIENS ¶22]; the quoted passage (Epistle to the Colossians I, 25–26 [AMIENS n5]) speaks of the secret hidden from all time and now shown to those judged worthy [AMIENS ¶23].

> «C’est-à-dire le SECRET qui a été caché de tout temps et de tout âge, mais qu’il manifeste maintenant à ceux qu’il en juge dignes.» (*That is to say the SECRET which has been hidden from all time and every age, but which he now makes manifest to those he judges worthy of it.*) [AMIENS ¶23]

### Saint Peter — *saint Pierre* {#e456}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

The cock is the attribute of saint Pierre, the true and flowing 'stone' on which the Christian edifice rests; after the third reiteration of the technique 'the cock will have crowed three times' [AMIENS ¶15]. As first Apostle he holds the two crossed keys of solution and coagulation and is the symbol of the volatile stone that fire makes fixed and dense; the text adds that he was crucified head downward [AMIENS ¶15]. In the third preface Canseliet speaks of occult forces aiming to destroy 'l’œuvre de Pierre' [PREF3 ¶14].

> «Alors le coq, attribut de saint Pierre, pierre véritable et fluente sur laquelle repose l’édifice chrétien, le coq aura chanté trois fois.» (*Then the cock, attribute of Saint Peter, the true and flowing stone on which the Christian edifice rests, the cock will have crowed three times.*) [AMIENS ¶15]

> «c’est lui qui est le symbole de la pierre volatile que le feu rend fixe et dense en la précipitant.» (*it is he who is the symbol of the volatile stone that fire makes fixed and dense by precipitating it.*) [AMIENS ¶15]

### Saint Thomas Aquinas — *saint Thomas d'Aquin* {#e432}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

An escutcheon attributed to him was imitated by Flamel on his arcade at the charnel house of the Innocents [PARIS.8 ¶2]. The type escutcheon, less obscure than Flamel's, existed until the Revolution on a window lighting the chapel of Saint-Thomas-d'Aquin in the Jacobins' convent, beside which were the schools of the 'Angelic Doctor' [PARIS.8 ¶4]. It was drawn and painted in 1787 from the window by a hermetist named Chaudet [PARIS.8 ¶5].

> «Les Curiositez de Paris, parues en 1716 chez Saugrain l’aîné, ajoutent qu’à côté de l’église se trouvaient les écoles du Docteur angélique.» (*The Curiositez de Paris, published in 1716 by Saugrain the elder, add that beside the church were the schools of the Angelic Doctor.*) [PARIS.8 ¶4]

### Saint-Germain-des-Prés — *Saint-Germain-des-Prés* {#e286}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

The 'célèbre abbatiale' whose well, bored at the back of the sanctuary, had marvellous water according to Abbo [PARIS.2 ¶21]. Witkowski (quoted) says a statue analogous to the Isis of Metz existed at Saint-Germain-des-Prés [INTRO.8 ¶13].

> «lequel était foré au fond du sanctuaire de la célèbre abbatiale.» (*which was bored at the back of the sanctuary of the famous abbey church.*) [PARIS.2 ¶21]

### Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie — *Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie* {#e121}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

A Paris church that Victor Hugo called a complete hieroglyph of hermetic science [INTRO.2 ¶4]. Flamel had its small portal built in 1389, covered with figures, including an angel holding a stone circle set with black marble and a fine gold cross (per abbé Vilain) [PARIS.8 ¶2]. Its statue of Saint Christopher perished with the building [INTRO.9 ¶14].

> «L’abbé Vilain nous apprend que le petit portail de Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie, que l’Adepte fit exécuter en 1389, était couvert de figures.» (*Abbé Vilain tells us that the small portal of Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie, which the Adept had made in 1389, was covered with figures.*) [PARIS.8 ¶2]

### Sainte-Chapelle — *Sainte-Chapelle* {#e194}

*place; Name as spelled in the text.*

Masterpiece of Pierre de Montereau, built 1245–1248 to receive the relics of the Passion, presenting a remarkable alchemical ensemble [PARIS.8 ¶8]. Its original portal, with two angels described by Victor Hugo (one with a hand in a vase, the other in a cloud), was remade, but its south windows survive intact [PARIS.8 ¶8]. The text offers a specimen from the fifth bay, first mullion, on the Massacre of the Innocents [PARIS.8 ¶8; PARIS.6 ¶22] and recommends the symbolic windows of the upper chapel and the great rose [PARIS.8 ¶8]. Its splendid blue rose is listed among the six-petalled star roses [INTRO.6 ¶14].

> «Il semble difficile de rencontrer ailleurs une collection plus considérable, sur les formules de l’ésotérisme alchimique, que celle de la Sainte-Chapelle.» (*It seems hard to find anywhere else a larger collection on the formulas of alchemical esotericism than that of the Sainte-Chapelle.*) [PARIS.8 ¶8]

### Salamander — *Salamandre* {#e312}

*term; Literal translation.*

The disc held by the figure of Calcination at Paris, the beast 'that lives in fire and feeds on fire' [PARIS.3 ¶19]. Stated: this fabulous lizard designates nothing other than the central salt, incombustible and fixed, which keeps its nature even in the ashes of calcined metals, called by the Ancients metallic Seed [PARIS.3 ¶19]. Cyrano Bergerac's duel of the Remora and the Salamander is, for Fulcanelli, the best and most initiatic description of the combat of the conjunction [PARIS.5 ¶12; BOURGES.1 ¶9]. The Porte-Rouge of Notre-Dame is decorated with salamanders [INTRO.2 ¶5]. Identified with the central salt (e313).

> «Ce lézard fabuleux ne désigne pas autre chose que le sel central, incombustible et fixe, qui garde sa nature jusque dans les cendres des métaux calcinés» (*This fabulous lizard designates nothing other than the central salt, incombustible and fixed, which keeps its nature even in the ashes of calcined metals*) [PARIS.3 ¶19]

Identified in the text with: [Central salt](#e313)

### Salomon Trismosin — *Salomon Trismosin* {#e280}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Author of La Toyson d'Or [PARIS.2 n8; PARIS.7 n4]. One figure of his book is almost the same as Abraham's third: an oak girdled at its foot by a golden crown gives birth to the hidden stream; white birds in its branches, a sleeping raven which a poorly dressed man on a ladder is about to seize, and two richly dressed sophists arguing without seeing the oak or the fountain [PARIS.2 ¶20]. He is the 'auteur célèbre' quoted for the short way: the body buried in an earthen vessel, a fire made in the earth that holds the glass [PARIS.7 ¶7; PARIS.7 n4]. His Toison d'Or comments the ludus puerorum, shown as a child on a hobby-horse [BOURGES.2 ¶28].

> «Signalons encore une figure du livre de Trismosin, qui est, à très peu près, semblable à la troisième d’Abraham.» (*Let us also point out a figure of Trismosin's book, which is very nearly the same as the third of Abraham.*) [PARIS.2 ¶20]

### Salt — *sel* {#e511}

*term; Literal translation.*

Several salts are named. After putrefaction and calcination a pure salt is born, clothed by cooking with the occult power of fire [BOURGES.2 ¶25]; the three flaming pomegranates stand for fire corporified in the red salt that is the philosophic Sulphur [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. In the cohobation the occult salt of the Red Lion is extracted with the spirit of the Green Lion [PARIS.5 ¶2]; the old man (dissolvent) meets the crowned king, the fixed, incombustible, pure metallic salt [PARIS.5 ¶12]. The Sign on the prepared mercury is called Sel des Sages, 'Sel' standing for 'Scel' (seal) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Elsewhere: the Salamander is the central salt, fixed and incombustible [PARIS.3 ¶19]; the sel des philosophes is the king crowned with glory born in the fire [PARIS.7 ¶19]; Sel céleste is the Philosophers' mercury in the dry way [PARIS.7 ¶4].

> «il naît alors un sel pur, que la cuisson revêt de la puissance occulte du feu» (*a pure salt is then born, which the cooking clothes with the occult power of fire*) [BOURGES.2 ¶25]

> «Et ce signe, les vieux auteurs l’ont appelé Sceau d’Hermes, Sel des Sages (Sel mis pour Scel), — ce qui jette la confusion dans l’esprit des chercheurs» (*And this sign the old authors called Seal of Hermes, Salt of the Sages (Sel put for Scel, seal) — which confuses the minds of seekers*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

### Salt of the philosophers — *sel des philosophes* {#e421}

*term; Literal translation.*

In the sublimation, the last fiery purification of the united compound, the pure incombustible principles rise in a different form: this is the salt of the philosophers, the king crowned with glory, born in the fire, who must rejoice in the following marriage [PARIS.7 ¶19]. The text quotes Hermes: Rex ab igne veniet, ac conjugio gaudebit et occulta patebunt [PARIS.7 ¶19]. On the Saint Anne dado only the king's head shows, rising from the flames; Gobineau de Montluisant says he wears a triple crown [PARIS.7 ¶19].

> «C’est là le sel des philosophes, le roi couronné de gloire, qui prend naissance dans le feu et doit se réjouir dans le mariage subséquent, afin, dit Hermès, que les choses occultes deviennent manifestes.» (*This is the salt of the philosophers, the king crowned with glory, who is born in the fire and must rejoice in the subsequent marriage, so that, says Hermes, hidden things may become manifest.*) [PARIS.7 ¶19]

### Saturn — *Saturne* {#e044}

*term; Usual English term.*

Black was given to Saturn, in spagyrics the hieroglyph of lead [PARIS.3 ¶27]. The 'reign of Saturn' at the start of the second work is the emblem of radical dissolution, decomposition and black [PARIS.5 ¶9]. The old man of the seventh medallion, 'our Mercury', is identified by the texts with Saturn who devoured his children, once painted green; Saturn as dissolvent is very old (the god Sèr with green flesh) [PARIS.5 ¶11]. The short way is called the 'Regimen of Saturn' [PARIS.7 ¶7]. The union of the two natures gives 'the Saturn of the Sages', an anagram of natures [PARIS.7 ¶22]. The crude subject is called Son or Child of Saturn, Lead of the Sages [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. Canseliet quotes Philalèthe: Saturn sees his image in the mirror of Mars [PREF2 ¶37].

> «le duo des natures que l’alchimiste travaille et dont la réunion fournit le Saturne des Sages, dénomination anagrammatique de natures.» (*the pair of natures the alchemist works, whose union yields the Saturn of the Sages, an anagrammatic name for natures.*) [PARIS.7 ¶22]

> «C’est le règne de Saturne qui va paraître, emblème de la dissolution radicale, de la décomposition et de la couleur noire.» (*It is the reign of Saturn that is about to appear, emblem of radical dissolution, of decomposition and of the black colour.*) [PARIS.5 ¶9]

Identified in the text with: [Natures](#e183)

### Scallop of Compostela — *Mérelle de Compostelle* {#e478}

*term; Literal translation of the name; mérelle is the pilgrim's scallop shell (coquille Saint-Jacques) as the text says.*

The scallop (coquille Saint-Jacques), paired with the heart on Jacques Cœur's palace, was the badge of pilgrims to Saint-Jacques [BOURGES.1 ¶5]. Stated: in secret symbolism it designates the principle Mercury, also called Traveller or Pilgrim, and is carried mystically by all who undertake the work and seek the star (compos stella) [BOURGES.1 ¶5]; Mercury is the Philosophers' blessed water, and large shells held holy water [BOURGES.1 n1]. Alchemists must make the long pilgrimage, half by land and half by sea, with staff and mérelle — 'pilgrims first, pilots after' [BOURGES.1 ¶5]. At the Hôtel Lallemant it recurs on the ceiling [BOURGES.2 ¶28] and on the credence, with wings and Neptune's trident, again marking the aqueous, volatile principle, the philosophic mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶30].

> «La Mérelle de Compostelle (pl. XXXIX), sur laquelle nous aurions bien des choses à remarquer, sert, dans la symbolique secrète, à désigner le principe Mercure, appelé encore Voyageur ou Pèlerin.» (*The Scallop of Compostela (pl. XXXIX), about which we would have many things to note, serves, in secret symbolism, to designate the principle Mercury, also called Traveller or Pilgrim.*) [BOURGES.1 ¶5]

> «Elle est portée mystiquement par tous ceux qui entreprennent le travail et cherchent à obtenir l’étoile (compos stella).» (*It is worn mystically by all who undertake the work and seek to obtain the star (compos stella).*) [BOURGES.1 ¶5]

### Seal of Hermes — *Sceau d'Hermes* {#e298}

*term; Literal translation of 'Sceau d'Hermes'; the text gives the names itself [BOURGES.2 ¶14].*

Stated: the 'Signe' that all philosophers recognise as marking outwardly the virtue, perfection and purity of their mercurial substance: crossed lines on the surface of the dissolvent when it has been canonically prepared, as on Saint Christopher's (Offerus's) belt at Bourges [BOURGES.2 ¶10, BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The whole work of the art consists in raising this mercury until it bears this sign [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Old authors called it Seal of Hermes, Salt of the Sages ('Sel' put for 'Scel', seal), the mark and imprint of the Almighty, his signature, Star of the Magi, Pole Star [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The pattern appears more clearly when gold is dissolved in the mercury [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The Epiphany cake is 'signée' like the matter [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Interpreted: the 'sceau magique', the six-pointed Star of the Magi on the compost that shows the mixture was canonically prepared, is the same sign [INTRO.6 ¶13]. The Raven is called by contrast the 'sceau canonique de l'Œuvre' [PARIS.3 ¶3].

> «Et ce signe, les vieux auteurs l’ont appelé Sceau d’Hermes, Sel des Sages (Sel mis pour Scel), — ce qui jette la confusion dans l’esprit des chercheurs, — la marque et l’empreinte du Tout-Puissant, sa signature» (*And this sign the old authors called the Seal of Hermes, Salt of the Sages (Salt put for Seal), which confuses seekers, the mark and imprint of the Almighty, his signature*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

Identified in the text with: [Star of the Magi](#e192)

### Seal of Solomon — *Sceau de Salomon* {#e193}

*term; The usual English term.*

The six-petalled star roses of several churches reproduce the traditional Seal of Solomon [INTRO.6 ¶14]. A footnote says the plant called Seal of Solomon owes the name to its stem, whose cross-section is starred like the magic sign attributed to the king of the Israelites, son of David [INTRO.6 n2]. On the Hôtel Lallemant chapel ceiling the starred seal of the king-magus Solomon on top of a pile of ingots fixes the sense of a ceiling panel: mercury below, the Absolute above [BOURGES.2 ¶24; BOURGES.2 ¶28]. The union of the two triangles of fire and water, sulphur and mercury, generates the six-pointed star, hieroglyph of the Work and of the realized Philosophers' Stone [BOURGES.2 ¶28].

> «C’est l’union des deux triangles du feu et de l’eau, ou du soufre et du mercure assemblés en un seul corps, qui génère l’astre à six pointes, hiéroglyphe de l’Œuvre par excellence et de la Pierre Philosophale réalisée.» (*It is the union of the two triangles of fire and water, or of sulphur and mercury joined in a single body, that generates the six-pointed star, hieroglyph of the Work par excellence and of the realized Philosophers' Stone.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶28]

### Secret fire — *feu secret* {#e037}

*term; Literal translation.*

The occult agent of the Work, distinct from common heat. Besides heat a second agent is needed, the 'secret or philosophical fire', which, excited by common heat, turns the wheel [INTRO.6 ¶3]. Philosophical calcination needs this agent, more like water than flame: the vital spark given by the Creator to inert matter, the spirit enclosed in things; Fulcanelli calls it the highest secret, which stopped him twenty years, and names it 'aqueous fire' or 'igneous water' [PARIS.3 ¶20]. Limojon: not actually hot, an igneous spirit put into a subject of the Stone's nature, which calcines, dissolves, sublimes [PARIS.3 ¶22]. It is the 'secret fire enclosed in a water', the universal dissolvent [PARIS.3 ¶23]; the only agent able to open, sublime and putrefy [PARIS.3 ¶11]. As 'fire of nature' it is a spirit spread over the globe, to be captured in a receptive body [PARIS.4 ¶13]. Canseliet: used with the universal spirit to separate heaven from earth [PREF2 ¶32].

> «Ce feu, ou cette eau ardente, est l’étincelle vitale communiquée par le Créateur à la matière inerte ; c’est l’esprit enclos dans les choses, le rayon igné, impérissable, enfermé au fond de l’obscure substance, informe et frigide.» (*This fire, or this burning water, is the vital spark communicated by the Creator to inert matter; it is the spirit enclosed in things, the igneous ray, imperishable, shut in the depths of the dark, formless and frigid substance.*) [PARIS.3 ¶20]

> «C’est ce dernier feu, excité par la chaleur vulgaire, qui fait tourner la roue et provoque les divers phénomènes que l’artiste observe dans son vaisseau» (*It is this latter fire, excited by common heat, that turns the wheel and brings about the various phenomena the artist observes in his vessel*) [INTRO.6 ¶3]

### Secret of Secrets — *Secret des Secrets* {#e459}

*term; Literal translation; the Latin form secretum secretorum is the text's own.*

Used twice for different points. At Amiens it names the great mystery of where to find the proper agent lost in metallurgical reduction, set beside the Verbum dimissum that Jesus confided to his Apostles according to saint Paul [AMIENS ¶21–22]. At Bourges the masters of the Middle Ages are said to have called 'secretum secretorum' the shell of the egg, i.e. the vessel of the Work (RER), which one must fully understand before undertaking anything [BOURGES.2 ¶34].

> «Que pouvons-nous donc dire ici même, en face du Secret des Secrets, devant ce Verbum dimissum dont nous avons déjà fait mention, et que Jésus confia à ses Apôtres, comme le témoigne saint Paul» (*What then can we say here, facing the Secret of Secrets, before that Verbum dimissum we have already mentioned, which Jesus confided to his Apostles, as Saint Paul testifies*) [AMIENS ¶22]

> «N’entreprenez rien, en conséquence, tant que vous n’aurez pas reçu toute la lumière sur cette coquille de l’œuf qualifiée secretum secretorum chez les maîtres du moyen âge.» (*Undertake nothing, therefore, until you have received full light on that shell of the egg which the masters of the Middle Ages called secretum secretorum.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶34]

### Sendivogius — *Sendivogius* {#e503}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited for the statement that prepared mercury looks like a stony, friable, flaky (feuilletée) mass [BOURGES.2 ¶16], the basis of the names terre feuillée and livre aux feuillets.

> «Sendivogius nous apprend que le mercure préparé offre l’aspect et la forme d’une masse pierreuse, friable et feuilletée.» (*Sendivogius teaches us that the prepared mercury has the look and form of a stony, friable and flaky mass.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶16]

### Senior Zadith — *Senior Zadith* {#e271}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named twice as the more likely author of the Azoth, attributed to Basil Valentine [PARIS.2 ¶8; PARIS.7 ¶5].

> «Dans le traité de l’Azoth, attribué au célèbre moine d’Erfurth, Basile Valentin, et qui serait plutôt l’oeuvre de Senior Zadith» (*In the treatise of the Azoth, attributed to the famous monk of Erfurt, Basil Valentine, and which would rather be the work of Senior Zadith*) [PARIS.2 ¶8]

### Servus fugitivus (runaway slave) — *servus fugitivus* {#e384}

*term; Literal translation of the Latin.*

Name for the solvent needed in the Work. The text states what it is: a mineral and metallic water, solid, brittle, looking like a stone and very easily liquefied; this water coagulated as a stony mass is the Alkahest and the universal Solvent [PARIS.5 ¶7]. It is set against common mercury, which Fulcanelli denies can become such a water [PARIS.5 ¶7].

> «Le servus fugitivus dont nous avons besoin est une eau minérale et métallique, solide, cassante, ayant l’aspect d’une pierre et de liquéfaction très aisée.» (*The servus fugitivus we need is a mineral and metallic water, solid, brittle, having the appearance of a stone and very easily liquefied.*) [PARIS.5 ¶7]

## T

### The Sibyl — *Sibylle* {#e518}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Asked what a Philosopher is, she answered: one who knows how to make glass — quoted in the advice to seek and make the vessel RER first [BOURGES.2 ¶36].

> «La Sibylle, interrogée sur ce qu’était un Philosophe, répondit : « C’est celui qui sait faire le verre. »» (*The Sibyl, asked what a Philosopher was, answered: 'It is he who knows how to make glass.'*) [BOURGES.2 ¶36]

## S

### Sigillum Sapientum — Seal of the Sages — *Sigillum Sapientum* {#e378}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Image of Huginus à Barma in which the artist holds a balance whose one pan outweighs the other in the apparent ratio of one to two; the text says it would correspond to Basile Valentin's advice to give a white swan to the double fiery man [PARIS.5 ¶6].

> «ce qui correspondrait au Sigillum Sapientum d’Huginus à Barma, où l’artiste tient une balance dont un plateau entraîne l’autre selon le rapport apparent d’un à deux» (*which would correspond to the Sigillum Sapientum of Huginus à Barma, where the artist holds a balance one pan of which outweighs the other in the apparent ratio of one to two*) [PARIS.5 ¶6]

### Solar sulphur — *soufre solaire* {#e245}

*term; Literal translation.*

What Saint Christopher signifies: the solar sulphur, identified in parentheses with Jesus, or nascent gold, raised on the mercurial waves and then carried by Mercury's own energy to the power of the Elixir [INTRO.9 ¶15].

> «C’est l’hiéroglyphe du soufre solaire (Jésus), ou de l’or naissant, élevé sur les ondes mercurielles et porté ensuite, par l’énergie propre de ce Mercure, au degré de puissance que possède l’Elixir.» (*It is the hieroglyph of the solar sulphur (Jesus), or of nascent gold, raised on the mercurial waters and then carried, by the proper energy of this Mercury, to the degree of power the Elixir possesses.*) [INTRO.9 ¶15]

### Solar tree — *arbre solaire* {#e391}

*term; Literal translation.*

The tree on the twelfth lower medallion of Paris, with three huge round fruits, a ram at its foot and a bird in its branches, is the solar tree described by the Cosmopolite in the Parable of the Traité de la Nature, from which the water must be extracted [PARIS.5 ¶1, PARIS.5 ¶15]. Saturn, says the Cosmopolite, put the fruit of the solar tree into ten parts of this water, the Water of life [PARIS.5 ¶15]. The medallion illustrates the second solution, of the red or white Sulphur by the philosophical water [PARIS.5 ¶15].

> «On retrouve ici l’arbre solaire que décrit le Cosmopolite dans la Parabole du Traité de la Nature, arbre duquel il faut extraire l’eau.» (*Here we find the solar tree described by the Cosmopolite in the Parable of the Treatise on Nature, a tree from which the water must be extracted.*) [PARIS.5 ¶15]

### Solve et coagula (dissolve and coagulate) — *Solve et coagula* {#e069}

*term; Latin kept; the English follows the book's own French gloss 'dissous et coagule' [PREF3 ¶12].*

Alchemical maxim. At Notre-Dame the 'overturned man' renders it: it teaches the elemental conversion by volatilising the fixed and fixing the volatile, as the quatrain says [PARIS.6 ¶12, PARIS.6 ¶13, PARIS.6 ¶16]. At the Hôtel Lallemant an overturned vase slipping from a lion's mouth is 'an original version of the solve et coagula of Notre-Dame' [BOURGES.2 ¶28]. St Peter holds the two crossed keys of solution and coagulation [AMIENS ¶15]. Canseliet: the ancient apophthegm gives the mode of operating [PREF3 ¶12].

> «l’homme retourné, qui traduit au mieux l’apophtegme alchimique solve et coagula, lequel enseigne à réaliser la conversion élémentaire en volatilisant le fixe et fixant le volatil» (*the overturned man, who best renders the alchemical apophthegm solve et coagula, which teaches how to carry out the elemental conversion by volatilising the fixed and fixing the volatile*) [PARIS.6 ¶12]

> «Solve et coagula ; dissous et coagule.» (*Solve et coagula; dissolve and coagulate. (French gloss is the book's.)*) [PREF3 ¶12]

### Songe de Polyphile — The Dream of Poliphilo — *Songe de Polyphile* {#e530}

*work; Name as spelled in the text; English the usual title.*

Named only as the work whose analysis by Grasset d'Orcet, in the Revue Britannique, sets out the rules for reading in the secret language [HENDAYE ¶9].

> «Quelques auteurs, et particulièrement Grasset d’Orcet, dans l’analyse du Songe de Polyphile, publiée par la Revue Britannique, les ont données assez clairement pour nous dispenser d’en parler après eux.» (*Some authors, and particularly Grasset d'Orcet, in the analysis of the Dream of Poliphilo published by the Revue Britannique, have given them clearly enough to spare us from speaking of them after them.*) [HENDAYE ¶9]

### Soufflot — *Soufflot* {#e126}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

His vandalism destroyed much of what the 16th-century souffleur could admire at Notre-Dame [INTRO.2 ¶7].

> «Le vandalisme de Soufflot a détruit en grande partie ce qu’au XVIe siècle le souffleur pouvait admirer.» (*Soufflot's vandalism destroyed in large part what the souffleur could admire in the 16th century.*) [INTRO.2 ¶7]

### Spagyric art — *spagyrie* {#e047}

*term; Usual English term.*

The 'old Spagyrie' greets the visitor at Notre-Dame with hermetic Philosophy [PARIS.1 ¶12]. The 'spagyric expression' of calcination is a likeness authors use; the Initiate's calcination differs fundamentally from the laboratory one [PARIS.3 ¶20]. In spagyrics Saturn is the hieroglyph of lead [PARIS.3 ¶27]; Paracelsus says black and white belong to the spagyric art [PARIS.3 ¶30]. In spagyric notation crosses mark the imperfect metals [PARIS.6 ¶10]; a graphic sign in the spagyric art stands for the vulgar name of the subject [BOURGES.2 ¶27]. Canseliet: 'lesser spagyric operations' beside the Great Work [PREF2 ¶40].

> «La Philosophie hermétique, la vieille Spagyrie lui souhaitent la bienvenue dans l’église gothique, le temple alchimique par excellence.» (*Hermetic Philosophy, the old Spagyric art, bid him welcome into the Gothic church, the alchemical temple par excellence.*) [PARIS.1 ¶12]

> «Telle est, du moins, l’expression spagyrique de la calcination, similitude dont usent les Auteurs pour servir d’exemple à l’idée générale que l’on doit avoir du travail hermétique.» (*Such at least is the spagyric expression of calcination, a likeness the Authors use as an example of the general idea one should have of the hermetic work.*) [PARIS.3 ¶20]

### Star — *étoile* {#e024}

*term; Literal translation.*

The sign of the Work. The star is the signature of the initial subject, as the Raven is the canonical seal of the Work [PARIS.3 ¶3]. The six-pointed 'Star of the Magi' radiating on the compost is a magic seal showing the mixture was canonically prepared [INTRO.6 ¶13]; the star guides the alchemist and announces the Saviour's birth [INTRO.6 ¶15]. The crossed lines on a properly prepared dissolvent are the Sign called Seal of Hermes, Star of the Magi, Pole star [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. The six-pointed star, union of the fire and water triangles, is the hieroglyph of the Work and of the realised Stone [BOURGES.2 ¶28]. At Amiens a master shows three disciples the 'hermetic star', and Rollin's rebus 'Seule' means 'the single star' [AMIENS ¶40]. Canseliet: the star is single yet double; one seen in the mirror, the other in the chemical heaven [PREF2 ¶21, PREF2 ¶26]; it also appears in lesser operations, always signalling transformation [PREF2 ¶40]; Fulcanelli dwelt on it from the start [PREF2 ¶44].

> «Le Corbeau est, en quelque sorte, le sceau canonique de l'Œuvre, comme l’étoile est la signature du sujet initial.» (*The Raven is, so to speak, the canonical seal of the Work, as the star is the signature of the initial subject.*) [PARIS.3 ¶3]

> «Le maître désigne à trois disciples l’astre hermétique sur lequel nous nous sommes déjà longuement étendu, l’étoile traditionnelle qui sert de guide aux Philosophes et leur indique la naissance du fils du soleil» (*The master points out to three disciples the hermetic star on which we have already dwelt at length, the traditional star that guides the Philosophers and shows them the birth of the son of the sun*) [AMIENS ¶40]

### Star of the Magi — *Etoile des Mages* {#e192}

*term; Literal translation.*

A radiating six-pointed figure (digamma) that shines on the surface of the compound, that is, above the crib where Jesus the Child-King lies; this magic seal shows the artist he has followed the right path and that the philosophic mixture was prepared canonically [INTRO.6 ¶13]. It is the star that guides the alchemist and announces the birth of the Saviour, whence the gathered texts on the Magi's star [INTRO.6 ¶15; INTRO.7 ¶1–36]. Among the names of the Sign of crossed lines that marks the canonically prepared mercurial dissolvent, the text lists Seal of Hermes, Salt of the Sages, the mark and signature of the Almighty, Star of the Magi, Pole Star [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Elsewhere the star is called the signature of the initial subject [PARIS.3 ¶3]. Chalcidius is said to have preserved a mention of the star of the Magi [INTRO.7 ¶16].

> «C’est une figure radiée, à six pointes (digamma), dite Etoile des Mages, qui rayonne à la surface du compost, c'est-à-dire au-dessus de la crèche où repose Jésus, l’Enfant-Roi.» (*It is a radiating figure with six points (digamma), called Star of the Magi, which shines on the surface of the compound, that is, above the crib where Jesus, the Child-King, lies.*) [INTRO.6 ¶13]

> «Ce sceau magique révèle à l’artiste qu’il a suivi le bon chemin, et que la mixtion philosophale a été préparée canoniquement.» (*This magic seal reveals to the artist that he has followed the right path, and that the philosophic mixture has been prepared canonically.*) [INTRO.6 ¶13]

Identified in the text with: [Seal of Hermes](#e298)

### Steel — *acier* {#e042}

*term; Literal translation.*

Name the Adepts give to what they draw from the belly of Aries, also called their magnet (Pernety) [PARIS.3 ¶23]. The anonymous Clef du Cabinet hermétique: the Cosmopolite describes the water of the Work under the names steel and magnet, since it attracts the influences of heaven; this steel is found in Aries, the beginning of spring [PARIS.4 ¶14]. Philalèthe's recipe speaks of 'our igneous dragon which hides our magic steel in its belly' [PREF2 ¶37].

> «Les Adeptes disent qu’ils tirent leur acier du ventre d’Ariès, et ils appellent aussi cet acier leur aimant.» (*The Adepts say they draw their steel from the belly of Aries, and they also call this steel their magnet.*) [PARIS.3 ¶23]

> «Il dit que cet acier se trouve dans Ariès, qui marque encore le commencement du Printems, lorsque le soleil parcourt le signe du Bélier» (*He says this steel is found in Aries, which also marks the beginning of Spring, when the sun passes through the sign of the Ram*) [PARIS.4 ¶14]

### Stone of the Philosophers — *Pierre des Philosophes* {#e506}

*term; Literal translation.*

Stated: Pierre des Philosophes and Pierre Philosophale are alike in kind and origin; the first is raw, the second, derived from it, is fully cooked and digested [BOURGES.2 ¶20]. The Golden Fleece names both the matter prepared for the Work and the final result [BOURGES.2 ¶20]. The fish is the hieroglyph of 'la pierre des Philosophes dans son premier état' [BOURGES.2 ¶14].

> «Pierre des Philosophes et Pierre Philosophale sont donc deux choses semblables, en espèce et en origine, mais la première est crue, tandis que la seconde, qui en dérive, est parfaitement cuite et digérée.» (*The Stone of the Philosophers and the Philosophers' Stone are thus two similar things, in kind and in origin, but the first is raw, whereas the second, which derives from it, is perfectly cooked and digested.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶20]

### Stone to the white — *pierre au blanc* {#e475}

*term; The text's own gloss: transmutation of base metals into silver (BOURGES.1 ¶3).*

Stated: the gift of the stone to the white is the transmutation of base metals into silver, perhaps the source of Jacques Cœur's title argentier [BOURGES.1 ¶3]. Fulcanelli thinks Jacques Cœur made it, or saw it made, from 'essencified' iron cooked three times [BOURGES.1 ¶4].

> «en d’autres termes de la transmutation des métaux vils en argent. D’où, peut-être, son titre d’argentier.» (*in other words of the transmutation of base metals into silver. Whence, perhaps, his title of argentier.*) [BOURGES.1 ¶3]

### Subject (of the Work) — *sujet* {#e224}

*term; Literal translation of the technical term.*

The first matter the artist must choose to begin the Work. The Black Virgins figure the primitive earth chosen as subject, the first matter as ore, black, heavy and brittle [INTRO.8 ¶4]; the Paris Virgin of the Observatory personifies the mysterious subject of Hermes [INTRO.8 ¶11]. The stylobate of Notre-Dame gives the lapidary name of the subject of the Sages [PARIS.2 ¶2]; the mirror is its hieroglyph [PARIS.5 ¶10], and this vulgar, despised subject becomes the Tree of Life [PARIS.5 ¶4]. The star is the signature of the initial subject [PARIS.3 ¶3]. It contains the indispensable agent [AMIENS ¶27]; a quoted commentator of the Lumière sortant des Ténèbres says it is to be sought in the mineral kingdom only [AMIENS ¶28]. Old authors designated its common name, as found in the mine, by the oak [BOURGES.2 ¶21]; it is also called Magnesia lunarii, Lead of the Sages, Son of Saturn, Terra sancta, with Aries as its celestial sign [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. With the dissolvent it forms the two natures [PARIS.7 ¶18].

> «Toutes les descriptions que nous ont laissées les Philosophes de leur sujet, ou matière première qui contient l’agent indispensable, sont fort confuses et très mystérieuses.» (*All the descriptions the Philosophers have left us of their subject, or first matter containing the indispensable agent, are very confused and most mysterious.*) [AMIENS ¶27]

> «le chêne a toujours été pris, par les vieux auteurs, pour désigner le nom vulgaire du sujet initial, tel qu’on le rencontre dans la mine.» (*the oak has always been used by the old authors to designate the common name of the initial subject, as it is found in the mine.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶21]

### Sublimation — *Sublimation* {#e345}

*term; The usual English term.*

Stated: the series of operations ending in the intimate union of sulphur and mercury; by the reiteration of the Eagles the exalted mercury sheds its gross, earthy parts and superfluous humidity and seizes a portion of the fixed body [PARIS.4 ¶3]. True sublimation brings the light out of the tomb to the surface [PARIS.4 ¶3]. Callimachus's swans describe these sublimations [PARIS.4 ¶5]. The prepared compound must undergo sublimation, the last igneous purification: the pure principles rise as the 'sel des philosophes', the crowned king born of fire [PARIS.7 ¶19]. Trismosin (quoted) calls his short method 'la vraye sublimation philosophique' [PARIS.7 ¶7]. The Doves of Diana hide the spiritualisation and sublimation of mercury [PARIS.6 ¶12]. The secret fire alone can open, sublime and putrefy (quoted) [PARIS.3 ¶11]. Identified with the Eagles (e344).

> «C’est par la réitération des Aigles ou Sublimation philosophique que le mercure exalté se dépouille de ses parties grossières et terrestres, de son humidité superflue, et s’empare d’une portion du corps fixe» (*It is by the reiteration of the Eagles or philosophical Sublimation that the exalted mercury strips off its gross and earthy parts, its superfluous humidity, and takes hold of a portion of the fixed body*) [PARIS.4 ¶3]

> «Les matières préparées et unies en un seul composé doivent subir la sublimation ou dernière purification ignée.» (*The matters prepared and united in one compound must undergo sublimation or last igneous purification.*) [PARIS.7 ¶19]

Identified in the text with: [Eagles](#e344)

### Sulphur — *soufre* {#e159}

*term; The usual English term.*

One of the two metallic principles: sulphur and mercury, sun and moon, are parents of the stone according to Hermes [PARIS.3 ¶39]; the bull figures Sulphur, the male principle [PARIS.4 ¶21]. The third water is called Mercury or Sulphur according to its quality or aspect [PARIS.2 ¶7]. The mercury-serpent absorbs the metallic sulphur; the compost is cooked into red sulphur, then Elixir, then universal Medicine [PARIS.3 ¶18]. The universal Spirit corporified in minerals is called Sulphur, agent of all metallic tinctures [PARIS.6 ¶23]. Sulphur or philosophical gold serves the multiplications [PARIS.7 ¶27]. The first of three identical operations yields Sulphur, medicine of the first order; the three grenades are fire corporified in this red salt [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. The text links soufre with Κυπρος, meaning dung [INTRO.4 ¶4].

> «nous dirons cependant que l’Esprit universel, corporifié dans les minéraux sous le nom alchimique de Soufre, constitue le principe et l’agent efficace de toutes les teintures métalliques.» (*we will say, however, that the universal Spirit, corporified in minerals under the alchemical name of Sulphur, is the principle and effective agent of all metallic tinctures.*) [PARIS.6 ¶23]

> «La première opération conduit d’abord au Soufre, ou médecine du premier ordre» (*The first operation leads first to the Sulphur, or medicine of the first order*) [BOURGES.2 ¶32]

### Sun — *Soleil* {#e258}

*term; The usual English term.*

Elias, the Sun, God the Father, is the emblem of the vital spirit; united with the Virgin (form) it gives living matter, Jesus [PARIS.1 ¶8]. Hebrew Jes means fire, sun, divinity [PARIS.1 ¶10]. The bull, consecrated to the sun, figures Sulphur, the male principle, since Hermes calls the sun the Father of the stone [PARIS.4 ¶21]. The sun marks gold; on the north portal the central circle symbolizes the Sun among the seven planetary metals [PARIS.6 ¶1; PARIS.6 ¶2; PARIS.6 ¶3; PARIS.6 ¶8]. At Amiens Fulcanelli argues that total absence of sunlight is the primordial condition of all generation, the sun destroying substances too young to resist it [AMIENS ¶37–38].

> «puisque le soleil est dit métaphoriquement, par Hermès, le Père de la pierre.» (*since the sun is metaphorically called by Hermes the Father of the stone.*) [PARIS.4 ¶21]

> «Il importe donc de retenir que le soleil est le destructeur par excellence de toutes les substances trop jeunes, trop faibles pour résister à son pouvoir igné.» (*It is therefore important to remember that the sun is the destroyer par excellence of all substances too young, too weak to resist its fiery power.*) [AMIENS ¶38]

## T

### Table d’Emeraude — The Emerald Tablet — *Table d’Emeraude* {#e490}

*work; Name as spelled in the text; English the usual title.*

Hermes' text, quoted for 'the wind carried it in its belly', which Fulcanelli glosses: the wind is the winged, volatile mercury carrying the reborn gold [BOURGES.2 ¶14].

> «« Le vent, — qui est le mercure ailé et volatil, — l’a porté dans son ventre », nous dit Hermès dans sa Table d’Emeraude.» (*'The wind — which is the winged and volatile mercury — carried it in its belly', Hermes tells us in his Emerald Tablet.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶14]

### Testamentum — Testament — *Testamentum* {#e376}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of Basile Valentin, cited for the statement that the whole body of Vitriol is to be recognised only as a Mirror of philosophical Science, in which our Mercury, Sun and Moon are seen to shine [PARIS.5 ¶4]. Fulcanelli uses it with the Cosmopolite and Moras de Respour to support the name Mirror of Art for the first matter [PARIS.5 ¶4].

> «Le corps entier du Vitriol ne doit être reconnu que pour un Miroir de la Science philosophique» (*The whole body of Vitriol must be recognised only as a Mirror of philosophical Science*) [PARIS.5 ¶4]

### Thales of Miletus — *Thalès de Milet* {#e141}

*person; Name as spelled in the text, with the usual English form.*

Said to have shared Tiresias's knowledge of the Language of the Birds; the text calls him one of several fictitious persons whose names speak eloquently in this science [INTRO.3 ¶9]. Named again among those able to understand the language of the gods [BOURGES.2 ¶21].

> «Il la partageait, dit-on, avec Thalès de Milet, Melampus et Apollonios de Tyane, personnages fictifs dont les noms parlent éloquemment, dans la science qui nous occupe» (*He shared it, they say, with Thales of Miletus, Melampus and Apollonius of Tyana, fictitious persons whose names speak eloquently in the science that concerns us*) [INTRO.3 ¶9]

### Theatrum Chemicum — Chemical Theatre — *Theatrum Chemicum* {#e464}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named only as the collection whose vol. VI (Argentorati, 1613) contains Haymon's Epistola as treatise 192 [AMIENS n7].

> «Haymon, Epistola de Lapidibus Philosophicis. Traité 192, t. VI du Theatrum Chemicum. Argentorati, 1613.» (*Haymon, Epistola de Lapidibus Philosophicis. Treatise 192, vol. VI of the Theatrum Chemicum. Argentorati, 1613.*) [AMIENS n7]

### Theseus — *Thésée* {#e168}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Thésée), with the usual English form.*

His fight with the Minotaur was shown at the centre of the Chartres labyrinth [INTRO.5 ¶1]; Ariadne's thread let him succeed [INTRO.5 ¶4]. The fable teaches true sublimation, 'making the eagle fly': Theseus is θεσ-ειος, organized, manifested light, separating from Ariadne, the spider, the empty husk [PARIS.4 ¶3]. Tristan's myth replicates his: Tristan kills the Morhout as Theseus the Minotaur, hieroglyph of making the Green Lion [BOURGES.1 ¶9].

> «Dans ce cas, Thésée est θεσ-ειος, la lumière organisée, manifestée, qui se sépare d’Ariane, l’araignée qui est au centre de sa toile» (*In this case Theseus is θεσ-ειος, organized, manifested light, which separates from Ariadne, the spider at the centre of her web*) [PARIS.4 ¶3]

> «Le mythe de Tristan de Léonois est une réplique de celui de Thésée.» (*The myth of Tristan of Leonois is a replica of that of Theseus.*) [BOURGES.1 ¶9]

### Thomas Corneille — *Thomas Corneille* {#e403}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Cited as stating that the grand masters of the Rose-Croix were called Brothers of the Cooked Dew, a meaning they themselves gave to the initials F. R. C.; his work is the Dictionnaire des Arts et des Sciences, article Rose-Croix [PARIS.6 ¶24; PARIS.6 n3].

> «Aussi, Thomas Corneille ne nous surprend-il pas lorsqu’il assure qu’on appelait les grands maîtres (139) de la Rose-Croix Frères de la Rosée-Cuite» (*So Thomas Corneille does not surprise us when he assures us that the grand masters of the Rose-Croix were called Brothers of the Cooked Dew*) [PARIS.6 ¶24]

### Three reiterations — *trois réitérations* {#e515}

*term; Literal translation.*

One same procedure repeated three times gives the three philosophic works, after Geber: the first gives the Sulphur (medicine of the first order), the second the Elixir (second order), the third the Philosophers' Stone (third order), differing in quality and power, not nature [BOURGES.2 ¶32]. Marked by '3 R' on the Lallemant ceiling [BOURGES.2 ¶28], by RERE, RER written three times and the three pomegranates [BOURGES.2 ¶31–32], and by the 'trois cuers' of Jacques Cœur's motto [BOURGES.1 ¶4]. At Amiens: a third reiteration of the cock-fox technique gives the stone, 'the cock will have crowed three times' [AMIENS ¶15]. The closing advice: calcine, return the pure part, reiterate a third time [BOURGES.2 ¶36].

> «La première opération conduit d’abord au Soufre, ou médecine du premier ordre ; la seconde opération, absolument semblable à la première, fournit l’Elixir, ou médecine du second ordre» (*The first operation leads first to the Sulphur, or medicine of the first order; the second operation, exactly like the first, yields the Elixir, or medicine of the second order*) [BOURGES.2 ¶32]

> «propriétés qu’il vous faudra augmenter à l’aide d’une troisième réitération de la même technique.» (*properties you will have to increase by a third reiteration of the same technique.*) [AMIENS ¶15]

### Tincture — *teinture* {#e336}

*term; The usual English term.*

Paracelsus (quoted): matter that reaches the fourth colour by reverberation is the tincture of things of its kind [PARIS.3 ¶30]. Stated: the Universal Spirit, corporified in minerals under the name of Sulphur, is the principle and active agent of all metallic tinctures [PARIS.6 ¶23]. An old axiom: every dry tincture is useless in its dryness, so the earth or salt must be redissolved in its own water [AMIENS ¶15]. The philosophers' mercury must have the virtue of dyeing, acquired only by preliminary preparations; kermes means 'qui teint en écarlate' [BOURGES.2 ¶22]. Ripley (quoted) calls the Green Lion the means to join tinctures between sun and moon [BOURGES.2 ¶5].

> «l’Esprit universel, corporifié dans les minéraux sous le nom alchimique de Soufre, constitue le principe et l’agent efficace de toutes les teintures métalliques.» (*the Universal Spirit, corporified in minerals under the alchemical name of Sulphur, constitutes the principle and effective agent of all metallic tinctures.*) [PARIS.6 ¶23]

> «un vieil axiome nous apprend que toute teinture sèche est inutile en sa siccité» (*an old axiom teaches us that every dry tincture is useless in its dryness*) [AMIENS ¶15]

### Tiresias — *Tirésias* {#e140}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Tirésias); the proposed form is the usual English spelling.*

Per mythology, the famous seer had perfect knowledge of the Language of the Birds, taught him by Minerva, goddess of Wisdom, and shared it with Thales, Melampus and Apollonius of Tyana, 'fictitious persons whose names speak eloquently' [INTRO.3 ¶9]. He is said to have lost his sight for revealing Olympus's secrets, lived seven, eight or nine human ages, and been man and woman in turn [INTRO.3 n3]. Few self-styled cabalists are Tiresiases able to understand [BOURGES.2 ¶21].

> «La mythologie veut que le célèbre devin Tirésias ait eu une parfaite connaissance de la Langue des Oiseaux, que lui aurait enseignée Minerve, déesse de la Sagesse.» (*Mythology has it that the famous seer Tiresias had a perfect knowledge of the Language of the Birds, which Minerva, goddess of Wisdom, is said to have taught him.*) [INTRO.3 ¶9]

> «Tirésias avait, dit-on, perdu la vue pour avoir dévoilé aux mortels les secrets de l’Olympe.» (*Tiresias had, they say, lost his sight for having revealed to mortals the secrets of Olympus.*) [INTRO.3 n3]

### To know, to be able, to dare, to keep silent — *Scire. Potere. Audere. Tacere.* {#e542}

*term; Literal translation; the text itself renders the four verbs in French (SAVOIR, POUVOIR, OSER, SE TAIRE, CONCL ¶9–16).*

Epigraph of the conclusion, attributed to Zoroaster [CONCL ¶1–2]. The conclusion unfolds it: observation, reasoning and meditation lead to KNOWING; imitation of nature, skill and long experience give POWER; patience, will and faith allow him to DARE; and the successful Sage, turning to the humble, keeps his vow of silence: the Adept must forever KEEP SILENT [CONCL ¶8–16]. Fulcanelli denies Cambriel's claim that the Saint-Marcel statue enacts 'le tacere de Zoroastre' [PARIS.7 ¶35].

> «Scire. Potere. Audere. Tacere.» (*To know. To be able. To dare. To keep silent.*) [CONCL ¶1]

> «Disciple anonyme et muet de la Nature éternelle, apôtre de l’éternelle Charité, il restera fidèle à son vœu de silence.» (*Anonymous and mute disciple of eternal Nature, apostle of eternal Charity, he will remain faithful to his vow of silence.*) [CONCL ¶14]

### Tourbe — The Turba — *Tourbe* {#e070}

*work; Name as spelled in the text; 'Turba' is the usual English form.*

Canseliet cites the Tourbe, through the voice of Baleus: the mother takes pity on her child, but the child is very hard toward her; he applies this to Nature and mankind [PREF3 ¶13].

> «Plusieurs alchimistes ont dit, et la Tourbe en particulier, par la voix de Baleus, que “la mère prend pitié de son enfant, mais que celui-ci est très dur envers elle.”» (*Several alchemists have said, and the Turba in particular, through the voice of Baleus, that 'the mother takes pity on her child, but the child is very hard toward her.'*) [PREF3 ¶13]

### Touvabarayougam — *Touvabarayougam* {#e538}

*term; Name as spelled in the text.*

The third age, that of bronze (airain): the cow of Virtue is reduced to two feet [HENDAYE ¶14].

> «Pendant la durée du Touvabarayougam, ou troisième âge, qui est celui d’airain, elle est réduite à deux pieds.» (*During the Touvabarayougam, or third age, which is that of bronze, she is reduced to two feet.*) [HENDAYE ¶14]

### Tower of Babel — *tour de Babel* {#e137}

*term; The usual English term.*

Tradition says men spoke the Language of the Birds before the Tower of Babel, which caused its perversion and its total oblivion for most [INTRO.3 ¶8]. The note reads it cabalistically: 'tour', the turn 'ba' used for 'bel' [INTRO.3 n2]. The tower belonged to the Chaldean ziggurats, faced in black, white and red-purple [PARIS.3 ¶31].

> «La Tradition nous assure que les hommes la parlaient avant l’édification de la tour de Babel, cause de sa perversion et, pour le plus grand nombre, de l’oubli total de cet idiome sacré.» (*Tradition assures us that men spoke it before the building of the tower of Babel, the cause of its perversion and, for most, of the total oblivion of this sacred idiom.*) [INTRO.3 ¶8]

> «Le tour, la tournure ba employée pour bel.» (*The turn, the turn of phrase ba used for bel.*) [INTRO.3 n2]

### Tradition — *Tradition* {#e005}

*term; Literal translation.*

The esoteric or philosophical transmission the book appeals to. The Gothic cathedral is 'sanctuary of the Tradition, of Science and of Art' [INTRO.1 ¶6]. The Tradition assures that men spoke the Language of the Birds before Babel [INTRO.3 ¶8] and that the Magi were guided by a star [BOURGES.2 ¶14]. Fulcanelli calls himself only 'a marker set on the great road of the esoteric Tradition' [BOURGES.2 ¶36]. Canseliet: the 'philosophical Tradition' holds that the adept is reborn like the phoenix [PREF1 ¶3]; the pseudonym was 'wanted by the Tradition' [PREF2 ¶2]. Canseliet also says the Hebrew Kabbalah means 'tradition' [PREF2 ¶45].

> «Pour nous, qui ne sommes qu’un jalon posé sur la grande voie de la Tradition ésotérique, nous ne réclamons rien, ni souvenir ni reconnaissance» (*As for us, who are only a marker set on the great road of the esoteric Tradition, we ask for nothing, neither remembrance nor gratitude*) [BOURGES.2 ¶36]

> «Sanctuaire de la Tradition, de la Science et de l’Art, la cathédrale gothique ne doit pas être regardée comme un ouvrage uniquement dédié à la gloire du christianisme» (*Sanctuary of the Tradition, of Science and of Art, the Gothic cathedral must not be regarded as a work dedicated solely to the glory of Christianity*) [INTRO.1 ¶6]

### Traité de l'Appropriation — Treatise on Appropriation — *Traité de l'Appropriation* {#e416}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Work of J.-F. Henckel, Paris, Thomas Hérissant, 1760, p. 375, § 416, the source of the passage on the four-day stone [PARIS.7 n7; PARIS.7 ¶10].

> «J.-F. Henckel, Traité de l’Appropriation. Paris, Thomas Hérissant, 1760, p. 375, § 416.» (*J.-F. Henckel, Treatise on Appropriation. Paris, Thomas Hérissant, 1760, p. 375, § 416.*) [PARIS.7 n7]

### Treasure of treasures — *trésor des trésors* {#e020}

*term; Literal translation.*

Phrase of the anonymous letter to Fulcanelli: having had the gift of God, the recipient now possesses 'the treasure of treasures' [PREF2 ¶8]. Interpreted: it designates what the achieved Work yields, since the same letter speaks of the work accomplished and the glass become luminous as the sun [PREF2 ¶11].

> «Enfin, vous possédez le trésor des trésors, rendons grâce à la Divine lumière qui vous en a fait participant.» (*At last you possess the treasure of treasures; let us give thanks to the Divine light which has made you a sharer in it.*) [PREF2 ¶8]

### Tree of Life — *Arbre de Vie* {#e371}

*term; The usual English term for it.*

The Tree of Life stands beside the Initiator on the second lower medallion of Paris and marks the end of the work [PARIS.5 ¶1, PARIS.5 ¶3]. The text identifies it with the Elixir or Philosophers' Stone: the vile and despised subject, the Mirror of Art, becomes in the end the Tree of Life, masterpiece of nature aided by human industry [PARIS.5 ¶4].
