> «Ce sujet, si vulgaire et méprisé, devient par la suite l’Arbre de Vie, Elixir ou Pierre philosophale, chef-d’oeuvre de la nature aidée par l’industrie humaine» (*This subject, so common and despised, afterwards becomes the Tree of Life, Elixir or Philosophers' Stone, masterpiece of nature aided by human industry*) [PARIS.5 ¶4]

Identified in the text with: [Elixir](#e247), [Philosophers' stone](#e215)

### Triomphe hermétique — The Hermetic Triumph — *Triomphe hermétique* {#e310}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

Book of Limojon de Saint-Didier, also titled 'la Pierre philosophale victorieuse', Amsterdam, Weitsten, 1699, and Desbordes, 1710, containing the Ancienne Guerre des Chevaliers with his commentary [PARIS.3 n8].

> «Augmentée d’un commentaire par Limojon de Saint-Didier, dans le Triomphe hermétique ou la Pierre philosophale victorieuse.» (*Augmented with a commentary by Limojon de Saint-Didier, in the Hermetic Triumph or the Victorious Philosophers' Stone.*) [PARIS.3 n8]

### Tristan of Lyonesse — *Tristan de Léonois* {#e479}

*person; Name as spelled in the text; English the usual form.*

Shown with Yseult on a corbel of the Palais Jacques-Cœur, in the forest of Morois, both gazing at a hollow stone [BOURGES.1 ¶7–8]. His myth is a replica of Theseus': he kills the Morhout as Theseus the Minotaur [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. It is the hieroglyph of the making of the Green Lion, whence his name Léonois or Léonnais, taught by Basile Valentin as the fight of eagle and dragon [BOURGES.1 ¶9]. The dissolvent so made reincrudes natural gold; this rejuvenation of the king is personified by Tristan, nephew of King Mark, uncle and nephew being chemically one thing [BOURGES.1 ¶10].

> «Nous retrouvons ici l’hiéroglyphe de fabrication du Lion vert, — d’où le nom de Léonois ou Léonnais porté par Tristan, — laquelle est enseignée par Basile Valentin sous la lutte des deux champions, l’aigle et le dragon.» (*We find here the hieroglyph of the making of the Green Lion — whence the name Léonois or Léonnais borne by Tristan — which Basile Valentin teaches under the fight of the two champions, the eagle and the dragon.*) [BOURGES.1 ¶9]

### Trédayougam — *Trédayougam* {#e537}

*term; Name as spelled in the text.*

The second age, corresponding to the silver age: Virtue weakens and the cow stands on three feet [HENDAYE ¶14].

> «Dans le Trédayougam ou second âge, lequel correspond à l’âge d’argent, elle s’affaiblit et ne se tient plus que sur trois pieds.» (*In the Trédayougam, or second age, which corresponds to the silver age, she weakens and stands on only three feet.*) [HENDAYE ¶14]

### Typus Mundi — The Type of the World — *Typus Mundi* {#e513}

*work; Name as spelled in the text.*

Small book, work of some Jesuit Fathers (Antuerpiæ, Joan. Cnobbaert, 1627) [BOURGES.2 n13], which takes up the subject of the angel who 'turns the world' like a top, shown on the Lallemant ceiling [BOURGES.2 ¶28].

> «l’ange « qui fait tourner le monde » à la façon d’une toupie, sujet repris et développé dans un petit livre intitulé : Typus Mundi, œuvre de quelques Pères Jésuites» (*the angel 'who makes the world turn' like a top, a subject taken up and developed in a small book entitled Typus Mundi, the work of some Jesuit Fathers*) [BOURGES.2 ¶28]

## U

### Universal solvent — *dissolvant universel* {#e321}

*term; Literal translation.*

The secret agent of the Work. The Préceptes du Père Abraham (quoted) call the primitive celestial water the universal solvent, in which sun and moon bathe and dissolve [PARIS.3 ¶21]. Fulcanelli: it is the secret fire enclosed in a water; the matter for preparing it is the subject of the fourth figure, the Ram [PARIS.3 ¶23]. The ninth figure reveals more of its fabrication: the materials of the hermetic vessel (oak), the spirit or 'feu de nature' spread over the globe which the artist must capture, and a receptive body, the magnet in the belly of Aries [PARIS.4 ¶13]. The Clef du Cabinet hermétique (quoted) calls it the 'Dissolvant général de toute la Nature' [PARIS.4 ¶14]. Stated: the needed 'servus fugitivus' is a mineral, metallic water, solid, brittle, stone-like and easily liquefied; this coagulated water in stony form is the Alkahest and the universal solvent [PARIS.5 ¶7]. This uncommon solvent allows the reincrudation of natural gold [BOURGES.1 ¶10]. The occult fountain of the Paris portal is a dissolvent able to penetrate all metals, gold in particular [PARIS.2 ¶6]. Identified with the Alkahest (e340).

> «C’est cette eau coagulée sous forme de masse pierreuse qui est l’Alkaest et le Dissolvant universel.» (*It is this water, coagulated in the form of a stony mass, that is the Alkahest and the universal Solvent.*) [PARIS.5 ¶7]

> «ce feu secret enclos dans une eau, qui constitue le dissolvant universel.» (*this secret fire enclosed in a water, which constitutes the universal solvent.*) [PARIS.3 ¶23]

Identified in the text with: [Alkahest](#e340)

### Universal spirit — *esprit universel* {#e038}

*term; Literal translation.*

The spirit of the world condensed in the Work. A Notre-Dame relief shows its condensation, forming the 'Bath of the stars' where chemical sun and moon bathe [PARIS.6 ¶22]. Corporified in minerals under the name Sulphur, it is the principle of all metallic tinctures; it is obtained only by decomposing the body [PARIS.6 ¶23]. The mineral blood is a condensation of the universal spirit, 'soul of all things', which happens at night and most in spring, hence May Dew [PARIS.6 ¶24]. At Amiens an Adept watches celestial dew fall on a mass, Magnesia or philosophical Magnet [AMIENS ¶34]; the nostoc is called its receptacle [AMIENS ¶35]. Read cabalistically, 'cuer' gives the Universal Spirit (ray of light) [BOURGES.1 ¶4]. The Chevalier Inconnu speaks of the 'âme générale du monde' fixed in common gold [PARIS.3 ¶8].

> «nous dirons cependant que l’Esprit universel, corporifié dans les minéraux sous le nom alchimique de Soufre, constitue le principe et l’agent efficace de toutes les teintures métalliques.» (*we will say, however, that the universal Spirit, corporified in minerals under the alchemical name of Sulphur, is the principle and effective agent of all metallic tinctures.*) [PARIS.6 ¶23]

> «que le sang minéral dont ils avaient besoin pour animer le corps fixe et inerte de l’or n’était qu’une condensation de l’esprit universel, âme de toute chose» (*that the mineral blood they needed to animate the fixed and inert body of gold was only a condensation of the universal spirit, soul of all things*) [PARIS.6 ¶24]

## T

### The Unknown Knight — *Chevalier Inconnu* {#e296}

*person; Name as spelled in the text, with our literal English.*

Author of La Nature à découvert (Aix, 1669) [PARIS.3 n2]. Quoted: in the second operation the artist fixes the general soul of the world in common gold; the putrefaction there, called the Raven's Head, is very long, and is followed by a third multiplication [PARIS.3 ¶8]. Fulcanelli draws from this that two successive operations are meant, one ending and the next beginning after the black colour, which is not the case of the coction [PARIS.3 ¶9].

> «Dans cette dite opération, la putréfaction, qu’ils appellent la Tête de Corbeau, est très longue.» (*In this said operation, the putrefaction, which they call the Raven's Head, is very long.*) [PARIS.3 ¶8]

## A

### A vaillans cuers riens impossible — To valiant hearts nothing is impossible — *A vaillans cuers riens impossible* {#e477}

*work; Motto as spelled in the text; English ours.*

Jacques Cœur's motto, presented as a rebus around three hearts [BOURGES.1 ¶4]. Read cabalistically, with 'cuer' in the spelling of the time, it yields: the statement of the universal Spirit (ray of light); the common name of the basic matter worked (iron); and the three reiterations needed to perfect both Magisteries (the three hearts) [BOURGES.1 ¶4]. The text gives these results without showing the word-steps.

> «En effet, lisons cuer avec l’orthographe de l’époque, et nous obtiendrons à la fois : 1° l’énoncé de l’Esprit universel (rayon de lumière) ; 2° le nom vulgaire de la matière basique ouvrée (le fer)» (*Indeed, let us read cuer with the spelling of the period, and we shall obtain at once: 1st the statement of the universal Spirit (ray of light); 2nd the common name of the basic matter worked (iron)*) [BOURGES.1 ¶4]

## V

### Varro — *Varron* {#e195}

*person; Name as spelled in the text (Varron; Varro in the footnote).*

Author of the Antiquitates rerum humanarum, who recalls the legend of Aeneas and gives as its reason the star of Venus seen daily until the Laurentine fields [INTRO.7 ¶1–2]. The footnote cites 'Varro, dans Servius, Æneid, t. III, p. 386' [INTRO.7 n2]. The French gloss of the Latin is the book's.

> «Ex quo de Troja est egressus Æneas, Veneris eum per diem quotidie stellam vidisse, donec ad agrum Laurentum veniret, in quo eam non vidit ulterius» (*From the time Aeneas left Troy, he saw by day, every day, the star of Venus, until he came to the Laurentine field, where he saw it no more (our rendering of the book's French gloss)*) [INTRO.7 ¶2]

### Vas spirituale (spiritual vessel) — *Vas spirituale* {#e254}

*term; Latin kept; the text glosses it as the Vase that contains the Spirit of things [PARIS.1 ¶7].*

Litany title of the Virgin, which the text glosses as the Vase containing the Spirit of things, the very essence of things [PARIS.1 ¶7]. It is followed by Etteilla's account of a vase full of celestial-astral liquor on the Magi's table, where 'the secret, the mystery was in the vase' [PARIS.1 ¶7].

> «les Litanies nous apprennent que la Vierge est le Vase qui contient l’Esprit des choses : Vas spirituale.» (*the Litanies teach us that the Virgin is the Vessel that contains the Spirit of things: Vas spirituale.*) [PARIS.1 ¶7]

### Veau d'Or (Vitulus Aureus) — The Golden Calf — *Veau d'Or* {#e049}

*work; Name as spelled in the text with its Latin title; English is our literal translation.*

Work of Jean-Frédéric Helvetius, from which Canseliet translates the account of Gril's star [PREF2 ¶40, PREF2 ¶41]; its first edition, of about 1664, is called unfindable [PREF2 n2].

> «Vers 1664 qui est l’année de l’édition princeps et introuvable du Vitulus Aureus.» (*Around 1664, which is the year of the first and unfindable edition of the Vitulus Aureus.*) [PREF2 n2]

### Venus — *Vénus* {#e157}

*term; The usual English name; the text gives Cypris, Greek Κυπρις, glossed 'l'impure' [INTRO.4 ¶4].*

The hermetic equivalent of the ank is the emblem of Venus or Cypris, common copper, which some rendered as airain and laiton to veil it [INTRO.4 ¶4]. The text links Κυπρος with Σουϕρος, sulphur, meaning dung [INTRO.4 ¶4]. In the planetary verse Venus figures airain (bronze/copper) [PARIS.6 ¶4]; per the Cosmopolite, Mars converts easily into Venus but not the reverse, and on the sarcophagus of the Notre-Dame north portal Mars and Venus flank the Sun [PARIS.6 ¶1, ¶2, ¶7, ¶8]. Aeneas saw the star of Venus by day until Laurentum (Varro, QUOTED) [INTRO.7 ¶1, INTRO.7 ¶2].

> «D’autre part, l’équivalent hermétique du signe ank est l’emblème de Vénus ou Cypris (en grec Κυπρις, l’impure), le cuivre vulgaire que certains, pour voiler davantage le sens, ont traduit par airain et laiton.» (*Moreover, the hermetic equivalent of the ank sign is the emblem of Venus or Cypris (Greek Κυπρις, the impure one), common copper, which some, to veil the sense further, have rendered as bronze and latten.*) [INTRO.4 ¶4]

> «Ce qu’est Saturne au plomb, Vénus l’est à l’airain ;» (*What Saturn is to lead, Venus is to bronze;*) [PARIS.6 ¶4]

### Verbum dimissum (the abandoned Word) — *Verbum dimissum* {#e003}

*term; Latin kept; the text equates it with the 'Parole perdue' (Lost Word) [PARIS.6 ¶25]; 'abandoned' renders dimissum, and the text cites Bernard Trévisan's book as La Parole délaissée, 'the abandoned Word' [PARIS.3 n4].*

Fulcanelli calls the extraction of the May Dew (Rosée de Mai) from a particular, abject body 'the Verbum dimissum of the Trévisan', the Lost Word of the medieval freemasons, which all hermetic fraternities hoped to recover and whose search was their whole purpose; he says here he touches the highest secret of the Work and keeps his oath [PARIS.6 ¶25]. Before the 'Secret of Secrets' he again names this Verbum dimissum, which Jesus entrusted to his Apostles, citing St Paul on the 'secret hidden from all ages' [AMIENS ¶22, AMIENS ¶23]. Canseliet: Fulcanelli's friends awaited from him the resolution of the Verbum dimissum [PREF1 ¶2]; Freemasonry still seeks it, while the Church which possesses this Word is abandoning it [PREF3 ¶14]. The text identifies it with the Lost Word (e050).

> «Que pouvons-nous donc dire ici même, en face du Secret des Secrets, devant ce Verbum dimissum dont nous avons déjà fait mention, et que Jésus confia à ses Apôtres, comme le témoigne saint Paul» (*What then can we say here, facing the Secret of Secrets, before this Verbum dimissum which we have already mentioned, and which Jesus entrusted to his Apostles, as St Paul bears witness*) [AMIENS ¶22]

Identified in the text with: [Lost Word](#e050)

### Vessel — *vaisseau* {#e356}

*term; Literal translation.*

The indispensable and very secret vessel of the Work, given varied names to hide both its use and its composition; it is generally called philosophical egg and Green Lion [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. It is considered in its matter and in its form: 'vase de nature' and 'vase de l'art' [BOURGES.2 ¶6]. Stated: RER is the vessel; it serves to cook and to unite radically the compost RERE [BOURGES.2 ¶34, ¶36]. No success is possible without perfect knowledge of the Vessel of the Philosophers and how to make it; the artist must make it himself; Pontanus failed two hundred times before knowing it; its shell was called 'secretum secretorum' [BOURGES.2 ¶34]. Fulcanelli advises against Basile Valentin's saying that whoever has the matter will find a pot to cook it [BOURGES.2 ¶34], points to the potter's art rather than glassmaking, and gives the sequence: make a vessel, then the compound, seal, heat to calcination, repeat three times [BOURGES.2 ¶36]. At Paris a woman holds an oak stave showing the materials of the hermetic vessel [PARIS.4 ¶13]; the Clef du Cabinet hermétique (quoted) describes a vessel of old oak like a barrel [PARIS.4 ¶14]. In the short way a simple crucible replaces the glass vase: one vessel, one matter, one furnace [PARIS.7 ¶7]. The Virgin is the 'Vase' containing the Spirit of things [PARIS.1 ¶7].

> «qu’il sera impossible d’obtenir le moindre succès dans l’Œuvre si l’on a pas une connaissance parfaite de ce qu’est le Vase des Philosophes ni de quelle manière il faut le fabriquer.» (*that it will be impossible to obtain the least success in the Work without perfect knowledge of what the Vessel of the Philosophers is and of how it must be made.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶34]

> «Quelques conseils encore ; cherchez tout d’abord RER, c'est-à-dire le vaisseau.» (*A few more pieces of advice: seek first RER, that is, the vessel.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶36]

### Vessel of nature — *vase de nature* {#e487}

*term; Literal translation.*

The vessel of the Work is seen two ways: in its matter, as 'vase de nature', and in its form, as 'vase de l’art' [BOURGES.2 ¶6]. The descriptions of the vessel called œuf philosophique and Lion vert (Batsdorff, the Cosmopolite, Aurach, the Toison d'Or, Ripley) concern its nature [BOURGES.2 ¶5–6]. The vessel of art is the egg: spherical or ovoid, clear thick glass (some advise Lorraine glass), neck long or short, sealable at the enameller's lamp [BOURGES.2 ¶6]. Interpreted: the vessel of nature belongs with the secret vessel RER, which the artist must know and make himself [BOURGES.2 ¶34–36].

> «D’après ces renseignements, il appert que le vase est doublement envisagé, et dans sa matière et dans sa forme, d’une part à l’état de vase de nature, de l’autre comme vase de l’art.» (*From this information it appears that the vessel is considered in two ways, both in its matter and in its form: on the one hand as vessel of nature, on the other as vessel of art.*) [BOURGES.2 ¶6]

### Victor Hugo — *Victor Hugo* {#e120}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Quoted with approval: Notre-Dame de Paris is the most satisfying summary of hermetic science, of which Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie was a complete hieroglyph [INTRO.2 ¶4]. Quoted on Guillaume de Paris, damned for attaching so infernal a frontispiece to the holy poem of the building [PARIS.6 ¶18]. He admired the two angels of the old Sainte-Chapelle portal, one with its hand in a vase, the other in a cloud [PARIS.8 ¶8].

> «comme l’a dit Victor Hugo, « l’abrégé le plus satisfaisant de la science hermétique, dont l’église de Saint-Jacques-la Boucherie était un hiéroglyphe si complet ».» (*as Victor Hugo said, 'the most satisfying summary of hermetic science, of which the church of Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie was so complete a hieroglyph'.*) [INTRO.2 ¶4]

> «« lequel, dit Victor Hugo, a sans doute été damné pour avoir attaché un si infernal frontispice au saint poème que chante éternellement le reste de l’édifice »» (*'who,' says Victor Hugo, 'was no doubt damned for having attached so infernal a frontispiece to the holy poem that the rest of the building sings eternally'*) [PARIS.6 ¶18]

### Vinceslas Lavinius de Moravie — *Vinceslas Lavinius de Moravie* {#e033}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Named once by Canseliet as speaking of the 'terrestrial heaven' in which the hermetic star shines, a subject Jacobus Tollius dwells on [PREF2 ¶27].

> «qu’on s’enquière de ce ciel terrestre dont parle Vinceslas Lavinius de Moravie et au sujet duquel s’appesantit Jacobus Tollius» (*that one inquire into this terrestrial heaven of which Vinceslas Lavinius of Moravia speaks and on which Jacobus Tollius dwells*) [PREF2 ¶27]

### Viollet-le-Duc — *Viollet-le-Duc* {#e083}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

One of the architects who restored Notre-Dame; art owes them some gratitude, but Science will never recover what it lost [INTRO.2 ¶7]. Canseliet: with Lassus he replaced the St Marcel statue with a copy around 1850 and moved the original to Cluny [PREF3 ¶23, PREF3 ¶45, PREF3 ¶48]; Grillot de Givry's account of his role is quoted and contested [PREF3 ¶28].

> «Et, si l’art doit quelque reconnaissance aux éminents architectes Toussaint, Geffroy Dechaume, Bœswillwald, Viollet-le-Duc et Lassus qui restaurèrent la basilique, odieusement profanée par l’Ecole, la Science ne retrouvera jamais ce qu’elle a perdu.» (*And if art owes some gratitude to the eminent architects Toussaint, Geffroy Dechaume, Bœswillwald, Viollet-le-Duc and Lassus who restored the basilica, odiously profaned by the School, Science will never recover what it has lost.*) [INTRO.2 ¶7]

### Virgin — *Vierge* {#e031}

*term; Literal translation.*

The Virgin-Mother, unveiled, is the personification of the primitive substance used by the creating Principle [PARIS.1 ¶5]; the Vase containing the Spirit of things [PARIS.1 ¶7]; the Seat of Wisdom, i.e. the Subject of hermetic Science; in metallic symbolism the Moon receiving the Sun's rays; Mary represents form, the Sun the vital spirit, Jesus the spirit incarnate [PARIS.1 ¶8]. Cathedrals are temples of the Mother [PARIS.1 ¶1]. Black Virgins figure the primitive earth, the first matter [INTRO.8 ¶4]. She is always in blue, corresponding to black [PARIS.3 ¶31], feet on the lunar crescent [AMIENS ¶39]. Canseliet: the 'mystical Virgin' is both 'our mother' and the 'hermetic sea'; the star above her announces conception and reflects the other star [PREF2 ¶26].

> «Car la Vierge-Mère, dépouillée de son voile symbolique, n’est autre chose que la personnification de la substance primitive dont se servit pour réaliser ses desseins, le Principe créateur de tout ce qui est.» (*For the Virgin-Mother, stripped of her symbolic veil, is nothing other than the personification of the primitive substance which the creating Principle of all that is used to carry out its designs.*) [PARIS.1 ¶5]

> «Les mêmes textes appellent encore Marie le Siège de la Sagesse, en d’autres termes le Sujet de la Science hermétique, de la sapience universelle.» (*The same texts also call Mary the Seat of Wisdom, in other words the Subject of the hermetic Science, of universal wisdom.*) [PARIS.1 ¶8]

### Virgin's Milk — *Lait de la Vierge* {#e268}

*term; The usual English term.*

The liquor extracted from 'our Magnesia', the matter of the Work [PARIS.2 ¶7]. Fulcanelli notes Greek gala means milk and that the mercury is also called Lait de Vierge (lac virginis) [BOURGES.2 ¶23]. Related images: the Azoth's siren pouring milk from her breasts into the sea [PARIS.2 ¶8] and the Brixen painting where the Virgin's milk and Christ's blood mingle as the Fountain of Life [PARIS.2 ¶10; PARIS.2 n6].

> «Or, nous avons vu que les anciens auteurs appellent la matière de l’Œuvre notre Magnésie et que la liqueur extraite de cette magnésie est dite Lait de la Vierge.» (*Now we have seen that the old authors call the matter of the Work our Magnesia, and that the liquor extracted from this magnesia is called the Virgin's Milk.*) [PARIS.2 ¶7]

> «Gala, en grec, signifie lait, et le mercure est encore appelé Lait de Vierge (lac virginis).» (*Gala in Greek means milk, and the mercury is also called Virgin's Milk (lac virginis).*) [BOURGES.2 ¶23]

### Virgini parituræ — To the Virgin who is to give birth — *Virgini parituræ* {#e217}

*work; Inscription as spelled in the text; the English follows the book's own French gloss [INTRO.8 ¶4].*

Inscription found on the base of both the Isis statues and the Black Virgins [INTRO.8 ¶4], and on the socle of the throne of Notre-Dame-sous-Terre at Chartres [INTRO.8 ¶5]. In Bigarne's account, monuments older than Christianity name the Virgin Virgo paritura, meaning the earth before its fecundation, soon to be animated by the sun's rays [INTRO.8 ¶4].

> «les unes et les autres montrent, sur leur soubassement, la fameuse inscription : Virgini parituræ ; à la Vierge qui doit enfanter.» (*both show on their base the famous inscription: Virgini parituræ; to the Virgin who is to give birth.*) [INTRO.8 ¶4]

> «désignent sous le nom de Virgo paritura, c'est-à-dire la terre avant sa fécondation, et que les rayons du soleil vont bientôt animer» (*designate by the name Virgo paritura, that is, the earth before its fecundation, which the sun's rays will soon animate*) [INTRO.8 ¶4]

### Vitriol — *Vitriol* {#e358}

*term; The usual English term.*

Basile Valentin named the Green Lion 'Vitriol vert' to reveal its hot, ardent, saline nature [PARIS.4 ¶17]. Basile (quoted, Testamentum): the whole body of Vitriol is a Mirror of philosophical science, in which our Mercury, Sun and Moon are seen [PARIS.5 ¶4]. Aurach's glass matras half filled with green liquor is 'le vitriol de Basile Valentin' [BOURGES.2 ¶5]. 'Vitriol végétal' is among the names of the nostoc designating the mineral principle [AMIENS ¶35]. The Philosophical Mercury is 'l'oeuf vitriolé' [PARIS.3 ¶18]. Identified with the Green Lion (e357).

> «Certains Adeptes, Basile Valentin est de ceux-là, l’ont nommé Vitriol vert, pour déceler sa nature chaude, ardente et saline» (*Some Adepts, Basil Valentine among them, have named it Green Vitriol, to reveal its hot, ardent and saline nature*) [PARIS.4 ¶17]

Identified in the text with: [Green Lion](#e357)

### Voile d'Isis — The Veil of Isis — *Voile d’Isis* {#e095}

*work; Title as spelled in the text, with a literal English rendering.*

A publication in which Marcel Clavelle (Jean Reyor) printed, in November 1932, the opinion on the Saint-Marcel statue that Canseliet quotes and rejects [PREF3 ¶34].

> «dans le Voile d’Isis de novembre 1932» (*in the Voile d'Isis of November 1932*) [PREF3 ¶34]

## W

### Weight of nature — *poids de nature* {#e377}

*term; Literal translation.*

The third lower medallion of Paris is the allegory of the weight of nature: the alchemist removes the veil from a balance [PARIS.5 ¶1, PARIS.5 ¶5]. The text cites the authors on the secret of weights (Basile Valentin's white swan to the double fiery man, the Sigillum Sapientum's ratio of one to two, the Cosmopolite, the Aphorismes Basiliens) and rejects the view that nature alone divides the quantities [PARIS.5 ¶6]. Philosophical mercury comes from a set part of sulphur absorbed by a set quantity of mercury, so in the ancient path the proportions must be known; if common gold is used in place of metallic sulphur, the weight becomes a matter of consistency, since excess solvent can be distilled off [PARIS.5 ¶6]. The balance is thus a sign of the ancient path, from which common gold seems excluded [PARIS.5 ¶6].

> «La balance, on le voit, constitue un indice précieux pour la détermination de la voie ancienne, de laquelle l’or paraît devoir être exclu.» (*The balance, as we see, is a valuable clue for determining the ancient path, from which gold appears to be excluded.*) [PARIS.5 ¶6]

### Wet way — *voie humide* {#e100}

*term; The usual English term; literal translation.*

The longer, more thankless of the two ways [PARIS.7 ¶3]; many authors treat only of it [PARIS.7 ¶3]. The 18th-century Encyclopedia calls it longer but more honoured [PARIS.7 ¶4]. Philalethes calls the long way tedious and fit only for the rich; Lenglet-Dufresnoy says it takes nearly eighteen months against eight days for the other [PARIS.7 ¶6]. Cyliani (QUOTED) follows it 'by duty' though the dry way is familiar to him [PARIS.7 ¶8, ¶9]. Canseliet sees both ways in the Saint Marcel legend [PREF3 ¶43].

> «l’une dite voie humide, plis longue mais plus en honneur, et l’autre, ou voie sèche, beaucoup moins appréciée» (*one called the wet way, longer but more honoured, and the other, or dry way, much less esteemed*) [PARIS.7 ¶4]

> «Je suis cette dernière, de préférence, par devoir, quoique la première me soit très familière : elle se fait avec une matière unique.» (*I follow the latter by preference, out of duty, although the former is very familiar to me: it is done with a single matter.*) [PARIS.7 ¶9]

### Wheel — *roue* {#e186}

*term; Literal translation; the text gives Rota as the medieval name of the central rose.*

The wheel is the alchemical hieroglyph of the time needed for the coction of the philosophic matter, and hence of the coction itself [INTRO.6 ¶3]; the steady fire kept through it is the wheel fire, while the secret fire, excited by common heat, is what makes the wheel turn [INTRO.6 ¶3]. At Amiens two wheels beside a watching philosopher are read as the sign of two revolutions acting in succession on the compound, or as the two natures in the conversion [AMIENS ¶6]. Linear and continuous coction needs the double rotation of one wheel, impossible to carve, hence two interlocked wheels: the first is the moist phase (elixation), the second the dry phase (assation), ending when the egg's contents appear granular or powdery [AMIENS ¶7]. Fulcanelli rejects the reading of this quatrefoil as Ezekiel's vision [AMIENS ¶4–6].

> «Or, la roue est l’hiéroglyphe alchimique du temps nécessaire à la coction de la matière philosophale et, par suite, de la coction elle-même.» (*Now the wheel is the alchemical hieroglyph of the time necessary for the coction of the philosophic matter, and consequently of the coction itself.*) [INTRO.6 ¶3]

> «En fait, la coction linéaire et continue exige la double rotation d’une même roue, mouvement impossible à traduire sur la pierre et qui a justifié la nécessité des deux roues enchevêtrées de manière à n’en former qu’une.» (*In fact, linear and continuous coction requires the double rotation of one and the same wheel, a movement impossible to render in stone, which justified the need for two wheels interlocked so as to form only one.*) [AMIENS ¶7]

### Wheel fire — *feu de roue* {#e187}

*term; Literal translation; the text explains the name from the wheel as hieroglyph of the coction.*

The sustained, constant and even fire the artist keeps up night and day during the coction; it is so called because the wheel signifies the coction [INTRO.6 ¶3]. It is distinct from the second agent, the secret or philosophic fire, which makes the wheel turn [INTRO.6 ¶3]. Its gentle, active beginning, symbolized by cold and winter, is the first phase of the second work, when the Rebis in the athanor suffers dislocation and the reign of Saturn begins [PARIS.5 ¶9]. Amiens has an ingenious rendering of it: a philosopher seated, watching, while its slow action goes on; once the labours of Hercules are done, the work is reduced to the ludus puerorum, the upkeep of the fire [AMIENS ¶3; AMIENS ¶6].

> «Le feu soutenu, constant et égal que l’artiste entretient nuit et jour au cours de cette opération, est appelé, pour cette raison, feu de roue.» (*The sustained, constant and even fire that the artist keeps up night and day during this operation is called, for this reason, wheel fire.*) [INTRO.6 ¶3]

> «C’est le début, actif et doux, du feu de roue symbolisé par le froid et par l’hiver» (*It is the beginning, active and gentle, of the wheel fire, symbolized by cold and by winter*) [PARIS.5 ¶9]

### White — *blanc* {#e330}

*term; Literal translation.*

The second principal colour, between black and red [PARIS.3 ¶25]; 'moyenne entre le noir et le rouge' [INTRO.6 ¶2]. As day follows night in Genesis, light follows darkness, with white as its signature; at this stage the matter is free of all impurity, washed and purified, appearing as solid granulations or brilliant corpuscles of adamantine lustre and dazzling whiteness [PARIS.3 ¶28]. Hence white stands for purity, simplicity, innocence, and is the colour of the Initiates [PARIS.3 ¶28]. Pierre Dujols (quoted) links Hebrew hur, heur ('to be white') to 'heureux', calls the 'bienheureux' equivalent of initiate, noble, pure, and the Cathars the Pure [PARIS.3 ¶28]. In Paracelsus (quoted) white follows black [PARIS.3 ¶30]. God is shown in white [PARIS.3 ¶31]. Tollius (quoted): 'l'Eau est blanche' [PARIS.3 ¶38].

> «Parvenue à ce degré, les Sages assurent que leur matière est dégagée de toute impureté, parfaitement lavée et très exactement purifiée.» (*Having reached this stage, the Sages assure us that their matter is freed of all impurity, perfectly washed and very exactly purified.*) [PARIS.3 ¶28]

## Z

### Ziggurats — *Ziguras* {#e338}

*term; The usual English term for the text's 'Ziguras'.*

Chaldean towers, usually of three storeys, to which the Tower of Babel belonged, clad in three colours, black, white and purple-red, cited as an example of the spread of the three colours of the Work [PARIS.3 ¶31].

> «En Chaldée, les Ziguras, qui furent ordinairement des tours à trois étages, et à laquelle appartenait la fameuse Tour de Babel, sont revêtues de trois couleurs : noire, blanche et rouge-pourpre.» (*In Chaldea, the Ziggurats, which were usually three-storeyed towers, and to which the famous Tower of Babel belonged, are clad in three colours: black, white and purple-red.*) [PARIS.3 ¶31]

### Zodiac — *zodiaque* {#e400}

*term; Literal translation.*

On the piers of the Portal of the Virgin the signs run upward Aries, Taurus, Gemini: the spring months that mark the start of the work and the right time for the operations [PARIS.6 ¶20]. Fulcanelli grants the zodiac might be merely astronomical, but Leo takes Cancer's place after Gemini, which he reads as the conjunction of the philosophical ferment (Lion) with the mercurial compound near the end of the fourth month of the first Work [PARIS.6 ¶21]. As a zodiac sign the Bull is the second month of the preparatory operations [PARIS.4 ¶21]. The rose window shows Gemini of the lower zodiac, one ruby, one emerald [PARIS.5 ¶17; PARIS.5 ¶18]. The north portal of Amiens is mostly the zodiac and its rustic scenes [AMIENS ¶16].

> «Or, à Gemini succède Leo, lequel usurpe la place de Cancer, rejeté sur le pilier opposé.» (*Now Gemini is followed by Leo, which usurps the place of Cancer, thrown back onto the opposite pier.*) [PARIS.6 ¶21]

### Zoroaster — *Zoroastre* {#e426}

*person; Name as spelled in the text.*

Fulcanelli denies that the saint Marcel of the Saint Anne porch acts out the 'tacere' of Zoroaster, as Cambriel claimed [PARIS.7 ¶35]. The conclusion opens with the motto Scire. Potere. Audere. Tacere., signed ZOROASTRE [CONCL ¶1; CONCL ¶2].

> «mais qu’il réalise en fait le tacere de Zoroastre, cela n’est pas et ne fut jamais.» (*but that he in fact enacts the tacere of Zoroaster, that is not so and never was.*) [PARIS.7 ¶35]
